CMRE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Case C1

A 26-year-old primigravida at 36 weeks gestation with a low-risk, uncomplicated pregnancy tells her midwife she wants to plan a home birth and asks for the evidence behind this choice before deciding for certain.

  1. The correct answer is D (Informed choice, giving the client balanced evidence so she can decide her own care): informed choice is a foundational pillar of the Canadian midwifery model, requiring midwives to provide complete, unbiased, evidence-based information about all reasonable options. The midwife's role is to educate and support, not to direct the client toward a predetermined outcome. This respects the client's autonomy as the decision-maker in her own care. Home birth for a genuinely low-risk client is a recognized and supported option within this model.
  2. The correct answer is D (Informed choice as an ongoing process rather than a single decision made once): informed choice is not a one-time event completed at a single visit but a continuous process that midwives revisit throughout pregnancy, labour, and postpartum as circumstances change. This allows clients to adapt decisions as new information arises. Continuity of care is a related but distinct concept describing consistency of provider, not the decision-making process itself. Recognizing this distinction supports genuinely client-centred practice.
  3. The correct answer is C (Continuity of care, where a known midwife or small team provides care throughout): continuity of care is a defining feature of the Canadian midwifery model, in which a known midwife or small consistent team follows the client across the prenatal, birth, and postpartum periods. This builds a trusting relationship and supports better recognition of subtle changes over time. It contrasts with fragmented, rotating-provider models common in some other maternity care systems. The other options describe arrangements not typical of Canadian midwifery practice.
Case C4

A midwife is providing care to a 33-year-old woman, gravida 2 para 1, at 39 weeks gestation who develops a blood pressure of 148/96 mmHg with 1+ proteinuria on dipstick at a routine prenatal visit. She has no symptoms and reports normal fetal movement.

  1. The correct answer is D (Initiate a consultation with an obstetrician while continuing to provide midwifery care): new-onset elevated blood pressure with proteinuria at term meets standard criteria for mandatory discussion or consultation under provincial guidelines, since it may indicate emerging preeclampsia. The midwife remains the primary care provider and continues involvement while seeking specialist input. Reassurance without escalation would delay recognition of a potentially serious condition. Waiting until the next visit could allow a worsening condition to go unaddressed.
  2. The correct answer is A (Consultation involves seeking another provider's opinion while the midwife remains the primary care provider): consultation is distinct from transfer of care because the midwife retains primary responsibility and continues to be involved, incorporating the specialist's recommendations into an ongoing plan. Transfer of care, by contrast, shifts primary responsibility to another provider. Clients frequently benefit from consultation without losing their known midwife's continued involvement. Understanding this distinction helps clients make informed decisions about recommended care.
  3. The correct answer is B (The exact blood pressure, proteinuria result, symptoms assessed, and the consultation made): thorough documentation of objective findings, the clinical reasoning applied, and any consultation undertaken is essential to support continuity of care and to demonstrate that appropriate action was taken. Vague or incomplete notes cannot later confirm what was actually assessed or decided. This level of detail also protects both the client and the midwife if care is later reviewed. General or partial notes fail to meet this professional standard.
  4. The correct answer is C (Discuss the risks and benefits again so the client can make a fully informed decision): informed choice requires that clients understand the reasoning behind recommendations well enough to decide freely, even when a specialist has made a suggestion. The midwife's role is to ensure the client has what she needs to decide, not to impose either urgency or delay improperly. Treating a specialist recommendation as automatically final removes the client's right to weigh her own options. Avoiding the topic would also fail to support genuine informed decision-making.
  5. The correct answer is A (Document the informed refusal in detail and continue providing ongoing care and monitoring): clients have the right to decline recommended care after being fully informed, and the midwife's professional obligation is to document this informed refusal thoroughly while continuing to offer safe, respectful care. Withdrawing care as a consequence of the client's decision would be inappropriate and could endanger her wellbeing. Overriding the client's autonomy by proceeding anyway would violate informed choice principles. Unnecessary reporting to a regulatory body is not warranted by an informed refusal alone.
  6. The correct answer is C (Midwifery regulation is provincial or territorial, so scope details vary while CMRE remains national): each province and territory has its own regulatory college that defines the specific scope of practice, prescribing authority, and requirements for midwives within its jurisdiction. The CMRE serves as a shared national entry-to-practice exam recognized across these separate regulatory systems. This structure allows for provincial flexibility while maintaining a consistent baseline competency standard. Assuming a single uniform national scope would misrepresent how Canadian midwifery regulation actually functions.
  7. The correct answer is A (They protect clients and maintain public trust in the profession's accountability and standards): ongoing professional obligations such as liability protection and registration maintenance exist to safeguard clients and uphold the credibility and safety of the profession as a whole. These requirements are not optional once a midwife is practising, and they apply across all practice settings, including hospital-based care. Framing them as revenue-driven or optional misunderstands their genuine regulatory and public protection purpose.
Case C11

A midwife completes a home visit and notes that her documentation for the visit was delayed by two days due to an unusually busy on-call period. She is now updating the client's chart from memory and her handwritten notes taken during the visit.

  1. The correct answer is A (Record the actual date and time of the entry, clearly noting it was completed late): accurate documentation requires that entries reflect the true date and time they are written, with any delay clearly noted, rather than appearing as though they were completed contemporaneously. Back-dating an entry misrepresents the record and can undermine its reliability if care is later reviewed. Skipping documentation for now leaves an incomplete and unsafe record. Combining multiple visits into one vague note fails to capture the detail needed for continuity of care.
  2. The correct answer is D (Draw a single line through the error, write the correction, and initial and date it): the standard method for correcting a documentation error preserves the original entry so it remains legible while clearly showing the correction, who made it, and when. This maintains the integrity and transparency of the health record. Using correction fluid or deleting an entry removes evidence of what was originally recorded, which can raise concerns if the chart is later reviewed. Leaving a known error uncorrected is never appropriate regardless of its perceived significance.
  3. The correct answer is B (Provide timely access to her own health record, since clients have a right to it): clients generally have a legal and professional right to access their own health information within a reasonable timeframe, and midwives are obligated to support this access. Denying access or creating unreasonable barriers, such as excessive fees, would violate this right. Records belong to the client's care relationship, not solely to the practice that created them. Requiring third-party contact before releasing records to the client herself is not an appropriate condition.
  4. The correct answer is C (Performing hand hygiene before and after every client contact, including glove use): consistent hand hygiene before and after every client contact, along with appropriate glove use, is the single most effective and fundamental infection control practice across all clinical settings, including home birth. Reusing gloves between clients or relying on non-sterile household supplies significantly increases the risk of cross-contamination. Cleaning instruments only when visibly soiled ignores the reality that pathogens are not always visible. Standard infection control practice does not depend on the setting being a home rather than a hospital.
  5. The correct answer is D (Wash the site immediately and follow her workplace's needlestick injury protocol): a needlestick injury requires immediate first aid, including washing the site, followed by prompt reporting and follow-up according to established occupational exposure protocols. Delaying this response increases the risk of undiagnosed exposure to bloodborne pathogens. Continuing to attend the birth without addressing the injury places the midwife's own health at unnecessary risk. Reusing the same gloves after an injury without proper attention would compound the safety concern.
  6. The correct answer is C (Bring a fully stocked emergency kit including resuscitation and hemorrhage supplies): emergency preparedness requires that a midwife carry complete, appropriate equipment to every birth, including one for an unfamiliar client, since risk cannot be reliably predicted in advance. Assuming an uncomplicated course based on limited information is an unsafe basis for reduced preparation. Waiting for backup before bringing in essential supplies could delay critical care if an emergency arises quickly. Relying on household supplies alone does not meet the standard required for safe emergency readiness.
  7. The correct answer is C (A clear, rehearsed plan for transport, receiving hospital contact, and required equipment): an effective emergency transfer protocol requires that logistics, communication pathways, and equipment needs be clearly defined and practised in advance, so that a real emergency does not require improvisation. Assuming paramedics alone will manage every detail overlooks the midwife's own role in providing a clear handover and continued care during transport. Delaying activation of the protocol until symptoms are severe reduces the time available to respond safely. Restricting planning discussions to only certain midwives undermines consistent team-wide readiness.
  8. The correct answer is A (Plan realistic travel times and identify backup attendants for difficult weather conditions): proactive planning that accounts for realistic travel conditions and identifies backup attendants directly reduces the risk of delayed arrival, which is an important part of emergency preparedness. Accepting lateness as unavoidable without adjustment fails to address a foreseeable and preventable risk. Removing the second-attendant requirement would reduce safety rather than improve it. Restricting births to daylight hours is not a practical or evidence-based solution to this specific problem.
Case C19

A midwife attending a home birth notes that immediately after delivery of the placenta, the client begins bleeding more heavily than expected, with an estimated blood loss approaching 600 mL and a soft, poorly contracted uterus on palpation.

  1. The correct answer is C (A soft, poorly contracted uterus consistent with uterine atony as the likely cause): a soft, poorly contracted uterus following delivery of the placenta is the classic finding associated with uterine atony, which is the most common cause of postpartum hemorrhage. Recognizing this finding promptly guides the midwife toward appropriate immediate management. A firm, well-contracted uterus would point away from atony as the cause. The other findings described are not consistent with the clinical picture presented in this case.
  2. The correct answer is D (Perform uterine massage and administer an appropriate uterotonic medication promptly): the standard immediate response to postpartum hemorrhage from suspected uterine atony includes fundal massage combined with prompt administration of an appropriate uterotonic medication to promote uterine contraction. Waiting to see if bleeding resolves on its own delays essential treatment during a time-critical emergency. Prioritizing the newborn assessment over an active maternal hemorrhage misallocates urgent attention. Repositioning alone while only planning assessment afterward does not address the underlying cause of bleeding.
  3. The correct answer is B (Call for emergency transport to hospital while continuing resuscitative measures on site): when bleeding continues and the client shows signs of hemodynamic compromise such as tachycardia despite initial management, urgent transfer to hospital is required while ongoing resuscitative measures are maintained during preparation and transport. Continuing to observe at home in this situation places the client at significant risk. Delaying the call for help until additional treatment is completed wastes critical time. Relying on informal transport arrangements instead of emergency services is unsafe for a client who may be deteriorating.
  4. The correct answer is D (A detailed written record of the event, actions taken, and outcomes for the chart): thorough documentation of an emergency event, including the timeline, interventions performed, and outcomes, is an essential part of professional follow-up that supports continuity of care and later review if needed. Assuming no further action is needed once the emergency resolves overlooks this professional responsibility. Relying only on verbal summaries among colleagues does not create a reliable record. Documentation belongs in the midwife's own chart in addition to any hospital-based reporting.
  5. The correct answer is B (Regularly checking expiry dates and storage conditions to keep medications usable): emergency medications must be regularly checked for expiry and stored under appropriate conditions to ensure they remain safe and effective when needed in a true emergency. Restocking only once a year without regular checks risks carrying expired or degraded medications between those intervals. Storing medications in locations chosen mainly for convenience rather than temperature needs can compromise their effectiveness. Assuming expired medications remain fully effective is an unsafe assumption that could delay appropriate treatment.
  6. The correct answer is B (Disciplinary action aimed at protecting the public and restoring safe practice standards): when a regulatory college confirms that a midwife practised outside her authorized scope, the resulting response is intended to protect the public and support a return to safe, accountable practice, which may include remediation, education, or other proportionate measures. Treating scope of practice as merely a suggestion misunderstands its binding regulatory nature. Automatic criminal charges are not the typical or appropriate response to a scope-of-practice finding. Immediate loss of licensure with only limited opportunity for review is not consistent with typical regulatory processes.
  7. The correct answer is C (Review her provincial college's scope of practice regulations before prescribing anything): when uncertain about her authority to prescribe a specific medication, a midwife's appropriate first step is to consult her own provincial college's regulations, since scope of practice and prescribing authority vary by jurisdiction. Assuming authority based on general impressions or another province's rules risks practising outside her legal scope. Directing the client to an unrelated pharmacist does not resolve the underlying question of the midwife's own authority. Careful verification protects both the client and the midwife.
Case C26

A midwife who trained and was registered in Alberta accepts a new position with a midwifery practice group in Nova Scotia. She begins preparing the paperwork needed to continue practising legally in her new province.

  1. The correct answer is D (Apply for registration with the Nova Scotia midwifery regulatory college before practising): because midwifery regulation is provincial, a midwife moving to practise in a new province must apply for and obtain registration with that province's regulatory college before legally attending births there. Registration does not automatically transfer between provinces despite shared national exam standards. There is no separate national licence issued by the Canadian Midwifery Regulators Council that substitutes for provincial registration. Practising before completing this registration would be outside her legal authority in the new jurisdiction.
  2. The correct answer is B (It provides one common entry standard that provincial colleges can rely on for registration): the CMRE exists as a shared national exam so that provincial regulatory colleges have a consistent, recognized measure of entry-level competency, even though each college retains its own registration authority and specific requirements. It does not replace provincial colleges or grant automatic licensure across the country. It is not limited only to internationally trained midwives, since Canadian-trained midwives also write the CMRE. Understanding this structure clarifies how national standards and provincial regulation work together.
  3. The correct answer is C (To ensure clients and the midwife are protected if a claim arises from care): professional liability protection exists to provide financial and legal protection for both clients and midwives in the event that a claim arises related to the care provided. It does not shield a midwife from being questioned about her clinical decisions, nor does it replace the ongoing need for accurate documentation. It also does not prevent legitimate complaints from being formally investigated. This requirement is a standard and necessary part of safe, accountable practice.
  4. The correct answer is B (It supports maintenance of current, evidence-based competence throughout her career): ongoing continuing education requirements exist to help midwives maintain up-to-date, evidence-based knowledge and skills throughout their entire career, not just during an initial period after registration. Framing this as purely optional or limited to the first year misrepresents its continuous nature. It applies broadly to all practising midwives, not only those involved in teaching. This obligation reflects the profession's commitment to ongoing quality and safety.
Case C30

A client planning a home birth is in active labour at 5 cm dilation when the fetal heart rate drops to 90 beats per minute and remains low despite maternal position change. The midwife has been managing this labour independently up to this point.

  1. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent transfer to hospital while continuing intrauterine resuscitation measures): a sustained fetal heart rate of 90 beats per minute despite maternal repositioning is a concerning finding requiring urgent transfer to hospital, while intrauterine resuscitation measures continue during preparation for transport. Continuing to monitor at home for an extended period risks delaying necessary intervention. Reassuring the client that this finding is typically normal overlooks its concerning persistence here and could delay appropriate action. Discontinuing monitoring altogether would remove essential information needed to guide the response.
  2. The correct answer is C (Explain that transfer for fetal concerns is a normal, appropriate part of safe care): reassuring the client that transferring for a fetal heart rate concern reflects appropriate, responsive care helps her understand that this decision supports safety rather than indicating any failure in her earlier birth planning. Agreeing that home birth planning was inappropriate is inaccurate and unhelpful given that low-risk planning was reasonable at the time. Avoiding the client's emotional question entirely neglects an important part of supportive care. Overstating certainty that the outcome could never have been anticipated is not an accurate or reassuring response.
  3. The correct answer is B (A concise summary of labour progress, interventions given, and the current fetal status): an effective handover to the receiving obstetric team requires a clear, concise summary of labour progress, any interventions already provided, and the current status of both mother and fetus, so the team can act quickly and appropriately. Providing only minimal identifying information would leave the receiving team without essential clinical context. Reciting the entire prenatal history in detail is not the priority during an urgent handover. Relying solely on the written chart without verbal communication risks delays in a time-sensitive situation.
  4. The correct answer is D (Address the concern directly and consider reporting it if the pattern continues): a repeated pattern of missing informed consent documentation raises a genuine professional and client safety concern that should be addressed directly with the colleague, with further reporting considered if the issue continues. Saying nothing allows a potentially unsafe pattern to continue unaddressed. Assuming good reasons without any follow-up does not fulfill the professional responsibility to raise concerns. Waiting for an actual client complaint before acting misses an opportunity for earlier, preventive correction.
  5. The correct answer is A (Consent given after understanding the purpose, risks, benefits, and available alternatives): informed consent requires that a client understand the purpose of a proposed intervention along with its risks, benefits, and reasonable alternatives before agreeing to proceed. It cannot be assumed simply because a client is in labour or has been admitted for care. It also must be obtained for each relevant intervention throughout care, not only once at the first prenatal visit. This principle applies to routine examinations as well as more significant procedures.
  6. The correct answer is B (Decline to sign, since documentation must accurately reflect the actual care provider): documentation must accurately reflect who actually provided the care being recorded, so co-signing an entry for a birth not personally attended would misrepresent the record. Signing to help a colleague finish paperwork faster does not justify creating an inaccurate record. The outcome of the birth being uncomplicated does not change the requirement for accurate attribution of care. Asking the client to confirm details does not resolve the underlying problem of misattributed documentation.
Case C36

A 30-year-old woman in her second pregnancy is choosing between a birth centre and hospital birth after an uncomplicated first birth at home. She meets with her midwife to discuss the differences between these two settings before making a final decision.

  1. The correct answer is B (Providing balanced, evidence-based information about both settings without steering the decision): the midwife's role in supporting an informed choice between a birth centre and hospital is to present balanced, accurate, evidence-based information about both settings, allowing the client to weigh her own priorities and decide. Choosing the setting based on personal preference or steering toward one option undermines the client's autonomy. Declining to discuss birth centres as an option would deny the client relevant information she is entitled to receive. This principle applies regardless of which setting the midwife personally works in most often.
  2. The correct answer is C (Yes, midwives commonly attend births across home, birth centre, and hospital settings): Canadian midwives are trained and authorized to attend births across home, birth centre, and hospital settings, which supports genuine continuity of care regardless of the client's chosen birthplace. Limiting midwifery attendance to only home or birth centre settings misrepresents the scope of practice in most provinces. Hospital birth attendance is not reserved exclusively for obstetricians. A physician does not need to be present throughout a midwife-attended hospital birth in the absence of a complication requiring consultation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Birth centres have transfer arrangements with a nearby hospital for complications requiring it): birth centres are designed for low-risk births and maintain established transfer arrangements with a nearby hospital in case a complication arises that falls outside the birth centre's scope. They are not equipped to manage every possible complication independently, which is why these arrangements exist. Refusing clients who might later need transfer would be inconsistent with how birth centres actually operate. Birth centres function as part of a connected system of care rather than in complete isolation from hospital services.
  4. The correct answer is A (A relationship-based approach that prioritizes time for education and shared decision-making): the client's positive experience of unhurried, thorough conversation reflects the midwifery model's emphasis on relationship-based care, where time for education and shared decision-making is prioritized rather than rushed. A volume-based approach focused on seeing many clients quickly would work against this value. Limiting visits strictly to routine measurements would leave little room for meaningful discussion. Referring most questions elsewhere would undermine the continuity and relational focus that defines this model.
  5. The correct answer is B (It provides evidence that the client received adequate information to decide freely): documenting an informed choice discussion creates a clear record demonstrating that the client received sufficient information to make her own decision, which supports both quality of care and accountability if the decision is ever reviewed later. This documentation matters regardless of whether the client later changes her mind. Suggesting it exists solely for billing purposes ignores its real clinical and professional significance. It does not replace the need for continued conversation as circumstances or the client's thinking evolve.
  6. The correct answer is D (Decline to share information without the client's consent, respecting confidentiality): client confidentiality must be maintained even when a partner requests information, and the midwife should decline to share details without the client's own consent. Assuming partners are automatically entitled to full access misunderstands the client's individual right to confidentiality. Selectively sharing information the midwife judges harmless without asking the client still breaches this principle. Asking the partner to keep a discussion private from the client herself is not an appropriate resolution to this request.
  7. The correct answer is C (Ask sensitively in a private setting and offer relevant resources and support): when intimate partner violence is suspected, the appropriate approach is to ask sensitively in a private, safe setting and to offer relevant resources and support based on the client's response. Avoiding the topic overlooks an important part of the midwife's role in supporting client safety and wellbeing. Confronting the partner directly could escalate risk to the client and is not an appropriate first step. Waiting until postpartum unnecessarily delays addressing a concern that may need timely attention during pregnancy.
  8. The correct answer is D (Records should be retained for the period required by provincial regulation): record retention policies should be based on the specific timeframes required by provincial regulation, which are designed to ensure records remain available when needed for continuity of care or later review. Discarding records once care ends, without regard to these requirements, would not meet this standard. Limiting extended retention only to complicated births ignores that any record may become relevant later. Leaving retention entirely to individual discretion would create inconsistent and potentially non-compliant practice.
Case C44

A client at 36 weeks gestation returns a positive vaginal-rectal swab for group B streptococcus. She plans a home birth and asks her midwife what this result means for her care during labour.

  1. The correct answer is B (Intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis should be discussed and offered once labour begins): a positive group B streptococcus result at this gestation means intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis should be discussed and offered once labour begins, in line with standard evidence-based practice to reduce the risk of neonatal infection. Assuming no action is needed ignores established guidance for this common finding. Immediate transfer to hospital before labour starts is not required based on this result alone. The plan should be established in advance rather than waiting for signs of maternal fever.
  2. The correct answer is D (Home birth remains an option if antibiotics can be given as recommended at home): a positive group B streptococcus result does not automatically preclude a home birth, provided that appropriate intrapartum antibiotics can be administered as recommended within the home setting. Framing home birth as unlikely to be permitted after this result overstates the actual clinical guidance. It does not make home birth mandatory either, since the decision remains the client's informed choice. It also does not require replacing midwifery involvement with hospital staff, since midwives commonly manage this situation directly.
  3. The correct answer is D (The positive result, the plan for antibiotics, and the client's informed choice discussion): thorough documentation of the positive result, the resulting plan for intrapartum antibiotics, and the informed choice discussion with the client is essential for continuity of care during labour, ensuring anyone involved in her care has complete and accurate information. Recording only that a swab was done without the result would leave a critical gap. Omitting the clinical result in favour of unrelated details would not support safe, informed care during labour. Complete documentation protects both the client and the midwife going forward.
Case C47

A midwife obtains verbal consent from a client for an artificial rupture of membranes during active labour. Afterward, the midwife realizes she has not yet written this discussion into the labour record.

  1. The correct answer is B (Promptly document the consent discussion, noting the time it actually occurred): when documentation is delayed, the appropriate correction is to document the discussion promptly and accurately note the actual time it occurred, ensuring the record reflects a true account of events. Leaving the chart incomplete because consent was verbal does not meet documentation standards, since verbal consent discussions still require written record. Asking the client to sign a form afterward stating consent was given earlier is not an appropriate substitute for timely, accurate documentation. Waiting until a much later visit to complete this record risks losing important detail.
  2. The correct answer is A (Informed consent, which requires understanding before agreeing to an intervention): explaining the risks and alternatives before performing a procedure reflects the principle of informed consent, which requires that a client understand relevant information before agreeing to an intervention. This principle applies to interventions performed in any setting, not only surgical procedures in hospital. It is not merely a legal formality, since it directly protects the client's ability to make a genuinely informed decision. It also is not limited to situations where a second midwife happens to be present.
  3. The correct answer is C (Respect the client's decision and document the informed refusal clearly in the chart): when a client changes her mind and declines further intervention, the midwife must respect this decision and document the informed refusal clearly, reflecting the client's ongoing right to change her decision at any point. Proceeding anyway because consent was initially given verbally disregards the client's current wishes. Ignoring the change of mind is inconsistent with the principle that consent can be withdrawn at any time. Delaying documentation until a supposedly final decision is made risks an inaccurate or incomplete record of what actually occurred.
  4. The correct answer is A (Overall clinical responsibility and supervision for the safety of care provided): when precepting a student midwife, the supervising midwife retains overall clinical responsibility and must actively supervise care to ensure client safety throughout the birth. This responsibility does not transfer to the student, regardless of how much of the hands-on care the student performs. It also does not end after the prenatal period, since supervision continues through the birth itself. Appropriate teaching relationships always maintain this ongoing accountability.
  5. The correct answer is C (A clear triage process with defined criteria for urgent versus routine calls): an effective after-hours call policy requires a clear triage process with defined criteria to distinguish urgent concerns from routine ones, ensuring timely and appropriate responses. Discouraging clients from calling outside office hours could delay recognition of a genuine concern. An informal system without defined criteria increases the risk of inconsistent or unsafe responses. Automatically routing every call to an emergency department is unnecessary and could bypass appropriate midwifery assessment for non-urgent concerns.
  6. The correct answer is C (To identify learning points and improve future safety of care provided): the primary purpose of a reflective review after an adverse outcome is to identify learning points and improve the safety of future care, supporting a culture of ongoing quality improvement. Assigning blame to a single provider does not serve this constructive, safety-focused purpose. Treating the review as a purely administrative task with no clinical value misunderstands its genuine importance. Avoiding further discussion after submission would undermine the value of the reflective process itself.
Case C53

A midwife is preparing for a home birth of a client at 41 weeks gestation. She reviews her neonatal resuscitation equipment, including a bag-valve-mask, suction device, and warming supplies, in the days before the estimated birth.

  1. The correct answer is C (Because some newborns require unexpected resuscitation even after a low-risk pregnancy): thorough preparation of neonatal resuscitation equipment is essential for every birth because some newborns unexpectedly require resuscitation even after an entirely low-risk pregnancy and labour, making preparedness a universal standard rather than one reserved for higher-risk cases. Limiting this preparation only to births beyond 41 weeks would leave many births inadequately prepared. Treating equipment checks as mainly a documentation exercise misunderstands their genuine clinical importance. This requirement reflects clinical necessity rather than a purely legal formality.
  2. The correct answer is A (Dry, stimulate, and position the newborn while assessing breathing and heart rate): the initial steps of newborn resuscitation for poor tone and irregular breathing involve drying, stimulating, and positioning the newborn while promptly assessing breathing and heart rate to guide further action. Delaying any response risks worsening the newborn's condition. Beginning chest compressions before completing an assessment skips essential steps in the standard resuscitation sequence. Prioritizing delivery of the placenta over the newborn's condition misallocates urgent attention during this critical moment.
  3. The correct answer is B (Begin positive pressure ventilation while a second attendant prepares for transfer): a persistently low heart rate with inadequate breathing after initial steps requires prompt initiation of positive pressure ventilation, while a second attendant simultaneously prepares for possible transfer given the ongoing concern. Continuing only drying and stimulation without escalating support would be insufficient for this presentation. Waiting further before escalating care risks a worsening outcome for the newborn. Postponing ventilation until arrival at hospital would dangerously delay essential, time-critical treatment.
  4. The correct answer is C (A clear timeline of resuscitation steps taken and the newborn's response to each): an effective handover after a neonatal resuscitation requires a clear timeline detailing which steps were taken and how the newborn responded at each stage, so hospital staff can continue care with complete information. Providing only the newborn's current appearance without detail on prior care would leave important gaps. Describing the birth as simply uncomplicated overall would misrepresent what actually occurred. Omitting handover information entirely would be an unsafe gap in communication during a transfer of care.
  5. The correct answer is A (It keeps skills and team coordination sharp for rare but serious emergencies): regular mock emergency drills help maintain sharp clinical skills and effective team coordination for emergencies that, while rare, can be serious and time-critical. Treating drills as having no real benefit to actual performance ignores established evidence supporting simulation-based training. Limiting their value to only new midwives overlooks the ongoing benefit for experienced practitioners as well. Drills complement, rather than replace, the need for properly maintained physical emergency equipment.
  6. The correct answer is C (Carrying additional stabilization supplies and establishing clear transport arrangements in advance): a midwife working in a remote community with longer transport times should adapt her preparedness by carrying additional stabilization supplies and establishing clear transport arrangements well in advance of need. Assuming standard urban preparation is sufficient overlooks the genuine impact of extended transport distances on emergency response. Declining to attend births solely due to distance would not appropriately balance access to care with safety planning. Relying mainly on family arrangements does not meet the standard expected for professional emergency preparedness.
  7. The correct answer is A (It confirms equipment is functional and available before it is urgently needed): regular review of oxygen and airway equipment confirms that essential supplies are functional and available before an emergency arises, when there would be no time to discover a problem. Treating this review as a mere formality misunderstands its role in preventing avoidable equipment failure. This kind of review matters for all birth settings, not only hospital-based births. Postponing review until an actual emergency reveals a problem defeats the entire purpose of proactive preparedness.
Case C60

A midwife is caring for a client whose labour has not progressed beyond 6 cm dilation for over four hours despite adequate contractions. The client is otherwise stable and wishes to continue labouring at the birth centre.

  1. The correct answer is C (Initiate a consultation with an obstetrician regarding the lack of labour progress): a prolonged plateau in cervical dilation despite adequate contractions meets standard criteria for consultation, since it may indicate a labour pattern requiring specialist input even while the client remains otherwise stable. Continuing to manage this pattern independently without seeking input would delay appropriate escalation. Discharging the client home is not an appropriate response to active labour with a concerning progress pattern. Delaying discussion with the client until progress further declines would be inconsistent with informed choice principles.
  2. The correct answer is C (Present the recommendation clearly and support the client's informed decision either way): the midwife's role after receiving a specialist recommendation is to present it clearly and support the client's own informed decision, whether she chooses to follow the recommendation immediately or take a little more time. Insisting on immediate transfer even when it conflicts with the client's wishes disregards her right to be involved in the decision. Withholding the recommendation to avoid upsetting her would violate informed choice principles. Deciding on her own and informing the client only afterward would similarly bypass the client's role in her own care.
  3. The correct answer is B (Remaining involved in a supportive role while the obstetric team leads management): once care is transferred for augmentation, the midwife appropriately continues in a supportive role, providing continuity and comfort, while the obstetric team leads the clinical management of the more complex situation. Withdrawing mostly from further involvement would remove valuable continuity of care for the client during a stressful transition. Attempting to retain full decision-making authority after transfer would conflict with the purpose of the transfer itself. Limiting her role mostly to paperwork would understate the meaningful supportive role midwives typically continue to play.
  4. The correct answer is D (It creates a clear record supporting the reasoning behind each shared decision made): thorough documentation of the consultation and transfer process creates a clear record of the reasoning behind each decision made along the way, supporting continuity of care and accountability regardless of the eventual outcome. Limiting thorough documentation only to unfavourable outcomes would leave many important cases inadequately recorded. Framing this documentation as relevant only for billing purposes ignores its broader clinical and professional significance. Written documentation does not replace the value of clear verbal handover between providers, and both are typically needed.
  5. The correct answer is B (Respect the client's confidentiality while still supporting her in the decision): the midwife must respect the client's confidentiality regarding her own health information, even when it affects shared decisions with a partner, while still supporting the client in navigating that decision herself. Informing the partner directly against the client's wishes would violate her right to confidentiality. Refusing to continue care unless she agrees to share information would be an inappropriate condition to place on continued care. Sharing information based solely on the midwife's own judgment of necessity similarly disregards the client's right to control her own information.
  6. The correct answer is D (Review and standardize her documentation practices to ensure completeness going forward): recognizing inconsistent documentation habits calls for a proactive review and standardization of practice to ensure all charts reliably capture key assessment findings. Continuing without change assumes incorrectly that documentation gaps carry no real risk. Focusing detailed documentation only on complicated visits leaves routine care inadequately recorded, when routine findings can also become clinically significant later. Waiting for a regulatory audit to prompt improvement is a reactive approach rather than the proactive standard expected of safe practice.
  7. The correct answer is D (It supports trust, communication, and better recognition of changes over time): continuity of care supports the development of trust and open communication between client and midwife, and it allows the provider to better recognize meaningful changes over the course of care. This benefit applies broadly across all birth settings, not only out-of-hospital planning. Framing continuity as merely an administrative convenience overlooks substantial evidence supporting its clinical value. It is valued as an evidence-based practice, not simply a legal formality.
Case C67

A regulatory college receives a complaint that a midwife did not adequately inform a client of the risks of a specific intervention before proceeding. The college opens a formal review of the midwife's practice in this case.

  1. The correct answer is B (To protect the public and maintain safe, accountable professional standards): the fundamental purpose of a regulatory college's complaint and discipline process is to protect the public and uphold safe, accountable professional standards across the profession. It is not intended to punish a midwife without fully weighing the facts, but rather to respond proportionately based on a fair review of the actual circumstances. It is also not designed to create records meant to discourage clients from ever choosing midwifery care. Documentation obligations continue independently of any single review process.
  2. The correct answer is A (It serves as evidence of what information was actually shared with the client): documentation of the informed consent discussion serves as important evidence in a regulatory review of what information was actually shared with the client at the time of care, which memory alone cannot reliably establish later. Assuming documentation has little bearing on the outcome underestimates its central role in these reviews. Relying only on the client's own account without the midwife's documentation leaves an incomplete picture. This kind of documentation is relevant regardless of whether the intervention involved a surgical procedure.
  3. The correct answer is A (A remediation plan focused on improving documentation practices going forward): when a review finds that documentation was incomplete but clinical care was otherwise appropriate, a proportionate response typically focuses on remediation aimed at improving documentation practices going forward, rather than an extreme measure disconnected from the actual findings. An extended revocation across several provinces would still be disproportionate to a documentation-focused concern. Treating documentation gaps as rarely relevant misunderstands their genuine importance to safe practice and accountability. Referral directly to criminal authorities without further college review would not match the nature of this finding.
  4. The correct answer is A (Consistently documenting informed consent discussions in detail at the time of care): the most effective way to prevent similar complaints going forward is to consistently and promptly document informed consent discussions in detail at the time care is provided, creating a reliable record for every relevant intervention. Relying on verbal discussions alone leaves no lasting evidence of what was actually communicated. Limiting documentation only to the most serious interventions would leave many relevant discussions unrecorded. Avoiding interventions altogether is not a realistic or appropriate response to a documentation-focused finding.
Case C71

A 36-week primigravida meets with her midwife to discuss group B streptococcus (GBS) screening. After the midwife explains the purpose, risks, and alternatives of the vaginal-rectal swab in plain language, the client states she understands but wishes to decline the test.

  1. The correct answer is D (Autonomy, since a fully informed competent adult may decline any recommended screening test): Informed choice rests on respecting a client's right to accept or decline any test or intervention once she has received balanced, accurate information in plain language. The midwife's role is to counsel, not coerce, and documenting a clear informed refusal honours the client's autonomy while preserving the therapeutic relationship. Beneficence and nonmaleficence describe the midwife's duty to promote good and avoid harm, but neither justifies overriding a competent client's decision. Justice concerns fair distribution of care, not the client's personal right to decline a specific test.
  2. The correct answer is B (Document the discussion, information provided, client's understanding, and her explicit decision to decline): Thorough documentation of an informed refusal protects the client's autonomy and creates a clear record that appropriate counselling occurred, including what information was shared and how she reached her decision. This standard applies across Canadian midwifery regulatory colleges and supports continuity of care if her GBS status becomes relevant later. Omitting documentation or relying on a generic form fails to capture the specific clinical discussion that took place. Recording only that screening was offered, without her reasoning, does not meet the standard for informed refusal.
  3. The correct answer is D (Offer intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis based on the presence of intrapartum risk factors for GBS): When GBS status is unknown, risk-factor-based management is standard, and prolonged rupture of membranes with maternal fever are recognized risk factors that warrant offering intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis. Her earlier refusal of the screening swab is a separate decision from intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis, and a new informed discussion should occur given the current clinical picture. Waiting for culture results in active labour is not feasible since results take days, not minutes. Cesarean birth is not indicated based on unknown GBS status alone.
Case C74

A 29-year-old client at 10 weeks gestation attends a prenatal visit with her midwife to discuss options for prenatal genetic screening. She has no personal or family history of genetic conditions and wants to understand the available choices before deciding whether to proceed.

  1. The correct answer is B (Screening tests estimate the probability of a condition while diagnostic tests give a definitive result): Establishing this distinction first allows the client to understand that a screening result is a risk estimate, not a diagnosis, and that further diagnostic testing such as chorionic villus sampling or amniocentesis would be needed to confirm any suspected condition. This foundational understanding supports genuinely informed decision-making rather than confusion between the two test categories. No prenatal genetic test, screening or diagnostic, is legally mandatory in Canada. Diagnostic and screening tests differ in both purpose and the certainty of their results.
  2. The correct answer is C (It estimates the risk of common trisomies such as trisomy 21, 18, and 13): NIPT analyzes cell-free fetal DNA fragments in the maternal bloodstream to calculate an adjusted risk score for the most common trisomies, and it is a highly sensitive screening test rather than a diagnostic one. A positive NIPT result still requires confirmation through diagnostic testing before any clinical decisions are made. NIPT does not assess structural anomalies, which are identified through ultrasound imaging instead. Because it is a screening test, an abnormal NIPT result never eliminates the recommendation for diagnostic confirmation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Refer for genetic counselling and offer diagnostic testing such as amniocentesis for confirmation): A high-risk NIPT result is a screening finding, not a diagnosis, so the appropriate response is referral for genetic counselling and the offer of diagnostic testing to confirm or rule out the suspected condition before any further decisions are made. Presenting the screening result as a certain diagnosis would misrepresent the test's true performance and could cause unnecessary distress. NIPT results are never treated as definitively diagnostic in Canadian practice. Repeating the same screening test will not provide the definitive answer that diagnostic testing offers.
  4. The correct answer is A (Screening is entirely optional, and declining it is a legitimate, respected personal choice): The informed choice model requires that prenatal genetic screening be offered, not imposed, and that clients understand they may accept or decline testing without any obligation to explain their reasoning or to pursue further testing if a screen returns high risk. Framing screening as mandatory or applying pressure contradicts the voluntary nature of the informed choice model central to Canadian midwifery practice. The right to decline applies to every client, regardless of personal or family history of genetic conditions.
  5. The correct answer is C (Arrange a qualified professional interpreter to support accurate, confidential communication throughout the visit): Culturally safe care requires accurate, confidential communication, and a trained professional interpreter ensures that clinical information, questions, and consent discussions are conveyed correctly without placing an inappropriate burden on family members. Using a child or family member as an interpreter risks miscommunication, breaches confidentiality, and can place the child in an inappropriate role. Gestures and simplified language cannot reliably convey complex clinical information such as risks, options, and consent. Delaying essential counselling denies the client timely access to informed prenatal care.
  6. The correct answer is A (Colostrum is produced in small amounts but is rich in antibodies and easily digested): Colostrum is a concentrated, low-volume first milk that matches a healthy newborn's small stomach capacity while providing important immunologic protection through antibodies and easily digested nutrients. Teaching clients this fact antenatally helps set realistic expectations about small early feeding volumes and reduces unnecessary formula supplementation. Colostrum is present during pregnancy and available from birth, not delayed by weeks. Its small volume is protective and expected, not a sign that overfeeding will occur.
Case C80

A first-time mother on day 3 postpartum calls her midwife reporting sore, cracked nipples and describes her baby's latch as making a clicking sound during feeds. The baby has had six wet diapers and three yellow, seedy stools in the past 24 hours.

  1. The correct answer is C (A shallow latch, which commonly causes clicking, nipple pain, and skin breakdown in feeding): A clicking sound during feeding, together with sore and cracked nipples, is a classic sign of a shallow latch where the baby is not taking in enough breast tissue, and it is best assessed through direct observation of a feed. While tongue-tie can sometimes contribute to latch difficulty, it should still be assessed and is not automatically unrelated to positioning support. Oversupply typically presents with different signs such as choking or rapid milk flow rather than clicking alone. Nipple damage and clicking are not normal findings and warrant assessment.
  2. The correct answer is A (Arrange to observe a feeding directly and assess positioning, attachment, and the baby's latch): Direct observation of a feed allows the midwife to identify the specific positioning or attachment issue causing pain and clicking, which is essential before recommending any correction or intervention. Recommending formula switching or a nipple shield without first assessing the latch skips the necessary clinical assessment and may not address the true cause. Dismissing the clicking sound as meaningless ignores a recognized clinical sign that warrants follow-up support. Early assessment and correction can prevent worsening nipple damage and support continued breastfeeding.
  3. The correct answer is C (Explain that this output pattern is reassuring and consistent with adequate milk intake): By day 3, six or more wet diapers and multiple yellow, seedy stools in 24 hours are recognized indicators of adequate milk transfer, even when a latch issue is causing maternal discomfort. This reassures the mother that feeding volume is currently sufficient while the latch problem is still addressed to prevent future supply or comfort issues. This output pattern does not suggest underfeeding, so telling her the baby is likely underfed would be inaccurate. Both stool colour and urine output are meaningful indicators used together to assess newborn feeding adequacy.
  4. The correct answer is C (Continuous labour support, upright positioning, movement, and warm water immersion or showering): These non-pharmacologic strategies are well supported by evidence for reducing perceived pain, increasing comfort, and supporting labour progress, and they form a core part of midwifery-led childbirth education. Encouraging fixed supine positioning throughout labour can reduce comfort and may slow progress compared with upright, mobile positions. Strict fasting is not routinely recommended for low-risk labouring clients and can affect energy levels. Continuous fetal monitoring is a clinical assessment tool, not a comfort measure, and is not required for every labouring client.
Case C84

A midwife provides care to a First Nations client who lives on-reserve, roughly two hours from the nearest hospital with obstetric services. The client strongly prefers to give birth at home surrounded by family and community, and she shares that she has had negative experiences with the healthcare system in the past.

  1. The correct answer is B (Acknowledge her experiences respectfully and reflect on how the midwife's own practice can build trust): Culturally safe care involves genuine acknowledgment of a client's lived experience, ongoing self-reflection by the provider, and actively working to build a relationship of trust rather than treating the disclosure as incidental. Dismissing her disclosure or avoiding the topic entirely fails to validate her experience and can reinforce previous harm. Assuming that all Indigenous clients share identical experiences or preferences is a generalization that undermines individualized, respectful care. Culturally safe practice centres the client's own perspective rather than the provider's assumptions.
  2. The correct answer is A (Discuss the realistic risks and benefits of distance and transport time honestly and respectfully): Informed choice about birthplace requires an honest, individualized discussion of the real implications of transport distance and time in the event of a needed transfer, presented respectfully and without judgment of her preference. Refusing to discuss home birth outright removes her ability to make an informed decision about her own care. Claiming that distance never affects safety would misrepresent real clinical risk. The decision belongs to the client, informed by accurate information, not to the midwife's personal preference.
  3. The correct answer is D (Continuity of care from one known midwife or a small consistent team throughout pregnancy): The continuity of care model, a defining feature of Canadian midwifery practice, allows a client to build a trusting relationship with a familiar provider over time, which is especially valuable for someone who has experienced negative or fragmented care in the past. Rotating through unfamiliar providers at every visit would undermine the trust-building this client needs. Rigidly short appointment times do not reflect the relationship-centred model of midwifery care. Requiring travel to a centralized hospital clinic conflicts with the flexible, community-based approach midwifery care can offer.
  4. The correct answer is C (Support the request and follow local policy for safely releasing the placenta to the client): Culturally safe midwifery practice accommodates meaningful cultural and family practices whenever they can be safely supported, and most jurisdictions have established policies allowing a client to take her placenta home following birth. Denying the request outright disregards the client's cultural practice without clinical justification. This is a routine request midwives can address directly, not one restricted to physicians alone. Ignoring or deflecting the request would be culturally unsafe and fails to respect a practice that is meaningful to her and her community.
  5. The correct answer is B (A clear emergency transport and transfer plan established well before her expected due date): For any client planning a home birth at a significant distance from hospital services, establishing a clear, individualized emergency transport and transfer plan well in advance of labour is essential to safe practice and should be discussed as part of ongoing antenatal counselling. Relying on informal arrangements such as a partner's willingness to drive does not replace a structured transfer plan involving appropriate emergency services. Asking a client to relocate is not an appropriate or realistic expectation. Details about the midwife's personal vehicle are irrelevant to safe transfer planning.
  6. The correct answer is C (Take a daily folic acid supplement as recommended before and throughout early pregnancy): Canadian public health guidance recommends daily folic acid supplementation before conception and through early pregnancy because it is well established to reduce the risk of neural tube defects such as spina bifida. Eliminating fibre or iron from the diet has no basis in reducing neural tube defect risk and could create other nutritional deficiencies. Raw or undercooked fish carries a risk of foodborne illness and mercury exposure and is specifically discouraged in pregnancy, not recommended daily. Folic acid supplementation remains one of the most evidence-based prenatal counselling points.
  7. The correct answer is A (Explore the client's situation supportively and connect her with harm-reduction resources and support): A harm-reduction, nonjudgmental approach encourages open disclosure by prioritizing trust, safety, and connection to appropriate supports rather than punishment or shame, which improves engagement with prenatal care. Refusing to continue care abandons a client who needs support and can push her away from any prenatal care at all. Reporting to child protection services without further clinical assessment or discussion is not the appropriate first response and may not reflect provincial reporting obligations at this stage. Expressing disapproval undermines trust and discourages honest future disclosure.
  8. The correct answer is D (It is a validated screening tool that identifies clients who may need further assessment): The EPDS is a widely used, validated screening instrument designed to identify postpartum clients who may benefit from further mental health assessment, but it is not itself a diagnostic tool for postpartum depression. A high score indicates the need for further evaluation, not a confirmed diagnosis, and it should never replace referral for follow-up care when scores are elevated. The tool specifically addresses mood and emotional wellbeing, not physical postpartum recovery. Using it correctly as a screening step supports timely identification and support for postpartum mental health concerns.
  9. The correct answer is A (Refer her for further mental health assessment and support while continuing ongoing midwifery care): An elevated EPDS score warrants referral for further mental health assessment and support, and the midwife should continue providing ongoing midwifery care and follow-up rather than discontinuing the therapeutic relationship. Telling the client she definitely has postpartum depression based on a screening score alone overstates what the tool can determine. Discontinuing care entirely abandons the client at a vulnerable time when continuity matters most. The EPDS is a well-validated tool, and an elevated score should never simply be dismissed or ignored.
  10. The correct answer is A (Place the baby alone, on their back, in a firm, empty crib or bassinet): Current safe sleep guidance in Canada recommends placing infants alone, on their back, on a firm surface free of soft bedding, pillows, and bumper pads to reduce the risk of SIDS and accidental suffocation. Stomach sleeping is associated with increased SIDS risk rather than decreased choking risk. Bed sharing with soft bedding increases the risk of suffocation and is specifically discouraged in safe sleep counselling. Soft mattresses and bumper pads are also associated with increased suffocation risk and should be avoided in the infant sleep space.
  11. The correct answer is D (A properly installed rear-facing infant car seat should be used for every car trip): Canadian guidelines recommend that infants ride in a properly installed rear-facing car seat for every car trip, regardless of distance, to provide appropriate protection for their developing neck and spine. An adult seatbelt alone does not provide adequate restraint or protection for a newborn's size and anatomy. Front-facing seats are not recommended for newborns and are used only once a child meets specific age and weight criteria. Distance travelled does not change the need for correct car seat use on every trip.
  12. The correct answer is A (Vitamin K reduces the risk of hemorrhagic disease of the newborn by supporting clotting): Newborns are born with low vitamin K stores, which is needed for the production of clotting factors, and vitamin K administration reduces the risk of hemorrhagic disease of the newborn, a rare but serious bleeding disorder. It has no established role in preventing jaundice or improving weight gain, which involve entirely different physiological mechanisms. Vitamin K administration is an evidence-based clinical recommendation, not an administrative requirement, and parents retain the right to accept or decline it as an informed choice.
Case C96

A client with one previous lower-segment cesarean birth for breech presentation is now pregnant again and meets with her midwife to discuss her options for this birth, including the possibility of a trial of labour after cesarean (TOLAC).

  1. The correct answer is B (TOLAC carries a small but real risk of uterine rupture along with a reasonable success rate): Balanced counselling about TOLAC should include both the reasonable likelihood of a successful vaginal birth for many candidates and the small but serious risk of uterine rupture, allowing the client to make a genuinely informed decision. No obstetric intervention, including TOLAC, guarantees a one hundred percent success rate. TOLAC can be a reasonable option for many clients with a single prior lower-segment cesarean, particularly one performed for a non-recurring indication such as breech presentation. Neither TOLAC nor repeat cesarean is entirely free of risk, and both carry distinct considerations.
  2. The correct answer is B (Most provincial guidelines recommend hospital birth with access to surgical services for TOLAC): Given the small but serious risk of uterine rupture associated with TOLAC, most Canadian provincial guidelines recommend that clients attempting TOLAC give birth in a hospital setting with immediate access to surgical and obstetric services, rather than at home or in a birth centre without that capacity. Recommending home birth as the safest setting for TOLAC contradicts this guidance and would be an unsafe recommendation. Birthplace is directly relevant to safety given the specific risks of TOLAC. There is no requirement to give birth outside her home province.
  3. The correct answer is D (Support her informed decision and coordinate collaborative care with the appropriate obstetric provider): Respecting the client's informed decision, the midwife should support her choice for a planned repeat cesarean and coordinate collaborative care with an obstetric provider, since performing the surgery itself is outside midwifery scope while ongoing prenatal support and coordination remain appropriate. Attempting to override her decision disregards her autonomy after a thorough, balanced discussion took place. Midwives can continue to provide valuable prenatal and coordinating care even when a client chooses a cesarean birth. Labelling her informed decision as unreasonable is inappropriate and does not reflect a client-centred model of care.
  4. The correct answer is D (Document the information shared, risks discussed, and the client's reasoning for her decision): Thorough, contemporaneous documentation of the information provided, the risks and benefits discussed, and the client's own reasoning best supports high-quality midwifery practice and provides a clear record that genuine shared decision-making occurred. Recording only the final decision without the surrounding discussion fails to capture how that decision was reached. Relying solely on memory without any chart entry does not meet professional documentation standards. Documentation should occur close to the time of the discussion rather than being delayed until after the birth.
  5. The correct answer is A (Explain the public and private banking options neutrally and support whatever choice she makes): Informed choice counselling requires presenting balanced, accurate information about both public and private cord blood banking options without steering the client toward a particular choice, then supporting whichever decision she reaches. Cord blood banking is entirely optional, never mandatory, and clients are free to decline it altogether. Discussing available options related to birth planning is within the scope of midwifery counselling. Both public and private banking exist as options in Canada, so recommending only one as the sole available choice would misinform the client.
  6. The correct answer is A (RhoGAM helps prevent Rh sensitization and is typically offered around 28 weeks gestation): Rh immune globulin is offered prophylactically, typically around 28 weeks gestation and again after birth if the baby is Rh-positive, to prevent Rh sensitization rather than to treat it after it has already occurred. The father's Rh status can inform the discussion but does not eliminate the recommendation to offer RhoGAM to an Rh-negative client, since paternity or Rh status is not always confirmed. RhoGAM is not a live vaccine and does not confer lifelong immunity, since repeat doses are needed with each pregnancy or sensitizing event.
  7. The correct answer is B (Provide balanced, unbiased information about risks and benefits, and refer if they choose it): Appropriate midwifery counselling on circumcision involves providing accurate, balanced information about the potential risks and benefits so parents can make an informed decision, and arranging appropriate referral if they choose to proceed, since circumcision is not typically performed by midwives themselves. Refusing to discuss the topic denies parents access to information relevant to their newborn care decisions. Recommending circumcision for every newborn regardless of parental wishes is not evidence-based or unbiased counselling. Circumcision is a parental choice with recognized medical considerations, not a practice that is never appropriate.
  8. The correct answer is B (Maintain access to professional interpreter services for all clinical visits when needed): Ensuring reliable access to professional interpreter services supports equitable, informed participation in care for clients who speak languages other than English or French, which is essential to genuine informed choice. Providing information only in English disadvantages clients who cannot fully understand it and undermines informed consent. Expecting clients to arrange their own translation places an unfair burden on them and risks inaccurate communication of clinical information. Limiting services to fluent English or French speakers would inappropriately restrict access to essential midwifery care based on language.
  9. The correct answer is D (The first stage begins with onset of labour and ends with full cervical dilation): The first stage of labour is defined as the period from the onset of regular labour contractions through to full cervical dilation, and it is typically divided into latent and active phases in standard childbirth education. Pushing and birth of the baby describe the second stage of labour, not the first. Delivery of the placenta describes the third stage of labour. Skin-to-skin contact is an important postbirth practice but is not itself a defined stage of labour.
  10. The correct answer is C (LAM requires exclusive breastfeeding, amenorrhea, and being within six months postpartum): The lactational amenorrhea method is effective only when all three specific conditions are met together, namely exclusive breastfeeding without regular supplementation, the absence of menstrual periods, and being within the first six months after birth. If menstruation returns, feeding becomes less frequent, or six months postpartum passes, LAM's effectiveness decreases significantly and another method should be discussed. Regular formula supplementation reduces breastfeeding frequency and lowers LAM's reliability rather than supporting it. Effectiveness is time-limited and condition-dependent, not indefinite or unconditional.
Case C106

A 32-year-old multigravida at 39 weeks gestation tells her midwife she wants a low-intervention labour and would like to decline continuous electronic fetal monitoring and routine intravenous access on admission. She has had one uncomplicated vaginal birth previously and is planning a hospital birth with her midwife.

  1. The correct answer is D (Discuss the evidence, risks, and benefits, then document and honour her informed decision): Supporting informed choice means giving the client balanced information about intermittent auscultation versus continuous monitoring so she can decide based on her own values and risk tolerance. Once she makes an informed decision, the midwife documents the discussion and respects her choice within a shared plan of care. Refusing continuous monitoring on request does not require automatic referral or unilateral override. Ongoing reassessment continues throughout labour regardless of the monitoring method chosen.
  2. The correct answer is B (For low-risk labour, intermittent auscultation at defined intervals is evidence-based and appropriate care): Intermittent auscultation performed at appropriate intervals is a recognized, evidence-based standard of fetal surveillance for low-risk labouring clients, not a fallback used only when equipment is unavailable. It allows greater mobility and less intervention while still detecting concerning fetal heart rate patterns when performed correctly. It is not restricted to out-of-hospital settings and does not eliminate the need for ongoing clinical assessment. The midwife tailors monitoring choice to risk status and informed preference.
  3. The correct answer is D (Recommend continuous electronic fetal monitoring and revisit informed choice given the new finding): A new clinical finding, such as difficulty interpreting the fetal heart rate, changes the risk picture and requires the midwife to revisit the earlier informed choice discussion with updated information. Continuing unchanged or discharging the client ignores a legitimate new concern that could affect fetal well-being. Informed choice is an ongoing process, not a single fixed decision that cannot be revisited as circumstances change. Documentation should reflect both the new finding and the updated discussion.
  4. The correct answer is B (Record the discussion, risks, benefits, alternatives, and the client's clearly stated decision fully): Complete documentation of an informed choice discussion protects both the client's autonomy and the midwife's professional accountability by capturing what information was shared and what decision the client made. A brief note lacking the risks, benefits, and alternatives does not demonstrate that true informed consent occurred. Documentation should happen at the time of the discussion regardless of whether a complication later develops. Verbal agreement alone is not a substitute for a complete clinical record.
  5. The correct answer is B (It means receiving balanced information so the client can make her own decisions): Informed choice in Canadian midwifery practice centers on the midwife providing complete, unbiased information about options, risks, and benefits so the client can reach decisions that reflect her own values throughout pregnancy, birth, and postpartum care. It is not limited to labour and birth alone, and it does not mean the midwife decides for her or that she must accept every recommendation. This principle underlies the collaborative, client-centered midwifery model of care used across Canada.
Case C111

A 30-year-old woman at 11 weeks gestation attends her first prenatal visit and asks her midwife about options for prenatal genetic screening, including non-invasive prenatal testing (NIPT) and the integrated prenatal screen. She has no personal or family history of genetic conditions and wants to understand her choices before deciding.

  1. The correct answer is C (Explain what each test screens for, its accuracy, and that screening remains optional): Appropriate informed choice counselling requires explaining the purpose, accuracy, and limitations of each screening option so the client can decide whether and which test suits her preferences. Screening is never mandatory, and a midwife should not universally recommend one test or refuse to accept a decision to decline. Ordering a test automatically without discussion bypasses the client's right to make an informed decision about her own prenatal care.
  2. The correct answer is D (NIPT screens for probability while amniocentesis provides a diagnostic result through invasive sampling): NIPT analyzes cell-free fetal DNA to estimate probability for certain conditions but is not diagnostic, while amniocentesis involves invasive sampling of amniotic fluid to provide a definitive diagnostic result. The two tests carry very different procedural risks, since amniocentesis involves a small risk of pregnancy loss that NIPT does not carry. Coverage and availability vary by province rather than following the stated pattern in the incorrect options.
  3. The correct answer is A (Respect and document her decision while remaining open to revisiting it if she wishes): Once a client makes an informed decision to decline screening, the midwife's role is to respect that choice, document it clearly, and continue providing supportive care without pressure to change her mind. Informed choice remains ongoing, so the midwife should stay open to future discussion if the client raises the topic again herself. Repeated pressure or mandatory referral despite a clear informed decision undermines client autonomy.
  4.
