The correct answer is C (Comparing non-pharmacological techniques with pharmacological options, including benefits and possible risks): Prenatal education about labour coping should present a balanced range of options so clients can make informed decisions that fit their own preferences and circumstances. Presenting only one category of options, or a single fixed plan for everyone, does not support genuine informed choice. Discussing pharmacological methods is well within the scope of midwifery prenatal education, alongside non-pharmacological techniques.
  5. The correct answer is B (They build knowledge and confidence about pregnancy, birth, and early parenting decisions): Prenatal classes are designed to build a client's knowledge base and confidence so she can participate actively in decisions about her pregnancy, birth, and early parenting. They complement, rather than replace, individualized prenatal counselling tailored to each client's specific needs. Attendance is generally optional and reflects client choice rather than a mandatory requirement of care.
Case C116

A first-time mother is three days postpartum and attends a home visit with her midwife. She is exclusively breastfeeding and reports nipple pain during latch and uncertainty about whether her newborn is feeding adequately. She asks for guidance on positioning and how to recognize effective milk transfer.

  1. The correct answer is D (Audible swallowing, softening breasts, and adequate wet and soiled diaper counts are present): Effective milk transfer is best assessed through observable signs such as audible swallowing during feeds, breast softening after feeding, and adequate newborn output through wet and soiled diapers. Continuous sleeping without waking, complete absence of any nipple sensation, or very short feeding sessions are not reassuring indicators and may instead suggest ineffective feeding. The midwife should assess these objective signs rather than relying on subjective impressions alone.
  2. The correct answer is B (Bring the newborn's chin and body close with a wide open mouth for latch): A shallow latch with the chin away from the breast often causes nipple pain and reduced milk transfer, so the midwife should teach positioning that brings the newborn's body and chin close with a wide open mouth to achieve a deeper latch. Nipple pain is not simply expected or normal and usually signals a correctable latch problem. Shallow latch does affect milk transfer and should be addressed rather than dismissed, and switching to bottle feeding is not the first-line teaching response.
  3. The correct answer is D (Explain that breast fullness varies and assess feeding effectiveness before recommending supplementation): Breast fullness naturally decreases as milk supply regulates and is not a reliable indicator of milk production on its own, so the midwife should assess actual feeding effectiveness, such as swallowing and newborn output, before considering supplementation. Recommending formula based on fullness alone could interrupt an otherwise adequate breastfeeding relationship. Dismissing the concern entirely or stating supplementation is never appropriate both fail to provide balanced, individualized counselling.
  4. The correct answer is B (Offer accurate information about feeding options and support whichever choice she makes): Supporting a client's confidence and autonomy in infant feeding decisions means providing accurate, balanced information about all feeding options and respecting whichever choice she ultimately makes for her family. Rigid schedules, discouraging her questions, or limiting her to a single acceptable method all undermine informed choice and client-centered care. The midwife's role is to inform and support, not to direct a predetermined outcome.
  5. The correct answer is B (Arrange a follow-up visit or call to reassess latch, weight, and feeding progress): Continuity of midwifery breastfeeding support includes planning a follow-up contact to reassess latch, weight gain, and overall feeding progress after teaching is provided. Waiting for a serious problem to develop before offering further contact, or providing no further follow-up at all, does not reflect the ongoing supportive relationship expected in midwifery care. Referring elsewhere immediately also does not match the midwife's scope in supporting breastfeeding.
  6. The correct answer is D (Arrange a qualified professional interpreter for important clinical discussions and informed consent): Culturally safe communication for a client with limited English proficiency requires access to a qualified professional interpreter, particularly for important clinical discussions and informed consent, to ensure she fully understands her care. Relying on family members can compromise accuracy, confidentiality, and comfort with sensitive topics. Simplified English alone or postponing counselling until her language skills improve does not meet her immediate right to understand her own care.
  7. The correct answer is B (Ask about her preferences, traditions, and support needs without making assumptions beforehand): Culturally safe care begins with asking the client directly about her preferences, traditions, and support needs rather than assuming what she wants based on generalizations about her community. Applying an identical generic plan to every client or avoiding cultural discussion altogether fails to individualize care. Genuine culturally safe practice recognizes each client's unique perspective and invites her voice into the care planning process.
  8. The correct answer is B (Explore her concerns respectfully and find culturally acceptable ways to meet her needs): Culturally safe communication requires the midwife to explore the client's specific concerns respectfully and work collaboratively to find ways of meeting her clinical needs that also honour her cultural and religious beliefs. Insisting on rigid adherence to standard recommendations, avoiding the topic, or proceeding without further discussion all fail to engage with her perspective. A respectful, individualized conversation supports both safety and cultural safety together.
  9. The correct answer is A (Reassure her that her decisions will be respected and supported without judgment): Culturally safe care requires reassuring the client that decisions shaped by her beliefs will be respected and supported without judgment, which builds trust and encourages open communication about her care. Suggesting that declining a recommendation could reduce her ongoing care, or stating that certain beliefs are incompatible with safe care, contradicts the principles of respectful, non-judgmental midwifery practice. Judgment or conditional support can discourage clients from disclosing important information in future visits.
  10. The correct answer is D (Discuss the lack of proven benefit and possible risks, then respect her decision): Informed choice counselling about placenta encapsulation involves explaining that current evidence does not demonstrate proven benefit and that some risks, such as infection, have been reported, while ultimately respecting the client's own decision. Refusing to discuss the topic denies her the information needed to decide, while strongly encouraging or absolutely forbidding the practice both overstate the evidence in one direction. Balanced, evidence-based counselling supports autonomous decision making.
  11. The correct answer is A (Review transfer indications, logistics, and likelihood in advance so she is prepared): Informed choice about home birth includes proactively reviewing the indications, logistics, and likelihood of hospital transfer well before labour so the client understands the process and feels prepared if it becomes necessary. Avoiding the topic to prevent anxiety, or waiting until an emergency arises, leaves the client unprepared and does not reflect complete informed consent. Minimizing the likelihood of transfer without discussion could also create a false sense of certainty.
  12. The correct answer is C (Yes, informed choice is ongoing and preferences can be revisited throughout the pregnancy): Informed choice is understood as an ongoing, dynamic process throughout pregnancy, so clients remain free to revisit and change earlier decisions about their birth plan as their circumstances or preferences evolve. Earlier documentation does not permanently lock in a decision, and changes are not limited only to scheduled appointments or contingent on midwife agreement. Supporting this flexibility respects the client's autonomy across the full course of care.
Case C128

A client at 20 weeks gestation is deciding between planning her birth at home, at a birth centre, or in hospital with her midwife. She asks for balanced information to help her make this decision and mentions some anxiety about safety in each setting.

  1. The correct answer is C (Present outcome evidence, risks, and benefits for each setting to support her decision): Informed choice about birthplace requires presenting balanced outcome evidence along with the risks and benefits of home, birth centre, and hospital settings so the client can weigh her options according to her own values. Recommending only one setting as universally safest, refusing to discuss an option within the midwife's scope, or choosing on the client's behalf all undermine her right to decide. A thorough, evidence-based discussion supports genuine autonomy.
  2. The correct answer is C (Evidence shows comparable safety to hospital birth for well-selected low-risk pregnancies): Research on planned home birth attended by a registered midwife for well-selected low-risk pregnancies generally shows comparable safety outcomes to planned hospital birth. Claiming substantially higher risk for every client, denying that Canadian evidence exists, or restricting appropriateness only to multiparous clients all misrepresent the current body of research. Accurate evidence-based information supports the client in making a genuinely informed decision.
  3. The correct answer is A (Her plan would be reassessed and revised through consultation if risk factors emerge): If a new risk factor develops after a client chooses home birth, her care plan would be reassessed, and consultation or transfer of care would be arranged as appropriate to the finding. A birthplace decision is never fixed permanently regardless of new clinical information, and continuing an unmodified home birth plan despite a significant new risk factor would not reflect safe, responsive practice. Ongoing reassessment protects both client and fetal well-being.
  4. The correct answer is D (Document her choice and continue ongoing risk assessment throughout ongoing pregnancy care): After a client makes an informed birthplace decision, the midwife documents the choice and continues the usual ongoing risk assessment throughout the remainder of pregnancy, since birthplace planning does not eliminate standard antenatal surveillance. Continuing to discourage her decision, ignoring the topic entirely, or referring her elsewhere unnecessarily all fail to respect her informed choice or provide appropriate continuity of care.
  5. The correct answer is D (They screen for treatable conditions early, and parents can decline after discussion): Newborn screening tests are designed to identify treatable conditions early so that intervention can begin promptly, and while widely recommended, parents retain the right to decline after an informed discussion in most Canadian jurisdictions. Describing the tests as providing no meaningful information, claiming parents cannot decline under any legislation, or stating results are never shared misrepresents both the clinical value and the informed choice principles that apply to newborn screening.
  6. The correct answer is B (Place the newborn on their back on a firm surface free of loose bedding): Current Canadian safe sleep guidance recommends placing the newborn on their back on a firm surface free of loose bedding, pillows, or soft objects to reduce the risk of sudden infant death syndrome. Prone positioning, unrestricted bed-sharing with soft bedding, and disregarding room temperature or surface firmness all contradict established safe sleep recommendations that prenatal education should accurately reflect.
Case C134

A client at 12 weeks gestation tells her midwife she does not want any prenatal genetic screening because of her personal and religious beliefs. She asks whether declining screening will affect the rest of her prenatal care.

  1. The correct answer is B (Confirm her prenatal care will continue as planned regardless of her decision): A client's decision to decline prenatal genetic screening does not affect her continued eligibility for midwifery care, and the midwife should confirm that her prenatal care will proceed as planned. Suggesting that care cannot continue, repeating the recommendation at every visit, or discouraging her decision strongly all place inappropriate pressure on a client who has made a clear, informed decision consistent with her beliefs.
  2. The correct answer is A (Documentation will describe the discussion and her decision using neutral, factual language): Respectful documentation of an informed choice discussion should use neutral, factual language describing what information was shared and what the client decided, without judgmental phrasing suggesting refusal against advice. Avoiding documentation entirely leaves no record of the informed consent process, while sharing information without consent or using judgmental wording both fail to meet professional documentation standards and client-centered communication principles.
  3. The correct answer is C (Yes, she can request screening later if it remains clinically appropriate to offer): Informed choice remains an ongoing process, so a client who initially declines screening can request it later in pregnancy if it remains clinically appropriate and gestational timing allows. Declining once does not permanently close the option, and access should not depend on the midwife's personal opinion of the reasonableness of her original decision or a specifically scheduled reassessment visit alone. Flexibility respects the client's evolving preferences.
  4. The correct answer is A (Ask about her specific practices and incorporate them into her postpartum care plan): Culturally safe postpartum care involves asking the client directly about her specific traditional practices and finding ways to incorporate them into her individualized care plan whenever safely possible. Refusing accommodation outright, recommending she abandon her traditions in favour of standard guidance, or planning care as though the request was never made all fail to respect her cultural values and autonomy in the postpartum period.
  5. The correct answer is A (Explain the purpose, risks of declining, and administration options, then support their decision): Informed choice counselling about vitamin K should explain its purpose in preventing bleeding, the risks associated with declining it, and the available administration options, then support whichever decision the parents make. Administering it without discussion, omitting risk information, or refusing to offer it unless requested all fail to provide the complete, balanced information required for genuine informed consent regarding a newborn intervention.
  6. The correct answer is B (Known caregiver relationships support trust, communication, and shared informed decision making): Continuity of care in the Canadian midwifery model, built around a known midwife or small team, strengthens trust and communication, which in turn supports more effective shared and informed decision making throughout pregnancy, birth, and postpartum care. This benefit extends across the full continuum of care rather than being limited to labour and birth alone, and research links continuity to improved client satisfaction and clinical outcomes.
  7. The correct answer is C (Ask the client to explain the information in her own words afterward): Asking the client to explain the information back in her own words is an effective way to confirm genuine understanding after an informed choice discussion, revealing any gaps or misunderstandings that a simple nod would not disclose. Relying on written material alone, skipping verbal discussion, or avoiding repetition and clarification when needed can all leave misunderstandings uncorrected and compromise truly informed consent.
Case C141

A 24-year-old woman with regular 28-day menstrual cycles presents to a midwifery clinic. Her last menstrual period was 6 weeks and 2 days ago by her recollection, and a home urine pregnancy test was positive. She wishes to confirm the pregnancy and establish prenatal care with a midwife.

  1. The correct answer is A (Confirm the pregnancy with an in-clinic urine hCG test and begin a full health history): a clinic-based confirmation and comprehensive history establish accurate baseline information for ongoing care. This includes menstrual, medical, obstetric, and social history that guides risk assessment. An immediate specialist referral is unnecessary for a low-risk client, and an anatomy scan is not performed this early in pregnancy. Delaying confirmation testing serves no clinical purpose when the client is already presenting for care.
  2. The correct answer is D (Add 7 days to the first day of the last menstrual period, then subtract three months): this is the standard formula for Naegele's rule, assuming a regular 28-day cycle. It provides a reasonable estimate of the due date based on menstrual history alone, pending confirmation by ultrasound. The other calculation methods described do not reflect the actual Naegele's rule formula and would produce inaccurate estimates. Ultrasound dating in early pregnancy remains the most accurate method overall.
  3. The correct answer is D (Between 8 and 14 weeks gestation, using crown-rump length measurement): first-trimester crown-rump length measurement provides the most accurate gestational age estimate of any ultrasound method. Accuracy decreases progressively as pregnancy advances, since fetal growth variation increases with gestational age. Femur length is used later for growth assessment rather than dating, and third-trimester scans are the least accurate for establishing gestational age. Early dating is prioritized whenever it is available.
  4. The correct answer is A (The ultrasound-based due date, since early ultrasound dating is more accurate than menstrual history): per SOGC guidelines, when a first-trimester or early second-trimester ultrasound (9+0 to 15+6 weeks) differs from menstrual dating by more than 7 days, the ultrasound estimate is used because it is more reliable. Menstrual dating can be inaccurate due to cycle irregularity or recall error. Averaging the two dates is not standard practice, and dating should never be chosen based purely on client preference rather than clinical evidence.
Case C145

A woman at 16 weeks gestation with an uncomplicated, low-risk pregnancy attends a routine prenatal visit with her midwife. Her blood pressure, weight, and fundal height are within expected limits, and fetal heart tones are auscultated at 150 beats per minute.

  1. The correct answer is A (Every 4 weeks, which is the standard interval for low-risk pregnancy until 28 weeks): this interval allows regular monitoring of maternal and fetal wellbeing without unnecessary visits for an uncomplicated pregnancy. Visit frequency increases later in pregnancy as risk of complications rises. Every 2 weeks is more frequent than needed at this stage, while every 6 weeks or visit-only-on-symptoms would delay detection of emerging concerns. The schedule reflects a balance between surveillance and client burden.
  2. The correct answer is C (A fetal heart rate of 150 beats per minute auscultated with a Doppler device): a normal fetal heart rate range is approximately 110 to 160 beats per minute, so 150 beats per minute is reassuring. A fundal height at the umbilicus is expected around 20 weeks, not 16 weeks, making that option inconsistent with normal findings. Absent heart tones or a rate under 100 beats per minute would both raise concern and require further assessment rather than reassurance.
  3. The correct answer is D (Blood pressure, weight trend, urinalysis, fundal height, and fetal heart auscultation): these core assessments are performed at every routine prenatal visit to monitor maternal and fetal wellbeing over time. Ultrasound is not repeated at every visit, and blood pressure monitoring continues throughout pregnancy rather than stopping after the first trimester. Routine cervical examinations before term are not part of standard low-risk prenatal care and are reserved for specific clinical indications.
  4. The correct answer is C (Refer for an ultrasound to assess fetal growth, amniotic fluid, and gestational age): a fundal height measurement significantly above expected for gestational age warrants further assessment to evaluate for causes such as excess amniotic fluid, multiple gestation, or a larger than expected fetus. Simple reassurance or repeating the measurement alone would delay identifying a treatable cause. Immediate hospital transfer is not warranted based on this isolated finding without further evaluation first.
  5. The correct answer is B (A vaginal-rectal swab will be collected between 35 and 37 weeks gestation): this timing window provides results that remain predictive of colonization status at the time of birth. A blood test or urine culture does not detect group B Streptococcus colonization, and waiting for symptoms would miss the many women who are colonized without any symptoms at all. Universal screening at this recommended interval is standard Canadian midwifery practice.
Case C150

A 30-year-old woman at 26 weeks gestation with no prior history of diabetes attends her midwife for routine prenatal care. She has no risk factors for gestational diabetes and her pregnancy has otherwise progressed normally.

  1. The correct answer is A (A 50-gram glucose challenge test with venous glucose measured one hour later): this is the standard initial screening test for gestational diabetes in an average-risk woman around 24 to 28 weeks gestation. A fasting glucose test alone or a random capillary check does not provide the standardized information needed for screening. Hemoglobin A1c reflects longer-term glucose control and is not the recommended screening tool for gestational diabetes in this population.
  2. The correct answer is C (A 75-gram oral glucose tolerance test to confirm or rule out gestational diabetes): an elevated 1-hour glucose challenge result requires a confirmatory diagnostic test rather than an assumption of either normal or diagnosed diabetes. Starting insulin without confirmation is inappropriate, and reassurance would ignore an abnormal screening result. Repeating the same screening test does not provide the diagnostic clarity that the confirmatory test offers.
  3. The correct answer is D (Consult with or refer to a physician for collaborative management of her care): gestational diabetes falls outside normal low-risk scope and requires collaborative care per provincial consultation guidelines. Continuing to manage independently would exceed appropriate scope, while fully discontinuing midwifery care is unnecessary since collaborative care can continue. Delaying consultation risks poor glycemic control and adverse outcomes, so prompt consultation is appropriate as soon as the diagnosis is confirmed.
  4. The correct answer is B (0.4 to 1.0 mg of folic acid daily, started before conception if possible): this dose range is recommended for women at low risk for neural tube defects, ideally beginning before conception since the neural tube closes very early in pregnancy. A 5 mg high dose is reserved for women with specific risk factors, not the general population. Starting supplementation only after the neural tube has formed, or relying on diet alone, would miss the critical early window for prevention.
  5. The correct answer is C (Eating small, frequent meals and trying ginger or vitamin B6 for symptom relief): these conservative measures are first-line management for typical nausea and vomiting of pregnancy when the client remains well hydrated and is not losing weight. Hospital admission is reserved for more severe presentations such as hyperemesis gravidarum. Fasting is not recommended and can worsen nausea, and an urgent ultrasound is not indicated based on typical early nausea alone.
  6. The correct answer is C (Begin daily fetal movement awareness around 26 to 28 weeks gestation onward): becoming familiar with a baby's usual movement pattern at this stage helps a woman recognize meaningful changes later. Waiting until movements are already reduced misses the value of establishing a baseline pattern first. Movement awareness is meaningful well before 34 weeks, and delaying until full term would remove an important tool for ongoing fetal wellbeing surveillance.
Case C156

A woman at 19 weeks gestation attends her scheduled anatomy ultrasound. The report notes normal fetal anatomy, appropriate interval growth, and a placenta located on the posterior uterine wall, several centimetres from the internal cervical os.

  1. The correct answer is D (Detailed assessment of fetal anatomy and screening for structural anomalies): the mid-pregnancy anatomy scan systematically examines fetal organs and structures for anomalies, in addition to reviewing growth and placental location. Dating is most accurate earlier in the first trimester, not at this scan. Fetal sex may be noted incidentally but is not the scan's purpose, and cervical length assessment is not the primary reason for this scheduled anatomy scan.
  2. The correct answer is C (This is a normal placental location that does not require any further follow-up): a placenta located several centimetres from the internal os with no overlap is a normal finding and does not indicate placenta previa or abruption. Neither cesarean planning nor prohibiting vaginal birth is warranted based on this reassuring result. Placental abruption is an acute clinical event, not an incidental ultrasound finding of location, so urgent consultation is not indicated here.
  3. The correct answer is D (No repeat scan is needed, since the placenta is well clear of the cervical os): follow-up placental imaging is reserved for cases where the placenta is low-lying or overlapping the cervical os, which is not the case here. Weekly scans or scans based purely on personal reassurance are not clinically indicated and would represent unnecessary use of ultrasound resources. There is no urgency requiring a scan within twenty four hours for this normal finding.
  4. The correct answer is A (Recommend an oral iron supplement and dietary counselling on iron-rich foods): a hemoglobin of 105 g/L reflects mild anemia of pregnancy, which is typically managed first with oral iron supplementation and dietary guidance. A blood transfusion is reserved for more severe or symptomatic anemia. Calling this value entirely normal would overlook a treatable finding, and urgent hematology referral is not required before a trial of first-line oral iron therapy.
  5. The correct answer is A (Administration of Rh immune globulin as routine antenatal prophylaxis): routine antenatal Rh immune globulin around 28 weeks gestation helps prevent sensitization even without a known sensitizing event. Waiting for partner blood type confirmation is not standard practice, and giving Rh immune globulin only after birth would miss the antenatal prophylaxis window. Weekly antibody screening alone, without prophylaxis, does not provide the same protective benefit.
Case C161

A woman attends her first comprehensive prenatal visit at 10 weeks gestation. Her midwife orders a standard panel of first-visit bloodwork and reviews the results with her at a follow-up visit two weeks later.

  1. The correct answer is C (Blood group and type, antibody screen, complete blood count, and rubella immunity): these tests form part of the standard first-visit prenatal bloodwork panel used to establish baseline maternal health information. A glucose challenge test is performed later in pregnancy, not at the first visit. Group B Streptococcus screening occurs near term, and a biophysical profile is an ultrasound-based assessment, not part of first-visit laboratory testing.
  2. The correct answer is C (Recommend rubella vaccination after birth, since it cannot be given during pregnancy): rubella vaccine is a live attenuated vaccine and is contraindicated during pregnancy, so vaccination is deferred until after birth. Administering it now would pose a theoretical risk to the fetus. Non-immune status is not without consequence, since rubella infection during pregnancy can cause serious fetal harm, so this finding should not be dismissed as clinically irrelevant.
  3. The correct answer is A (HIV screening, offered as part of routine antenatal care to every pregnant woman): universal HIV screening is recommended for all pregnant women in Canada regardless of individual risk factors, since early identification allows interventions that greatly reduce transmission. Hepatitis C, tuberculosis, and toxoplasmosis screening are not universally recommended for every pregnant woman in the absence of specific risk factors or symptoms.
  4. The correct answer is B (A repeat antibody screen at 28 weeks gestation before Rh immune globulin): repeating the antibody screen before administering Rh immune globulin at 28 weeks confirms that sensitization has not developed since the initial visit. A repeat complete blood count is not specifically indicated by this blood group result, and repeating the blood typing test itself is unnecessary once confirmed. Stating that no further blood group related testing is needed would overlook this standard practice.
  5. The correct answer is C (Round ligament pain, a common and benign discomfort of pregnancy): sharp, brief groin or lower abdominal pain triggered by sudden movement, with normal vital signs and fetal heart rate, is typical of round ligament stretching as the uterus grows. Preterm labour, placental abruption, and appendicitis would typically present with additional concerning findings such as persistent pain, bleeding, or abnormal vital signs, none of which are described here.
  6. The correct answer is C (Eating smaller meals, avoiding lying down soon after eating, and elevating her head at night): these conservative lifestyle measures are first-line management for typical pregnancy-related heartburn caused by relaxation of the lower esophageal sphincter and uterine pressure. Urgent cardiac assessment is not indicated for classic positional heartburn symptoms. Complete food avoidance is unnecessary, and prescription medication is reserved for cases where conservative measures have already failed.
  7. The correct answer is B (Increasing dietary fibre, fluid intake, and daily physical activity): these conservative measures are the first-line approach for constipation in pregnancy, which is common due to hormonal effects on bowel motility. Stimulant laxatives are typically reserved for cases where dietary measures have failed. Reassurance without any recommendation ignores a treatable discomfort, and gastroenterology referral is not needed before simple conservative measures have been tried.
Case C168

A woman at 36 weeks gestation calls her midwife reporting that she has noticed fewer fetal movements than usual over the past several hours. She has no bleeding, pain, or leaking of fluid, and her pregnancy has been low risk throughout.

  1. The correct answer is D (Lie down, drink something cold, and focus on counting movements for the next hour): this practical approach helps clarify whether reduced movement reflects a truly quiet baby or a genuine change requiring assessment. Waiting for a scheduled visit or reassuring her that reduced movement is never a concern could delay identification of a problem. Advising complete bed rest is not an evidence-based response to a single report of reduced movement.
  2. The correct answer is C (Come in promptly for fetal heart rate assessment and further evaluation): a confirmed reduction in fetal movements after a period of focused counting warrants prompt clinical assessment to check fetal wellbeing. Continuing to monitor at home for another week, taking no action, or scheduling a routine appointment weeks later would all delay evaluation of a potentially significant change and are not appropriate responses to this finding.
  3. The correct answer is B (The current assessment is reassuring, and she should continue monitoring movements): a normal fetal heart rate and reassuring nonstress test indicate current fetal wellbeing, and ongoing movement awareness remains appropriate. This single reassuring result does not mean monitoring will never be needed again in the future. An emergency cesarean section is not indicated by a reassuring assessment, and this result has no bearing on the client's estimated due date.
  4. The correct answer is C (A daily vitamin D supplement is generally recommended, alongside adequate dietary calcium intake): routine vitamin D supplementation is commonly recommended during pregnancy since dietary and sun exposure sources are often insufficient, and calcium intake supports fetal skeletal development. Calcium is not contraindicated in pregnancy, and dismissing vitamin D supplementation as unnecessary ignores standard guidance. High-dose vitamin D is not the recommended approach, and supplementation is not restricted to the third trimester only.
  5. The correct answer is B (Air travel is generally safe at this gestation, with periodic walking to reduce clot risk): air travel is generally considered safe for uncomplicated pregnancies at this gestation, with simple precautions such as periodic walking to reduce venous thromboembolism risk. Air travel is not absolutely contraindicated after the first trimester for a low-risk pregnancy, and routine surgical clearance is not required. Remaining seated the entire flight would actually increase, rather than reduce, clotting risk.
Case C173

A 24-year-old woman with a positive home pregnancy test attends a midwife's clinic for her first prenatal visit. Her last menstrual period was approximately 9 weeks ago by her report, but her cycles are irregular. She has no significant medical history and is otherwise well.

  1. The correct answer is B (An ultrasound performed between 8 and 12 weeks gestation to measure the crown-rump length): First trimester crown-rump length measurement is the most accurate method for establishing gestational age, particularly when menstrual history is unreliable. Naegele's rule depends on regular, well-remembered cycles and becomes inaccurate with irregular periods. Second-trimester biometry is less precise for dating because normal fetal growth variation increases with gestational age. Quickening is a subjective and highly variable marker that should never be used to establish an estimated due date.
  2. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin, blood group and antibody screen, rubella immunity, and infectious disease serology): These baseline investigations are routinely offered to every pregnant client at the first prenatal visit regardless of risk factors. A fasting glucose tolerance test for pre-existing diabetes is reserved for clients with specific risk factors, not offered universally at booking. Group B Streptococcus swabbing is performed later in pregnancy, around 36 to 38 weeks, not at booking. Maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein alone is an outdated and incomplete approach compared with current combined first and second trimester screening options.
  3. The correct answer is A (Nuchal translucency ultrasound and maternal serum markers, performed between 11 and 14 weeks): This combination forms standard first trimester screening for chromosomal conditions and must be completed within this specific gestational window. Maternal age alone is an outdated and insufficient screening approach on its own. Cell-free DNA testing is not offered universally to every client and is typically reserved for those at increased risk or who request it. Amniocentesis is a diagnostic, invasive test performed later in pregnancy, not a first-line screening tool at this gestation.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reassure her that nausea is common in early pregnancy and suggest small frequent meals and ginger): Mild nausea with tolerated oral intake and stable weight is a normal early pregnancy discomfort managed with simple, evidence-based measures. Referral for intravenous antiemetics is not warranted for mild symptoms with normal hydration and weight. Restricting food and fluid intake would be inappropriate and could worsen her symptoms and nutritional status. Urgent hyperemesis workup is not indicated when there is no vomiting, dehydration, or weight loss described.
Case C177

A 30-year-old multigravida at 28 weeks gestation attends a routine prenatal visit with her primary midwife. Her pregnancy has been uncomplicated to date, and her blood pressure and urinalysis are within normal limits at today's visit.

  1. The correct answer is C (Every 2 to 3 weeks until 36 weeks gestation): The standard schedule for low-risk pregnancy increases visit frequency to every 2 to 3 weeks between 28 and 36 weeks before moving to weekly visits after 36 weeks. Monthly visits are appropriate earlier in pregnancy but not during this later interval. Weekly visits beginning at 28 weeks are more frequent than standard guidelines recommend for an uncomplicated pregnancy. Visits based only on symptoms would miss important routine screening and assessment for a low-risk client.
  2. The correct answer is A (This measurement is within the expected range and should be plotted on a growth chart): A fundal height of 27 cm at 28 weeks gestation falls within the normal range and should be tracked over time on a growth chart to identify trends. This single measurement does not meet criteria for suspected growth restriction, which requires a more significant discrepancy or a concerning trend. There is no indication here that her original dating is inaccurate. Nothing in this finding suggests excess amniotic fluid requiring urgent referral.
  3. The correct answer is D (The client reports a clear and sustained decrease in usual fetal movement pattern): A sustained change in a client's usual fetal movement pattern warrants further clinical evaluation rather than reassurance alone. Cephalic presentation with the back on the maternal left is a normal and expected finding. A fetal heart rate of 140 beats per minute is within the normal range and reassuring. Consistent daily fetal movement is a reassuring finding that supports fetal wellbeing.
  4. The correct answer is D (The first day of the client's last normal menstrual period and her usual cycle length): This information is the foundation for calculating an initial estimated due date and determines whether early dating ultrasound is needed for confirmation. Age and obstetric history are important but do not establish gestational age. Body mass index and prepregnancy weight are relevant to risk assessment but not to dating the pregnancy. Family history of multiple gestation does not contribute to establishing an estimated due date.
  5. The correct answer is B (Early ultrasound dating improves accuracy for later interpretation of fetal growth and screening results): Establishing accurate dates early in pregnancy improves the reliability of later fundal height trends, growth assessments, and screening test interpretation. Ultrasound is offered as informed choice, not a legal requirement, and clients may decline it. Ultrasound dating does not replace the need for routine prenatal bloodwork, which serves entirely different purposes. Fetal sex determination is not the clinical purpose of early dating ultrasound, and sex is often not reliably visible at this early gestation.
Case C182

A 33-year-old woman at 26 weeks gestation attends her midwife to discuss screening for gestational diabetes. She has no personal history of diabetes but her mother was diagnosed with type 2 diabetes in her 50s. She asks about the testing options available to her.

  1. The correct answer is B (Between 24 and 28 weeks gestation using a glucose challenge or tolerance test): This gestational window is the standard time for routine gestational diabetes screening in an average-risk pregnancy. Screening this early, at 8 to 10 weeks, would occur before the physiologic changes that typically reveal gestational diabetes have developed. Waiting until macrosomia is suspected would miss the benefit of earlier identification and management. Random glucose measurements at every visit are not the recommended standardized screening approach.
  2. The correct answer is A (A fasting value of 5.1 mmol/L or higher): A fasting plasma glucose at or above this threshold on the 75 gram oral glucose tolerance test meets diagnostic criteria for gestational diabetes on its own. A value of 3.5 mmol/L or lower is well below the normal range and not associated with diabetes. Values between 4.0 and 4.5 mmol/L fall within the normal, non-diagnostic range. A fasting value meeting the diagnostic threshold can independently establish the diagnosis without requiring the other timed values to also be abnormal.
  3. The correct answer is A (Consult with an obstetrician or endocrinologist while continuing to provide ongoing midwifery care): Canadian midwifery consultation guidelines support a shared care model where the midwife consults with a specialist while remaining involved in the client's ongoing care whenever appropriate. Complete transfer with no continued midwifery involvement is more restrictive than necessary for well-managed gestational diabetes. Simply repeating the test without initiating consultation and management delays appropriate care. Gestational diabetes does require additional monitoring, so reassurance alone would be an inadequate and unsafe response.
  4. The correct answer is B (The midwife should discuss the risks and benefits and support her right to decline screening): Informed choice is a core principle of Canadian midwifery care, and clients have the right to decline any screening test after receiving balanced information. Insisting on the test as a condition of continued care would violate this principle. Discharging a client for declining a screening test is inappropriate and disproportionate. Performing any test without consent is never acceptable regardless of how routine the test is considered.
  5. The correct answer is B (Increased fetal surveillance such as growth ultrasounds is typically recommended beyond routine low-risk care): A diagnosis of gestational diabetes, even when well controlled, typically warrants additional fetal growth surveillance beyond standard low-risk prenatal care. Stating that no additional surveillance is ever indicated ignores the recognized association between gestational diabetes and altered fetal growth patterns. Surveillance should not be delayed until a second complication develops, since gestational diabetes alone is the indication. Surveillance should be increased, not reduced, compared with a pregnancy without this diagnosis.
Case C187

A 29-year-old woman at 36 weeks gestation attends a routine prenatal visit. Her pregnancy has been low risk throughout, and her midwife reviews the plan for remaining prenatal screening and preparation for labour and birth.

  1. The correct answer is D (Between 35 and 37 weeks gestation): Group B Streptococcus colonization status can change over time, so swabbing is timed close to term to best predict status at the time of birth. Testing at 11 to 14 weeks would not reliably reflect colonization status near labour. Waiting until labour has begun would not allow enough time to plan intrapartum management. The anatomy scan visit at 18 to 20 weeks is far too early for this screening to remain accurate at the time of birth.
  2. The correct answer is D (Intravenous antibiotic prophylaxis is offered once active labour begins or membranes rupture): Intrapartum intravenous antibiotic prophylaxis is the recommended management for a positive group B Streptococcus swab to reduce the risk of neonatal transmission. Colonization can lead to serious neonatal infection, so no intervention would not be appropriate. A positive result alone is not an indication for cesarean birth. Antibiotics for group B Streptococcus are given intravenously during labour, not orally in advance of labour.
  3. The correct answer is D (A repeat hemoglobin level to screen for anemia before labour): A repeat hemoglobin in the third trimester helps identify anemia that could affect the client's tolerance of blood loss at birth. Rubella immunity does not need to be repeated once immunity is confirmed earlier in pregnancy. Blood group and antibody screening is not routinely repeated on a weekly basis for a low-risk client. First trimester screening panels are specific to that gestational window and are not repeated later in pregnancy.
  4. The correct answer is B (The client can expect care from her known midwife or small care team through labour and postpartum): Continuity of care is a core feature of the Canadian midwifery model, providing consistent care from a known midwife or small team across pregnancy, birth, and postpartum. Being assigned different unfamiliar providers at each stage is the opposite of this model of care. Continuity of care extends through labour and postpartum, not just the prenatal period. Continuity of care does not dictate place of birth, which remains the client's informed choice.
  5. The correct answer is A (Physiologic plasma volume expansion in pregnancy can lower hemoglobin values even without true iron deficiency): Plasma volume increases proportionally more than red cell mass in pregnancy, which can lower hemoglobin values and requires repeat testing to distinguish physiologic change from true anemia. Hemoglobin does not simply rise throughout pregnancy, so this framing is inaccurate. Hemoglobin testing does not determine fetal blood type, which requires separate testing. Hemoglobin values are directly affected by pregnancy physiology, so testing is clinically meaningful rather than purely precautionary.
  6. The correct answer is A (It helps screen for early signs of preeclampsia when combined with blood pressure assessment): Urinalysis for protein alongside blood pressure measurement helps identify early signs of hypertensive disorders of pregnancy such as preeclampsia. While urinalysis can also detect infection, that is not its only purpose in prenatal care. Urinalysis complements blood pressure measurement rather than replacing it. Ongoing urinalysis at remaining visits, not a single third trimester test, supports early detection of developing concerns.
Case C193

A 31-year-old woman at 34 weeks gestation contacts her midwife because she feels her baby has been moving less than usual over the past day compared to her normal pattern. She has otherwise had an uncomplicated pregnancy.

  1. The correct answer is D (Have her attend for prompt clinical assessment including fetal heart auscultation and further evaluation): A report of decreased fetal movement compared with a client's own usual pattern warrants prompt clinical assessment rather than reassurance alone. Decreased movement should never be dismissed by phone as always normal, since it can be an early sign of fetal compromise. Waiting a full week would delay identification of a potentially concerning change. Recommending caffeine to stimulate movement is not an evidence-based or appropriate clinical response.
  2. The correct answer is D (Advise her on ongoing fetal movement awareness and provide clear guidance for when to seek further care): With a reassuring fetal heart rate and movements felt during assessment, ongoing guidance on movement awareness and clear return precautions is the appropriate next step. Discharging her from care would be inappropriate after a concerning symptom, even with reassuring findings. A single reassuring assessment does not justify an immediate cesarean birth. Stopping further prenatal visits would remove ongoing surveillance that remains important for the rest of her pregnancy.
  3. The correct answer is D (Becoming familiar with her baby's usual daily movement pattern and reporting any clear change): Current guidance emphasizes maternal awareness of her own baby's individual movement pattern rather than counting to a fixed target number. Attempting to auscultate fetal heart rate independently is not a reliable or recommended self-monitoring method. Home non-stress testing with personal equipment is not an accepted standard of care. Braxton Hicks contractions reflect uterine activity, not fetal wellbeing, and should not be used as a substitute marker.
  4. The correct answer is B (A non-stress test using external fetal heart rate monitoring): The non-stress test is the most commonly used method of formal antenatal fetal surveillance in Canadian practice when further evaluation beyond clinical assessment is indicated. Maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein is a screening test for structural anomalies, not a tool for ongoing fetal surveillance. Nuchal translucency is a first trimester screening measurement and cannot be repeated later in pregnancy for this purpose. A group B Streptococcus swab assesses maternal colonization status, not fetal wellbeing.
  5. The correct answer is C (Become familiar with her baby's individual normal pattern rather than counting to a fixed number): Current guidance favours maternal awareness of an individual baseline movement pattern over rigid formal counting protocols. Requiring exactly ten movements within a fixed hour every day is a more rigid approach than current recommendations support. Fetal movement awareness is a valuable part of ongoing pregnancy monitoring, not something that should be dismissed entirely. Awareness of movement pattern should continue throughout the third trimester, not be limited to a single assessment.
  6. The correct answer is A (It provides a simple ongoing screen for appropriate interval fetal growth over time): Serial fundal height measurement offers a simple, low-cost screening tool to track interval fetal growth throughout pregnancy. It is not the only method of estimating fetal weight, since ultrasound biometry also serves this purpose when indicated. Fundal height does not replace ultrasound assessment when ultrasound is clinically indicated for other reasons. While palpation can help assess presentation, fundal height measurement itself is specifically about tracking growth.
  7. The correct answer is A (Folic acid, ideally started before conception and continued through pregnancy): Folic acid supplementation, ideally started before conception, is specifically recommended to reduce the risk of neural tube defects. High dose vitamin C beginning in the second trimester has no established role in preventing neural tube defects. Calcium carbonate limited to the final trimester does not address neural tube development, which occurs very early in pregnancy. Vitamin E has no established evidence supporting a role in preventing neural tube defects.
  8. The correct answer is B (Unpasteurized dairy products and undercooked meat due to listeria and toxoplasmosis risk): These specific foods carry recognized risks of listeria and toxoplasmosis infection, which can seriously affect a pregnancy, so they are appropriately identified as food safety concerns. Iron needs actually increase during pregnancy, so avoiding dietary iron sources would be inappropriate advice. Whole grain breads and cereals are a healthy, recommended part of a balanced pregnancy diet, not a food to avoid. Pasteurization specifically reduces the risk of these infections, so pasteurized dairy does not carry the same risk as unpasteurized products.
  9. The correct answer is D (Explain this is a common, benign discomfort related to uterine growth and suggest gentle position changes): Round ligament pain with position change is a common, benign discomfort of pregnancy related to uterine growth and stretching of supporting ligaments. Ectopic pregnancy is not a concern at 16 weeks with an already confirmed intrauterine pregnancy. This pattern of pain does not indicate preterm labour and should not be described in such absolute terms. Strong opioid analgesics are not appropriate first-line treatment for a mild, benign musculoskeletal discomfort.
  10. The correct answer is A (Typically between 10 and 12 weeks gestation, though timing can vary by individual): Fetal heart tones can usually be detected with a handheld Doppler starting around 10 to 12 weeks gestation, with some individual variation. Four weeks gestation is far too early, well before a fetal heart has even begun to develop. Doppler auscultation is reliable well before the third trimester once the fetal heart is developed enough to detect. Doppler is a standard and reliable tool for assessing fetal heart tones throughout pregnancy once this early threshold is reached.
  11. The correct answer is C (Routine supplementation is individualized based on dietary intake and repeat hemoglobin results): Iron supplementation decisions are individualized, guided by dietary assessment and repeat hemoglobin testing rather than applied uniformly. Requiring high dose iron for every client regardless of hemoglobin ignores individual variation and risks unnecessary side effects. Iron supplementation can be appropriate when clinically indicated, so stating it is never appropriate is inaccurate. Iron needs actually increase during pregnancy, so this statement misrepresents normal pregnancy physiology.
  12. The correct answer is A (Routine weighing at every visit is not required, and weight is assessed based on individual clinical indication): Current practice does not require routine weighing at every single visit, and weight is instead assessed when clinically indicated for that individual. Requiring weighing at every visit without exception does not reflect current individualized practice. Weight remains relevant beyond the first visit when there is a clinical reason to reassess it. Weight gain patterns can be clinically relevant to maternal and fetal wellbeing, so this statement is inaccurate.
  13. The correct answer is D (It assesses fetal anatomy, growth, and placental location in detail during this window): The 20 week anatomy ultrasound provides detailed assessment of fetal structural anatomy, growth, and placental location. While gestational age may be reviewed, confirming the due date is not the primary purpose of this detailed anatomy scan. This ultrasound does not replace the need for further growth assessment later in pregnancy if clinically indicated. Determining fetal sex may be possible but is not the clinical purpose that drives this scan being offered.
Case C206

A 24-year-old G1P0 presents at approximately 9 weeks gestation by last menstrual period for her booking visit with a community midwife. Her cycles are normally regular at 30 days. She reports mild nausea in the mornings but no bleeding or pain.

  1. The correct answer is C (Early first trimester ultrasound crown rump length measurement): Crown rump length measured between 7 and 13 weeks gestation is the most accurate method of establishing gestational age, with an accuracy of plus or minus three to five days. Even a client with regular cycles can have variable timing of ovulation. Fundal height at a single early visit is not precise enough for dating, and quickening is subjective and highly variable between individuals.
  2. The correct answer is D (Add one day to the due date for each day her cycle exceeds 28 days): Naegele's rule assumes a 28 day cycle with ovulation on day 14. A longer follicular phase delays ovulation, so each additional day beyond 28 pushes ovulation and conception later, requiring the same number of days added to the calculated due date. Subtracting days would move the estimate in the wrong direction, and ignoring cycle length reduces dating accuracy for someone with a known irregular pattern.
  3. The correct answer is A (Rising levels of human chorionic gonadotropin affecting the chemoreceptor trigger zone): Nausea and vomiting of pregnancy typically begin around six weeks gestation, coinciding with the rapid rise in human chorionic gonadotropin, believed to act on the chemoreceptor trigger zone and gastrointestinal motility. Blood volume expansion occurs later and is not the accepted mechanism. Fetal movement is not perceptible this early, and iron stores are not a recognized cause of first trimester nausea.
  4. The correct answer is B (Ultrasound crown rump length measurement): When menstrual dates are unreliable, an ultrasound scan performed in the first trimester and measuring crown rump length is the most accurate method available for establishing gestational age. A single beta hCG level reflects a wide range of possible gestational ages and cannot date a pregnancy precisely. Bimanual examination gives only a rough estimate, and progesterone levels are not used clinically for dating.
  5. The correct answer is D (Gestational age is calculated from the first day of the last menstrual period): By convention, gestational age is dated from the first day of the last menstrual period, approximately two weeks before ovulation and conception actually occur. Fetal or conceptual age is a separate, less commonly used measure that starts at fertilization and runs roughly two weeks behind gestational age. A positive urine test date has no fixed relationship to ovulation and is never used for dating.
Case C211

A 30-year-old G1P0 attends her booking visit with a registered midwife at 10 weeks gestation. She has no significant medical history and takes a prenatal vitamin daily. Blood work and a physical assessment are planned for today's visit.

  1. The correct answer is C (Blood group and antibody screen, complete blood count, and rubella immunity): Standard first visit bloodwork in Canada includes blood group and Rh status with an antibody screen, a complete blood count to assess for anemia, rubella immunity, and screening for infections such as syphilis, hepatitis B, and HIV. A fasting lipid panel and routine thyroid testing are not part of universal prenatal screening. Karyotype testing is only offered based on specific risk factors, not routinely.
  2. The correct answer is C (Repeat antibody screening and consideration of anti D immunoglobulin prophylaxis): An Rh negative client requires ongoing antibody screening throughout pregnancy and is offered anti D immunoglobulin, typically around 28 weeks and after any sensitizing event, to prevent alloimmunization if the fetus is Rh positive. Rh status alone is not an indication for genetic counselling. It does not change the timing of a routine anatomy ultrasound, and it has no relationship to prenatal vitamin use.
  3. The correct answer is A (A positive immune result indicates current immunity, though vaccination in pregnancy is not recommended): A positive rubella immunity screen confirms she currently has protective antibodies, but because the vaccine is a live attenuated product, it cannot be given during pregnancy. Immunity can occasionally wane over time, so lifelong protection is not guaranteed. The screen has no relationship to varicella, and a positive immune result does not itself trigger vaccination.
  4. The correct answer is A (Universal hepatitis B screening in pregnancy allows timely newborn immunoprophylaxis if positive): All pregnant clients in Canada are screened for hepatitis B surface antigen regardless of reported risk factors, because many carriers are asymptomatic and unaware of their status. Identifying a positive result allows the newborn to receive hepatitis B immunoglobulin and vaccine shortly after birth to prevent transmission. Hepatitis B can be transmitted perinatally, and screening is performed early in pregnancy, not reserved for the third trimester.
  5. The correct answer is C (It establishes a reliable gestational age that guides later interpretation of growth and screening): An accurate gestational age obtained early in pregnancy is the foundation for interpreting fundal height, fetal growth trends, the timing of aneuploidy and anatomy screening, and decisions about postdates management later in care. Fetal sex determination is not the purpose of dating ultrasound and is often not reliably visible this early. It does not replace bloodwork, and while multiple gestation may be identified incidentally, that is not the primary indication for offering it universally.
  6. The correct answer is D (Reassess fundal height technique and consider an ultrasound if the discrepancy persists): A single fundal height measurement more than two to three centimetres below expected gestational age warrants a careful recheck of technique, maternal bladder emptying, and fetal position, with an ultrasound for growth assessment if confirmed. Referring immediately for induction is not appropriate for an isolated growth discrepancy in an otherwise well pregnancy. Providing reassurance with no follow up risks missing true fetal growth restriction, and a complete blood count does not evaluate fetal growth.
  7. The correct answer is A (Maternal obesity or a full bladder at the time of measurement): Symphysis fundal height measurement is a screening tool with known limitations, and its accuracy is reduced by maternal obesity, a full bladder, fetal lie, amniotic fluid volume, and measurement technique. Blood type has no bearing on fundal height accuracy, and neither does the time of day the visit occurs. Influenza vaccination status is unrelated to uterine measurement.
  8. The correct answer is D (From approximately 20 to 36 weeks gestation in a singleton pregnancy): Between roughly 20 and 36 weeks, symphysis fundal height in centimetres approximates the number of completed gestational weeks within about two to three centimetres in most singleton pregnancies with a normally positioned uterus. Before 20 weeks the uterus is not yet reliably palpable above the symphysis, and after 36 weeks fetal descent and lie can reduce accuracy. The correlation does exist within that window, so it is not entirely absent.
  9. The correct answer is D (Reassessing for polyhydramnios, macrosomia, or inaccurate dates before further action): A fundal height measurement significantly larger than expected should prompt consideration of common causes such as polyhydramnios, a large for gestational age fetus, multiple gestation, or a dating discrepancy, often clarified with an ultrasound. Immediate transfer for a fetal anomaly is not warranted from this finding alone. Movement counting remains valuable and should continue, and dismissing the finding without reassessment risks missing a clinically relevant cause.
  10. The correct answer is B (A client with a body mass index in the obese range where fundal height is unreliable): When maternal body habitus is expected to make fundal height measurement unreliable, serial ultrasound is a more appropriate method for tracking fetal growth. A client with a normal singleton pregnancy and average body habitus is well served by routine fundal height screening. A client who declines screening would not be having growth actively monitored by ultrasound, and at 12 weeks the fundus is not yet reliably palpable for this comparison regardless.
Case C221

A 29-year-old G1P0 at 32 weeks gestation attends a routine visit. She reports that fetal movements feel less frequent than in previous weeks, though she has not counted them formally. The fetal heart rate by handheld Doppler is 140 beats per minute.

  1. The correct answer is C (Teach a structured fetal movement counting method and arrange timely follow up): A subjective report of reduced fetal movement should be taken seriously and followed by structured counting, such as counting to ten movements within a defined time period, along with clear guidance on when to seek further assessment. A brief reassuring Doppler check does not rule out reduced movement over time. Movement does not universally decrease as a normal third trimester pattern, and induction is not indicated from this history alone.
  2. The correct answer is D (Non stress test or biophysical profile as per local guidelines): Confirmed decreased fetal movement warrants further fetal surveillance, typically a non stress test or biophysical profile, to assess fetal wellbeing and guide next steps in care. A blood group and antibody screen has no role in assessing acute fetal wellbeing. A glucose challenge test screens for gestational diabetes and is unrelated to this presentation, and genetic counselling is not indicated by reduced movement alone.
  3. The correct answer is C (Fetal movement patterns vary between individual fetuses but should remain consistent for each fetus over time): Each fetus tends to establish its own individual pattern of movement, and the clinically relevant sign is a change from that established baseline rather than an absolute universal number. Movement is not expected to steadily decline through the third trimester as a normal pattern. Very infrequent movement is not considered reassuring, and movement counting remains a valuable adjunct even when heart tones are currently reassuring.
  4. The correct answer is A (Continue daily fetal movement awareness and report any further significant change promptly): A reassuring non stress test reflects fetal wellbeing at that point in time and does not eliminate the value of ongoing movement awareness, since a future change could still occur and should be reported. A single reassuring test does not mean surveillance is never needed again, and it is not an indication for induction. Routine prenatal visits should continue on the usual recommended schedule.
  5. The correct answer is D (The specific number matters less than noticing her own baby's usual pattern and any change from it): Current counselling emphasizes maternal awareness of her own baby's typical movement pattern, with any significant change from that established pattern being the key sign to report, rather than a single rigid universal count. Requiring hourly counting every hour is not standard guidance and is impractical. Movement should remain perceptible throughout the third trimester up to labour, and movement after meals is not a specifically reassuring or expected pattern on its own.
  6. The correct answer is D (This rate falls within the normal fetal heart rate range of 110 to 160 beats per minute): A baseline fetal heart rate of 110 to 160 beats per minute is considered within the normal range at any gestational age where it can be auscultated, and 150 beats per minute falls comfortably within that range. It does not represent tachycardia, generally defined as sustained above 160. The normal range is not limited to the first trimester, and handheld Doppler can typically detect fetal heart tones reliably from around 10 to 12 weeks gestation onward.
Case C227

A 26-year-old G1P0 at 26 weeks gestation attends a routine visit. Her midwife recommends a 50 gram glucose challenge test as part of routine gestational diabetes screening. The client has no personal or family history of diabetes.

  1. The correct answer is D (Universal screening between 24 and 28 weeks is recommended regardless of individual risk factors): Screening for gestational diabetes between 24 and 28 weeks gestation is recommended for all pregnant clients in Canada, not only those with identifiable risk factors, because many cases occur without a prior family history. The 50 gram challenge test does not require fasting beforehand. An elevated result on this initial screen is not diagnostic on its own and requires a follow up diagnostic oral glucose tolerance test.
  2. The correct answer is A (Proceed to a diagnostic 75 gram oral glucose tolerance test): An elevated result on the initial glucose challenge screen is followed by a diagnostic oral glucose tolerance test, most commonly using a 75 gram load with fasting and timed blood samples, to confirm or rule out gestational diabetes. A diagnosis and treatment plan should never be based on the screening test alone. Absence of risk factors does not justify disregarding an abnormal screening result.
  3. The correct answer is D (Untreated gestational diabetes increases risks such as macrosomia and neonatal hypoglycemia even without maternal symptoms): Gestational diabetes is frequently asymptomatic for the pregnant person, yet untreated hyperglycemia can lead to fetal macrosomia, shoulder dystocia, and neonatal hypoglycemia after birth, which is why universal screening has real clinical value. The condition affects fetal outcomes directly, not only maternal health. Screening guides real management decisions, and clinically significant hyperglycemia does not reliably produce noticeable maternal symptoms before fetal risk accumulates.
  4. The correct answer is A (It is performed between 36 and 38 weeks to guide intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis decisions): Group B streptococcus screening with a combined vaginal and rectal swab is recommended in this later gestational window because colonization status can change, and a positive result guides the offer of intravenous antibiotic prophylaxis during labour to reduce early onset neonatal infection. It is not performed at booking since early colonization status does not predict status at term. A positive result does not itself indicate a need for cesarean birth, and the swab targets colonization rather than urinary infection.
  5. The correct answer is D (A repeat screen detects any new antibodies that may have developed, particularly relevant if she is Rh negative): Repeating the antibody screen later in pregnancy, especially around 28 weeks for Rh negative clients, identifies any newly developed antibodies that could affect fetal or newborn management, since sensitization can occur at any point during pregnancy. The initial result is not inherently unreliable, this test does not determine fetal blood type directly, and it does not replace newborn screening performed after birth.
  6. The correct answer is B (Routine urine testing helps screen for proteinuria and asymptomatic bacteriuria at appropriate points in care): Urine testing in pregnancy retains clinical value for detecting proteinuria, relevant to hypertensive disorders, and for identifying asymptomatic bacteriuria, treated in pregnancy to reduce the risk of pyelonephritis. It has no connection to determining fetal sex. Blood pressure measurement remains a separate and essential component of every visit and is not replaced by urinalysis.
Case C233

A 22-year-old client presents for preconception counselling and states she plans to try to conceive within the next few months. She currently takes no vitamins or supplements.

  1. The correct answer is D (Folic acid supplementation starting before conception and continuing into early pregnancy): Folic acid supplementation beginning at least two to three months before conception and continuing through the first trimester reduces the risk of neural tube defects, since the neural tube closes very early before many people realize they have conceived. Iron alone does not address this risk. High dose vitamin C has no established role in supporting conception. Waiting until a positive pregnancy test misses the critical early window for neural tube protection.
  2. The correct answer is D (A daily multivitamin containing 0.4 to 1.0 milligrams of folic acid): For someone at average risk with no personal or family history of neural tube defects, a daily prenatal multivitamin containing 0.4 to 1.0 milligrams of folic acid is generally recommended, started before conception and continued through early pregnancy. A single one time dose does not provide the sustained level needed. Dietary folate alone is not considered sufficiently reliable, and 20 milligrams daily is far above what is recommended for average risk care.
  3. The correct answer is C (A daily supplement of approximately 400 to 600 international units is commonly recommended in Canada): Health Canada and most prenatal vitamin formulations provide vitamin D in the range of approximately 400 to 600 international units daily, supporting maternal and fetal bone health, given limited sun exposure across much of the country for large parts of the year. Vitamin D is not contraindicated at recommended doses. Sun exposure alone is not a reliable year round source in the Canadian climate, and vitamin D has an established role in calcium metabolism and bone health.
  4. The correct answer is C (Iron needs increase in pregnancy and a prenatal vitamin with iron is generally continued, with dosing adjusted if anemia develops): Iron requirements rise substantially in pregnancy to support expanding maternal blood volume and fetal needs, so a standard prenatal vitamin containing iron is typically continued even with a normal booking hemoglobin, with additional supplementation added if anemia is later identified. Stopping iron immediately after one normal result ignores ongoing increased demand later in pregnancy. High dose supplementation beyond what is needed is not appropriate without an identified deficiency.
  5. The correct answer is D (Attention to adequate vitamin B12, iron, and omega 3 fatty acid intake or supplementation): A vegan diet in pregnancy warrants specific attention to nutrients less available from plant sources alone, including vitamin B12, iron, and long chain omega 3 fatty acids, often requiring targeted supplementation beyond a standard prenatal vitamin. Protein intake is not typically in excess or requiring restriction on a well planned vegan diet, and vitamin B12 deficiency is a genuine and well recognized risk without supplementation.
  6. The correct answer is B (Small frequent meals, ginger, and reassurance that symptoms often improve after the first trimester): For nausea and vomiting of pregnancy without signs of dehydration or significant weight loss, first line management includes dietary modification such as small frequent meals, ginger, and reassurance that symptoms commonly ease by the end of the first trimester. Hospital admission is not warranted when oral intake and hydration are maintained. Stopping oral intake entirely would worsen the situation, and typical symptom severity is not an indication for termination counselling.
  7. The correct answer is D (Signs of dehydration, ketonuria, or significant weight loss): Findings such as clinical dehydration, ketones in the urine, or significant weight loss suggest a more severe picture, potentially hyperemesis gravidarum, and warrant further assessment beyond simple reassurance. Morning predominance of nausea is a typical and expected pattern. Onset around six weeks gestation is consistent with the usual timeline. Mild food aversions with a generally preserved appetite are common and not concerning on their own.
  8. The correct answer is A (Fatigue is a common first trimester symptom related to rising progesterone and metabolic changes): Significant fatigue is a very common and expected symptom in early pregnancy, related to rising progesterone levels, increased metabolic demands, and cardiovascular adaptation, typically improving somewhat by the second trimester. It is not automatically diagnostic of anemia without supporting bloodwork. Fatigue is a well recognized first trimester symptom, not one confined to later pregnancy, and it has no established relationship to fetal wellbeing on its own.
  9. The correct answer is C (Both are common early pregnancy symptoms related to hormonal changes and the growing uterus pressing on the bladder): Breast tenderness relates to rising estrogen and progesterone preparing breast tissue for lactation, while increased urinary frequency relates to increased pelvic blood flow and the enlarging uterus pressing on the bladder before it rises out of the pelvis. These findings alone do not indicate a urinary tract infection without other supporting symptoms. They are common in singleton pregnancies as well, and they are not specific indicators of ectopic pregnancy.
  10. The correct answer is A (Hormonal fluctuations in early pregnancy commonly affect mood, though significant distress should still be assessed): Fluctuating levels of estrogen and progesterone in early pregnancy are commonly associated with mood changes, and while this is a normal experience for many, the midwife should still screen for and take seriously any signs of significant distress. It is inaccurate to dismiss any hormonal contribution to mood. Emotional lability alone is not evidence of a diagnosable disorder, and mood changes can occur at any point in pregnancy.
Case C243

A 31-year-old G2P1 at 22 weeks gestation reports intermittent sharp pain in her right lower abdomen that occurs with sudden movements or position changes, lasting only seconds and resolving quickly. She has no fever, bleeding, or urinary symptoms.

  1. The correct answer is C (Round ligament pain related to stretching as the uterus grows): Sharp, brief, movement related pain in the lower abdomen, often on one side, without fever, bleeding, or other concerning features, is a classic presentation of round ligament pain caused by stretching of the ligaments supporting the growing uterus. This history is not consistent with appendicitis, which typically produces persistent worsening pain. It does not fit placental abruption, which usually presents with persistent pain, bleeding, or a tense uterus, nor preterm labour, which involves regular contractions.
  2. The correct answer is D (Slow position changes and gentle stretching, with follow up if pain pattern changes): For typical round ligament pain, reassurance combined with practical strategies such as moving more slowly between positions, gentle stretching, and support garments is appropriate, along with clear guidance to return if the pain pattern changes. Strict bed rest is not indicated for this benign and self limited condition. Stopping all activity is unnecessary, and antibiotics have no role since there is no indication of an infectious process.
  3. The correct answer is A (Persistent pain accompanied by fever, bleeding, or uterine tenderness): The presence of fever, vaginal bleeding, or a tender or contracting uterus alongside abdominal pain shifts the clinical picture away from simple round ligament pain and warrants further assessment for other causes such as infection or preterm labour. Brief position related twinges that resolve quickly remain consistent with the typical benign pattern. Pain specifically with quick standing and mild discomfort relieved by rest both fit the expected round ligament presentation rather than a warning sign.
  4. The correct answer is C (Progesterone mediated relaxation of smooth muscle slowing gastrointestinal motility): Elevated progesterone in pregnancy relaxes smooth muscle throughout the body, including the gastrointestinal tract, which slows motility and commonly contributes to constipation, along with reduced activity and iron supplementation in some clients.
