Increased blood volume does not directly cause bowel slowing. Vitamin D status has no established link to bowel motility, and fetal movement does not cause maternal constipation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Relaxation of the lower esophageal sphincter combined with upward pressure from the growing uterus): Progesterone relaxes the lower esophageal sphincter while the enlarging uterus displaces abdominal contents and increases intra abdominal pressure, together promoting gastroesophageal reflux and the classic postprandial and positional burning sensation of heartburn in pregnancy. This presentation does not fit a new food allergy. It is not a recognized sign of preeclampsia, and this typical postprandial burning pattern does not suggest a cardiac arrhythmia.
  6. The correct answer is B (Increased venous pressure from the enlarging uterus combined with progesterone related vein wall relaxation): Varicose veins in pregnancy commonly develop due to increased venous pressure in the lower extremities from the enlarging uterus compressing pelvic veins, combined with progesterone induced relaxation of vein walls and increased blood volume. They are a common benign finding, not evidence of deep vein thrombosis, which typically presents with unilateral swelling, pain, and warmth. Dietary sodium is not the established cause, and varicose veins have no relationship to fetal cardiac health.
Case C249

A 25-year-old G1P0 at 30 weeks gestation asks her midwife to explain the cardiovascular changes happening in her body during pregnancy, since she has noticed her resting heart rate is higher than before pregnancy.

  1. The correct answer is A (Increased cardiac output is achieved partly through an increased heart rate alongside increased stroke volume): Normal pregnancy involves a substantial increase in cardiac output, achieved through a combination of increased stroke volume and a modest increase in resting heart rate, typically rising by about 10 to 20 beats per minute by the third trimester. Heart rate does not decrease as blood volume rises. Heart rate and cardiac output are directly related in this context, and a mild expected increase in an otherwise well pregnancy is not itself a sign of cardiac disease.
  2. The correct answer is D (Approximately 40 to 50 percent above pre pregnancy levels): Maternal blood volume increases substantially during a normal singleton pregnancy, typically by around 40 to 50 percent above pre pregnancy levels, driven mainly by plasma volume expansion that outpaces the rise in red cell mass. This is a well established and expected change, not a stable or decreasing value. A 5 percent increase would substantially understate the true physiological expansion that occurs.
  3. The correct answer is C (Physiological hemodilution occurs because plasma volume expands more than red cell mass): Because plasma volume increases proportionally more than red blood cell mass during pregnancy, a mild physiological drop in measured hemoglobin concentration is expected and is often called dilutional anemia, distinct from true iron deficiency anemia. This finding does not automatically mean a transfusion is needed and should not be dismissed outright as an error. Hemoglobin is expected to change across pregnancy rather than stay static.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Progesterone increases respiratory drive and the diaphragm is elevated by the enlarging uterus): Progesterone increases respiratory drive and a subjective sense of increased effort to breathe, while the growing uterus progressively elevates the diaphragm, contributing to a common sensation of mild breathlessness even when oxygen saturation is normal. This common pattern does not itself indicate cardiac disease. It is not automatically explained by anemia without supporting bloodwork, and normal maternal saturation does not indicate any issue with fetal oxygen delivery.
  2. The correct answer is B (Systemic vascular resistance decreases in mid pregnancy due to progesterone mediated vasodilation): Progesterone induced vasodilation lowers systemic vascular resistance, producing a physiological dip in blood pressure that typically reaches its lowest point around the middle of pregnancy before gradually rising again toward term. Blood pressure does not rise in a steady straight line from conception. A modest expected dip at this stage is not evidence of hemorrhage, and it has no established connection to placental function.
  3. The correct answer is B (Mild dependent edema is common in later pregnancy and can be managed with rest and leg elevation): Mild, symmetric, dependent swelling of the lower legs that improves overnight is a common and generally benign finding in later pregnancy, related to increased venous pressure and fluid retention, managed conservatively with rest, leg elevation, and monitoring. With normal blood pressure and urinalysis, this presentation does not indicate preeclampsia. Some degree of dependent swelling is expected rather than something that should never occur, and this bilateral, improving pattern is not consistent with deep vein thrombosis.
  4. The correct answer is A (Approximately every four weeks until 28 weeks, every two to three weeks until 36 weeks, then weekly until birth): The conventional low risk prenatal visit schedule increases in frequency as pregnancy progresses, from roughly monthly visits earlier in pregnancy to visits every two to three weeks in the early third trimester and weekly visits from around 36 weeks until birth. A single early visit followed by one near the due date would miss important ongoing assessment. Purely weekly visits from the first trimester are more frequent than needed, and a fixed monthly schedule throughout does not reflect the increased surveillance appropriately offered as term approaches.
  5. The correct answer is C (More frequent visits allow earlier detection of complications such as hypertension that can develop later in pregnancy): Conditions such as hypertensive disorders of pregnancy and fetal growth concerns can develop or progress even after a previously uncomplicated course, so increasing visit frequency in the third trimester supports earlier detection and timely intervention. This schedule reflects genuine clinical rationale rather than tradition or convenience alone, and the underlying reasoning is clinical, not merely administrative.
  6. The correct answer is A (The standard schedule is recommended for safety, but care plans can be discussed collaboratively based on her individual circumstances): The midwifery model of care centers informed choice, so while the standard visit schedule is recommended because it supports timely detection of emerging concerns, a midwife should discuss the reasoning behind it and work collaboratively with the client on a plan that respects her autonomy alongside safety. It would not be accurate to say she has no input. Reducing visit frequency does carry potential implications that should be discussed, and this conversation is not restricted only to very late pregnancy.
  7. The correct answer is C (Early detection of hypertensive disorders of pregnancy such as preeclampsia): Routine blood pressure measurement at every visit allows early identification of rising blood pressure trends that may indicate the development of gestational hypertension or preeclampsia. This is a genuinely valuable diagnostic screening tool, not a purely conventional practice. It is not used as a measure of anxiety, and it complements rather than replaces urine testing, since both findings together inform assessment for hypertensive disorders.
Case C259

A 33-year-old G1P0 at 11 weeks gestation is discussing prenatal screening options for chromosomal conditions with her midwife. She wants to understand her choices before deciding.

  1. The correct answer is A (It combines maternal serum markers with nuchal translucency ultrasound measurement to estimate risk): First trimester combined screening integrates maternal serum biomarkers with an ultrasound measurement of nuchal translucency, typically performed between about 11 and 14 weeks, to generate an individualized risk estimate for certain chromosomal conditions. It is a screening test that provides a probability, not a definitive diagnosis. It is performed in the first trimester, not after 20 weeks, and it is non invasive, distinct from invasive diagnostic procedures like amniocentesis.
  2. The correct answer is A (Options for further diagnostic testing such as non invasive prenatal testing or amniocentesis): A higher risk screening result prompts a discussion of further testing options, which may include non invasive prenatal testing for additional refinement of risk or definitive diagnostic testing such as amniocentesis, allowing the client to make an informed choice. This finding has no bearing on labour management. It would never be appropriate to automatically refer for termination based on a screening result alone, and simply repeating the identical test provides no additional diagnostic clarity.
  3. The correct answer is D (Respect and support her informed decision while continuing routine pregnancy care): Prenatal aneuploidy screening is optional, and after a thorough informed discussion, respecting and supporting a client's decision to decline is fully consistent with the midwifery model's emphasis on informed choice, with routine care continuing unaffected. Screening cannot be presented as mandatory. Declining an optional test is not grounds for discharge from care, and framing a fully informed decision as noncompliance misrepresents the nature of informed choice.
  4. The correct answer is A (Detailed fetal anatomy, placental location, and amniotic fluid volume): The second trimester anatomy ultrasound, typically performed around 18 to 20 weeks, provides a detailed assessment of fetal anatomical structures, placental location including screening for placenta previa, and amniotic fluid volume. Fetal sex may often be visible if desired, but that is not the scan's primary purpose. The scan does not measure maternal blood pressure, and images alone do not directly determine fetal chromosomal number, which requires genetic testing.
  5. The correct answer is D (A screening test estimates risk or probability, while a diagnostic test provides a definitive result): Screening tests, such as first trimester combined screening or non invasive prenatal testing, generate a probability or risk estimate rather than a definitive answer, whereas diagnostic tests such as chorionic villus sampling or amniocentesis analyze fetal cells directly to provide a definitive result. Screening tests are generally non invasive, in contrast to diagnostic tests which are invasive. There is a meaningful and clinically important distinction between these two categories.
Case C264

A 29-year-old G1P0 at 36 weeks gestation attends her weekly visit. Her midwife performs Leopold maneuvers and determines the fetus is in a cephalic presentation with the back on the client's left side.

  1. The correct answer is D (To determine fetal lie, presentation, and position through abdominal palpation): Leopold maneuvers are a systematic series of abdominal palpation techniques used to assess fetal lie, presenting part, and position, information important for planning care as term approaches. They do not assess fetal heart rate variability, which requires auscultation or electronic monitoring. They are an abdominal assessment and do not evaluate cervical dilation or effacement, and while they can suggest an impression of fluid volume, they do not precisely estimate it.
  2. The correct answer is B (Continue routine care with reassessment of presentation at subsequent visits): A cephalic presentation at 36 weeks is the expected and reassuring finding for a term pregnancy, so routine care continues with ongoing reassessment of presentation at subsequent visits. External cephalic version is a procedure used to attempt turning a breech presenting fetus and is not relevant here. A cephalic presentation does not indicate any need for cesarean section, and fetal surveillance should continue rather than stop as pregnancy nears term.
  3. The correct answer is D (Confirm the finding with ultrasound and discuss options including external cephalic version): A suspected breech presentation identified on abdominal palpation at 36 weeks should be confirmed with ultrasound, after which options including external cephalic version, mode of birth planning, and consultation with an obstetrician can be discussed. Proceeding directly to birth planning without confirmation skips an important diagnostic step. Leopold maneuvers are a recognized and valid method for assessing presentation, and an immediate cesarean booking without further discussion does not reflect appropriate shared decision making.
  4. The correct answer is A (They influence decisions about planned place and mode of birth and any need for consultation): Fetal presentation and position near term meaningfully influence discussions about planned place of birth, anticipated mode of birth, and whether consultation with an obstetrician is warranted, particularly for a non cephalic presentation. These findings are directly relevant to birth planning. They provide no information about the specific timing of labour onset, and assessing presentation before labour begins allows proactive planning rather than waiting until labour is already underway.
  5. The correct answer is C (The widest part of the fetal head has passed into the pelvic inlet): Engagement refers to the point at which the biparietal diameter, the widest transverse dimension of the fetal head, has passed through the pelvic inlet, often assessed clinically as the station reaching around zero relative to the ischial spines. It does not mean the fetus has completed descent through the birth canal. Engagement can occur before labour begins, particularly in a first pregnancy, and it has no relationship to fetal heart rate patterns.
  6. The correct answer is A (Station describes the level of the presenting part relative to the ischial spines and helps track fetal descent): Fetal station describes the position of the leading bony point of the presenting part relative to the maternal ischial spines, expressed on a scale from minus three to plus three, and tracking it helps document fetal descent as pregnancy and labour progress. Station is distinct from cervical dilation, measured separately in centimetres. It has no relationship to amniotic fluid volume, and documenting it before labour begins remains a useful part of routine antepartum assessment.
Case C270

A 40-year-old G1P0 at 41 weeks gestation attends a routine visit. Her pregnancy has been low risk and uncomplicated throughout, with a confirmed dating ultrasound performed at 9 weeks gestation.

  1. The correct answer is C (The risks and benefits of expectant management versus induction of labour beyond 41 weeks): Pregnancy extending beyond 41 weeks carries a gradually increasing risk profile, so current practice supports a shared discussion of the risks and benefits of continued expectant management with increased surveillance versus induction of labour. Immediate transfer regardless of her wishes does not reflect informed, collaborative decision making. Postdates pregnancy is exactly the situation where increased fetal surveillance becomes more important, and there is no automatic indication for cesarean section based on gestational age alone.
  2. The correct answer is D (Increased fetal surveillance such as non stress testing and amniotic fluid assessment): When a client chooses expectant management beyond 41 weeks, increased antenatal surveillance, commonly including non stress testing and assessment of amniotic fluid volume, is recommended to monitor fetal wellbeing more closely. Standard visit frequency alone would not provide the added reassurance appropriate here. Fetal movement counting remains valuable and should continue, and reducing visit frequency runs counter to the recommended approach of closer monitoring in a postdates pregnancy.
  3. The correct answer is A (Advanced maternal age is associated with a modestly increased risk of stillbirth, which factors into the shared decision making): Advanced maternal age is an independent factor associated with a modestly increased risk of stillbirth, particularly as pregnancy extends past the due date, so it is reasonably incorporated into the shared discussion of expectant management versus induction at this gestational age. It is not accurate to say maternal age has no relevance to postdates risk. Maternal age is relevant beyond aneuploidy screening, and rather than eliminating the discussion, it is a factor that should be openly discussed.
  4. The correct answer is B (Membrane sweeping may help promote spontaneous labour onset and carries a low risk profile): Membrane sweeping performed during a vaginal examination can help promote the onset of spontaneous labour in some clients and is generally considered a low risk, low intervention option that can be offered as part of postdates management. It is not equivalent in effectiveness to formal medical or mechanical induction. It can be offered before 42 weeks as part of ongoing postdates discussions, and it does not require hospital admission or continuous fetal monitoring.
  5. The correct answer is B (A pregnancy that continues beyond 42 completed weeks of gestation): Postterm pregnancy is specifically defined as one continuing beyond 42 completed weeks of gestation, distinct from simply passing the estimated due date, which by itself is a common and expected occurrence for many pregnancies. Passing the due date by a day or even a week does not meet the clinical definition of postterm. The term describes gestational duration and is unrelated to findings at the first prenatal visit or to a diagnosis of gestational diabetes.
  6. The correct answer is A (She has not yet reached the gestational age where induction is routinely recommended for an otherwise low risk pregnancy): For an otherwise low risk pregnancy, routine discussion of induction is generally reserved for pregnancies extending closer to or beyond 41 weeks, so at 40 weeks and 3 days, continued expectant management with ongoing surveillance remains appropriate. Midwives can and do refer for or discuss induction when clinically indicated, so it is not accurate to say it is never offered. There is no standard recommendation to induce at exactly 40 weeks, and gestational age is in fact a central factor in this decision.
Case C276

A 26-year-old primigravida at 34 weeks gestation presents for a routine antenatal visit. Her blood pressure is 148/96 mmHg, repeated one hour later at 150/94 mmHg. She reports a mild frontal headache since yesterday but denies visual changes or epigastric pain. Urine dipstick shows 1+ protein, and fetal movements are normal.

  1. The correct answer is C (Complete blood count, liver enzymes, serum creatinine, and uric acid levels): these investigations screen for hemolysis, thrombocytopenia, hepatic involvement, and renal impairment associated with preeclampsia with severe features. Results guide the decision to consult obstetrics or transfer care. Blood glucose, GBS, and thyroid testing do not evaluate hypertensive disease of pregnancy and would delay appropriate assessment.
  2. The correct answer is C (Thrombocytopenia and elevated liver enzymes accompanying her non-severe range hypertension today): a blood pressure of 152/98 mmHg falls below the severe range, which requires a systolic reading of 160 mmHg or higher or a diastolic reading of 110 mmHg or higher. However, the accompanying thrombocytopenia and liver enzymes twice the upper limit of normal independently meet criteria for preeclampsia with severe features and require urgent obstetric consultation, regardless of the blood pressure or proteinuria level. Isolated trace proteinuria, a resolved headache, or normal fetal movements do not by themselves indicate severe disease. Severe features change management and mandate prompt transfer of care.
  3. The correct answer is A (Care must be transferred to obstetric care given the severe features): preeclampsia with severe features falls outside midwifery scope of practice and requires transfer to obstetric care for ongoing management, including consideration of timing of birth. Home birth is no longer appropriate once severe features are confirmed. Oral antihypertensives alone or simple co-attendance do not address the need for specialist obstetric management.
Case C279

A 33-year-old G2P1 at 28 weeks gestation completes a 75 gram oral glucose tolerance test after an abnormal 50 gram screen. Results show a fasting glucose of 5.6 mmol/L, one-hour value of 11.2 mmol/L, and two-hour value of 9.1 mmol/L. She has no prior history of diabetes.

  1. The correct answer is D (All three values exceed threshold, confirming a diagnosis of gestational diabetes): under the Canadian two-step diagnostic approach for the 75 gram oral glucose tolerance test, at least two abnormal values (fasting 5.3, one-hour 10.6, two-hour 9.0 mmol/L) are required to confirm gestational diabetes, and this client's fasting, one-hour, and two-hour values all exceed these thresholds. This confirms gestational diabetes rather than a normal or borderline result. No further testing is needed to establish the diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is A (Begin nutritional counselling and daily self-monitoring of blood glucose levels): initial management of gestational diabetes centres on dietary modification, physical activity, and self-monitoring of blood glucose, with medication reserved for cases where these measures do not achieve target glucose control. Insulin is not first-line therapy, an elective cesarean is not automatically indicated, and this diagnosis does not require discontinuing midwifery care.
  3. The correct answer is B (Consult for possible initiation of insulin or oral hypoglycemic therapy): persistent fasting hyperglycemia despite adherence to diet and exercise indicates that lifestyle measures alone are insufficient, and consultation regarding pharmacologic therapy is warranted. Reassurance, stopping monitoring, or increasing carbohydrate intake would worsen glucose control and are not appropriate. Timely escalation of care reduces the risk of macrosomia and related complications.
  4. The correct answer is C (Shoulder dystocia related to fetal macrosomia at the time of birth): estimated fetal weight at the 92nd percentile reflects macrosomia, a known complication of poorly controlled gestational diabetes that increases the risk of shoulder dystocia during birth. Intrauterine growth restriction and oligohydramnios are not consistent with this finding, and placenta previa is unrelated to glycemic control. Birth planning should anticipate this increased risk.
  5. The correct answer is B (Avoid any vaginal examination and arrange urgent transfer for ultrasound assessment): painless bright red bleeding in a client with a previous cesarean section raises strong suspicion for placenta previa, and a digital vaginal examination could provoke severe hemorrhage if previa is present. Ultrasound assessment of placental location is required before any further evaluation. Reassurance or delaying assessment could miss a serious complication.
  6. The correct answer is A (Care is transferred to obstetrics, with cesarean section planned near term): complete placenta previa fully covering the cervical os is an absolute contraindication to vaginal birth and falls outside midwifery scope of practice, requiring transfer to obstetric care with cesarean section planned near term. A trial of labour or continued home birth planning would put mother and baby at serious risk. Ongoing consultation is essential regardless of whether bleeding has stopped.
Case C285

A 35-year-old G4P2 at 36 weeks gestation calls her midwife reporting sudden severe abdominal pain and dark red vaginal bleeding that began twenty minutes ago. On arrival, her uterus is tense and tender between contractions, and she appears pale and anxious.

  1. The correct answer is C (Placental abruption, requiring immediate emergency transfer to hospital by ambulance): sudden abdominal pain, dark vaginal bleeding, and a tense, tender uterus are classic features of placental abruption, an obstetric emergency requiring immediate emergency transfer. This presentation is not consistent with normal labour, a urinary tract infection, or round ligament pain. Prompt recognition and transfer are essential to protect both mother and fetus.
  2. The correct answer is B (Significant placental separation causing fetal hypoxia and reduced oxygen delivery): persistent fetal bradycardia in the setting of suspected abruption indicates that placental separation is compromising oxygen and nutrient delivery to the fetus. This is not a normal variation, a sleep cycle, or simply maternal anxiety, and represents a true fetal emergency. Urgent transfer and continuous monitoring are required.
  3. The correct answer is C (Disseminated intravascular coagulation, which can develop rapidly following major abruption): severe placental abruption can trigger consumption of clotting factors and precipitate disseminated intravascular coagulation, a life-threatening complication requiring close monitoring of coagulation status during transfer. Gestational diabetes, chronic hypertension, and hyperemesis gravidarum are unrelated to acute abruption. Early recognition allows timely management of bleeding risk.
Case C288

A 24-year-old primigravida at 30 weeks gestation presents with regular painful contractions every five minutes for the past two hours. Vaginal examination reveals a cervix that is 3 cm dilated and 70% effaced. Membranes are intact and fetal heart rate is reassuring.

  1. The correct answer is B (Preterm labour, requiring urgent consultation and transfer to hospital care): regular painful contractions with progressive cervical dilation and effacement at 30 weeks gestation confirm preterm labour, which requires urgent consultation and transfer for further management including fetal surveillance and consideration of tocolysis and steroids. Braxton Hicks contractions, round ligament pain, and term labour preparations do not apply at this gestation.
  2. The correct answer is D (They accelerate fetal lung maturity and reduce risk of newborn complications): antenatal corticosteroids are administered in anticipated preterm birth to accelerate fetal lung maturation, reducing the risk of respiratory distress syndrome and other complications of prematurity. They do not stop contractions, treat infection, or lower maternal blood pressure. Their benefit is greatest when given before an anticipated preterm birth within the coming days.
  3. The correct answer is B (Attendance of a neonatal resuscitation team skilled in preterm newborn care): birth at 30 weeks and 2 days gestation carries a significant risk of respiratory and other complications of prematurity, so a neonatal resuscitation team skilled in preterm care should be arranged in advance. A routine six week postpartum visit, early discharge, or delayed cord clamping alone do not address the immediate needs of a preterm newborn.
  4. The correct answer is A (Umbilical artery Doppler studies to assess placental blood flow function): symphysis-fundal height lag over consecutive visits raises suspicion for fetal growth restriction, and umbilical artery Doppler studies assess placental function and are a key part of further ultrasound evaluation. A nuchal translucency measurement is only relevant earlier in pregnancy, and a biophysical profile or glucose test would not appropriately follow at this stage.
  5. The correct answer is A (Urgent consultation with obstetrics for further fetal surveillance and delivery planning): an estimated fetal weight below the 5th percentile combined with abnormal umbilical artery Doppler findings indicates significant fetal growth restriction with placental compromise, requiring urgent obstetric consultation for surveillance and delivery planning. Reassurance, a routine follow up in six weeks, or immediate induction without assessment would not appropriately address this finding.
Case C293

A 37-year-old G1P0 at 36 weeks gestation calls her midwife reporting that she has noticed significantly fewer fetal movements over the past 24 hours compared to her usual pattern. She has no vaginal bleeding, contractions, or headache.

  1. The correct answer is C (Come in promptly for a non-stress test to assess fetal heart rate): a maternal report of significantly reduced fetal movement warrants prompt clinical assessment, typically beginning with a non-stress test to evaluate fetal heart rate patterns and wellbeing. Waiting a week, using caffeine to stimulate movement, or simple reassurance without assessment could delay recognition of a compromised fetus.
  2. The correct answer is D (A biophysical profile or contraction stress test for further evaluation): a non-reactive non-stress test requires further evaluation of fetal wellbeing, typically with a biophysical profile or contraction stress test, rather than discharge or a delayed repeat test. Proceeding directly to cesarean section without further assessment is not appropriate unless additional findings support it. Further testing helps determine whether delivery is indicated.
  3. The correct answer is C (Urgent transfer to hospital for further assessment and likely delivery planning): a biophysical profile score of 4 out of 10 with reduced amniotic fluid indicates significant fetal compromise, requiring urgent transfer to hospital for further assessment and likely delivery. Routine follow up, continued expectant home management, or a dietitian referral would not adequately address this concerning result.
  4. The correct answer is A (Routinely at 28 weeks gestation, and again after birth if indicated): standard Canadian practice recommends antenatal Rh immune globulin at 28 weeks gestation for unsensitized Rh-negative women, with a further postpartum dose if the baby is confirmed Rh-positive. Waiting until labour, giving it only at the first visit, or reserving it only for invasive testing would leave the client unprotected against sensitization during the pregnancy.
  5. The correct answer is D (Administer Rh immune globulin within 72 hours of the birth): postpartum Rh immune globulin should be administered within 72 hours of birth to an unsensitized Rh-negative woman whose infant is confirmed Rh-positive, to prevent sensitization for future pregnancies. Withholding it, delaying it to the six week visit, or reserving it only for a planned second pregnancy would increase the risk of alloimmunization.
  6. The correct answer is A (Protect her from injury and position her on her left side): during an eclamptic seizure, the priority is protecting the client from injury and positioning her on her left side to maintain airway patency and optimize uteroplacental perfusion. Restraining her limbs or inserting objects into her mouth can cause injury, and leaving her unattended is unsafe during an active seizure.
  7. The correct answer is D (HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of preeclampsia requiring urgent transfer): right upper quadrant pain with hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and thrombocytopenia are the defining features of HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of preeclampsia requiring urgent transfer for obstetric management. This presentation is not explained by appendicitis, normal third trimester discomfort, or gestational diabetes complications.
  8. The correct answer is C (Hypertension documented and present before 20 weeks of this pregnancy): chronic hypertension is defined by blood pressure elevation present before pregnancy or diagnosed before 20 weeks gestation, distinguishing it from gestational hypertension, which develops after 20 weeks. Resolution after birth, proteinuria, or family history do not distinguish between these two diagnoses at this stage. Early identification guides ongoing surveillance.
  9. The correct answer is B (Superimposed preeclampsia, requiring prompt obstetric consultation and closer monitoring): new proteinuria and worsening blood pressure control in a client with pre-existing chronic hypertension indicate superimposed preeclampsia, which requires prompt obstetric consultation and increased surveillance. This is not simply expected progression of chronic hypertension, gestational diabetes, or a urinary tract infection, and requires a change in management.
  10. The correct answer is D (Monochorionic twins share a placenta and risk twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome): monochorionic diamniotic twins share a single placenta and are at risk for twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome, which requires more frequent ultrasound surveillance to detect early. This increased monitoring is not simply to confirm sex or to plan an automatic cesarean section, and twin pregnancies do require additional ultrasound monitoring beyond routine dating scans.
  11. The correct answer is B (Consult regarding initiation of insulin or another glucose lowering medication): persistently elevated postprandial glucose despite adherence to diet indicates that lifestyle measures alone are not achieving target control, warranting consultation regarding pharmacologic therapy. Skipping meals, dismissing the readings, or prescribing bed rest would not appropriately address ongoing hyperglycemia and could worsen maternal and fetal outcomes.
  12. The correct answer is B (Placental insufficiency limiting the transfer of oxygen and nutrients to the fetus): placental insufficiency is the most common identifiable cause of intrauterine growth restriction in clinical practice, limiting the transfer of oxygen and nutrients needed for fetal growth. Excessive maternal weight gain, gestational diabetes, and postterm pregnancy are more commonly associated with fetal overgrowth rather than growth restriction.
  13. The correct answer is B (Sudden abdominal pain, a tense uterus, and dark colored vaginal blood): sudden abdominal pain, uterine tenderness or tension, and dark colored vaginal blood are classic features distinguishing placental abruption from placenta previa, which typically presents with painless bright red bleeding and a soft uterus. Bleeding only after intercourse or brief painless spotting are not typical of either serious cause of antepartum hemorrhage.
  14. The correct answer is A (A negative result helps predict a low likelihood of preterm birth soon): fetal fibronectin testing has strong negative predictive value, meaning a negative result helps reassure that preterm birth is unlikely within the next one to two weeks. A positive result does not confirm active labour, and the test does not replace cervical length assessment or assess fetal lung maturity.
  15. The correct answer is B (Become familiar with her baby's usual pattern and report any change): current guidance encourages clients to become familiar with their own baby's usual pattern of movement and to report any noticeable decrease or change promptly, rather than relying on a fixed counting method every hour. Movement does not necessarily increase steadily each week, and fetal movement remains a meaningful indicator of wellbeing throughout pregnancy.
  16. The correct answer is C (Administer Rh immune globulin promptly given the risk of fetomaternal hemorrhage): external cephalic version carries a risk of fetomaternal hemorrhage even when the procedure is successful and uncomplicated, so Rh immune globulin should be administered promptly to an unsensitized Rh-negative client following the procedure. Waiting for visible bleeding or delaying until after birth would not adequately protect against sensitization.
Case C309

A 34-year-old primigravida at 36 weeks gestation attends an urgent midwifery assessment reporting a severe frontal headache and epigastric pain. Her blood pressure is 168/112 mmHg on repeat measurement, and urinalysis shows 3+ proteinuria. She has brisk deep tendon reflexes with two beats of clonus.

  1. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent transfer to hospital for obstetric assessment and possible magnesium sulfate): a severe headache, epigastric pain, blood pressure of 168/112 mmHg, heavy proteinuria, and clonus together indicate severe preeclampsia with imminent risk of eclampsia. This falls outside the midwife's scope for independent management and requires urgent obstetric consultation and transfer. Delaying care with home rest or oral medication alone would place both mother and fetus at serious risk. Timely transfer allows blood pressure control and consideration of magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis.
  2. The correct answer is B (To prevent progression to eclamptic seizures given her severe features): magnesium sulfate is used in severe preeclampsia specifically for seizure prophylaxis, not as a primary antihypertensive or tocolytic. It reduces central nervous system excitability, lowering the risk of the eclamptic convulsions that severe preeclampsia can progress to. Blood pressure is managed separately with antihypertensive agents such as labetalol or nifedipine. Fetal lung maturity, if needed, is addressed with corticosteroids rather than magnesium sulfate.
  3. The correct answer is C (Hyperreflexia with sustained clonus indicating central nervous system irritability): clonus reflects heightened neuromuscular excitability and is a recognized clinical marker of impending eclampsia in severe preeclampsia. Proteinuria and blood pressure values help establish the diagnosis and severity but do not directly predict seizure risk in the same way. Gravidity is a background risk factor for developing preeclampsia rather than a marker of imminent seizure. Neurological signs like clonus and severe headache are the features that most directly signal cerebral irritability.
Case C312

A 31-year-old gravida 2 para 1 at 26 weeks gestation completes a 75 g oral glucose tolerance test after an abnormal screening result. Her fasting glucose is 5.4 mmol/L, one-hour value is 11.2 mmol/L, and two-hour value is 9.1 mmol/L. She has no prior history of diabetes.

  1. The correct answer is A (She meets diagnostic criteria for gestational diabetes on two values): her one-hour and two-hour results both clearly exceed threshold, and her fasting value of 5.4 mmol/L also exceeds the fasting cutoff under both the Canadian two-step criteria (5.3 mmol/L) and the IADPSG one-step criteria (5.1 mmol/L), meaning all three of her values are actually abnormal. This confirms gestational diabetes rather than a borderline or inconclusive result. Describing only the fasting value as abnormal would understate the extent of her glucose intolerance. A repeat test is not indicated once diagnostic criteria have already been met.
  2. The correct answer is C (Referral for nutritional counselling and self-monitoring of blood glucose levels): dietary modification and self-monitoring are the recommended first-line management strategy for newly diagnosed gestational diabetes before pharmacologic treatment is considered. Insulin is reserved for cases where glucose targets are not achieved through lifestyle measures. Gestational diabetes carries real risks to both mother and baby, so reassurance without a management plan would be inappropriate. A full transfer of primary care is not required for uncomplicated, well-controlled gestational diabetes.
  3. The correct answer is C (Consult with an obstetrician or endocrinologist regarding pharmacologic treatment): when dietary management fails to achieve target glucose levels, consultation regarding pharmacologic therapy such as insulin or metformin is the appropriate next step. Continuing midwifery-led care alone without escalation would leave hyperglycemia unmanaged. Stopping glucose monitoring at this point would remove the information needed to guide further treatment. Poor glucose control increases risks such as macrosomia and stillbirth, so escalation of care is required.
  4. The correct answer is D (Neonatal hypoglycemia in the hours immediately following birth): infants exposed to maternal hyperglycemia produce excess insulin in utero, and once the maternal glucose supply is removed at birth, this can cause rebound hypoglycemia. This is a well-established and commonly monitored complication in babies of mothers with gestational diabetes. Neonatal hyperthyroidism and congenital cytomegalovirus are unrelated to maternal glucose control. Polycythemia can occur with poorly controlled diabetes but is not caused by maternal iron deficiency.
  5. The correct answer is B (Direct her to hospital urgently for assessment without a vaginal exam): painless bright red bleeding in the third trimester with a known low-lying placenta raises strong concern for placenta previa, which requires urgent obstetric assessment. A vaginal examination must be avoided until previa has been excluded, since it can trigger severe hemorrhage. Reassurance and delayed follow-up would not address the potential seriousness of ongoing bleeding. Urgent transfer for imaging and obstetric review is the safest course of action.
  6. The correct answer is A (It could provoke severe hemorrhage if placenta previa is confirmed): inserting fingers or instruments into the cervix when the placenta covers or lies near the internal os can disrupt placental vessels and precipitate massive bleeding. Infection risk and client comfort are not the primary reasons this examination is avoided in this context. Equipment availability is not the limiting factor, since the concern is the physical risk of the examination itself. This is why any suspected previa is assessed by ultrasound rather than digital examination.
Case C318

A 27-year-old primigravida at 30 weeks gestation reports regular tightening every eight minutes over the past two hours, low back pain, and increased vaginal discharge. On assessment, the midwife finds the cervix is 2 cm dilated and 60% effaced.

  1. The correct answer is D (Consult obstetrics urgently for assessment of threatened preterm labour): regular contractions with cervical change at 30 weeks gestation meets criteria for threatened preterm labour, which is outside independent midwifery management and requires prompt obstetric consultation. Reassurance as normal Braxton Hicks contractions would be inappropriate given the documented cervical change. Home measures like fluids and a bath do not address a true threatened preterm labour presentation. Timely consultation allows for interventions such as corticosteroids and further monitoring.
  2. The correct answer is C (To accelerate fetal lung maturity and reduce respiratory complications): antenatal corticosteroids are given between viable gestation and around 34 to 36 weeks to promote surfactant production and reduce neonatal respiratory distress syndrome if preterm birth occurs. They do not act as tocolytics to stop contractions directly. They are not indicated for treating maternal infection. Corticosteroids also do not lower maternal blood pressure, which is managed with different medications when needed.
  3. The correct answer is D (Progressive cervical dilation and effacement on serial examination): documented cervical change over time is the key clinical feature distinguishing true labour from false labour or isolated contractions. A single episode of mild tightening without cervical change is more consistent with false labour. Subjective changes in fetal movement intensity are not diagnostic of labour status. A reassuring fetal heart rate pattern reflects fetal wellbeing but does not confirm or exclude true labour.
Case C321

A 33-year-old gravida 1 is confirmed at her 12-week dating ultrasound to be carrying a diamniotic dichorionic twin pregnancy conceived spontaneously. She and her partner had been planning a home birth with their midwife and ask whether this is still possible.

  1. The correct answer is B (Hospital birth with obstetric consultation is recommended for twin gestation): twin pregnancies carry higher risks of complications such as preterm birth, malpresentation, and postpartum hemorrhage, so current guidelines recommend hospital birth with obstetric involvement rather than a planned home birth. This does not mean the midwife cannot remain involved in her care, but the birthplace plan should shift to a hospital setting. Restricting care exclusively to a family physician is not accurate, since midwives commonly continue collaborative care. A birth centre alone would not provide the obstetric resources this pregnancy may require.
  2. The correct answer is D (Dichorionic twins carry lower risk than monochorionic twins for complications): chorionicity determines whether twins share a placenta, and monochorionic twins face additional risks such as twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome that dichorionic twins do not. This distinction directly informs the intensity of antenatal surveillance required. Chorionicity does not improve dating accuracy, which is based on crown-rump length measurement. It also does not predict fetal sex or indicate how the pregnancy was conceived.
  3. The correct answer is C (Serial growth ultrasounds are recommended more frequently than in singletons): twin pregnancies are at higher risk of growth discordance and growth restriction, so more frequent ultrasound assessment of fetal growth is recommended compared to singleton pregnancies. Relying on fundal height alone is less reliable in twin pregnancies because uterine size is affected by two fetuses. A single third-trimester scan would not provide adequate ongoing surveillance. Omitting additional growth surveillance would miss the increased risk profile of a twin pregnancy.
  4. The correct answer is A (Preterm labour and birth before thirty-seven weeks gestation): twin pregnancies have a significantly higher rate of spontaneous preterm labour and birth compared to singleton pregnancies, largely due to uterine overdistension. Post-term pregnancy is actually less common with twins, since birth typically occurs earlier. A prolonged latent phase is not a recognized twin-specific complication. Gestational hypertension is actually more, not less, common in twin pregnancies.
  5. The correct answer is B (It requires a shared placenta, which dichorionic twins do not have): twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome results from abnormal vascular connections within a single shared placenta, a feature unique to monochorionic twin pregnancies. Since this client's twins are dichorionic with two separate placentas, this specific condition cannot occur. The syndrome is not linked to a particular gestational age threshold, maternal diabetes, or method of conception. Explaining this distinction can help reassure the client while clarifying that other twin-specific risks still apply.
Case C326

A 25-year-old primigravida is confirmed Rh negative at her booking bloodwork, while the father of the baby is Rh positive. She is currently 28 weeks gestation and has had no bleeding, trauma, or invasive procedures during this pregnancy.

  1. The correct answer is B (Administer Rh immune globulin now as routine antenatal prophylaxis): standard Canadian practice offers Rh immune globulin at around 28 weeks gestation to Rh-negative clients with an Rh-positive or unknown partner, even without a specific sensitizing event, to prevent alloimmunization. Waiting for antibody screen results is not necessary before giving routine prophylaxis at this timepoint. A blood transfusion is not indicated in this scenario. Reassurance that Rh status is irrelevant would overlook the ongoing risk of silent fetomaternal hemorrhage throughout pregnancy.
  2. The correct answer is C (To prevent maternal sensitization to fetal Rh-positive red blood cells): Rh immune globulin works by neutralizing fetal Rh-positive cells that may enter maternal circulation, preventing the mother's immune system from forming anti-D antibodies. It has no role in treating infection, reducing diabetes risk, or managing blood pressure. Preventing sensitization protects future pregnancies from hemolytic disease of the newborn. This is why prophylaxis is offered proactively rather than only after a confirmed sensitizing event.
  3. The correct answer is B (She is already sensitized, and Rh immune globulin will no longer help): a positive antibody screen for anti-D means maternal sensitization has already occurred, and Rh immune globulin cannot reverse this or prevent further antibody production. Instead, this pregnancy requires close monitoring for signs of fetal hemolytic disease. A higher dose of immune globulin would not be effective once sensitization has occurred. The result does not confirm the fetus's own Rh status and does not eliminate the need for ongoing surveillance.
  4. The correct answer is D (Arrange prompt clinical assessment including fetal heart rate monitoring): a maternal report of decreased fetal movement warrants timely clinical evaluation, typically including fetal heart rate monitoring and further assessment as indicated, since it can be an early sign of fetal compromise. Waiting 48 hours would delay identification of a potentially serious problem. While movement patterns can vary, dismissing the concern without assessment is not appropriate. Immediate cesarean section is not warranted without first confirming fetal status through assessment.
  5. The correct answer is D (Refer for an ultrasound to assess fetal growth and wellbeing): a symphysis-fundal height measurement lagging significantly behind gestational age raises concern for fetal growth restriction and warrants further evaluation with ultrasound. Repeating the measurement in four weeks without further investigation would delay identifying a growth problem. Fundal height, while an imperfect screening tool, is not entirely unreliable and a significant discrepancy should prompt follow-up. Immediate induction is not appropriate without first confirming the growth concern and assessing fetal wellbeing.
  6. The correct answer is D (Chronic hypertension present before the current pregnancy began): pre-existing hypertension impairs placental blood flow and is a well-established risk factor for fetal growth restriction. A normal body mass index and typical maternal age in their late twenties are not risk factors for growth restriction. A single prior uncomplicated vaginal birth does not increase this risk. Recognizing risk factors like chronic hypertension helps guide appropriate antenatal surveillance.
  7. The correct answer is A (Gestational hypertension requiring increased surveillance without immediate transfer): new-onset hypertension after 20 weeks without proteinuria or other severe features is classified as gestational hypertension, which requires closer monitoring but not automatic hospital transfer. Severe preeclampsia requires proteinuria or other severe features that are absent here. Chronic hypertension would predate the pregnancy, which is not described in this scenario. Eclampsia refers specifically to seizures and is not applicable to this presentation.
  8. The correct answer is D (HELLP syndrome, a severe variant requiring urgent obstetric management): the combination of hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets alongside preeclampsia defines HELLP syndrome, a serious complication requiring urgent obstetric care. Gestational thrombocytopenia is a mild, isolated finding and would not explain the full laboratory picture described. Acute fatty liver of pregnancy presents differently and is a distinct condition. Disseminated intravascular coagulation can complicate severe HELLP but is not the primary diagnosis being described here.
  9. The correct answer is B (Placental abruption requiring urgent obstetric transfer and assessment): sudden severe pain, a tense and tender uterus, and dark vaginal bleeding are classic features of placental abruption, a significant obstetric emergency. Placenta previa typically presents with painless bright red bleeding rather than pain and uterine tenderness. Braxton Hicks contractions do not cause a tense, tender uterus or significant bleeding. Preterm labour alone would not typically produce this degree of pain and dark bleeding together.
  10. The correct answer is C (Consult obstetrics regarding admission, antibiotics, and corticosteroid administration): preterm premature rupture of membranes at 33 weeks requires obstetric consultation for management including antibiotics to reduce infection risk and corticosteroids for fetal lung maturity. Discharging her home would overlook the risks of infection and preterm birth associated with ruptured membranes. A digital examination should be avoided due to increased infection risk once membranes have ruptured. This presentation is not normal discharge and requires further evaluation and management.
  11. The correct answer is C (An additional dose of Rh immune globulin following this sensitizing event): a potentially sensitizing event such as bleeding after trauma requires its own dose of Rh immune globulin, separate from the routine antenatal dose given later in pregnancy. Omitting the additional dose would leave a window of risk for sensitization from this specific event. A blood transfusion is not indicated based on this scenario alone. Amniocentesis is an invasive procedure and is not the appropriate response to a sensitizing event.
  12. The correct answer is C (Between twenty-four and twenty-eight weeks gestation for most clients): universal screening for gestational diabetes in average-risk clients is typically performed between 24 and 28 weeks gestation, when insulin resistance from placental hormones is most likely to reveal glucose intolerance. Screening at the first booking visit is reserved for clients with specific risk factors for pre-existing diabetes. Waiting until the third trimester after 36 weeks would delay diagnosis and management. Screening should be offered universally rather than only when a family history is reported.
  13. The correct answer is A (A previous spontaneous preterm birth in an earlier pregnancy): a history of prior spontaneous preterm birth is one of the strongest known predictors of preterm labour in a subsequent pregnancy. A previous uncomplicated term birth is actually reassuring rather than a risk factor. Maternal age in the early thirties alone is not a significant risk factor for preterm labour. Conceiving without assisted reproductive technology does not increase preterm labour risk on its own.
  14. The correct answer is B (Consult obstetrics for increased fetal surveillance and delivery planning): the combination of oligohydramnios and fetal growth below the tenth percentile suggests placental insufficiency and requires obstetric consultation for further surveillance and timing of delivery. Continuing routine care with a delayed follow-up would not adequately address this significant finding. Reduced fluid at 34 weeks is not an expected normal finding and should not be dismissed. Increasing oral fluids alone is not an evidence-based treatment for this combination of findings.
  15. The correct answer is B (Angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors, due to fetal renal toxicity risk): ACE inhibitors are contraindicated in pregnancy because they are associated with fetal renal damage, oligohydramnios, and other serious fetal complications, particularly with second and third trimester exposure. Labetalol, nifedipine, and methyldopa are all considered acceptable first-line antihypertensive options during pregnancy. Recognizing which medications must be changed at the start of pregnancy is an important safety consideration. This client's medication regimen would need to be reviewed and switched promptly.
Case C341

A 32-year-old G1P0 client at 34 weeks gestation presents to her midwife with a blood pressure of 152/98 mmHg on two occasions four hours apart. She reports a mild frontal headache and states her rings feel tight. Urinalysis shows 2+ protein on dipstick, and she denies visual disturbances or epigastric pain.

  1. The correct answer is A (Consult with an obstetrician promptly given new-onset hypertension with significant proteinuria): New-onset hypertension after 20 weeks combined with 2+ proteinuria meets criteria for preeclampsia, a condition outside the midwife's independent scope. Prompt obstetric consultation ensures appropriate maternal and fetal surveillance and timely escalation of care if severity worsens. Delaying assessment risks progression to severe features or eclampsia.
  2. The correct answer is D (Severe persistent headache unrelieved by acetaminophen with visual scotomata): Severe headache unresponsive to analgesia combined with visual scotomata are classic prodromal symptoms of impending eclampsia, reflecting cerebral edema and vasospasm. This constellation requires urgent transfer for assessment and consideration of magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis. Mild edema, position-related discomfort, and increased frequency are common minor pregnancy symptoms and do not indicate this severity.
  3. The correct answer is B (Platelet count of 90 x10^9/L with elevated liver transaminases): HELLP syndrome is defined by hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets, so a platelet count of 90 with elevated transaminases fits this pattern directly. This combination signals a severe variant of preeclampsia requiring urgent obstetric management. The other listed values are within normal limits and would not suggest HELLP.
  4. The correct answer is B (Consult with an obstetrician or endocrinologist regarding initiating pharmacologic therapy): Persistent fasting hyperglycemia despite adherent dietary management indicates that lifestyle measures alone are insufficient, and pharmacologic therapy such as insulin or metformin is often required. Consultation ensures the client receives appropriate specialist input for glycemic control outside independent midwifery management. Continuing without escalation risks fetal macrosomia and other complications of poorly controlled glucose.
  5. The correct answer is C (Increased monitoring for fetal macrosomia through serial growth ultrasounds): Poorly controlled gestational diabetes increases the risk of fetal macrosomia due to fetal hyperinsulinemia, making serial growth ultrasounds important for surveillance and birth planning. This monitoring helps identify complications such as shoulder dystocia risk before birth. Karyotype testing and cervical length are not specifically indicated by gestational diabetes.
  6. The correct answer is C (Placental hormones increase insulin resistance beyond what maternal pancreas can compensate for): Gestational diabetes arises when placental hormones such as human placental lactogen increase maternal insulin resistance, and the pancreas cannot sufficiently compensate to maintain normal glucose levels. This is a physiological response to pregnancy rather than permanent beta cell destruction. Fetal insulin does not cross the placenta to affect maternal glucose directly.
  7. The correct answer is D (New-onset proteinuria with a sudden rise in blood pressure from baseline): Superimposed preeclampsia in a client with chronic hypertension is suggested by new proteinuria or a sudden worsening of blood pressure control beyond her established baseline. Stable readings, mild edema, and expected weight gain are consistent with ongoing chronic hypertension rather than a new complication. This distinction is important for timely obstetric consultation.
Case C348

A 31-year-old G3P2 client at 33 weeks gestation calls her midwife reporting sudden, painless, bright red vaginal bleeding that soaked one pad. She has no contractions and reports normal fetal movement. Her 20-week anatomy scan noted a low-lying placenta. She is hemodynamically stable with a blood pressure of 118/74 mmHg.

  1. The correct answer is D (Placenta previa causing painless bleeding due to a low-lying placenta): Painless, bright red bleeding in a client with a known low-lying placenta is the classic presentation of placenta previa, where the placenta overlies or is near the cervical os. Placental abruption typically presents with pain and uterine tenderness, which are absent here. The known ultrasound finding strongly supports this diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent transfer to hospital for obstetric assessment and ultrasound confirmation): Any antepartum bleeding in a client with a known low-lying placenta warrants urgent hospital assessment to confirm the diagnosis and monitor maternal and fetal status. A home vaginal exam is contraindicated and could provoke severe hemorrhage. Delaying assessment could allow a significant bleed to go unrecognized.
  3. The correct answer is C (Digital vaginal examination, which risks provoking severe hemorrhage from the placenta): Digital vaginal examination in confirmed placenta previa can disrupt the placental edge and provoke life-threatening hemorrhage, so it is strictly avoided until the placental location is clarified or delivery occurs. Abdominal palpation, external monitoring, and blood pressure checks are safe non-invasive assessments. This restriction is a key safety principle in managing suspected previa.
  4. The correct answer is D (Cesarean birth, since the placenta completely covers the cervical os): Complete placenta previa, where the placenta fully covers the internal cervical os, is an absolute indication for cesarean birth because vaginal birth would cause catastrophic hemorrhage. This is not amenable to a vaginal birth trial or home birth under any circumstance. Cesarean is planned electively before labour onset when possible.
  5. The correct answer is C (Placental abruption, given the pain, uterine tenderness, and hemodynamic instability): The combination of painful bleeding, a firm tender uterus, reduced fetal movement, and hypotension with tachycardia is classic for placental abruption, where premature placental separation causes concealed or revealed hemorrhage. Placenta previa and cervical polyps typically present without this degree of pain and instability. This presentation requires emergency obstetric management.
Case C353

A 26-year-old G1P0 client at 31 weeks gestation reports regular tightening every six minutes for the past two hours, associated with low back pain and pelvic pressure. On examination, her cervix is 2 cm dilated and 60% effaced. Fetal heart rate is reassuring and she has no vaginal bleeding.

  1. The correct answer is C (Progressive cervical dilation and effacement accompanying regular painful contractions): True preterm labour is distinguished from Braxton Hicks contractions by objective cervical change occurring alongside regular, painful uterine activity. Braxton Hicks contractions are typically irregular and do not cause cervical dilation or effacement. This client's documented dilation and effacement confirm true labour.
  2. The correct answer is A (Antenatal corticosteroids to accelerate fetal lung maturity before likely preterm birth): Antenatal corticosteroids significantly reduce neonatal respiratory distress syndrome and other complications when given before anticipated preterm birth, making them a priority intervention at 31 weeks. Induction is not appropriate management for preterm labour itself. Iron supplementation does not address the acute risk of preterm birth.
  3. The correct answer is B (It delays birth briefly to allow corticosteroids and transfer time to occur): Tocolytic medication is used to temporarily suppress contractions for a short window, providing time for corticosteroids to take effect and for safe transfer to an appropriate level of neonatal care. It is not intended or expected to halt labour permanently or replace corticosteroid administration. This distinction helps set realistic expectations for the client.
  4. The correct answer is A (A previous spontaneous preterm birth at 33 weeks in an earlier pregnancy): A prior spontaneous preterm birth is one of the strongest known risk factors for recurrent preterm labour in a subsequent pregnancy. Normal body mass index, typical maternal age, and a singleton conception without assisted reproduction are not risk factors on their own. This history should prompt closer surveillance for signs of preterm labour.
Case C357

A 33-year-old G2P1 client is confirmed at her 12-week dating ultrasound to have a monochorionic diamniotic twin pregnancy. She and her partner are excited but have questions about how this will change her care compared to her previous singleton pregnancy.

  1. The correct answer is C (Twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome due to shared placental vascular connections): Monochorionic twins share a single placenta with vascular connections that can lead to twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome, a risk not present in dichorionic twins with separate placentas. This complication requires specific ultrasound surveillance for early detection. The other listed conditions are not specifically tied to chorionicity in this way.
  2. The correct answer is D (More frequent ultrasound surveillance to monitor growth discordance and fluid volumes): Monochorionic twin pregnancies require more frequent ultrasound surveillance than singleton or dichorionic pregnancies to detect early signs of twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome, such as growth discordance or amniotic fluid discrepancies. A single anatomy scan is insufficient given this elevated risk. Routine blood pressure and iron care should continue as usual.
  3. The correct answer is C (Consultation with an obstetrician, since monochorionic twins fall outside midwifery-led home birth): Monochorionic diamniotic twin pregnancies carry risks that place them outside the scope of independent midwifery-led care and home birth, requiring obstetric consultation for shared or transferred care. Fetal presentation alone does not change this underlying chorionicity-related risk. This recommendation reflects standard provincial consultation guidelines.
  4. The correct answer is A (Developing twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome requiring urgent specialist referral): Significant discordance in estimated fetal weight combined with abnormal amniotic fluid volumes between monochorionic twins is a hallmark presentation of twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome. This finding requires urgent referral to a specialist centre for further assessment and possible intervention. It is not a normal variant or expected finding in twin pregnancies generally.
  5. The correct answer is B (Twin pregnancies have a substantially higher risk of spontaneous preterm birth): Twin pregnancies, and particularly monochorionic twins, carry a significantly elevated risk of spontaneous preterm birth compared to singleton pregnancies due to uterine overdistension and other factors. This risk should be discussed proactively so the client understands warning signs to watch for. It is not equivalent to or lower than singleton risk.
  6. The correct answer is B (Dichorionic twins have separate placentas and generally carry lower shared-circulation risk): Dichorionic twins each have their own placenta, which avoids the shared vascular connections responsible for twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome seen in monochorionic twins. This generally results in a lower risk profile related to shared circulation, although twin pregnancy still carries elevated risks overall. Chorionicity meaningfully affects surveillance needs and risk counselling.
  7. The correct answer is D (Shared care with an obstetrician is recommended given the higher-order multiple gestation): Higher-order multiple pregnancies such as triplets carry substantially increased risks and require shared care with an obstetrician rather than midwifery care alone. This ensures appropriate specialized surveillance for complications common to higher-order multiples. Complete transfer out of midwifery involvement is not necessarily required if shared care is arranged.
  8. The correct answer is A (Consultation with an obstetrician for ongoing surveillance of suspected fetal growth restriction): An estimated fetal weight below the 5th percentile meets criteria for suspected fetal growth restriction, warranting obstetric consultation for further surveillance even when initial Doppler and fluid findings are reassuring. Reassurance alone would risk missing evolving placental insufficiency. Ongoing monitoring, not simple routine follow-up, is required.
  9. The correct answer is B (Significantly worsening placental insufficiency requiring urgent obstetric reassessment and possible birth): Absent end-diastolic flow in the umbilical artery indicates significantly worsening placental insufficiency and represents a serious deterioration in fetal status. This finding requires urgent obstetric reassessment, as it may necessitate early birth to prevent further compromise. It is not a reassuring or benign finding in growth-restricted pregnancies.
  10. The correct answer is D (Growth restriction implies a pathological process limiting growth potential, unlike constitutional smallness): Intrauterine growth restriction reflects a pathological process, often placental insufficiency, that prevents a fetus from reaching its genetically determined growth potential, whereas small for gestational age may simply reflect constitutional smallness with no underlying pathology. Not every small for gestational age fetus has placental insufficiency. This distinction guides how closely a pregnancy needs to be monitored.
Case C367

A 30-year-old G2P1 client at 36 weeks gestation calls stating she has noticed reduced fetal movement over the past 24 hours compared to her usual pattern. She has no bleeding, pain, or leaking of fluid, and her pregnancy has otherwise been uncomplicated to this point.

  1. The correct answer is A (Advise her to attend for prompt fetal heart rate assessment and monitoring): A report of reduced fetal movement in the third trimester warrants prompt clinical assessment, including fetal heart rate monitoring, to evaluate fetal wellbeing rather than waiting or relying on reassurance alone. Delaying assessment could allow evolving fetal compromise to go undetected. This is standard practice for any decreased movement complaint.
  2. The correct answer is C (Continue routine care but seek prompt reassessment if movement decreases again): A reassuring non-stress test and heart rate assessment support continuing routine antenatal care, but the client should be advised to seek prompt reassessment if she notices decreased movement again, since a single reassuring test does not guarantee ongoing wellbeing. Discontinuing all monitoring would be inappropriate. Induction is not indicated based on reassuring findings.
  3. The correct answer is D (An anterior placenta cushioning and reducing the sensation of fetal movements): An anterior placenta can physically cushion fetal movements against the uterine wall, reducing a client's subjective perception of movement even when the fetus is well. This is a recognized benign explanation that should still prompt assessment to confirm reassuring status. Fetal position, blood type, and early gestational age are not typically linked to this specific perception issue at 36 weeks.
  4. The correct answer is C (Arrange urgent obstetric ultrasound to confirm fetal cardiac activity or demise): Absent fetal heart tones on handheld Doppler after previously normal movement requires urgent ultrasound confirmation of fetal cardiac status, since a handheld Doppler alone is not definitive. Reassurance or delaying assessment would be inappropriate given the seriousness of this finding. This situation requires immediate escalation rather than routine follow-up.
Case C371

A 24-year-old G1P0 client is confirmed Rh negative at her booking bloodwork, and her partner's blood type is unknown. She has not previously received Rh immune globulin. She is currently at 28 weeks gestation and has had no bleeding, trauma, or invasive procedures this pregnancy.

  1. The correct answer is A (Offer routine antenatal Rh immune globulin now, since her partner's status is unknown): Routine antenatal Rh immune globulin at around 28 weeks is offered to Rh negative clients whose partner's Rh status is unknown, since fetal Rh status cannot otherwise be confirmed. Withholding it or waiting until birth increases the risk of unrecognized sensitization. This is standard preventive practice regardless of the absence of bleeding or trauma.
  2. The correct answer is D (An additional dose should be given promptly regardless of the earlier routine dose): Abdominal trauma is a sensitizing event that warrants an additional dose of Rh immune globulin regardless of whether a routine antenatal dose was already given earlier in pregnancy. Waiting for bleeding to develop or her next scheduled visit could delay necessary prophylaxis. Protection from a prior dose does not extend indefinitely to cover new sensitizing events.
  3. The correct answer is A (Administer Rh immune globulin within 72 hours of birth to prevent sensitization): When a newborn is confirmed Rh positive, the Rh negative mother should receive Rh immune globulin within 72 hours of birth to prevent sensitization from fetal blood exposure during delivery. The antenatal dose alone does not provide sufficient ongoing protection through the birth event. Timely postpartum administration is essential regardless of future pregnancy plans.
  4. The correct answer is A (It prevents maternal antibody formation that could harm this or future pregnancies): Rh immune globulin works by preventing the maternal immune system from forming antibodies against fetal Rh positive blood cells, protecting both the current and future pregnancies from hemolytic disease. It has no role in treating anemia or preventing preeclampsia. This preventive purpose applies even in the absence of overt complications.
  5. The correct answer is A (Consultation with an obstetrician for specialized monitoring of fetal hemolytic risk): Once a client is already sensitized with a positive antibody screen, Rh immune globulin can no longer prevent harm, and specialized obstetric monitoring for fetal hemolytic disease, including serial antibody titers and Doppler surveillance, becomes essential. Routine midwifery care alone is insufficient in this situation. This reflects the need for care outside independent midwifery scope.
  6. The correct answer is D (Rh isoimmunization risk applies to Rh negative clients, so it does not apply here): Rh isoimmunization is a concern for Rh negative individuals who may be exposed to Rh positive fetal blood, so a client who is herself Rh positive is not at risk of forming these antibodies regardless of her partner's status. Rh immune globulin is not indicated for her. This distinction is important for accurate client counselling.
  7. The correct answer is C (Gestational hypertension, given new-onset elevated blood pressure after 20 weeks without proteinuria): New-onset hypertension after 20 weeks gestation in a client with no prior history, in the absence of proteinuria or other severe features, meets the definition of gestational hypertension. Chronic hypertension would have predated this pregnancy, and preeclampsia requires proteinuria or other systemic features not present here. This distinction guides the appropriate level of surveillance.
  8. The correct answer is A (Increased surveillance with more frequent visits and obstetric consultation as indicated): Gestational hypertension requires increased surveillance, including more frequent blood pressure checks and proteinuria screening, along with obstetric consultation to monitor for progression to preeclampsia. Reassurance without monitoring risks missing worsening disease. Complete transfer away from midwifery is not automatically required if care remains well coordinated.
  9. The correct answer is D (Birth is generally recommended around 37 to 39 weeks depending on severity and trends): Current evidence supports planned birth around 37 to 39 weeks for gestational hypertension, balancing the risks of prolonged pregnancy against those of earlier birth, with timing individualized based on severity and blood pressure trends. Delaying well beyond 41 weeks or requiring immediate birth regardless of status are not standard approaches. This timing reflects evidence-based guidelines.
Case C380

A 34-year-old G4P3 client at 36 weeks gestation with a history of smoking and prior abruption presents with sudden onset of constant abdominal pain and dark vaginal bleeding. Her uterus is firm and tender on palpation, and the fetal heart rate shows late decelerations.

  1. The correct answer is A (Her history of smoking and a prior placental abruption in an earlier pregnancy): Smoking and a prior history of placental abruption are well-established risk factors that significantly increase the likelihood of recurrent abruption in a subsequent pregnancy. Her parity, gestational age, and age below advanced maternal age are not specific risk factors for this event. Recognizing these risk factors supports appropriate antenatal counselling and vigilance.
  2. The correct answer is D (Arrange emergency transfer to hospital for urgent obstetric assessment and delivery): Late decelerations with painful bleeding and a tender, firm uterus indicate a probable placental abruption with fetal compromise, requiring emergency transfer for urgent obstetric assessment and likely delivery. Outpatient follow-up or home management would be dangerously inadequate. This presentation constitutes an obstetric emergency.
  3. The correct answer is D (Placental abruption is primarily a clinical diagnosis, and ultrasound often fails to detect it): Placental abruption is diagnosed primarily on clinical grounds, including pain, uterine tenderness, bleeding, and fetal heart rate changes, because ultrasound has limited sensitivity and often misses smaller or acute abruptions. Waiting for ultrasound confirmation would dangerously delay urgent management. This is why clinical judgment guides emergency transfer decisions.
  4. The correct answer is A (A mild, partial abruption or marginal placental bleed with stable findings): A small amount of dark bleeding with mild discomfort but a soft, non-tender uterus and reassuring fetal heart rate is more consistent with a mild, partial abruption or a marginal placental bleed rather than a severe abruption or other emergency. Severe abruption typically presents with significant pain and instability, which are absent here. This milder presentation still requires assessment but reflects lower acuity.
Case C384

A 28-year-old G1P0 client at 29 weeks gestation is diagnosed with preterm labour after presenting with regular contractions and cervical change. She is transferred to hospital where the obstetric team discusses antenatal corticosteroids, magnesium sulfate for neuroprotection, and tocolysis.

  1. The correct answer is A (To reduce the risk of cerebral palsy in the infant if birth occurs preterm): Magnesium sulfate given before anticipated preterm birth has a neuroprotective effect that reduces the risk of cerebral palsy in the infant, which is its primary purpose in this context. It does not reliably stop contractions or treat infection, and it is not primarily used to lower maternal blood pressure in this scenario. This distinguishes its purpose from that of tocolytics or antihypertensives.
  2.
