The correct answer is D (Maximum benefit occurs when birth happens within seven days of the course): Antenatal corticosteroids provide the greatest reduction in neonatal respiratory complications when birth occurs within seven days of administration, which is why timing relative to anticipated birth matters clinically. They can still be given once labour has begun if birth is not imminent. They are not restricted only to gestations beyond 34 weeks.
  3. The correct answer is C (It briefly delays birth to allow corticosteroids and magnesium sulfate time to work): Tocolysis is used to create a short window of delay in birth, allowing time for corticosteroids to promote fetal lung maturity and for magnesium sulfate to provide neuroprotection, rather than to permanently halt labour. It does not replace the need for appropriate level of care transfer. This realistic explanation helps the client understand the goal of treatment.
  4. The correct answer is A (Consultation with neonatology regarding likely outcomes and care at this gestation): At 29 weeks gestation, neonatology consultation is important to discuss likely outcomes, expected level of neonatal care required, and to prepare the family for what to expect if preterm birth occurs. Dietitian referral and home birth planning are not appropriate priorities in this acute preterm labour situation. This consultation supports informed decision making for the family.
  5. The correct answer is A (Continue involvement in shared care and support alongside the obstetric and neonatal team): Even when a birth occurs in a shared or transferred care context due to preterm labour, the midwife can continue to provide support and involvement alongside the obstetric and neonatal teams rather than withdrawing entirely. This continuity of relationship is valued within the midwifery model of care. Complete withdrawal is not necessary or appropriate.
  6. The correct answer is A (A negative result has strong value in predicting she will not deliver soon): Fetal fibronectin testing has a high negative predictive value, meaning a negative result is reassuring that birth is unlikely within the next one to two weeks, which helps avoid unnecessary intervention. A positive result does not guarantee imminent birth in every case. It is not a substitute for cervical length assessment but can complement it.
  7. The correct answer is C (Consultation and transfer for antibiotics, monitoring, and consideration of corticosteroids is indicated): Preterm premature rupture of membranes at 30 weeks requires consultation and transfer for management including prophylactic antibiotics, monitoring for infection, and consideration of corticosteroids to support fetal lung maturity. Digital vaginal examination should be avoided to reduce infection risk. Discharge home without this specialized management would be inappropriate given the risks involved.
  8. The correct answer is A (Continued midwifery-led care with planned birth around her expected due date is reasonable): Well-controlled diet-managed gestational diabetes with reassuring fetal growth and fluid findings allows for continued midwifery-led care and a plan for birth around her expected due date, rather than automatic transfer or early induction. Home birth is not automatically contraindicated when control and growth remain reassuring. Individualized shared decision making guides this plan.
  9. The correct answer is D (She has an increased lifetime risk and should have regular glucose screening postpartum): Gestational diabetes is associated with a significantly increased lifetime risk of developing type 2 diabetes, so regular postpartum glucose screening is recommended regardless of whether diet alone or insulin was required during pregnancy. This risk does not disappear once the pregnancy ends. Ongoing screening supports early detection and prevention.
  10. The correct answer is C (To identify and treat neonatal hypoglycemia resulting from fetal hyperinsulinemia in utero): Infants of mothers with gestational diabetes are at risk of neonatal hypoglycemia because chronic fetal exposure to elevated maternal glucose leads to fetal hyperinsulinemia, which persists briefly after birth once the maternal glucose supply stops. Postpartum glucose screening allows prompt identification and treatment of this risk. It is not related to congenital anomalies or jaundice screening directly.
Case C394

A 25-year-old G1P0 client with chronic hypertension is found at 33 weeks gestation to have an estimated fetal weight at the 3rd percentile with reduced amniotic fluid volume and abnormal umbilical artery Doppler showing reversed end-diastolic flow.

  1. The correct answer is D (Urgent obstetric consultation given severe growth restriction with reversed end-diastolic flow): Reversed end-diastolic flow on umbilical artery Doppler represents a severe and concerning finding indicating significant placental compromise, warranting urgent obstetric consultation and likely closer monitoring or early birth. Routine follow-up or reassurance would be dangerously inadequate given this severity. This finding requires immediate escalation of care.
  2. The correct answer is A (Chronic hypertension can reduce placental blood flow, limiting fetal nutrient and oxygen delivery): Chronic hypertension can impair placental perfusion, reducing the delivery of oxygen and nutrients to the fetus and contributing to growth restriction over time. This is a recognized mechanism linking maternal vascular disease to impaired fetal growth. It is not solely a direct fetal blood pressure effect or limited to later gestational ages.
  3. The correct answer is C (Early birth is likely indicated given the severity of the Doppler findings): Severe Doppler abnormalities such as reversed end-diastolic flow, combined with significant growth restriction, typically indicate that the risks of continuing the pregnancy outweigh the risks of prematurity, making early birth likely indicated. Continuing expectant management without intervention would risk fetal demise. Tocolysis is not appropriate in this context since prolonging the pregnancy is not the goal.
  4. The correct answer is A (Heavy maternal smoking throughout pregnancy, which impairs placental blood flow): Heavy maternal smoking is a well-established risk factor for intrauterine growth restriction because nicotine and other substances impair placental blood flow and oxygen delivery to the fetus. A healthy body mass index, uncomplicated first pregnancy, and regular safe exercise are not risk factors for this condition. Identifying modifiable risk factors like smoking supports targeted counselling.
Case C398

A 30-year-old G1P0 client with a dichorionic diamniotic twin pregnancy is now at 36 weeks gestation. Twin A is vertex presentation and twin B is breech. She has had an uncomplicated pregnancy and asks her midwife about her options for birth.

  1. The correct answer is B (Consultation with obstetrics is indicated given the twin pregnancy and breech second twin): A twin pregnancy with a breech-presenting second twin falls outside standard midwifery-led scope and warrants obstetric consultation to plan an appropriate mode of birth. Home birth would not be appropriate given this combination of factors. Standard singleton planning does not adequately address the added complexity here.
  2. The correct answer is C (Vaginal birth of a breech second twin may be possible with obstetric support): With appropriate obstetric support and expertise, vaginal birth of a breech second twin is often possible and does not automatically necessitate cesarean birth for either twin. This depends on factors such as estimated fetal weight and provider experience. Presentation of the second twin is directly relevant to birth planning discussions.
  3. The correct answer is D (Continuous monitoring of twin B, who remains at risk until birth is complete): Once twin A is born, twin B remains at risk for complications such as cord prolapse, placental abruption, or malposition, making continuous monitoring essential until twin B's birth is also complete. Discontinuing monitoring or delaying assessment would be unsafe. The interval between twin births is a recognized period of heightened risk.
  4. The correct answer is D (Twin pregnancies carry an increased risk of postpartum hemorrhage from uterine overdistension): Twin pregnancies increase the risk of postpartum hemorrhage because uterine overdistension impairs effective uterine contraction after birth, reducing natural hemostasis. This is an important point for active management planning after birth. The risk is not lower than singleton birth nor unrelated to the multiple gestation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Birth is generally recommended around 37 to 38 weeks for uncomplicated twins): Current evidence supports planned birth around 37 to 38 weeks for uncomplicated dichorionic diamniotic twin pregnancies, balancing the risks of stillbirth from prolonged twin pregnancy against those of earlier birth. This differs from singleton pregnancy recommendations, which typically extend closer to 40 weeks. Delaying twin birth as long as singleton pregnancies would increase risk unnecessarily.
  6. The correct answer is B (Shared amniotic sac creates a high risk of cord entanglement requiring intensive monitoring): Monoamniotic twins share a single amniotic sac, creating a significant risk of umbilical cord entanglement that can compromise fetal circulation, requiring intensive monitoring and typically inpatient surveillance. This risk is substantially higher than in diamniotic twin pregnancies. Management therefore differs significantly and cannot be treated as equivalent to a singleton pregnancy.
Case C404

A 26-year-old Rh negative G2P1 client experiences a threatened miscarriage with light vaginal bleeding at 10 weeks gestation. Ultrasound confirms an ongoing pregnancy with a normal fetal heart rate detected. Her partner's Rh status is unknown.

  1. The correct answer is A (Administer Rh immune globulin promptly given the bleeding episode at this gestation): Any vaginal bleeding in an Rh negative client, including a threatened miscarriage, is a potential sensitizing event and warrants prompt administration of Rh immune globulin regardless of gestational age. Waiting for the bleeding to fully resolve or for partner status confirmation would delay necessary prophylaxis. This proactive approach reduces the risk of future sensitization.
  2. The correct answer is C (It prevents sensitization from fetal blood exposure that could affect future pregnancies): Rh immune globulin prevents maternal sensitization to fetal Rh positive blood cells that may enter the maternal circulation during a bleeding event, protecting future pregnancies from hemolytic disease even if the current pregnancy continues normally. It does not treat the bleeding itself or maternal anemia. This preventive rationale applies regardless of the pregnancy's apparent stability.
  3. The correct answer is C (Rh immune globulin reduces but does not entirely eliminate the risk of sensitization): Rh immune globulin significantly reduces but does not completely eliminate the risk of maternal sensitization, so rare cases of sensitization can still occur despite appropriate administration. This does not necessarily indicate an error in timing or a laboratory mistake. Understanding this residual risk is part of accurate client counselling.
  4. The correct answer is B (Rh immune globulin should be given following this invasive procedure given her Rh status): Invasive prenatal procedures such as chorionic villus sampling carry a risk of fetomaternal hemorrhage, making Rh immune globulin administration appropriate afterward for an Rh negative client with an unknown or Rh positive partner. Waiting for bleeding to occur or delaying until birth would miss this sensitizing event. This is a recognized indication distinct from routine antenatal dosing.
Case C408

A 40-year-old G3P2 client at 40 weeks gestation with well-controlled gestational hypertension reports she has noticed subjectively decreased fetal movement over the past day. She has no other new symptoms and her blood pressure today is stable at her baseline.

  1. The correct answer is A (Arrange prompt fetal heart rate monitoring given her risk factors and reported symptom): Advanced maternal age combined with hypertension are additional risk factors for placental insufficiency, so a report of decreased fetal movement in this context warrants prompt fetal heart rate monitoring rather than reassurance or delay. Her stable blood pressure today does not eliminate the need for assessment of the reported symptom. Timely evaluation is especially important given her combined risk profile.
  2. The correct answer is C (Urgent obstetric consultation given the non-reassuring fetal heart rate findings today): Reduced variability with late decelerations on the fetal heart rate tracing is a non-reassuring pattern suggesting possible placental insufficiency or fetal compromise, requiring urgent obstetric consultation rather than routine discharge or reassurance. This finding is particularly significant given her existing risk factors. Prompt escalation supports timely intervention if needed.
  3. The correct answer is B (Both advanced maternal age and hypertension can contribute to placental insufficiency near term): Advanced maternal age and hypertension are both independently recognized contributors to placental insufficiency, and their combination can compound risk near term, which is relevant context for today's non-reassuring findings. Neither factor alone should be dismissed as irrelevant. Understanding both contributing factors supports appropriate ongoing surveillance.
Case C411

A 24-year-old primigravida at 39 weeks gestation phones her midwife reporting mild, irregular contractions every 8 to 10 minutes for the past 3 hours. Membranes are intact, fetal movements are normal, and she denies vaginal bleeding or loss of fluid. She lives 15 minutes from the birth centre.

  1. The correct answer is D (Continue resting and eating at home until contractions become stronger and more frequent): This primigravida remains in the early latent phase, with mild, irregular contractions still 8 to 10 minutes apart, intact membranes, and normal fetal movement. Encouraging rest, nourishment, and staying home supports normal physiologic labour and avoids unnecessary early admission and intervention. She should be given clear safety-netting advice to call back if the pattern changes or warning signs develop. Immediate admission, sedation, or arranging induction transfer are not indicated for a well, low-risk client in early labour.
  2. The correct answer is B (The posterior cervical position noted on the examination): A posterior cervix is one of the five Bishop score components and contributes fewer points than an anterior position, lowering overall favourability for imminent labour progress. Dilation, effacement, and station are the other scored components, but position and consistency also matter clinically. Duration of symptoms, distance from the birth centre, and absence of bleeding are important pieces of the overall clinical picture but are not part of the Bishop score itself. Recognizing which findings are scored components helps the midwife counsel the client accurately about expected progress.
  3. The correct answer is C (Any sudden gush of fluid, bright red bleeding, or reduced fetal movement): These are recognized red-flag symptoms in a normal early labour that warrant reassessment regardless of the contraction pattern, since they may signal rupture of membranes, bleeding, or reduced fetal wellbeing. Mild, intermittent backache and steady contraction intensity are expected features of normal labour progress and do not require an urgent call on their own. Fatigue from a wakeful night is common and manageable with rest and support rather than urgent reassessment. Clear safety-netting ensures the client knows when to seek help promptly without unnecessary early contact for expected findings.
  4. The correct answer is D (The active phase begins once the cervix reaches roughly 6 cm dilation with regular contractions): Current definitions place the start of the active first stage at approximately 6 cm dilation accompanied by a regular, effective contraction pattern, reflecting updated understanding of normal labour curves. Contraction frequency alone, without reference to dilation, does not define this transition. Rupture of membranes and fetal station changes can occur at any point in labour and do not themselves define the phase transition. Understanding this threshold helps guide realistic expectations about labour progress for clients and care providers.
Case C415

A 30-year-old gravida 2 para 1 at 40 weeks gestation is admitted to the birth centre in active labour. She is low risk, membranes are intact, and she plans intermittent fetal heart auscultation. Contractions are now every 3 to 4 minutes, lasting 60 seconds, and moderate to strong.

  1. The correct answer is B (Every 15 to 30 minutes, timed after a contraction ends): Standard guidelines for intermittent auscultation in low-risk active first-stage labour recommend listening every 15 to 30 minutes, ideally immediately after a contraction, to assess the fetal response to labour. Once-hourly checks are too infrequent to detect emerging concerns in active labour. Continuous electronic monitoring is reserved for clients with risk factors or specific indications, not routine low-risk care. Waiting only for a reported change in movement would miss the recommended structured surveillance schedule for active labour.
  2. The correct answer is B (This is a reassuring finding consistent with normal fetal wellbeing at this time): A baseline of 135 beats per minute with good variability and no decelerations after a contraction reflects a normal, reassuring fetal heart pattern for ongoing low-risk labour. This finding supports continuing routine intermittent auscultation rather than escalating to continuous monitoring or urgent consultation. A full biophysical profile is not needed to interpret a reassuring intrapartum auscultation finding. Recognizing normal findings prevents unnecessary intervention while maintaining appropriate vigilance throughout labour.
  3. The correct answer is A (Auscultate after every contraction, or at minimum every 5 minutes during pushing): Guidelines increase the frequency of fetal heart auscultation during the second stage because of the physiologic stress of active pushing, typically recommending assessment after every contraction or at least every 5 minutes. Continuing the earlier active-phase frequency of every 30 minutes would not provide adequate surveillance during pushing. Stopping auscultation entirely during pushing removes essential fetal surveillance at a critical time. Maternal pulse checks do not substitute for fetal heart assessment and would miss changes specific to the fetus.
  4. The correct answer is A (Intermittent auscultation allows more mobility and is not associated with increased caesarean rates): Evidence supports intermittent auscultation as appropriate for low-risk labour because it preserves maternal mobility and comfort while continuous electronic monitoring in low-risk populations is associated with increased intervention rates without improved outcomes. Continuous monitoring is available in Canadian hospital settings and is used selectively based on risk, not withheld from everyone. It is not restricted to clients who decline pain relief, and intermittent auscultation is a practice recommendation, not a legal mandate. This evidence base supports individualized fetal surveillance choices for low-risk clients.
  5. The correct answer is C (Continuous labour support, freedom of movement, and immersion in warm water): These measures have strong supporting evidence for reducing perceived pain, shortening labour in some studies, and improving overall satisfaction with the birth experience. Strict bed rest, fasting, and minimal communication are outdated practices not supported by current evidence and may worsen coping. Routine early amniotomy and continuous monitoring are interventions for specific indications, not general comfort measures for a normal labour. Offering evidence-based comfort options respects informed choice and supports normal physiologic labour.
Case C420

A 22-year-old primigravida at 38 weeks gestation is 5 cm dilated, fully effaced, membranes intact, contractions every 3 minutes lasting 70 seconds. Two hours later a repeat examination shows 6 cm dilation with the same contraction pattern and reassuring fetal heart tones on auscultation.

  1. The correct answer is C (This rate of change falls within the expected range for normal active labour): Advancing from 5 cm to 6 cm over two hours with a strong, regular contraction pattern and reassuring fetal heart tones is consistent with normal, though sometimes gradual, active labour progress, particularly in a primigravida. This pace does not indicate dystocia requiring augmentation, nor should it be framed to the client as labour having stalled. Assessing cervical change through vaginal examination, not continuous electronic monitoring, is the standard method for tracking dilation. Recognizing normal variation in progress supports appropriate, non-interventionist care.
  2. The correct answer is B (The cervix has thinned out completely from its original length to become paper thin): Full effacement refers to complete thinning of the cervix from its normal thickness to a paper-thin structure, which is distinct from dilation, which describes the opening of the cervical os. Descent of the fetal head past the ischial spines describes station, not effacement. Uterine contraction strength moving the fetus into the pelvis describes engagement and descent, separate concepts from cervical effacement. Clear, accurate explanations help clients understand labour progress terminology correctly.
  3. The correct answer is B (Upright or side-lying positions with freedom to move as she prefers): Encouraging position changes and mobility during active labour supports comfort and can assist labour progress, and is appropriate for a client with no contraindications. Strict supine positioning with a flat bed is an outdated approach not supported by current evidence for normal labour. Lithotomy positioning with legs in stirrups is unnecessary for normal labouring care and reserved for specific procedures. Restricting a client to one fixed chair unnecessarily limits movement that can otherwise support comfort and progress.
  4. The correct answer is D (First stage latent and active phases, second stage, then third stage delivering the placenta): This reflects the standard division of labour, beginning with the first stage split into latent and active phases culminating in full dilation, followed by the second stage ending in birth of the baby, and the third stage ending with delivery of the placenta. The other sequences incorrectly reorder these stages and misassign which stage accomplishes birth of the baby versus the placenta. Accurate understanding of this sequence supports clear communication with clients about labour progress. This framework underlies most clinical documentation and guideline language used in midwifery practice.
Case C424

A client labouring at home with her midwife is 7 cm dilated and finding contractions increasingly intense. She has been using slow breathing and her partner's hand for counter-pressure, but she now asks about additional non-pharmacologic options before considering anything else.

  1. The correct answer is A (Immersion in a warm birth pool along with continued counter-pressure on her lower back): Warm water immersion combined with continued physical comfort measures like counter-pressure reflects appropriate escalation of non-pharmacologic support for a client in active labour requesting additional options. Recommending immediate epidural transfer or opioid injection does not respect her stated preference to explore non-pharmacologic options first. Withdrawing support and dimming lights without offering any active comfort measure fails to meet her expressed need for additional coping strategies. Layering non-pharmacologic measures according to client preference reflects individualized, evidence-based midwifery care.
  2. The correct answer is D (It is associated with shorter labours, less need for pain medication, and higher satisfaction): Evidence on continuous labour support consistently shows benefits including shorter labour duration, reduced need for pharmacologic pain relief, and improved satisfaction with the birth experience. It does not guarantee a pain-free labour and should be understood as one supportive measure among several, not a replacement for all others. Continuous support has shown benefit when provided by doulas, midwives, or trained companions, not exclusively physicians. Understanding this evidence helps partners and support people appreciate their valuable role during labour.
  3. The correct answer is D (Whatever upright, side-lying, or hands-and-knees position feels most comfortable to her): Supporting client-led position choice during a normal second stage respects both comfort and physiologic benefits of upright or lateral positioning for fetal descent. Forcing a specific position such as flat on the back with knees to chest immediately removes autonomy without clinical justification. Requiring her to stand completely still against a wall or lie prone with hips elevated are not standard, evidence-supported approaches to second-stage positioning. Individualized, flexible positioning reflects current best practice for normal spontaneous birth.
  4. The correct answer is C (Upright positions can improve pelvic diameters and use gravity to assist fetal descent): Upright positioning is associated with physiologic advantages including improved pelvic outlet dimensions and gravity-assisted descent compared with supine lithotomy positioning. It does not eliminate the possibility of perineal trauma, which depends on multiple factors beyond position alone. Evidence does not support upright positioning shortening the first stage of labour by several hours, as its main benefits relate to the second stage. Fetal heart auscultation remains necessary regardless of maternal position chosen for birth.
  5. The correct answer is B (Spontaneous pushing following the client's own urges rather than fixed directed counting): Current recommended practice favours spontaneous, client-led pushing in response to her own urges over rigid directed pushing with sustained breath-holding and counting, particularly for a normal, low-risk second stage. Sustained breath-holding with fixed counting has been associated with less favourable outcomes compared with spontaneous efforts. Withholding all pushing effort despite a strong urge is not appropriate once full dilation and descent support active bearing down. Restricting pushing to only the supine position with assisted legs is unnecessary for a normal second stage.
Case C429

A 27-year-old gravida 1 para 0 reaches the second stage of labour at a birth centre. She is fully dilated, the fetal head is visible at the introitus with pushing, and fetal heart tones remain reassuring. She is in a hands-and-knees position and pushing spontaneously.

  1. The correct answer is C (Applying gentle, warm perineal support while guiding a controlled, gradual birth of the head): Gentle perineal support combined with encouraging a slow, controlled birth of the head is an evidence-based technique associated with reduced severe perineal trauma. Routine episiotomy for every birth regardless of perineal appearance is not supported by current evidence and increases unnecessary trauma. Encouraging maximal forceful pushing during crowning increases the risk of rapid, uncontrolled delivery of the head and greater trauma. Fundal pressure to speed birth of the head is not a recommended technique and carries safety concerns.
  2. The correct answer is A (Allow restitution of the head to occur before assisting with the anterior shoulder): After birth of the head, allowing spontaneous restitution, the head turning to align with the shoulders, is a normal part of the mechanism of labour that should occur before gentle assistance with the anterior shoulder. Clamping and cutting the cord immediately after the head is born, before the body is delivered, is not standard practice and interrupts normal physiology. Asking the client to stop pushing entirely at this point would not support the ongoing mechanism of birth. Deep suctioning before the shoulders are delivered is not recommended for a vigorous newborn and is unnecessary at this stage.
  3. The correct answer is B (Placing the baby skin-to-skin on the mother's chest and drying while assessing tone and colour): Immediate skin-to-skin contact with drying and ongoing assessment of tone and colour reflects current best practice for a vigorous term newborn after normal birth. Taking the baby to a separate warmer immediately for examination delays important early bonding and thermoregulation benefits without medical indication. Routine bulb suctioning is not recommended for a vigorous newborn with clear fluid. Delaying skin-to-skin contact until weighing and measuring are complete is not consistent with current guidance prioritizing early contact.
  4. The correct answer is C (A score of 9, reflecting acrocyanosis as the only deducted point): a heart rate over 100, strong cry, active movement, a vigorous grimace in response to stimulation, and a pink body with blue extremities corresponds to full points in four categories (heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, and reflex irritability) and one point deducted for colour due to acrocyanosis, giving a total score of 9. Blue colouring limited to the hands and feet does not require scoring the entire assessment as zero. A perfect score of 10 is not accurate here because acrocyanosis is a recognized, common finding that is scored, not ignored. Heart rate is only one of five components and does not alone determine the total score.
  5. The correct answer is D (It provides a quick, structured snapshot of the newborn's transition and need for support): The Apgar score is designed as a rapid, structured assessment at 1 and 5 minutes to evaluate a newborn's immediate transition and guide any need for support at that moment. It is not intended as a definitive predictor of long-term neurologic outcome and should not be used that way. It does not replace ongoing newborn assessment over the following hours and days. It has no role in assessing placental separation, which is evaluated through separate maternal signs.
Case C434

A client has just given birth to a healthy term infant at home with her midwife. The infant is vigorous and placed skin-to-skin. The client and her midwife discussed active management of the third stage during prenatal visits and she has chosen this approach today.

  1. The correct answer is A (Prophylactic uterotonic administration, controlled cord traction, and uterine massage after delivery): Active management of the third stage classically combines a prophylactic uterotonic given after birth of the baby, controlled cord traction to assist placental delivery, and uterine massage afterward to support tone. Waiting for spontaneous separation without any of these components describes physiologic, not active, management. Immediate cord clamping alone without medication does not constitute the full active management approach. Fundal pushing without medication or controlled traction is not the recommended technique within active management.
  2. The correct answer is B (Controlled cord traction with counter-pressure applied above the pubic bone on the uterus): Controlled cord traction performed with simultaneous counter-pressure on the uterus above the pubic bone is the correct technique to assist placental delivery within active management, reducing the risk of cord avulsion or uterine inversion. Firm, unguided pulling without counter-pressure increases these risks. Fundal pushing alone without cord traction is not the standard active management technique. Waiting a further hour before assisting is inconsistent with the timely approach of active management once separation signs appear.
  3. The correct answer is A (Continue periodic fundal checks and monitor vaginal bleeding while supporting skin-to-skin contact): Ongoing assessment of uterine tone and bleeding alongside continued skin-to-skin contact reflects appropriate fourth-stage care after a normal, uncomplicated third stage. Giving an additional uterotonic when the fundus is already firm and bleeding is normal is unnecessary. Discontinuing all further assessment immediately after placental delivery would miss important fourth-stage monitoring for delayed hemorrhage. Separating mother and baby to complete the newborn examination is not necessary and interrupts important early bonding and breastfeeding support.
  4. The correct answer is A (Allowing spontaneous placental separation and delivery without a uterotonic or controlled cord traction): Physiologic, or expectant, management of the third stage involves waiting for the placenta to separate and deliver spontaneously, typically supported by maternal effort, without prophylactic uterotonic medication or controlled cord traction. Giving a uterotonic immediately after birth of the head describes active, not physiologic, management. Immediate clamping with firm pulling on the cord is inconsistent with the hands-off approach of physiologic management. Manual removal of the placenta is a specific intervention for complications, not a routine step in either approach.
  5. The correct answer is B (Improved neonatal iron stores, an accepted benefit despite a small increased risk of phototherapy for jaundice): evidence supports delayed cord clamping for improving neonatal iron stores in the months after birth. This benefit is accepted as outweighing a small but real increased risk of phototherapy for jaundice, which is a recognized trade-off rather than a reason to avoid delayed clamping. It does not guarantee reduced maternal postpartum hemorrhage, which depends on other factors in third-stage management. It does not eliminate the need for newborn assessment after birth. Delayed clamping does not shorten the third stage compared with immediate clamping, and timing of clamping is a separate consideration from placental delivery itself.
Case C439

A term infant is born vigorous at a birth centre after a normal spontaneous vaginal birth. The baby is placed skin-to-skin with the mother, dried, and covered with a warm blanket. The parents ask their midwife about the immediate newborn assessment that will be done over the next hour.

  1. The correct answer is D (Perform a brief visual assessment skin-to-skin and delay the full examination for at least an hour): Current recommended practice supports a brief visual assessment while the infant remains skin-to-skin, with the comprehensive physical examination delayed for at least the first hour to prioritize bonding, warmth, and early feeding. Separating the baby immediately for a full examination removes these important early benefits without medical indication. Bathing before skin-to-skin contact is not recommended and can interfere with thermoregulation and early bonding. Delaying all assessment, including Apgar scoring, until six hours of age is inconsistent with the standard timing of Apgar scoring at 1 and 5 minutes.
  2. The correct answer is A (A score of 10, indicating an excellent transition to extrauterine life): a heart rate above 100, strong cry, good tone, active movement, a vigorous response to stimulation, and fully pink colour represents full points across all five Apgar categories (heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, reflex irritability, and colour), giving a total score of 10 and indicating an excellent transition. This finding does not indicate a need for immediate resuscitation, moderate respiratory distress, or mild depression, since every component described reflects the highest scoring level. Recognizing a fully reassuring score supports continued routine newborn care rather than unnecessary intervention. Accurate scoring depends on correctly matching each described finding to its corresponding point value.
  3. The correct answer is D (It supports thermoregulation, early breastfeeding initiation, and maternal-infant bonding): Early, uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact is well supported by evidence for maintaining newborn temperature, encouraging early breastfeeding behaviours, and promoting bonding between parent and infant. It does not eliminate the need for later blood glucose monitoring in infants who have specific risk factors for hypoglycemia. It does not replace vitamin K administration, which addresses a separate physiologic need. Skin-to-skin contact supports but does not guarantee prevention of jaundice, which depends on multiple factors.
  4. The correct answer is B (It is routinely offered by intramuscular injection to prevent vitamin K deficiency bleeding): Standard Canadian midwifery practice includes routinely offering vitamin K by intramuscular injection shortly after birth to prevent vitamin K deficiency bleeding, a rare but serious newborn condition. It is offered as a standard part of newborn care across Canadian midwifery practice, not withheld universally. It is given prophylactically rather than only after bleeding has already occurred, since prevention is the goal. Its timing is not tied to when the cord is clamped, and informed choice discussions occur regardless of clamping timing.
  5. The correct answer is B (Heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, reflex irritability, and colour): The Apgar scoring system is built from these five specific components, each scored from zero to two points at 1 and 5 minutes after birth. Birth weight, gestational age, head circumference, length, and temperature are important newborn measurements but are not part of the Apgar score itself. Blood pressure, oxygen saturation, glucose, and temperature are separate clinical parameters assessed independently of Apgar scoring. Cervical dilation, station, effacement, and contraction pattern relate to maternal labour assessment, not newborn scoring.
  6. The correct answer is C (A previous uncomplicated vaginal birth and a current uncomplicated, low-risk pregnancy): Provincial eligibility guidelines for planned home birth generally consider obstetric history and current pregnancy risk status, and an uncomplicated previous vaginal birth combined with a current low-risk pregnancy supports appropriate candidacy. Personal preference alone, without meeting clinical risk criteria, is not sufficient to determine eligibility. Proximity to hospital services is a relevant safety consideration but is not the primary determinant of eligibility on its own. Attending all prenatal appointments reflects engaged care but does not itself establish clinical eligibility for home birth.
  7. The correct answer is D (A second birth attendant and emergency equipment are arranged for every planned home birth): Standard Canadian midwifery practice requires a second qualified attendant and appropriate emergency equipment to be present at every planned home birth, regardless of how low risk the pregnancy appears. Home birth is not reserved only for the very lowest risk category to the exclusion of standard safety preparation, and equipment is always brought regardless of perceived risk level. A single midwife attending alone without backup or transfer planning does not meet standard practice requirements. Equipment is a routine standard, not something arranged only upon specific client request.
  8. The correct answer is C (For eligible low-risk clients, planned home birth has comparable safety with fewer interventions): Research on planned home birth with a qualified attendant for eligible low-risk clients generally shows comparable safety outcomes to planned hospital birth, along with fewer interventions such as instrumental delivery or caesarean birth. It is not accurate to state that home birth is always significantly less safe regardless of eligibility, since risk status and attendant qualification are key factors. Planned home birth does not eliminate the possibility of intrapartum transfer, which remains part of safe planning. It does not require identical equipment and staffing to a hospital operating room, since home birth care is designed around a different, appropriately equipped model.
  9. The correct answer is A (Meconium-stained fluid, abnormal fetal heart findings, or lack of progress despite adequate contractions): These findings represent recognized indications for intrapartum transfer from a planned home birth to hospital, since they fall outside the scope of normal, low-risk home birth management. A client changing her mind about pushing position is a normal part of supporting client autonomy and does not itself indicate a need for transfer. Contractions becoming more frequent and intense as labour progresses is an expected, normal finding. Requesting to labour in a warm bath is a normal comfort measure choice and not an indication for transfer.
Case C448

A 29-year-old primigravida planning a hospital birth with her midwife arrives at 4 cm dilation with regular contractions every 4 minutes. Membranes are intact, fetal heart tones are reassuring on auscultation, and she has no risk factors identified during pregnancy.

  1. The correct answer is D (Maternal vital signs, fetal heart auscultation, contraction pattern, and a focused history review): A comprehensive but appropriately scoped admission assessment for a low-risk client includes vital signs, fetal heart auscultation, review of the contraction pattern, and a focused history to confirm ongoing low-risk status. Routine intravenous access and continuous electronic monitoring for every client is not supported by evidence for low-risk labour and represents unnecessary intervention. A full blood panel and ultrasound are not routine admission requirements for a client with no identified risk factors. An automatic anesthesia consultation regardless of preference does not reflect individualized, client-centred admission assessment.
  2. The correct answer is A (Yes, mobility and shower use are encouraged for a low-risk client with reassuring findings): Encouraging mobility and shower use during active labour supports comfort and normal physiologic progress for a low-risk client with reassuring findings, and is consistent with evidence-based hospital midwifery care. Requiring continuous bed rest and monitoring for every admitted client is not supported by current evidence for low-risk labour. Water immersion, including showers, is permitted in many hospital settings and is not universally prohibited. Movement should not be conditional on accepting an epidural, since mobility is valuable independent of pain management choices.
  3. The correct answer is C (Light eating and drinking as desired can generally be supported for a low-risk labouring client): Current evidence supports allowing light eating and drinking as desired during labour for low-risk clients, since prolonged fasting offers no clear benefit and may reduce energy and comfort. Complete fasting once labour becomes active is an outdated blanket restriction not supported by current guidelines for low-risk clients. Restricting intake to intravenous fluids only is unnecessary for a client without complications. Eating should not be made conditional on receiving an epidural, since the two are independent considerations.
  4. The correct answer is B (It supports trust and informed decision-making, which is linked with improved birth experiences): Continuity of care with a known midwife or small team supports the development of trust and facilitates informed decision-making throughout pregnancy and birth, and is associated with improved client experience and satisfaction. It is a valued feature of the midwifery model but is not a universal legal requirement dictating every birth setting. Continuity of care does not eliminate the need for physician consultation when clinical findings fall outside normal scope. It supports, but cannot guarantee, that labour will progress without any need for additional support measures.
Case C452

A 26-year-old gravida 1 para 0 in active labour at a birth centre reaches full dilation and begins pushing. She is fully mobile and has been changing positions throughout labour, currently kneeling and leaning forward over a birth ball.

  1. The correct answer is D (Gradual descent of the fetal head with each contraction and effective bearing-down effort): Normal progress in a spontaneous second stage is reflected by gradual descent of the fetal head coordinated with contractions and effective maternal bearing-down effort. No change in station despite an hour of strong contractions would raise concern rather than reflect normal progress. The fetal head receding fully after every contraction, rather than showing net gradual descent, would also be an atypical finding requiring closer assessment. A sudden loss of fetal heart tones is a significant abnormal finding requiring immediate action, not a sign of normal progress.
  2. The correct answer is B (Many upright and side-lying positions, including kneeling, are appropriate for a normal spontaneous birth): A range of upright, side-lying, and hands-and-knees positions, including kneeling, are appropriate choices for a normal spontaneous vaginal birth based on client comfort and preference. Requiring a supine position for birth regardless of comfort is not supported by current evidence favouring flexible positioning. Restricting birth position to only semi-sitting in a hospital bed unnecessarily limits client choice without clinical justification. Clients are not required to commit to one fixed position before labour begins, since position can reasonably change as labour progresses.
  3. The correct answer is D (Applying gentle perineal support and encouraging a slow, controlled birth of the head): Gentle perineal support with encouragement of a slow, controlled birth of the head can be adapted to different maternal positions, including kneeling, to reduce the risk of perineal trauma. Insisting on a position change immediately before crowning removes client autonomy without clear clinical benefit. A routine episiotomy performed only because of position choice, rather than clinical indication, is not supported by current evidence. Encouraging maximal forceful pushing through crowning increases the risk of rapid, uncontrolled delivery and greater perineal trauma.
  4. The correct answer is C (A first-degree tear often heals well on its own and may not require suturing): A first-degree perineal tear involves only the skin and is often superficial enough to heal well without sutures, depending on its exact characteristics and approximation of the edges. It is not accurate to say every first-degree tear requires immediate hospital transfer for surgical repair. First-degree tears do not involve the anal sphincter, which would instead characterize a more severe, higher-degree tear requiring specialist referral. A tear of this kind can typically be assessed and classified at the time of birth rather than only weeks later.
  5. The correct answer is D (Engagement, descent, flexion, internal rotation, extension, restitution, and external rotation): This reflects the standard, well-established sequence of the cardinal movements of labour for a fetus in an occiput anterior position as it navigates the pelvis and is born. The other listed sequences incorrectly reorder key movements, such as placing external rotation or restitution before the fetal head has even engaged or descended. Understanding this correct sequence helps the midwife recognize and support normal mechanisms of labour as they naturally occur. This knowledge underlies safe, hands-on support during a normal vaginal birth.
  6. The correct answer is C (Consistent presence and reassurance can reduce anxiety and support the client's own coping strategies): Continuous, one-on-one support during labour helps reduce anxiety and reinforces the client's own coping mechanisms, independent of any single physical comfort technique used. It is valuable for all labouring clients, not only those planning an epidural. It does not replace essential clinical tasks such as fetal heart auscultation, which must continue as part of ongoing assessment. This kind of support is beneficial regardless of whether the birth is a first or subsequent labour.
Case C458

A 35-year-old gravida 3 para 2 at 41 weeks gestation calls her midwife reporting contractions every 6 to 7 minutes for 4 hours, gradually increasing in strength. On examination the cervix is 3 cm dilated, 60% effaced, station -1, and soft in consistency.

  1. The correct answer is B (The soft consistency of the cervix noted on this examination): Cervical consistency is one of the five Bishop score components, and a soft cervix contributes more favourably to the score than a firm one, suggesting readiness for progress. Gestational age of 41 weeks and parity are important pieces of overall clinical context but are not scored components of the Bishop score itself. The reported duration of contractions before the call reflects the labour timeline but is also not part of the Bishop score. Recognizing which specific findings feed into this score supports accurate clinical assessment and communication.
  2. The correct answer is D (Continue close monitoring at home with a clear plan for when to return for reassessment): A multiparous client at 3 cm with a soft, effacing cervix and a reassuring overall picture can often continue to be monitored closely at home with clear guidance on when to return, respecting the normal course of early labour. Immediate admission for induction is not indicated based solely on reaching 41 weeks in the presence of otherwise reassuring findings, though ongoing surveillance near this gestation is appropriate. Discharging her with no further follow-up planned would miss important ongoing safety-netting. Recommending caesarean birth is not supported by this clinical picture and would represent an unwarranted intervention.
  3. The correct answer is C (Planning for the possibility of a rapid active phase and arranging timely reassessment): A history of quickly progressing previous labours is an important consideration for a multiparous client, since active labour can advance rapidly, making timely reassessment and readiness for birth especially important. Assuming this labour will proceed identically without any reassessment ignores the individual variability that still exists between labours. Delaying examination until contractions are very close together could risk missing rapid progress in this context. Dismissing the relevance of her obstetric history would overlook an important factor in safe labour planning.
  4. The correct answer is C (Dilation, effacement, station, consistency, and cervical position): These five factors together make up the standard Bishop score used to assess cervical readiness for labour during a vaginal examination. Fetal heart rate and contraction characteristics are assessed separately as part of overall labour surveillance, not as Bishop score components. Maternal vital signs are important general assessments but are unrelated to this specific cervical scoring system. Gestational age, maternal weight, parity, fundal height, and fetal presentation are relevant clinical details but are not part of the Bishop score itself.
  5. The correct answer is C (Staying home with rest and nourishment supports coping and normal progress toward active labour): Encouraging rest, light nourishment, and hydration during early labour supports a client's coping and allows normal physiologic progress toward the active phase without unnecessary early intervention. Coming in early does not reliably shorten the overall length of a normal first labour and can instead increase exposure to unnecessary intervention. Fasting during early labour is not required and may reduce energy needed for the work ahead. Rest at home has a meaningful, evidence-supported effect on coping during the early labour phase.
Case C463

A client gives birth to a healthy term infant at a birth centre following an uncomplicated labour. She chose active management of the third stage in advance. The infant is vigorous and placed skin-to-skin while the midwife prepares to manage delivery of the placenta.

  1. The correct answer is A (Shortly after birth of the baby, typically within the first minute after delivery): Active management protocols typically call for administering the prophylactic uterotonic shortly after the birth of the baby, often within about one minute, to help reduce the risk of postpartum hemorrhage. Waiting until after the placenta has already delivered removes the prophylactic timing benefit intended by active management. Giving the medication before the shoulders are born, during the second stage, is not the recommended timing. Reserving the medication only for when heavy bleeding is already occurring changes the approach from prophylactic to treatment, which is a different clinical scenario.
  2. The correct answer is A (Delay clamping for at least one to three minutes even within an active management plan): Delayed cord clamping for at least one to three minutes can be incorporated into an active management plan for a vigorous infant, supporting placental blood transfer while the uterotonic and controlled cord traction proceed as planned. Clamping within the first ten seconds regardless of infant status removes the benefits associated with delayed clamping. Clamping only after full placental separation is not the standard recommended timing, since it would unnecessarily prolong the interval. Avoiding cord clamping for several hours is not standard practice and is unrelated to supporting healthy iron stores through appropriately timed delay.
  3. The correct answer is D (Inspect the placenta and membranes for completeness and assess uterine tone and bleeding): After delivery of the placenta, inspecting it and the membranes for completeness, along with assessing uterine tone and estimating blood loss, are essential immediate steps to identify any retained tissue or early signs of hemorrhage. Discarding the placenta without inspection would miss an important safety check for retained products. Waiting an hour to check the uterus or estimate blood loss delays recognition of a potentially serious postpartum complication. Separating mother and baby simply to weigh the placenta is unnecessary and interrupts important early bonding time.
  4. The correct answer is A (Continued monitoring of maternal bleeding and tone alongside support for early breastfeeding and bonding): Ongoing assessment of uterine tone and bleeding, paired with continued support for breastfeeding and bonding, reflects appropriate fourth-stage care after a normal birth with reassuring findings. Immediate discharge without further monitoring would miss important surveillance for delayed postpartum complications. Routine separation of mother and baby for maternal rest is not necessary when both are stable and bonding well. Discontinuing vital sign checks entirely after placental delivery would overlook the ongoing risk of postpartum hemorrhage during the fourth stage.
  5. The correct answer is B (It allows continued placental blood transfer to the infant during the early minutes after birth): Delayed cord clamping is physiologically supported because it allows continued transfer of placental blood to the infant during the minutes following birth, contributing to improved iron stores. It does not prevent normal placental separation, which proceeds through its own physiologic process independent of clamping timing. It is not universally required to prevent maternal hemorrhage, since hemorrhage prevention depends on multiple factors including uterotonic use. Delayed clamping has a clear physiologic rationale rather than being based solely on personal preference.
  6. The correct answer is A (The placenta separates and is born spontaneously, often assisted by maternal effort and gravity): Physiologic management relies on spontaneous placental separation and delivery, often supported by maternal pushing effort and gravity, without the addition of a uterotonic medication. Controlled cord traction is a component of active management, not physiologic management, and would not be routinely performed if a uterotonic is declined. Immediate cord clamping is not a required feature of physiologic management and is a separate decision from third-stage approach. Continuous intravenous fluids are not a required part of physiologic management.
Case C469

A term infant is born at home following a normal, uncomplicated labour. The baby is placed skin-to-skin immediately, dried, and covered. At 1 minute the baby has a heart rate of 110 beats per minute, slow irregular breathing, some flexion of the limbs, a grimace to stimulation, and a pink body with blue extremities.

  1. The correct answer is B (A score of 7, reflecting mild depression across several components at this time): A heart rate over 100, slow irregular breathing, some flexion, a grimace response, and a pink body with blue extremities corresponds to a combined score of 7, reflecting mild depression across a few categories rather than a single perfect or critically low score. A score of 10 would require full points in every category, which does not match slow, irregular breathing or partial tone and reflex findings described here. A score of 2 would drastically understate several reassuring elements present, such as the heart rate and some responsiveness. A score of 9 would not adequately reflect the multiple partial deductions described across breathing, tone, and reflex findings.
  2. The correct answer is B (Continue drying, stimulating, and keeping the infant warm skin-to-skin while reassessing closely): A 1-minute score of 7 reflects mild depression that often responds well to ongoing drying, gentle stimulation, and warmth while the midwife closely reassesses the infant over the next few minutes. Immediately separating the infant to begin positive pressure ventilation is not indicated for a heart rate above 100 with some spontaneous effort and responsiveness. Delaying further assessment until the full newborn examination is inconsistent with the close monitoring needed after a mildly depressed initial score. Transporting to hospital before further observation is premature without first reassessing the infant's response to initial supportive measures.
  3. The correct answer is B (The infant has made a good transition and now shows a reassuring Apgar score of 10): Improvement from a 1-minute score of 7 to a 5-minute score reflecting full points across all categories indicates a good transition to extrauterine life and a reassuring outcome. This improvement does not indicate a need for urgent transfer, since the trend and current findings are reassuring. A valid 5-minute score can absolutely differ from and improve upon an earlier score, and this does not make it invalid. Separating the infant from the mother because of an earlier lower score is not warranted once the infant shows clear improvement and reassuring findings.
  4. The correct answer is C (A brief period of transition is common, and the improved 5 minute score is reassuring): It is common and expected for some newborns to show mild initial depression at 1 minute that resolves with brief supportive care, and the improved 5-minute score here is a reassuring sign of successful transition. A score below 8 at 1 minute does not always indicate a serious ongoing problem, particularly when it improves promptly with routine supportive measures. The score does not need to be exactly 10 at every interval to be considered a trustworthy and useful clinical tool. A deduction at 1 minute alone does not automatically require hospital transfer when the infant responds well to initial care.
  5. The correct answer is B (Active management uses a uterotonic and controlled cord traction, while physiologic management awaits spontaneous separation): This accurately distinguishes the two approaches, with active management combining a uterotonic and controlled cord traction and physiologic management relying on spontaneous placental separation without these interventions. The two approaches do not use the same medication at the same time, since physiologic management specifically omits the uterotonic. Physiologic management does not consistently take less time than active management in every birth, and duration can vary. Active management is available in both home and hospital settings, not restricted to only one birthplace.
  6. The correct answer is A (Water immersion during labour and birth can be offered to eligible low-risk clients): Water immersion for both labour and birth can be safely offered to eligible, low-risk clients as part of individualized, evidence-informed comfort measures. It is not prohibited across every Canadian jurisdiction, and many midwifery practices support it when appropriate. It is not restricted only to the third stage, since immersion is commonly used during the first and second stages as well. Continuous electronic fetal monitoring is not a requirement for water immersion, since intermittent auscultation remains appropriate for a low-risk client.
Case C475

A client labouring in a birth pool at a birth centre is fully dilated and beginning to push. The fetal head is visible with contractions, fetal heart tones remain reassuring, and the water temperature has been checked and maintained appropriately by the midwife.

  1. The correct answer is A (Keep the birth intermittent and hands off, monitoring temperature and fetal heart tones regularly): A hands-off approach with regular monitoring of water temperature and fetal heart tones supports a safe water birth while allowing the natural process of birth to proceed. Actively pulling the baby to the surface as soon as any part of the head appears can cause harm and interrupts the natural mechanism of birth. Removing the client from the pool the moment pushing begins would defeat the purpose of a planned water birth without clinical indication. Stopping fetal heart auscultation once in the water is inappropriate, since surveillance must continue throughout labour and birth.
  2. The correct answer is B (Keep the baby's head above the water surface and support skin-to-skin contact with the mother): Once born, the baby should be brought to and kept above the water surface promptly to support breathing, followed by skin-to-skin contact with the mother as in any normal birth. Submerging the baby again after birth is not appropriate once the infant is breathing and out of the water. Removing the baby immediately for examination away from the mother is unnecessary for a vigorous newborn and interrupts important early contact. Delaying assessment of colour and tone until fully dried would miss timely observation of the newborn's transition.
  3. The correct answer is C (Repeated movement between air and water can stimulate the baby to breathe before full delivery): Keeping the baby's head submerged until it is fully born, rather than moving it repeatedly between air and water, avoids stimulating a breathing reflex before the infant is completely delivered, which could increase the risk of water aspiration. This practice has a clear clinical safety rationale rather than being simply a matter of habit or appearance. It is not intended to make the birth look more comfortable for observers, but to protect the newborn during the transition. Water births do not always take significantly longer than land births, and this practice is unrelated to duration.
  4. The correct answer is D (The leading edge of the fetal head is level with the ischial spines): A station of 0 indicates that the leading edge of the fetal presenting part is level with the ischial spines, generally corresponding to an engaged head. A station of 0 does not mean the head has already been born and is visible at the introitus, which would be a much lower, more advanced station. It also does not describe a head still well above the pelvic inlet and unengaged, which would be a negative station further from 0. A station 3 cm below the spines would be described as a more advanced positive station, not station 0.
  5. The correct answer is C (Movement and position changes, warm water immersion, and continuous supportive presence): These measures reflect first-line, evidence-informed comfort options for a client wishing to avoid pharmacologic pain relief during a normal labour. Strict immobility combined with withholding food and fluids is an outdated approach not supported by current evidence and may worsen coping. Routine early amniotomy is an intervention with specific indications, not a general comfort measure, and carries its own risks. Scheduled opioid injections are a pharmacologic approach, which does not align with the client's stated preference to avoid such measures.
  6. The correct answer is D (This period supports thermoregulation, early feeding, and bonding while allowing close observation): Keeping the newborn skin-to-skin and largely undisturbed for the first hour supports thermoregulation, early breastfeeding initiation, and bonding, all while the midwife continues close visual observation of the infant. There is no requirement to delay weighing and bathing for a full 24 hours after every birth, since the recommended delay applies specifically to that important first hour period. The practice is based on physiologic and bonding benefits, not simply the availability of a scale at a home birth. Skin-to-skin contact is recommended broadly for vigorous newborns, not only for infants who required resuscitation.
Case C481

A 32-year-old G1P0 at 40 weeks gestation calls her midwife reporting contractions every 6 minutes, lasting 45 seconds, for the past 3 hours. She is talking comfortably between contractions, ambulating at home, and her membranes are intact. Fetal movements remain normal.

  1. The correct answer is D (Latent phase of the first stage of labour): Regular but manageable contractions with a woman still able to talk comfortably and ambulate typically indicate the latent phase, when cervical change is slow and coping strategies remain effective. Active labour is usually marked by stronger, more frequent contractions and more rapid dilation. Transition and second stage present with much more intense contractions and an urge to push. The midwife should continue to monitor progress rather than intervene at this stage.
  2. The correct answer is D (Advise her to rest, stay hydrated, and call back if contractions intensify): Latent labour is normally managed with reassurance, comfort measures, and continued monitoring at home rather than early intervention. Hospital admission or augmentation during latent labour increases the likelihood of unnecessary interventions without improving outcomes. Artificial rupture of membranes is not indicated for a woman coping well in early labour. Supporting normal physiology respects the midwifery model of informed, low-intervention care.
  3. The correct answer is B (Go to assess her, since this pattern suggests active labour has begun): A change to stronger, more frequent contractions suggests progression into active labour, warranting an in-person assessment of maternal and fetal wellbeing and cervical status. Continuing to advise waiting without assessment could delay identification of complications or rapid progress. This pattern is inconsistent with ongoing latent labour. Direct assessment allows the midwife to plan for birth setting and support appropriately.
  4. The correct answer is A (The Bishop score suggests the cervix is unfavourable for induction): A firm, posterior, minimally dilated cervix with the head high above the spines yields a low Bishop score, indicating the cervix is not yet ready for induction. A favourable score requires more dilation, effacement, and a softer, more anterior cervix with descent. These findings do not represent active labour or imminent membrane rupture. This information guides shared decision-making about induction timing and method.
  5. The correct answer is C (Low dilation, poor effacement, and a firm, posterior cervix): These unfavourable Bishop score components predict a longer, less successful induction and support using a cervical ripening agent before oxytocin. Fetal heart rate, blood pressure, and fetal movement are important assessments but are not part of the Bishop score itself. Ripening agents help soften and open the cervix before augmentation begins. This stepwise approach improves the likelihood of a successful vaginal birth.
Case C486

A midwife is providing intrapartum care for a healthy 26-year-old G2P1 in active labour at a birth centre, low risk throughout pregnancy, with clear amniotic fluid and reassuring fetal movements.

  1. The correct answer is A (Intermittent auscultation with a Doppler device): For a low-risk woman in active labour, intermittent auscultation is the recommended and evidence-based method of fetal surveillance, allowing mobility and supporting normal physiological birth. Continuous electronic monitoring and invasive methods are reserved for higher-risk situations or when concerns arise. Frequent biophysical profiles are not part of routine intrapartum surveillance. This approach reflects appropriate care for uncomplicated labour.
  2. The correct answer is B (At least every 15 to 30 minutes, before and after a contraction): Guidelines recommend auscultating the fetal heart rate at this frequency during active labour, listening through and immediately after a contraction to detect decelerations. Auscultating only every two hours or solely on report of decreased movement would miss significant changes. Continuous monitoring is not required for low-risk intermittent auscultation candidates. This frequency balances surveillance with supporting mobility and normal labour.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reposition the woman and reassess promptly, escalating if it persists): A prolonged deceleration below the normal baseline requires immediate intrauterine resuscitation measures such as repositioning, and close reassessment to determine whether the pattern resolves or requires urgent consultation and transfer. This finding is not normal and should not simply be documented and continued. Waiting an hour would delay recognition of possible fetal compromise. Prompt action protects fetal wellbeing while preserving time for a safe response.
  4. The correct answer is D (Continue intermittent auscultation at the recommended frequency and document findings): Once the fetal heart rate has returned to a reassuring pattern, ongoing intermittent auscultation at the standard interval with thorough documentation is appropriate, as the isolated deceleration has resolved. Automatic transfer or cesarean section is not warranted when reassurance has been achieved. Discontinuing monitoring entirely would be unsafe. Continued vigilant surveillance supports early detection if the pattern recurs.
  5. The correct answer is B (Hydrotherapy, position changes, and continuous labour support): Non-pharmacological measures such as warm water immersion, movement, upright positions, and continuous one-to-one support are first-line comfort strategies that align with the midwifery model and often reduce perceived pain effectively. Transfer for epidural is not the first response to a request for comfort options at home. Opioids carry side effects and are not the initial non-pharmacological choice. Prolonged supine bed rest can slow labour progress and reduce comfort.
  6. The correct answer is A (It may improve fetal positioning and reduce back pain): Forward-leaning positions can help rotate an occiput posterior fetus, relieve pressure on the sacrum, and reduce back pain during contractions. This position does not guarantee a shorter labour or eliminate the need for additional comfort measures later. Perineal support remains necessary regardless of the positions used during labour. Encouraging maternal movement and position changes supports comfort and can aid fetal descent.
Case C492

A 24-year-old G1P0 at 39 weeks reaches full cervical dilation after an uncomplicated first stage. She begins to feel a strong urge to push, and the fetal head is visible at the introitus with contractions.

  1. The correct answer is C (Full cervical dilation with the urge to push and descent): The second stage begins with complete cervical dilation and continues until the birth of the baby, typically marked by a strong urge to push as the fetus descends. Membrane rupture and bloody show can occur at various points and do not define the second stage. Regular contractions every five minutes are characteristic of the first stage. Recognizing this transition helps the midwife prepare appropriately for birth.
  2. The correct answer is B (Apply gentle counter-pressure and support a slow, controlled birth of the head): Gentle hands-on guidance and encouraging a slow, controlled birth of the head reduce the speed of stretch on the perineum and lower the risk of tearing. Forceful pushing with every contraction and fundal pressure both increase trauma risk. Routine episiotomy is not evidence-based and is reserved for specific indications. This approach reflects perineal protection best practice.
  3. The correct answer is C (Presence of a nuchal cord around the fetal neck): After the head is born, the midwife routinely checks for a nuchal cord so it can be reduced or managed appropriately before the shoulders and body are born. Placental separation and blood type are not assessed at this exact moment. The time labour began is documented information, not an immediate action at birth. This quick check helps ensure a smooth and safe delivery of the shoulders.
  4. The correct answer is C (Gentle downward traction for the anterior shoulder, then upward for the posterior): Following the normal mechanism of labour, gentle downward guidance helps birth the anterior shoulder under the pubic arch, followed by upward guidance to release the posterior shoulder over the perineum. Strong upward traction on the head first is not part of normal technique and can cause injury. Continuous fundal pressure is not indicated for a normal birth. This sequence mirrors the natural cardinal movements of labour.
  5. The correct answer is A (Dry the baby, place skin-to-skin, and assess tone, colour, and respiratory effort): Immediate drying, skin-to-skin contact, and a rapid assessment of tone, colour, and breathing support thermoregulation, bonding, and early detection of any transition difficulty. Immediate cord clamping within seconds is not recommended when the baby is vigorous, since delayed clamping benefits are well established. Moving the baby away from the mother for assessment interrupts bonding unnecessarily. Assessment should not be delayed until after the placenta is delivered, since the newborn's transition needs prompt attention.
Case C497

A midwife has just supported the birth of a healthy newborn for a G3P2 woman with no history of postpartum hemorrhage. The client has consented to active management of the third stage.

  1. The correct answer is D (Administration of a prophylactic uterotonic after birth of the baby): Active management typically includes prophylactic uterotonic administration, controlled cord traction, and uterine massage, which together reduce the risk of postpartum hemorrhage compared with physiologic management. Waiting for spontaneous separation without intervention describes physiologic management, not active management. Delaying the uterotonic until after placental expulsion negates its preventive benefit. Manual removal is not routine and is reserved for retained placenta.
  2. The correct answer is A (Continue to monitor for signs of separation while assessing bleeding): In the absence of excessive bleeding, continued observation for signs of placental separation such as cord lengthening and a uterine fundal change is appropriate before intervening further. Manual removal is reserved for prolonged retention or hemorrhage, not routine early monitoring. Strong traction without separation signs risks uterine inversion or cord avulsion. The client should never be discharged with the placenta undelivered.
  3. The correct answer is A (Inspect the placenta and membranes for completeness and assess the uterus): A thorough inspection of the placenta and membranes helps identify retained fragments that could cause later hemorrhage or infection, alongside ongoing uterine and bleeding assessment. Discarding the placenta without inspection could miss a significant finding. Waiting an hour to assess bleeding delays recognition of a developing hemorrhage. Transfer is not indicated when the third stage has progressed normally with a well-contracted uterus.
  4. The correct answer is D (Allowing spontaneous placental separation without a prophylactic uterotonic): Physiologic management involves waiting for spontaneous signs of separation and delivery of the placenta without prophylactic uterotonics or early cord clamping, relying on maternal effort and uterine contraction. Giving oxytocin early or performing controlled cord traction before separation are features of active management. Early cord clamping within a minute is not part of physiologic third stage practice. This approach respects the woman's informed choice while requiring vigilant observation.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Lengthening of the umbilical cord and a gush of blood): Classic signs of placental separation include lengthening of the visible cord, a small gush of blood, a change in the shape and firmness of the uterine fundus, and slight ascent of the fundus. Blood pressure changes and fetal heart rate are not relevant once the baby is born. An immediate urge to push after birth is not a reliable separation sign. Recognizing these signs allows safe, physiologic delivery of the placenta.
Case C502

A term newborn is born vigorous at a birth centre after an uncomplicated spontaneous vaginal birth. The midwife dries the baby and places them skin-to-skin with the mother.

  1. The correct answer is A (Heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, reflex irritability, and colour): The APGAR score assesses these five components at one and five minutes of age to quickly evaluate the newborn's transition to extrauterine life. Blood pressure, weight, and head circumference are separate newborn measurements, not part of APGAR. Cervical findings relate to maternal labour assessment, not the newborn. Maternal bleeding and uterine tone are third stage assessments, unrelated to the APGAR score.
  2. The correct answer is A (A score of 9, reflecting a normal, well-transitioning newborn): Each of the five APGAR components would score 2 except colour, which scores 1 due to peripheral cyanosis (acrocyanosis), giving a total of 9, a reassuring result.
