A score of 3 or 5 would suggest significant compromise requiring intervention, which does not match this vigorous presentation. Acrocyanosis alone does not warrant a perfect score of 10, since central colour must be fully pink for that component to score 2. This score reflects a healthy, appropriately transitioning newborn.
  3. The correct answer is C (Document both scores and continue routine newborn assessment and care): Documenting the one and five minute APGAR scores and proceeding with routine ongoing assessment is appropriate when the newborn is transitioning well. Repeating APGAR scoring every five minutes for an hour is not standard practice for a reassuring newborn. Transfer is not indicated based on reassuring scores alone. Skin-to-skin contact should be maintained during the early newborn period rather than interrupted unnecessarily for routine examination, which can be performed while the baby remains with the mother.
  4. The correct answer is A (To identify any signs requiring urgent attention while supporting bonding): An early gentle assessment while skin-to-skin allows the midwife to check for signs of distress or anomalies needing prompt attention without disrupting maternal-newborn bonding and thermoregulation. Predicting feeding times or long-term growth percentiles is not the purpose of this assessment. This initial check does not replace the more comprehensive newborn examination performed later. Balancing thorough assessment with minimal disruption reflects family-centred midwifery care.
Case C506

A low-risk multiparous client at 39 weeks is planning a home birth with her midwife, having had two previous uncomplicated vaginal births with no postpartum or neonatal complications.

  1. The correct answer is B (A history of uncomplicated vaginal births with no risk factors present): A reassuring obstetric history combined with an uncomplicated current pregnancy supports continued eligibility for home birth under a low-risk care pathway. A prior significant postpartum hemorrhage, current hypertensive disease, or breech presentation would each typically prompt consultation or a recommendation for hospital birth. Ongoing risk assessment throughout pregnancy and labour is essential regardless of parity. This client's history is reassuring for continued home birth planning.
  2. The correct answer is C (Emergency medications, resuscitation equipment, and a clear transfer plan): Midwives attending home births carry emergency medications and neonatal resuscitation equipment and maintain a clear plan for rapid transfer to hospital if needed, even for low-risk clients. Preparing only comfort items would leave the team unable to respond to an emergency. Home birth still requires the same level of emergency readiness as any birth setting. Photography scheduling is not a clinical safety preparation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Ongoing assessment of maternal and newborn wellbeing in the home setting): When a home birth proceeds normally, the midwife continues postpartum monitoring and follow-up visits in the home, consistent with the continuity of care model, unless a concern arises. Automatic transfer is unnecessary when both mother and newborn are stable. Providing no further contact after birth would fail to meet standard postpartum care expectations. Postpartum assessment begins soon after birth, not after a six week delay.
  4. The correct answer is A (It can improve pelvic dimensions and aid fetal descent): Upright and hands-and-knees positions may increase pelvic diameters, use gravity effectively, and support fetal descent and rotation. These positions do not guarantee freedom from perineal trauma or a specific duration of the second stage. Fetal heart rate monitoring remains necessary regardless of maternal position. Supporting the woman's preferred position is part of respecting informed choice in midwifery care.
  5. The correct answer is B (Spontaneous pushing may reduce fatigue and support normal physiology): Following the woman's own urge to push, rather than routine directed coaching, is associated with less maternal fatigue and supports normal physiological second stage progress for many low-risk women. Evidence does not consistently show spontaneous pushing lengthens the second stage or increases fetal risk. Directed pushing is not required for every woman and should be individualized. Supporting spontaneous efforts aligns with evidence-informed, woman-centred practice.
Case C511

A midwife is caring for a G1P0 client whose labour has progressed from 4 cm to 8 cm dilation over 4 hours with strong regular contractions, descending head, and no maternal or fetal concerns.

  1. The correct answer is B (A normal rate of progress for active labour in a primigravida): A dilation rate of roughly 1 cm per hour during active labour is within the expected normal range for a first-time labouring woman with reassuring maternal and fetal status. This rate is neither abnormally slow, which would warrant further assessment, nor precipitously fast. The presentation clearly indicates active labour is well underway, not that it has yet to begin. Continued supportive care and monitoring remain appropriate.
  2. The correct answer is B (Assess for reversible causes such as maternal position, hydration, and bladder fullness): A slowing or arrest of progress with weakening contractions should prompt assessment for correctable contributing factors, such as an unemptied bladder, dehydration, or an unhelpful maternal position, before considering further intervention. Proceeding directly to cesarean section skips appropriate stepwise assessment. This pattern is a change from her earlier progress and does warrant follow up, not simple reassurance. Discharging a client at 8 cm dilation from a supervised labour setting would be inappropriate and unsafe.
  3. The correct answer is D (Simple supportive measures can resolve some causes of slowed progress): This outcome demonstrates that correctable factors, once addressed, can allow labour to resume its normal course without pharmacological augmentation. The earlier slowing was not necessarily a sign of fetal compromise, and no such concern was noted. Oxytocin was not required in this case since progress resumed with supportive care alone. The rapid recovery of normal progress does not indicate pelvic inadequacy.
  4. The correct answer is C (The transition phase leading into the second stage of labour): Rectal pressure and an involuntary urge to push near full dilation are hallmark signs of the transition period as the woman moves from the first into the second stage of labour. This presentation does not describe latent labour, which involves milder, more manageable contractions. The fourth stage occurs after the placenta is delivered, well beyond this point. These symptoms are not consistent with false labour, given confirmed full dilation.
Case C515

A G1P0 client in active labour at a hospital birth unit under midwifery care is using intermittent nitrous oxide and hydrotherapy for pain management, supported continuously by her midwife and partner.

  1. The correct answer is D (Reduced likelihood of interventions and improved maternal satisfaction): Continuous one-to-one labour support is associated with reduced use of interventions such as instrumental birth and improved maternal satisfaction with the birth experience. It does not eliminate labour pain entirely or guarantee a fixed reduction in labour duration. Continuous support does not remove the possibility that a medical consultation may still become necessary. This evidence supports the value of consistent, personalized support throughout labour.
  2. The correct answer is C (It provides mild analgesia and relaxation when self-administered during contractions): Nitrous oxide offers mild pain relief and relaxation and is typically self-administered by the woman just before and during a contraction, allowing her to control the dose. It does not eliminate all sensation, and continuous use between contractions is not the intended method. It functions independently and does not require concurrent epidural use. This makes it a flexible, low-intervention option for many labouring women.
  3. The correct answer is B (A waterproof Doppler device should be used for auscultation in the pool): A waterproof handheld Doppler allows the midwife to safely continue intermittent auscultation while the woman remains in the birth pool, preserving the comfort benefits of hydrotherapy. Discontinuing fetal heart rate assessment during water immersion would be unsafe. Continuous electronic monitoring is not required and is often impractical in water. Requiring her to exit the pool for every auscultation would negate much of the benefit of hydrotherapy.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reassess the labour and consider consultation regarding possible augmentation): Crossing the action line signals that progress has fallen behind the expected rate and prompts a fuller reassessment, often including consultation with an obstetric provider about options such as augmentation. This finding should not be disregarded, since it is a recognized indicator of concerning progress. Moving straight to cesarean section skips appropriate stepwise evaluation. Sending the client home at this point in active labour would be inappropriate.
  5. The correct answer is B (Discuss amniotomy or oxytocin augmentation with the client and consulting provider): When disproportion has been reasonably ruled out and contractions are adequate, augmentation options such as amniotomy or oxytocin are appropriate to discuss collaboratively with the client and consulting provider. Moving directly to cesarean section without trying augmentation would not reflect a stepwise, evidence-based approach. Monitoring should be continued, not discontinued, during augmented labour. Telling the client no action is needed would ignore the identified delay in progress.
Case C520

A 33-year-old G4P3 at 38 weeks arrives at the birth centre reporting strong contractions that began only 90 minutes ago, now 2 minutes apart, with an urge to push.

  1. The correct answer is A (A precipitous labour pattern given her multiparity and rapid onset): Multiparous women often labour more quickly than first-time mothers, and this presentation, rapid onset with strong frequent contractions and an urge to push, is consistent with precipitous labour. This pattern is inconsistent with false labour. Assuming labour will take many more hours would be unsafe given how quickly she is progressing. Her obstetric history of prior births is directly relevant to anticipating a faster course.
  2. The correct answer is A (Prepare immediately for imminent birth and call for additional support): With a visible fetal head and full dilation, birth is imminent, so the priority is preparing the immediate environment and calling for additional support rather than delaying. Insisting on further exams or waiting would risk an unattended or unsupported birth. Transporting her by vehicle at this point would be unsafe given how close birth is. Rapid, calm preparation supports a safe outcome for mother and baby.
  3. The correct answer is A (Increased risk of postpartum hemorrhage due to precipitous labour): Precipitous labour is associated with an increased risk of postpartum hemorrhage, so the midwife should closely monitor uterine tone and bleeding in the immediate postpartum period. This situation does require attentive monitoring beyond the usual baseline, not less. A precipitous labour does not automatically mean the placenta will require manual removal. Transfer is not automatically required if the mother and newborn remain stable and well.
  4. The correct answer is D (From the onset of regular contractions with cervical change to full dilation): The first stage of labour spans the period from the onset of regular, effective contractions causing cervical change through to complete dilation of the cervix. The second stage covers full dilation to birth, and the third stage covers birth to placental delivery. The period after placental delivery is often referred to as the fourth stage, not the first. Understanding these stages helps guide appropriate assessment and support.
  5. The correct answer is B (At least every 5 minutes, ideally after each contraction): During the second stage, closer surveillance is recommended, with auscultation at least every five minutes and ideally after each contraction, given the increased physiological demands on the fetus. Auscultating only once at the start would miss important later changes. Waiting a full hour is far too infrequent for this stage. Surveillance should follow clinical guidelines rather than depend solely on maternal request.
  6. The correct answer is C (A soft, anterior cervix that is 80% effaced and 4 cm dilated): Greater dilation, more effacement, a soft consistency, and an anterior position all contribute to a higher, more favourable Bishop score. A firm, posterior, minimally dilated and effaced cervix reflects an unfavourable score. A high fetal station similarly lowers the score rather than raising it. A long, uneffaced posterior cervix is the opposite of a favourable finding.
  7. The correct answer is B (Position changes can promote comfort and support fetal descent and rotation): Frequent position changes can enhance comfort, use gravity effectively, and encourage optimal fetal positioning and descent throughout labour. There is meaningful physiological benefit to movement, contrary to the idea that it has none. Remaining fixed in one position is not generally preferred and may slow progress. These benefits are relevant throughout labour, not only during the second stage.
  8. The correct answer is B (Progressive cervical dilation accompanied by increasingly strong, regular contractions): Active labour is characterized by progressive dilation alongside stronger, more frequent, regular contractions, reflecting effective uterine activity. A complete absence of contractions would suggest labour has stalled or not truly begun. A fetal heart rate that high would be markedly abnormal and require urgent evaluation. Painless bleeding without contractions raises concern for a placental abnormality rather than reflecting normal labour.
  9. The correct answer is B (It allows additional placental blood transfusion, improving newborn iron stores): Delaying cord clamping for at least one to several minutes allows continued placental transfusion, increasing newborn blood volume and iron stores, which supports healthy development. This practice does have a measurable, beneficial effect, contrary to having none. It is generally recommended for vigorous term newborns rather than avoided. Delayed clamping typically refers to a period of one to several minutes, not a full hour.
  10. The correct answer is A (Encourage breathing techniques to avoid pushing against an incompletely dilated cervix): Pushing before full dilation can cause cervical swelling or trauma, so guiding the woman with breathing techniques to manage the urge until dilation is complete is appropriate. Encouraging forceful pushing at this point could cause harm. The urge to push is a meaningful physiological signal, not one to dismiss entirely. Artificial rupture of membranes is not an appropriate response to premature pushing urges.
  11. The correct answer is D (To promote uterine contraction and reduce the risk of hemorrhage): Gentle fundal massage after placental delivery encourages the uterus to contract firmly, which helps reduce the risk of postpartum hemorrhage. It does not directly relate to newborn bonding or newborn respiratory assessment, which are separate priorities. It also has no relationship to predicting the timing of a future menstrual period. This simple intervention is an important part of routine third stage care.
  12. The correct answer is B (Normal fundal dominance of uterine contractions in effective labour): Fundal dominance, where the contraction begins and is strongest at the fundus and spreads downward, is the normal, effective pattern of uterine activity in labour. This is not an abnormal pattern requiring augmentation, nor is it a warning sign of uterine rupture. It is a general feature of coordinated labour contractions and is not limited to post-term pregnancies. Recognizing this pattern helps confirm that labour is progressing physiologically.
  13. The correct answer is B (Provide calm reassurance, coaching, and reinforce that birth is progressing): Emotional support, calm reassurance, and clear coaching are core midwifery skills that help a woman regain a sense of control during the intense second stage. Anxiety alone during active pushing is not an indication for cesarean section or general anesthesia. Leaving the room during this vulnerable moment would withdraw needed support rather than provide it. Anxiety at this stage is common and does not by itself indicate a serious complication.
  14. The correct answer is C (The fetal head rotates to align the occiput with the maternal symphysis): Internal rotation is a cardinal movement in which the fetal head rotates within the pelvis so the occiput moves toward the maternal symphysis pubis, facilitating passage through the pelvic outlet. The head does not remain fixed in one position throughout descent in a normal mechanism. Shoulder rotation occurs later, after the head has been born. Internal rotation is a mechanism of vertex presentations, not specific to breech births.
  15. The correct answer is A (Ongoing reassessment for risk factors throughout pregnancy, labour, and birth): Midwifery care involves continuous reassessment of risk status throughout pregnancy, labour, and birth, since new findings can emerge at any point that change the appropriate care pathway. A single assessment at the first visit would fail to capture changes over time. Risk status can and does change as pregnancy and labour progress. Ongoing assessment, not an absence of it, is a defining feature of safe midwifery practice.
  16. The correct answer is A (A normal, reassuring fetal heart rate pattern): A baseline within the normal range with normal variability and no decelerations is a reassuring finding indicating fetal wellbeing. This pattern does not itself warrant urgent transfer or suggest fetal heart block. It also does not automatically indicate maternal fever, which would need to be assessed separately through maternal vital signs. Continued routine intermittent auscultation is appropriate in this situation.
  17. The correct answer is D (To increase perineal tissue elasticity and reduce the risk of tearing): Warm compresses and perineal massage during the second stage may help increase tissue elasticity and reduce the likelihood or severity of perineal trauma during birth. These techniques do not affect the need for ongoing fetal heart rate monitoring. They cannot guarantee that no support will be needed at the moment of birth. They are relevant to the second stage, not to first stage cervical dilation.
  18. The correct answer is C (Fetal heart rate, fluid colour and amount, and time of rupture): After spontaneous rupture of membranes, the midwife should promptly assess fetal heart rate, note the colour, amount, and odour of the fluid, and document the time of rupture, as these findings guide ongoing care. Assessing fluid colour alone would miss important fetal wellbeing information. Blood pressure alone does not capture the relevant post-rupture assessment needs. Some assessment is always warranted following membrane rupture, not none at all.
  19. The correct answer is B (The combination of cervical change, descent, and contraction pattern over time): Normal labour progress is assessed through the interplay of cervical dilation and effacement, fetal descent, and an effective contraction pattern evaluated over time, not any single measure alone. The exact time contractions began is only one small piece of the overall picture. A woman's preference about duration does not determine physiological progress. Fluid colour is relevant to a different assessment and does not by itself indicate labour progress.
  20. The correct answer is A (Acrocyanosis with a pink central body and vigorous cry): Bluish discoloration limited to the hands and feet, with a pink trunk and a vigorous cry, is a normal transitional finding in a healthy newborn. Persistent central cyanosis of the trunk and face is abnormal and requires prompt assessment. An absence of respiratory effort for several minutes would indicate significant compromise requiring resuscitation. Marked floppiness with no spontaneous movement is also an abnormal finding needing urgent attention.
  21. The correct answer is D (It builds trust and supports individualized, informed decision-making throughout care): Continuity of care allows a trusting relationship to develop, supporting communication, individualized planning, and informed choice throughout the pregnancy and birth journey. It does not guarantee a specific labour duration. Ongoing risk assessment remains necessary regardless of the continuity model used. Continuity of care is a core feature of the midwifery model generally, not limited to home birth clients.
  22. The correct answer is B (It may reasonably last up to a few hours with adequate progress): For a first-time mother, the second stage can reasonably last a few hours provided there is ongoing descent and reassuring maternal and fetal status, particularly with epidural analgesia or without directed pushing pressure. Expecting completion within exactly fifteen minutes is unrealistic for many primigravidas. A five minute limit would be far too short and does not reflect typical physiology. While duration should be individualized, ongoing lack of progress does eventually warrant reassessment rather than having no limit at all.
  23. The correct answer is A (The head realigning with the shoulders after birth of the head): Restitution is the natural movement of the fetal head back into alignment with the shoulders immediately after the head is born, undoing the internal rotation that occurred during descent. Placental separation is a distinct third stage event, unrelated to restitution. Cervical involution and uterine involution both refer to postpartum changes over subsequent days and weeks, not this immediate mechanism of birth.
  24. The correct answer is A (Regular strong contractions with progressive cervical dilation past about 4 to 6 cm): Active labour is generally recognized by the combination of strong, regular, and increasingly frequent contractions along with progressive cervical dilation typically beyond 4 to 6 cm. Occasional mild contractions without cervical change reflect latent labour or prelabour, not active labour. Bloody show alone, without contractions, is a sign that labour may be approaching but is not itself active labour. Braxton Hicks contractions are irregular practice contractions and do not indicate true active labour.
  25. The correct answer is D (A normal three-vessel cord with an apparently complete placenta): A three-vessel cord, consisting of two arteries and one vein, along with a placenta and membranes that appear intact and complete, represent normal, reassuring postpartum findings. This is not an abnormal finding requiring urgent neonatal transfer. The newborn still requires the standard ongoing assessment regardless of this placental finding. A normal placental exam does not indicate a need for maternal transfusion.
  26. The correct answer is A (Confirming she remains low risk, appropriate for intermittent auscultation): Supporting mobility with intermittent auscultation is appropriate as long as the ongoing risk assessment confirms she remains a suitable low-risk candidate for this approach. Refusing outright without considering her actual status would not respect informed choice appropriately. Requiring continuous monitoring for every client regardless of risk does not reflect individualized, evidence-based care. Any plan should be discussed collaboratively with the client rather than decided without her involvement.
  27. The correct answer is C (It reflects normal uterine activity allowing adequate fetal oxygenation): Adequate relaxation between contractions allows uterine blood flow to be restored, supporting ongoing fetal oxygenation, and is a normal and reassuring feature of effective labour. This finding does not indicate ineffective contractions, since normal labour requires periods of full relaxation between contractions. It is not a warning sign of uterine rupture, which would present differently. The presence of a contraction pattern with normal resting tone is consistent with labour having begun, not the opposite.
  28. The correct answer is B (Delaying the bath preserves vernix and supports thermoregulation and bonding): Delaying the newborn's first bath allows the protective vernix to remain on the skin, supports temperature stability, and preserves early skin-to-skin bonding time with the parent. This practice has clear, evidence-based benefits, contrary to having none. Bathing does not need to occur within the first minutes of life and is often better delayed. Delaying the bath is not associated with an increased infection risk and is generally considered protective.
  29. The correct answer is A (A brief physiological lull sometimes occurring near the end of the first stage): A temporary lull in contraction frequency or intensity can occur physiologically near the end of the first stage before the second stage begins, particularly if maternal and fetal status remain reassuring. This does not necessarily mean labour has stopped permanently. Immediate augmentation is not automatically indicated when reassurance is otherwise present and the lull may resolve on its own. Cesarean section would not be the appropriate first response to this reassuring, transient finding.
  30. The correct answer is C (Development of a fetal heart rate abnormality outside the reassuring range): A concerning change in fetal heart rate outside the normal reassuring pattern is a recognized trigger for consultation or transfer of care to an appropriate provider. A client's preference for an upright position or intermittent monitoring reflects normal, appropriate midwifery-led care rather than a red flag. Normal progression from latent into active labour over a reasonable timeframe is an expected part of physiological labour, not a trigger for consultation.
  31. The correct answer is B (Times of key events, assessments performed, and maternal and newborn status): Thorough documentation should capture the timing of key labour and birth events, the assessments performed and their findings, and the ongoing status of both mother and newborn, supporting continuity of care and legal accountability. Recording only the birth weight would omit essential clinical detail. The newborn's name alone provides no clinical information. A vague general statement without specifics would not meet the documentation standard expected of professional midwifery practice.
Case C551

A 24-year-old primigravida at 39 weeks gestation is in active labour at a hospital birth. Her midwife notes the fetal heart rate tracing shows late decelerations with reduced variability following each contraction, while maternal vital signs remain stable and there is no vaginal bleeding.

  1. The correct answer is A (Uteroplacental insufficiency reducing oxygen transfer to the fetus specifically during each contraction): late decelerations that consistently follow a contraction with reduced variability reflect a delay between the peak of uterine pressure and the fetal heart rate response, which is the classic pattern of uteroplacental insufficiency. Cord compression typically produces variable, not late, decelerations that vary in shape and timing. A primary fetal arrhythmia would not correlate so consistently with the contraction pattern, and maternal hyperthermia would more typically produce a baseline tachycardia rather than this late deceleration pattern.
  2. The correct answer is B (Reposition the woman onto her left side and start an intravenous fluid bolus): maternal repositioning off the vena cava together with an intravenous fluid bolus improves uteroplacental blood flow and is the recommended first-line intrauterine resuscitation measure for a concerning fetal heart rate pattern. Discontinuing oxytocin should happen promptly if it is infusing, not only after variability has already disappeared completely. Moving directly to operative delivery skips appropriate conservative measures, and increasing contraction strength would worsen fetal oxygenation rather than improve it.
  3. The correct answer is C (Consult obstetrics urgently and arrange transfer for further fetal assessment): a persistently nonreassuring pattern that fails to improve with intrauterine resuscitation measures falls outside normal midwifery scope and requires prompt obstetric consultation and transfer for further evaluation. Continuing routine monitoring or waiting several hours delays necessary escalation of care. Discharging the woman home would remove essential continuous fetal surveillance during an active concern, which is not appropriate given this presentation.
  4. The correct answer is D (Shoulder dystocia caused by impaction of the anterior shoulder behind the pubic symphysis): the turtle sign, where the fetal head retracts against the perineum after delivery, together with failed gentle traction, is the classic presentation of shoulder dystocia from an impacted anterior shoulder. A nuchal cord would not typically prevent shoulder delivery in this way. Normal restitution does not involve retraction against the perineum, and breech presentation would have been evident well before the head delivered.
  5. The correct answer is D (Call for help, note the time, and guide the woman into McRoberts position with suprapubic pressure): calling for additional help, documenting the time, and beginning with McRoberts maneuver and suprapubic pressure is the recommended first-line, evidence-based approach to shoulder dystocia. Fundal pressure can worsen impaction and increase the risk of uterine rupture or further injury. Performing an episiotomy alone does not resolve a bony obstruction, and asking the woman to push harder without repositioning does not address the underlying impaction.
Case C556

A woman gives birth to a healthy infant at a birth centre following an uncomplicated labour. Approximately fifteen minutes after the placenta delivers, the midwife notices the uterus feels soft and poorly contracted, with continuous bright red vaginal bleeding now estimated at around six hundred millilitres.

  1. The correct answer is C (Uterine atony preventing adequate compression of the spiral arterioles at the placental site): a soft, poorly contracted uterus with ongoing bright red bleeding after placental delivery is the classic presentation of uterine atony, the most common cause of postpartum hemorrhage. A cervical laceration would typically present with a well-contracted uterus and persistent bleeding rather than a boggy uterus. Retained fragments and coagulopathy are less likely explanations given the described uterine tone and are not supported by the information provided in this scenario.
  2. The correct answer is B (Begin fundal massage immediately while calling for a second care provider and IV access): fundal massage stimulates uterine contraction and is the immediate first response to postpartum hemorrhage from suspected atony, alongside calling for help and establishing intravenous access. Waiting several minutes or having the woman ambulate delays essential intervention during active bleeding. Delaying uterotonic administration until the placenta is fully inspected similarly postpones a time-sensitive treatment that should begin without delay in this situation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Perform bimanual uterine compression while arranging urgent transfer to hospital care): when fundal massage and initial uterotonics fail to control bleeding, bimanual compression provides mechanical control of hemorrhage while arrangements are made for urgent transfer to a higher level of care. Discontinuing uterotonics or simply observing would allow ongoing blood loss to continue unchecked. Applying ice packs does not address the underlying atony, and this level of hemorrhage requires more than continued observation alone.
  4. The correct answer is A (A firm, well-contracted uterus together with ongoing steady vaginal bleeding): bleeding that continues despite a firm, well-contracted uterus suggests a source other than atony, such as a genital tract laceration or retained tissue, and should prompt further assessment beyond uterine massage alone. A boggy uterus responding to massage, bleeding decreasing with oxytocin, or a uterus remaining soft despite compression are all still consistent with atony as the primary cause rather than pointing toward an alternative source.
Case C560

During a routine vaginal examination in active labour, the midwife suddenly feels a pulsating loop of umbilical cord lying below the presenting fetal part. The membranes have just ruptured spontaneously, and the fetal heart rate on the monitor has dropped abruptly to eighty beats per minute.

  1. The correct answer is A (Manually elevate the presenting part off the cord while calling for emergency transfer): manually elevating the presenting part relieves pressure on the compressed cord and restores fetal circulation while emergency transfer is arranged, making it the immediate priority in cord prolapse. Rupturing remaining membranes or having the woman walk would not address the cord compression and could worsen the emergency. Documenting and delaying reassessment ignores the urgent, time-critical nature of this presentation.
  2. The correct answer is C (A knee-chest position or exaggerated Sims position with the hips elevated): these positions use gravity to shift the presenting part away from the pelvis and reduce pressure on the prolapsed cord during transport. A supine position with legs flat does not relieve pressure on the cord in the same way, standing upright would increase downward pressure rather than relieve it, and a lithotomy position does not shift the presenting part off the cord as effectively as the recommended positions.
  3. The correct answer is C (Pushing the cord back into the vagina or handling it unnecessarily): manipulating or attempting to replace a prolapsed cord can cause vasospasm and further compromise fetal circulation, so this action should be avoided. Keeping the cord warm and moist, continuing fetal heart rate monitoring, and communicating clearly with the receiving hospital team are all appropriate and recommended actions during ongoing management of a cord prolapse.
  4. The correct answer is C (Refer her for external cephalic version by a qualified provider after discussing risks and benefits): offering referral for external cephalic version after a full discussion of risks and benefits supports informed choice and is an appropriate, evidence-based next step for a woman with a frank breech presentation and no contraindications. Claiming breech always resolves spontaneously is inaccurate, planning a home birth in breech position is outside safe scope, and proceeding straight to induction ignores the presentation and available options entirely.
  5. The correct answer is A (Consultation with obstetrics to discuss planned cesarean birth versus vaginal breech criteria): when external cephalic version is unsuccessful, current Canadian practice generally involves obstetric consultation to discuss the woman's options, including planned cesarean birth or vaginal breech birth under strict criteria. Proceeding to independent home vaginal breech birth without consultation falls outside safe collaborative practice. Transferring care regardless of informed choice, or delaying discussion until labour begins, does not support timely, informed decision making.
Case C565

A woman labouring with a planned trial of labour after cesarean suddenly reports severe, unremitting abdominal pain that persists between contractions rather than easing. The fetal heart rate tracing shows a prolonged deceleration, and the presenting part that was previously palpable is no longer felt on abdominal examination.

  1. The correct answer is A (Uterine rupture occurring at the site of the previous cesarean scar): sudden severe unremitting pain, a prolonged fetal heart rate deceleration, and loss of a previously palpable presenting part in a woman attempting labour after cesarean are classic warning signs of uterine rupture at the prior scar. Normal second stage transition does not present this way, a urinary tract infection would not cause loss of the presenting part, and round ligament pain is typically mild and unrelated to fetal station.
  2. The correct answer is B (Call for emergency assistance and arrange immediate transfer for emergency cesarean birth): suspected uterine rupture is an obstetric emergency requiring immediate emergency assistance and transfer for emergency cesarean birth to protect both maternal and fetal outcomes. Oral analgesia and delayed reassessment do not address a time-critical emergency. Increasing oxytocin could worsen the rupture, and continuing to labour at home ignores the seriousness of these findings entirely.
  3. The correct answer is C (Ongoing intra-abdominal hemorrhage occurring secondary to the ruptured uterus): new hypotension and tachycardia alongside worsening pain in this context most likely reflect ongoing internal bleeding from the ruptured uterus, signaling hemodynamic compromise. A vasovagal response would not typically cause sustained worsening vital sign changes of this severity. These findings are not consistent with normal postpartum recovery, since birth has not yet occurred, and they represent far more than simple anxiety about a changed birth plan.
  4. The correct answer is D (Maternal positioning, oxygen, IV access, and continuous monitoring of vital signs): while awaiting emergency transport, the midwife should prioritize supportive stabilizing measures including positioning, supplemental oxygen, intravenous access, and continuous monitoring of both maternal and fetal status. Detailed documentation should not delay essential clinical intervention. Waiting for a partner to arrive or delaying oxygen administration would both inappropriately postpone time-critical supportive care during this emergency.
Case C569

A primigravida has been in active labour for eight hours with regular contractions occurring roughly every three minutes. On reassessment, her cervix remains at six centimetres of dilation, unchanged from four hours earlier, though the fetal heart rate has stayed reassuring throughout this entire time.

  1. The correct answer is B (Arrest of the active phase, commonly termed failure to progress): a complete lack of cervical change over four hours in a woman who has entered the active phase, despite an adequate contraction pattern, is consistent with arrest of the active phase, also called failure to progress. This differs from protraction, which describes labour advancing more slowly than expected while still making some progress. It is not normal latent phase labour, since active labour has already been established. It is not precipitous labour, which involves unusually rapid progress, and it is not false labour, since true cervical change had occurred earlier before this plateau began.
  2. The correct answer is C (Contraction quality, fetal position, and the adequacy of the pelvis for descent): systematically assessing contraction adequacy, fetal position, and the maternal pelvis addresses the classic contributing factors of failure to progress, often summarized as the powers, the passenger, and the passage. Maternal preference for pain medication, the timing of her last meal, and whether her support person is present are all reasonable considerations in overall care but do not identify the underlying cause of slow labour progress.
  3. The correct answer is B (Encouraging position changes and mobility to support spontaneous fetal rotation): position changes and mobility can help promote rotation of a persistently occiput posterior fetus and are an appropriate, low-risk midwifery intervention when contractions are already adequate. Recommending cesarean birth immediately skips reasonable conservative measures. Increasing oxytocin when contractions are already adequate is not indicated, and strict supine bed rest can actually hinder rather than support fetal rotation in this situation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Prolonged pregnancy beyond forty-one to forty-two weeks with a normal assessment): prolonged pregnancy beyond forty-one to forty-two weeks is a recognized, evidence-based indication for induction of labour, reflecting the increasing risks associated with continued pregnancy at this gestation. Maternal request alone without a medical indication does not meet standard criteria for induction within midwifery scope. An occiput posterior position alone that remains otherwise reassuring, and scheduling around a weekend preference, are not accepted clinical indications for induction.
  5. The correct answer is B (Amniotomy or oxytocin augmentation after carefully reassessing the current contraction pattern): when contractions are adequate but infrequent and cervical progress has slowed despite conservative measures like ambulation and hydration, amniotomy or oxytocin augmentation after reassessment is an appropriate next step. Moving straight to cesarean birth without attempting augmentation skips a reasonable intermediate option. Discontinuing monitoring or sedating the woman does not address the underlying slow progress and could reduce important ongoing surveillance.
  6. The correct answer is A (Arrange immediate emergency transfer to hospital with continuous monitoring en route): painful vaginal bleeding with a tense, persistently tender uterus between contractions is concerning for placental abruption, which requires immediate emergency transfer with continuous monitoring during transport. Continuing the home birth plan or waiting to see if bleeding slows ignores a potentially life-threatening complication. Performing a vaginal examination first could worsen bleeding if there is an undiagnosed placental abnormality and should not delay urgent transfer.
  7. The correct answer is A (Umbilical cord compression occurring specifically during each uterine contraction): variable decelerations that drop sharply with contractions and recover quickly are classically caused by transient umbilical cord compression during each contraction. Chronic uteroplacental insufficiency more typically produces late decelerations rather than this variable pattern. Maternal fever would more likely cause a baseline tachycardia, and fetal anemia from vasa previa is a rare cause not suggested by this specific tracing pattern.
  8. The correct answer is D (Maternal repositioning, intravenous fluids, oxygen, and discontinuing oxytocin if it is infusing): this combination of measures addresses the most common reversible contributors to fetal hypoxia and represents standard first-line intrauterine resuscitation for a nonreassuring fetal heart rate pattern. Performing an amniotomy regardless of membrane status does not directly improve fetal oxygenation. Increasing oxytocin would worsen contractions and fetal stress, and encouraging active pushing is not appropriate before the second stage or during a concerning pattern.
  9. The correct answer is D (Internal rotational maneuvers or delivery of the fetal posterior arm): when McRoberts maneuver and suprapubic pressure fail to resolve shoulder dystocia, internal rotational maneuvers or delivery of the posterior arm are the recommended next steps in the standard escalation sequence. Strong fundal pressure is not recommended and can increase the risk of injury. Proceeding to bedside cesarean birth is not feasible once the head has delivered, and pulling firmly on the head risks brachial plexus injury.
  10. The correct answer is A (Suspected fetal macrosomia occurring in the context of gestational diabetes): fetal macrosomia, particularly in the setting of gestational diabetes, is one of the most strongly recognized risk factors for shoulder dystocia due to disproportionate shoulder growth relative to head size. A previous uncomplicated birth of an average-sized infant is reassuring rather than a risk factor. Multiparity alone without other risk factors, and a short second stage under thirty minutes, are not independently strong predictors of shoulder dystocia.
  11. The correct answer is A (Artificial rupture of membranes performed with an unengaged presenting part): rupturing membranes when the presenting part is not yet engaged allows fluid and cord to escape past the presenting part, creating significant risk for cord prolapse. Spontaneous rupture with a well-applied vertex, a normal longitudinal lie, or rupture after full dilation with an engaged head are all situations with a low risk of cord prolapse compared to an unengaged presenting part at rupture.
  12. The correct answer is B (Frank breech presentation with an adequate maternal pelvis and no other risk factors): a frank breech presentation with an adequate pelvis and no additional risk factors is the type of presentation most consistent with recognized criteria supporting consideration of planned vaginal breech birth. Footling breech with a large estimated fetal weight, a low-lying placenta, or a hyperextended fetal neck are all factors that increase risk and would generally favour recommending planned cesarean birth instead.
  13. The correct answer is D (Sudden loss of a previously effective contraction pattern with severe constant pain): a sudden change from an established, effective contraction pattern to constant severe pain is a classic and specific warning sign of uterine rupture, reflecting disruption of the uterine wall. Contractions steadily increasing in strength and frequency represent normal labour progress. Mild discomfort resolving between contractions and an increasing urge to push are both typical, expected features of a normally advancing labour.
  14. The correct answer is B (Amniotic fluid embolism requiring immediate resuscitation and emergency transfer): sudden severe respiratory distress, profound hypotension, and unresponsiveness with no preceding bleeding or fever are classic features suggesting amniotic fluid embolism, a rare but life-threatening obstetric emergency requiring immediate resuscitation and transfer. Normal transition anxiety, mild hypoglycemia, and expected fatigue would not produce this degree of acute cardiovascular and neurological collapse.
  15. The correct answer is B (Prolonged labour that required oxytocin augmentation for several hours before birth): prolonged labour, particularly when it requires extended oxytocin augmentation, is a well-recognized risk factor for uterine atony and subsequent postpartum hemorrhage due to uterine muscle fatigue. A spontaneous birth after a short first stage, an uncomplicated singleton pregnancy, and delayed cord clamping are not independently associated with an increased risk of postpartum hemorrhage in the way prolonged augmented labour is.
  16. The correct answer is B (Oxytocin administered intramuscularly or intravenously as the first-line uterotonic agent): oxytocin is the first-line uterotonic recommended in Canadian practice for both prevention and initial treatment of postpartum hemorrhage related to uterine atony, given its rapid onset and established safety profile. Acetaminophen addresses pain and fever, not uterine tone. Prophylactic antibiotics are not a routine component of active management of the third stage, and antihypertensive medication does not address bleeding from atony.
  17. The correct answer is A (A nonreassuring fetal heart rate pattern that fails to resolve with resuscitative measures): a persistently nonreassuring fetal heart rate pattern that does not improve with resuscitative measures falls outside the scope of ongoing home birth care and requires urgent transfer for further assessment and management. A second stage lasting forty-five minutes with reassurance, a request for extra support, or clear fluid with a reassuring fetal heart rate are all situations that do not, by themselves, indicate an urgent need for transfer.
Case C586

A 30-year-old G2P1 at 39 weeks gestation is in active labour at a birth centre, dilated 6 cm. The fetal heart rate baseline is 145 bpm with moderate variability. The midwife notes recurrent variable decelerations to 90 bpm lasting 45 seconds, recovering promptly between contractions.

  1. The correct answer is A (Umbilical cord compression during contractions): Variable decelerations that are abrupt in onset, vary in shape and depth, and recover promptly are the classic pattern associated with intermittent umbilical cord compression during uterine contractions or fetal movement. The reassuring baseline of 145 bpm and moderate variability support ongoing adequate fetal oxygenation despite these episodes. Uteroplacental insufficiency typically produces late decelerations rather than variable ones. Prompt recovery between contractions is reassuring and distinguishes this from a more concerning pattern.
  2. The correct answer is C (Maternal position change to relieve cord compression): The first-line, non-invasive intervention for variable decelerations thought to reflect cord compression is a change in maternal position, such as lateral positioning, to shift fetal position relative to the cord. This is simple, low-risk, and often effective before considering further intervention. Immediate transfer is not warranted while the baseline and variability remain reassuring. Oxytocin would worsen the situation by increasing contraction frequency and cord compression.
  3. The correct answer is B (Consult obstetrics and arrange transfer for further assessment): A slow return to baseline combined with reduced variability represents a change toward a less reassuring pattern that falls outside normal midwifery scope for ongoing sole management. Consultation with obstetrics and arranging transfer allows for further assessment and access to additional monitoring or intervention if needed. Continuing to watch without escalating risks delayed recognition of fetal compromise. Reassurance that variable decelerations are always benign is inaccurate given this evolving pattern.
  4. The correct answer is B (Manually elevate the presenting part off the cord and call for emergency transfer): Umbilical cord prolapse with a pulsating cord is an obstetric emergency requiring immediate manual elevation of the presenting part to relieve cord compression while arranging urgent emergency transfer. Attempting to replace the cord above the presenting part is not recommended and can increase cord trauma or compression. Having the client walk risks further cord compression and delay. Repeating examination wastes critical time in a time-sensitive emergency.
  5. The correct answer is A (Knee chest position or exaggerated Sims position with hips elevated): These positions use gravity to shift the presenting part away from the pelvis, relieving pressure on the prolapsed cord while awaiting emergency transfer and definitive delivery. Supine positioning with legs flat does not relieve pressure on the cord and may worsen compression. Left lateral with the head elevated does not provide the same gravitational relief. Standing upright would increase pressure on the cord rather than relieve it.
Case C591

A primigravida at term is pushing in second stage. After the fetal head delivers, it retracts firmly against the perineum, resembling a turtle sign, and gentle traction fails to deliver the anterior shoulder.

  1. The correct answer is C (Shoulder dystocia with impaction of the anterior shoulder): The turtle sign, where the fetal head retracts against the perineum after delivery, combined with failed gentle traction, is the classic presentation of shoulder dystocia from impaction of the anterior shoulder behind the pubic symphysis. Normal restitution does not involve retraction against the perineum. Persistent occiput posterior would present differently prior to head delivery. A nuchal cord alone would not cause this retraction pattern after the head is already born.
  2. The correct answer is D (McRoberts maneuver with suprapubic pressure): McRoberts positioning combined with suprapubic pressure is the recommended first-line maneuver for shoulder dystocia, as it flattens the sacral promontory and rotates the pubic symphysis to help free the impacted shoulder. Fundal pressure is contraindicated as it can worsen impaction and increase risk of uterine rupture. Cesarean preparation is not feasible once the head has already delivered. The Zavanelli maneuver is reserved for extreme, rare circumstances after other maneuvers fail.
  3. The correct answer is C (Internal rotation maneuvers to rotate the posterior shoulder): When McRoberts and suprapubic pressure fail, internal maneuvers such as rotating the posterior shoulder or delivering the posterior arm are the recommended next steps to resolve the dystocia. Increased fundal pressure remains contraindicated regardless of prior maneuvers attempted. An episiotomy alone without internal maneuvers does not address the bony impaction causing the dystocia. Simply repeating McRoberts without adding further technique delays resolution of an emergency.
  4. The correct answer is D (Brachial plexus injury and clavicular fracture on the affected side): Shoulder dystocia carries a recognized risk of neonatal brachial plexus injury and clavicular fracture from the forces applied during resolution, so the newborn examination should specifically assess arm movement, symmetry, and the clavicles. Bilateral hip dysplasia is more associated with breech presentation, not shoulder dystocia. Cephalohematoma relates to instrumental delivery pressure on the skull, not shoulder impaction. Diaphragmatic hernia is a congenital anomaly unrelated to shoulder dystocia mechanics.
  5. The correct answer is C (Consult obstetrics regarding mode of birth and possible transfer of care): A confirmed breech presentation at the onset of labour falls outside normal midwifery scope for planned vaginal breech birth in most settings and requires consultation with obstetrics to discuss safe mode of birth. Proceeding with a planned home birth without this consultation would be inappropriate given established risk. Attempting version in active labour with an engaged breech is not appropriate. Vaginal breech birth requires specific consultation given the associated risks.
  6. The correct answer is C (External cephalic version performed at a facility with cesarean capability): When breech presentation is identified before labour, offering external cephalic version at a facility equipped for emergency cesarean is standard evidence-based practice to attempt to achieve a cephalic presentation and reduce cesarean risk. Scheduling cesarean without discussing version first removes an appropriate option from the client. Moxibustion has limited evidence and should not be presented as the sole recommended intervention. Expectant management without offering version is not consistent with current guidelines.
  7. The correct answer is C (Prepare for urgent delivery given the concern for fetal compromise): A fetal heart rate that remains low after release of external pressure during version raises significant concern for fetal compromise such as cord entanglement or placental abruption, warranting preparation for urgent delivery. Reattempting the version immediately would be unsafe given the concerning fetal status. Discharging the client home is inappropriate in the setting of a non-reassuring fetal heart rate. Repeating the version after a rest period delays necessary urgent assessment and action.
Case C598

Immediately after a vaginal birth of a 4200 gram infant following a prolonged second stage, the client's uterus feels soft and boggy on fundal palpation. Bright red blood is pooling steadily beneath her, with an estimated blood loss already reaching 600 mL.

  1. The correct answer is D (Uterine atony related to overdistension from macrosomia): A soft, boggy uterus with ongoing bright red bleeding after birth of a large infant following a prolonged second stage is most consistent with uterine atony, as overdistension from macrosomia impairs effective uterine contraction. Retained products from a prior pregnancy is not a relevant mechanism here. Coagulopathy typically presents with more diffuse bleeding and is less likely as a first consideration. A cervical laceration would typically present with a firm uterus and bright red bleeding despite good tone.
  2. The correct answer is D (Bimanual uterine massage while calling for assistance and oxytocin): For suspected uterine atony, immediate bimanual uterine massage combined with calling for help and administering a uterotonic such as oxytocin is the appropriate first-line management to restore uterine tone and control bleeding. Manual removal of the placenta is not indicated if the placenta has already delivered and atony is the cause. A Bakri balloon is a later intervention after uterotonics have been tried. Rapid transfer without attempting bedside intervention delays treatable first-line management.
  3. The correct answer is C (Administer a second uterotonic and arrange emergency transfer to hospital): When first-line measures fail to control ongoing significant hemorrhage, escalating to a second uterotonic agent while arranging emergency transfer for further management is the appropriate next step. Waiting further delays necessary escalation of care in an ongoing emergency. Discontinuing oxytocin would remove a helpful uterotonic already in use. Delaying transfer for placental confirmation is inappropriate when maternal hemorrhage is the immediate priority.
  4. The correct answer is A (Uterine rupture requiring immediate emergency transfer): Sudden severe tearing abdominal pain persisting between contractions, a change in contraction pattern, new bleeding, and difficulty locating fetal heart tones together are classic warning signs of uterine rupture, an obstetric emergency requiring immediate transfer. Normal transition pain does not persist between contractions in this severe, tearing quality. Round ligament pain is typically milder and unrelated to labour progress changes. Braxton Hicks contractions do not cause this constellation of severe findings.
  5. The correct answer is C (Previous classical cesarean incision through the uterine fundus): A classical cesarean incision through the muscular fundus carries a substantially higher risk of uterine rupture during subsequent labour compared to a low transverse incision, making trial of labour after this type of scar contraindicated in most circumstances. A previous low transverse incision carries a much lower rupture risk. A prior successful vaginal birth after cesarean is actually reassuring, not a risk factor. Spontaneous labour onset without induction agents is generally lower risk than induced or augmented labour.
Case C603

A client who gave birth thirty minutes ago suddenly becomes pale, diaphoretic, and confused while breastfeeding. Her blood pressure is 78/48 mmHg and heart rate is 128 bpm. The uterus is firm and vaginal bleeding appears minimal.

  1. The correct answer is D (Concealed hemorrhage such as a vaginal or broad ligament hematoma): Hypotension, tachycardia, and confusion with a firm uterus and minimal visible bleeding point toward a concealed source of blood loss such as a vaginal or broad ligament hematoma, which can accumulate significant volume internally. A simple vasovagal response would not typically cause sustained hypotension and confusion. This presentation is not consistent with normal physiologic postpartum changes. An anxiety reaction would not explain the objective hypotension and tachycardia present.
  2. The correct answer is B (Call for emergency assistance, establish IV access, and initiate fluid resuscitation): Signs of hemodynamic instability such as hypotension, tachycardia, and confusion require immediate emergency response including calling for help, establishing IV access, and beginning fluid resuscitation while the underlying cause is identified. Reassuring the client and continuing routine activity ignores objective signs of shock. Simple documentation and delayed reassessment risks deterioration. Oral fluids alone are inadequate for suspected significant blood loss with hemodynamic compromise.
  3. The correct answer is B (Urgent transfer to hospital for surgical evaluation and possible evacuation): A tense, expanding vulvar hematoma associated with hemodynamic instability requires urgent transfer for surgical assessment and possible evacuation, as conservative measures alone are unlikely to control ongoing concealed bleeding. A warm compress does not address an expanding hematoma and delays necessary care. Discharging the client home is unsafe given the maternal instability already present. Vulvar hematomas of this size are not reliably self-limiting and require evaluation.
  4. The correct answer is B (Gestational age advancing beyond 41 weeks with reassuring fetal surveillance): Post-term pregnancy beyond 41 weeks is a recognized indication for induction of labour to reduce risks associated with prolonged pregnancy, even when fetal surveillance remains reassuring, since the risk of stillbirth and other complications rises with advancing gestation. Maternal request based on convenience alone is not an accepted medical indication. A reassuring biophysical profile at exactly 40 weeks does not itself indicate a need for induction. Normal fluid volume alone does not constitute an indication for induction.
  5. The correct answer is A (Discontinue the oxytocin infusion and reposition the client): Uterine tachysystole with contractions occurring too frequently and lasting too long, combined with late decelerations suggesting fetal compromise, requires immediately stopping the oxytocin infusion and repositioning the client to reduce uterine activity and improve placental perfusion. Increasing the rate would worsen both the tachysystole and fetal status. Continuing without change risks ongoing fetal compromise. An additional bolus would further exacerbate the excessive uterine activity already present.
  6. The correct answer is D (Fetal tachycardia secondary to maternal intrapartum infection): Rising fetal heart rate baseline with reduced variability in the setting of maternal fever and prolonged rupture of membranes strongly suggests intrapartum infection or chorioamnionitis affecting the fetus. This is not a normal or expected labour finding. Dehydration alone would not typically produce this combination of maternal fever and rising fetal tachycardia together. Reduced variability near full dilation without other findings would not explain the fever and rising baseline seen here.
  7. The correct answer is A (Consult obstetrics and arrange transfer to hospital for further management): Suspected chorioamnionitis with a concerning fetal heart rate pattern requires consultation with obstetrics and transfer to hospital for further assessment, monitoring, and treatment such as antibiotics, as this falls outside independent midwifery management. Continuing home labour support risks delayed treatment of infection affecting both mother and fetus. Oral antipyretics alone do not address the underlying infection or fetal risk. Prolonged rupture of membranes with fever and fetal compromise is not an expected or low-risk finding requiring no action.
  8. The correct answer is A (Fetal bradycardia lasting greater than ten minutes with minimal variability): A deceleration lasting ten minutes or longer is reclassified as fetal bradycardia rather than a prolonged deceleration, and when combined with minimal variability this is a highly concerning pattern indicating significant fetal compromise requiring urgent evaluation and action. Early decelerations mirroring contractions reflect benign head compression and are considered reassuring. Accelerations of this magnitude are a reassuring sign of fetal wellbeing. Moderate variability with a stable normal baseline is a reassuring finding, not one of concern.
Case C611

A G3P2 client at term has been pushing for three hours with adequate contractions and a fully dilated cervix. The fetal head remains at station 0 despite maternal effort, and the midwife suspects the fetal position is occiput posterior.

  1. The correct answer is D (Palpation of the posterior fontanelle toward the maternal sacrum): In occiput posterior position, the fetal occiput and posterior fontanelle are directed toward the maternal sacrum, which can be confirmed on vaginal examination. Palpation of the anterior fontanelle at the symphysis would suggest a deflexed or different position, not classic occiput posterior. A transverse sagittal suture suggests occiput transverse position rather than posterior. An easily palpable chin anteriorly would suggest a face presentation, not occiput posterior.
  2. The correct answer is B (Hands and knees or asymmetric positions such as lunges): Hands and knees positioning and asymmetric maternal positions such as lunges use gravity and pelvic asymmetry to encourage rotation of a persistent occiput posterior fetus and are recommended non-invasive strategies. Strict supine positioning does not facilitate rotation and may worsen comfort and progress. Continuous lithotomy positioning restricts pelvic mobility needed for rotation. Lying flat with legs in stirrups similarly limits the pelvic movement helpful for encouraging rotation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Consult obstetrics regarding arrest of descent and possible transfer): Arrest of descent despite adequate contractions and prolonged active pushing falls outside normal midwifery scope for continued independent management and warrants obstetric consultation regarding further options, including possible transfer for operative delivery. Continuing to push indefinitely without escalation risks maternal and fetal complications. Fundal pressure is not recommended and does not address the underlying cause of arrested descent. Increasing intake and brief rest alone does not address a true arrest of descent requiring evaluation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Continue providing continuity of care and emotional support during transfer): Even when obstetric consultation leads to transfer of care, the midwife's ongoing role includes continuity of care, communication, and emotional support for the client throughout the transfer and subsequent care. Withdrawing from care entirely abandons the therapeutic relationship the client has built with her midwife. Insisting on continuing a home birth despite a clear consult recommendation is inappropriate and unsafe. Attempting an operative vaginal delivery independently is outside midwifery scope of practice.
  5. The correct answer is C (Advise immediate transfer for assessment given risk of preterm labour): Regular contractions with increasing pelvic pressure at 34 weeks, especially with a history of precipitous labours, warrants prompt assessment for preterm labour given the risk of rapid progression and the need for interventions such as antenatal corticosteroids if appropriate. Reassuring her that history means nothing changes ignores a genuine risk factor for rapid preterm birth. Advising her to remain home for another day risks an unattended or precipitous preterm birth. A warm bath does not reliably slow true preterm labour and delays necessary assessment.
  6. The correct answer is C (Time from head delivery to body delivery and maneuvers performed in sequence): Thorough documentation of a shoulder dystocia event should include the head-to-body delivery interval and the specific sequence of maneuvers performed, as this information is essential for clinical review and any subsequent newborn assessment. Recording only Apgar scores omits critical details of the emergency management performed. A general note of an uncomplicated delivery misrepresents an event that required active management. Pain scores alone do not capture the clinically relevant details of the emergency.
Case C617

A client at 39 weeks with an uncomplicated pregnancy arrives at the birth centre in active labour. Two hours after admission, she suddenly complains of a sharp stabbing sensation and reports feeling as though something moved. Contractions stop abruptly and the fetal heart rate becomes undetectable by Doppler.

  1. The correct answer is D (Uterine rupture with possible fetal expulsion into the abdominal cavity): A sudden sharp pain, sensation of movement, abrupt cessation of contractions, and loss of fetal heart tones together suggest uterine rupture with possible expulsion of the fetus into the abdominal cavity, a catastrophic obstetric emergency. A normal transition phase does not cause abrupt cessation of contractions with loss of fetal heart tones. Fetal descent alone would not cause a sudden inability to locate heart tones combined with contraction cessation. This presentation is far too specific and severe to be explained by maternal anxiety alone.
  2. The correct answer is D (Call emergency medical services for immediate transfer while beginning resuscitation measures): Suspected uterine rupture with loss of fetal heart tones is a life-threatening emergency for both mother and fetus, requiring immediate activation of emergency services alongside any indicated resuscitation measures while awaiting transport. Continuing to search for heart tones alone delays necessary emergency response. Simply reassuring the client and encouraging rest ignores the severity of the situation. Waiting for contractions to resume spontaneously is inappropriate given the suspected catastrophic emergency.
  3. The correct answer is B (Signs of hypovolemic shock with a rapidly distending, tender abdomen): Uterine rupture typically causes significant intra-abdominal or intraperitoneal bleeding, leading to signs of hypovolemic shock along with a rapidly distending and increasingly tender abdomen as blood accumulates. A firm, well-contracted fundus with minimal discomfort would be reassuring and inconsistent with rupture. Stable vital signs with a soft, non-tender abdomen would not support this diagnosis. Continued strong regular contractions would be unlikely given the abrupt cessation already described in this scenario.
  4. The correct answer is A (Call for emergency transport, begin resuscitation, and continue supportive care until arrival): Suspected amniotic fluid embolism is a rare but catastrophic maternal emergency requiring immediate activation of emergency services, initiation of resuscitation measures such as airway support and circulatory support, and continued supportive care until advanced care arrives. Delaying the call for help risks maternal death from a rapidly progressing emergency. Attempting to manage this entirely at home is unsafe given the severity and resources required. Focusing solely on fetal monitoring neglects the immediate life-threatening maternal emergency taking priority.
Case C621

A midwife attends a planned home birth for a 30-year-old multiparous client at term. Immediately after the fetal head delivers, it retracts firmly against the perineum, the chin retracts, and gentle downward traction fails to bring the anterior shoulder under the pubic bone.

  1. The correct answer is A (The turtle sign, with the head retracting tightly against the perineum): The turtle sign, tight retraction of the head against the perineum with failure of restitution, is the classic clinical sign of shoulder dystocia. It signals that the anterior shoulder has become impacted behind the pubic symphysis. Recognizing it promptly allows the midwife to call for help and begin an obstetric maneuver sequence without wasting time on ineffective traction. Delayed recognition increases the risk of fetal hypoxia and brachial plexus injury.
  2. The correct answer is D (Call for additional help and emergency transport, and note the time): The first priority is calling for additional help, including emergency transport for a home birth, and noting the time of head delivery so elapsed time can be tracked. Fundal pressure is contraindicated because it can further impact the shoulder and increase the risk of uterine rupture. Episiotomy does not relieve a bony obstruction and is not a first step. Cutting the cord too early removes a potential source of fetal oxygenation before the shoulders are free.
  3. The correct answer is D (Hyperflex both maternal hips sharply up toward her abdomen): McRoberts maneuver involves sharply hyperflexing both maternal hips toward the abdomen, which straightens the sacrum relative to the lumbar spine and rotates the symphysis pubis cephalad. This often frees the impacted anterior shoulder without any internal manipulation. Side-lying, squatting, or leg extension do not produce the same pelvic rotation and are not the recommended first maneuver for shoulder dystocia.
  4. The correct answer is D (Rupture of membranes occurring with an unengaged fetal presenting part): Cord prolapse most often occurs when membranes rupture while the presenting part is unengaged or poorly applied to the cervix, leaving space for the cord to slip past it. A well applied head, an uncomplicated term singleton, and a short healthy first stage are all reassuring features rather than risk factors. Malpresentation, polyhydramnios, and a high station at rupture are the classic warning signs to assess.
Case C625

A client at 40 weeks gestation is in active labour in hospital with continuous fetal monitoring. The tracing shows a baseline of 130 beats per minute with recurrent decelerations that begin after the peak of each contraction and return to baseline only after the contraction has ended.

  1. The correct answer is C (Late decelerations, suggesting possible uteroplacental insufficiency at present): Decelerations that begin after the contraction peak and resolve after the contraction ends are late decelerations, which reflect a delayed hypoxic response and raise concern for uteroplacental insufficiency. Early decelerations mirror the contraction and reflect benign head compression. Variable decelerations have an abrupt onset unrelated to contraction timing. A sinusoidal pattern has a smooth undulating waveform and a different underlying mechanism entirely.
  2. The correct answer is B (Reposition the client to left lateral and give an intravenous fluid bolus): Repositioning to the left lateral position relieves aortocaval compression and improves uteroplacential blood flow, and an intravenous fluid bolus can improve maternal circulating volume and placental perfusion. Ambulation without fluids does not address the suspected hypoxic mechanism. Increasing oxytocin would worsen uterine activity and further compromise oxygen delivery. Amniotomy does not correct the underlying decelerations and adds an unnecessary intervention at this point.
  3. The correct answer is A (Consult obstetrics urgently regarding the possible need for expedited delivery): Persistent and worsening late decelerations despite corrective measures indicate ongoing fetal compromise that is outside normal midwifery scope, so urgent obstetric consultation regarding expedited delivery is required. Continuing routine monitoring or waiting an hour delays necessary escalation. Switching to intermittent auscultation reduces the information available exactly when continuous data is most needed. Progress in labour does not override a worsening fetal heart rate pattern.
  4. The correct answer is C (A previous classical vertical incision made through the uterine corpus): A previous classical vertical incision involves the contractile upper uterine segment and carries a substantially higher rupture risk in labour, so vaginal birth after caesarean is contraindicated with this history. A single low transverse incision carries a much lower baseline risk and is generally supported for trial of labour. A prior successful vaginal birth after caesarean actually lowers subsequent risk. A well healed low transverse scar from two years ago is reassuring, not concerning.
Case C629

Ten minutes after a home birth, a 33-year-old multiparous client has delivered the placenta. The midwife notes continuous bright red vaginal bleeding, a soft boggy uterine fundus above the umbilicus, and an estimated blood loss already approaching six hundred millilitres.

  1. The correct answer is C (Uterine atony, which remains the most common cause of postpartum hemorrhage): A soft, boggy fundus with ongoing bleeding after placental delivery is the classic presentation of uterine atony, which is the most common cause of postpartum hemorrhage. A cervical laceration typically presents with a firm, well contracted uterus despite ongoing bleeding. Retained fragments would be suspected if the placenta was inspected and found incomplete, which is not described here. An inherited coagulopathy is a much less common cause and is not suggested by this scenario.
  2. The correct answer is B (Perform firm bimanual uterine compression while also massaging the fundus): Firm bimanual compression combined with fundal massage stimulates uterine contraction and directly addresses atony while other measures are organized. Withholding uterotonic medication delays effective treatment of the most likely cause. Standing and walking increases the risk of syncope during active hemorrhage and does not treat atony. Ice packs alone do not stimulate uterine contraction and are not an adequate primary intervention.
  3. The correct answer is B (Ergometrine, given intramuscularly to produce further uterine contraction): When oxytocin alone is insufficient, ergometrine given intramuscularly is an appropriate second line uterotonic that produces sustained uterine contraction, provided the client has no hypertensive disorder. A sedative does not address the bleeding source. An antiemetic without an additional uterotonic leaves the atony untreated. Local anesthetic has no role in managing hemorrhage from uterine atony.
  4. The correct answer is A (Activate emergency transfer to hospital without any further delay at all): Ongoing hemorrhage with new maternal tachycardia signals hemodynamic compromise that exceeds what can be safely managed at home, so immediate emergency transfer to hospital is the priority. Continuing home management and reassessing later delays access to blood products and surgical management. Discontinuing intravenous access removes a route needed for fluid and medication administration during a hemorrhage. Waiting for a family physician introduces unnecessary delay when obstetric and emergency services are needed urgently.
  5. The correct answer is A (Refer for obstetric consultation, since breech birth needs careful hospital planning): Breech presentation falls outside normal scope for planned home birth in most Canadian jurisdictions, so the appropriate step is obstetric consultation to discuss options such as external cephalic version, planned caesarean, or a carefully selected hospital vaginal breech birth.
