Proceeding without consultation bypasses necessary shared decision making. Home breech birth does not carry equivalent risk to cephalic birth. Vaginal breech birth can be appropriate in a hospital setting with the right criteria, so it is not never appropriate.
  6. The correct answer is D (Placenta previa that was confirmed on her most recent ultrasound scan): Placenta previa is an absolute contraindication to external cephalic version because manipulation of the fetus over a low lying placenta risks severe hemorrhage. A normal fluid volume and a reassuring non-stress test are actually reassuring prerequisites that support attempting the procedure safely. Maternal request to attempt the procedure is appropriate informed choice, not a contraindication in itself.
  7. The correct answer is B (Amniotic fluid embolism, treated as an urgent differential diagnosis here): Sudden collapse immediately following rupture of membranes in labour should raise urgent suspicion for amniotic fluid embolism, a rare but life threatening event requiring immediate resuscitation and emergency transfer. Treating it as a simple vasovagal episode, exhaustion, or a benign positional blood pressure drop would delay recognition of a true obstetric emergency and risk further maternal deterioration.
  8. The correct answer is B (Arrest of dilation, representing an abnormal active phase labour pattern): No cervical change over four hours despite adequate uterine activity in the active phase meets criteria for arrest of dilation, an abnormal labour pattern warranting assessment of contraction strength, fetal position, and pelvic adequacy. This is not latent phase behaviour, since six centimetres is within the active phase. It is the opposite of precipitous labour, and it is not a finding that can be left unassessed or undocumented.
Case C637

During a planned home birth, a client's membranes rupture spontaneously while the fetal head remains high and unengaged. The midwife performs a vaginal examination and feels a pulsating loop of umbilical cord below the presenting part.

  1. The correct answer is C (Overt umbilical cord prolapse, which represents a true obstetric emergency): A pulsating loop of cord palpable below the presenting part after rupture of membranes confirms overt umbilical cord prolapse, an obstetric emergency because cord compression between the presenting part and the pelvis can rapidly cause fetal hypoxia. This is not a nuchal cord finding, is not clinically insignificant, and is not an expected or benign occurrence after rupture of membranes.
  2. The correct answer is B (Knee chest position, or alternatively an exaggerated Sims position): Knee chest position, or an exaggerated Sims position on the left side with hips elevated, uses gravity to shift the presenting part away from the cord and reduce compression during transport. Supine positioning can worsen compression and cause aortocaval compression. Semi sitting or standing positions would allow the presenting part to settle further onto the cord rather than relieving pressure.
  3. The correct answer is B (Manually elevate the presenting part off the cord and call emergency services): The midwife should keep gloved fingers in the vagina to manually elevate the presenting part off the cord, reducing compression, while emergency services are called immediately for urgent transfer to hospital for delivery. Pushing the cord back in is not recommended and risks further compromise. Delaying the emergency call wastes critical time. Clamping and cutting the cord while the fetus remains undelivered would eliminate fetal oxygenation entirely.
  4. The correct answer is B (When the placenta has still not delivered within about thirty minutes): With active management of the third stage, a placenta that has not delivered within about thirty minutes is generally considered retained and warrants further assessment and consultation regarding management. Five minutes is too short a window to diagnose a retained placenta under active management. Twenty-four hours is far beyond a safe threshold given the ongoing bleeding risk. Assessment should not wait for heavy bleeding to appear before the time based threshold is applied.
Case C641

A client attempting vaginal birth after a previous low transverse caesarean is in active labour in hospital when she suddenly reports severe, constant abdominal pain that does not ease between contractions, and the fetal heart rate tracing shows a prolonged deceleration.

  1. The correct answer is A (Uterine rupture occurring along the site of her previous caesarean scar): Sudden severe constant abdominal pain that persists between contractions, combined with a prolonged fetal heart rate deceleration in a client attempting vaginal birth after caesarean, is a classic presentation of uterine rupture along the scar. Normal transition pain still eases between contractions. A urinary tract infection or round ligament pain would not typically present with this combination of constant pain and acute fetal heart rate change.
  2. The correct answer is C (Loss of previously confirmed fetal station, or newly palpable fetal parts): Loss of fetal station on vaginal examination, or fetal parts becoming palpable through the abdominal wall, suggests the fetus has moved through a uterine defect and points toward rupture rather than abruption. A board-like tender uterus and dark bleeding with a firm uterus are more typical of placental abruption. A reassuring unchanged fetal heart rate would not fit either emergency and contradicts the scenario already described.
  3. The correct answer is D (Urgent obstetric consultation with preparation for an emergency caesarean birth): Suspected uterine rupture is a life threatening obstetric and fetal emergency requiring immediate obstetric consultation and rapid preparation for emergency caesarean birth. Continued expectant monitoring risks fetal death and maternal hemorrhage. Oxytocin would worsen uterine trauma and is contraindicated. Discharge is never appropriate when this diagnosis is suspected, regardless of any temporary easing of pain.
  4. The correct answer is D (Her prior low transverse caesarean combined with this current attempted VBAC): A previous caesarean scar, even a low transverse one, carries an inherent though small risk of rupture during a subsequent trial of labour, which is the relevant risk factor here. A pregnancy with no prior uterine surgery would carry only the baseline population risk. An earlier uncomplicated vaginal birth and consistent prenatal attendance are protective or neutral factors, not risks for uterine rupture.
  5. The correct answer is C (Trauma, such as an unrecognized cervical or vaginal laceration): A firm, well contracted uterus with ongoing brisk bleeding points away from atony and toward a genital tract laceration, the trauma category of the 4 T's framework, requiring careful inspection of the cervix and vagina under adequate light. Tone problems present with a soft boggy uterus, which is not described here. Tissue and thrombin causes are less likely as the first suspicion when the uterus is already firm and well contracted.
  6. The correct answer is D (Amniotic fluid embolism, a rare and life threatening obstetric emergency): Sudden respiratory distress, cyanosis, hypotension, loss of consciousness, and coagulopathy occurring together in labour is the classic and life threatening presentation of amniotic fluid embolism. An anxiety attack, mild dehydration, or a vasovagal response would not produce cardiovascular collapse and disseminated coagulopathy together in this way, and none explains the full clinical picture described.
  7. The correct answer is B (Call the emergency response team immediately and begin full resuscitation): Amniotic fluid embolism requires an immediate multidisciplinary emergency response, including airway management, cardiovascular support, and preparation for possible perimortem caesarean if maternal status deteriorates further. Waiting to see if symptoms resolve delays lifesaving intervention. A mild analgesic and routine monitoring, or a simple position change, are entirely inadequate responses to a maternal cardiovascular collapse of this severity.
  8. The correct answer is B (An estimated fetal weight that falls within a normal range for gestation): An estimated fetal weight within the normal range, along with an adequate maternal pelvis and a flexed fetal head, are among the key criteria supporting a trial of planned vaginal breech birth in a hospital setting. A prior unrelated caesarean is not itself a supportive criterion. A significantly hyperextended head or a markedly large estimated fetal weight are both recognized contraindications rather than supportive findings.
Case C649

In hospital, a fetal head delivers after a difficult second stage and immediately retracts against the perineum. McRoberts positioning and suprapubic pressure have already been attempted without success in freeing the anterior shoulder.

  1. The correct answer is D (The Woods screw maneuver, aiming to rotate the posterior shoulder): When McRoberts and suprapubic pressure fail, the Woods screw maneuver, rotating the posterior shoulder to disimpact the anterior shoulder, is an appropriate next step in the shoulder dystocia algorithm. Repeating the same failed position is unlikely to produce a different result. Fundal pressure is contraindicated and can worsen impaction or cause rupture. Hysterectomy is an extreme and inappropriate step for a shoulder dystocia at this stage.
  2. The correct answer is A (Delivering the posterior arm by sweeping it across the fetal chest): Delivering the posterior arm, by sweeping it across the fetal chest and out, reduces the diameter of the shoulders and often resolves the dystocia when rotational maneuvers have failed. Abandoning further attempts is not appropriate while a stepwise algorithm still has options. Strong direct traction on the head and neck risks serious brachial plexus and cervical spine injury. Waiting passively delays a solution the fetus urgently needs.
  3. The correct answer is D (The Zavanelli maneuver, replacing the fetal head to allow caesarean birth): The Zavanelli maneuver, cephalic replacement followed by emergency caesarean birth, is an extremely rare last resort technique used only when every other maneuver in the shoulder dystocia algorithm has failed. Repeating an already failed maneuver multiple times wastes critical time. Discharging the client is never appropriate with an undelivered fetus in this situation. Increasing oxytocin does nothing to resolve a bony obstruction at the shoulders.
  4. The correct answer is B (Acute uterine inversion, which is a rare but genuinely serious emergency): A smooth mass at the vaginal opening with an absent abdominal fundus after placental delivery is the classic presentation of acute uterine inversion, a rare emergency that can rapidly cause severe hemorrhage and shock and requires immediate manual replacement and urgent obstetric involvement. A retained membrane fragment, a vulvar hematoma, or an unrelated vaginal wall prolapse would not explain the absent fundus finding.
Case C653

A client in hospital continues to bleed heavily after birth despite uterine massage and two uterotonic medications. Bloodwork returns showing a markedly prolonged clotting time, low fibrinogen, and generalized oozing from intravenous line sites.

  1. The correct answer is D (Disseminated intravascular coagulation, complicating this ongoing obstetric hemorrhage): Prolonged clotting times, low fibrinogen, and generalized oozing from line sites in the context of ongoing severe hemorrhage indicate disseminated intravascular coagulation, a serious complication where clotting factors and platelets become depleted faster than they can be replaced. An isolated laceration would not cause abnormal coagulation testing. This laboratory pattern is not a normal postpartum finding, and it is far more severe than routine anemia from expected blood loss.
  2. The correct answer is D (Cryoprecipitate, or a fibrinogen concentrate, as directed by the care team): Cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate specifically replaces fibrinogen and is indicated when levels are critically low during obstetric hemorrhage with coagulopathy. Packed red cells alone restore oxygen carrying capacity but do not correct a clotting factor deficiency. Oral iron acts far too slowly to address an acute coagulopathy. Normal saline alone dilutes remaining clotting factors further and does not replace fibrinogen.
  3. The correct answer is A (The massive transfusion protocol, for this severe and ongoing hemorrhage): Severe ongoing hemorrhage with developing coagulopathy warrants activation of the massive transfusion protocol, which provides rapid, balanced access to red cells, plasma, platelets, and fibrinogen products. A routine outpatient follow up is entirely inappropriate during an active emergency. A single unit crossmatch is insufficient for the scale of blood product support required. Discharge planning has no place while hemorrhage and coagulopathy are still active.
  4. The correct answer is A (Uterine tamponade using a balloon device, such as a Bakri balloon): When medical management is insufficient, uterine tamponade with a balloon device applies direct pressure to the uterine cavity and can control ongoing hemorrhage while further surgical options, such as compression sutures or hysterectomy, are considered if needed. Stopping fluids and blood products would worsen an already unstable client. Sending the client home is never appropriate during active severe hemorrhage. Passive waiting risks fatal outcomes at this stage.
  5. The correct answer is B (Umbilical cord compression, or alternatively a placental abruption occurring now): A sudden, sustained fetal bradycardia is most concerning for acute umbilical cord compression, such as prolapse, or placental abruption, both of which can rapidly reduce fetal oxygen delivery and require urgent assessment. Fetal sleep cycles reduce variability rather than causing sustained bradycardia. A monitor artifact should only be assumed after a careful reassessment confirms the true fetal heart rate, not as a default first assumption for a sustained low rate.
  6. The correct answer is B (Occult cord prolapse, with the cord lying alongside the presenting part): When the cord cannot be seen or directly palpated but a sudden fetal heart rate change occurs after rupture of membranes, an occult cord prolapse, where the cord lies alongside rather than below the presenting part, should be suspected. Overt prolapse specifically requires the cord to be visible or clearly palpable below the presenting part. The fetal heart rate changes rule out the idea that there is no cord involvement, and this is not a benign nuchal cord finding.
  7. The correct answer is D (Manually elevate the presenting part off the pelvis with a gloved hand): Manually elevating the presenting part off the pelvis with a gloved hand reduces pressure on the cord and can improve fetal heart rate patterns while urgent obstetric assessment and possible delivery are arranged. Remaining flat on the back can worsen aortocaval compression and does not relieve cord pressure. Increasing oxytocin would intensify contractions and worsen compression. There is no fluid remaining to check, and further amniotomy is not indicated here.
  8. The correct answer is A (Giving a prophylactic uterotonic medication right after the baby is born): Active management of the third stage includes prompt administration of a prophylactic uterotonic, usually oxytocin, after the baby is born, along with controlled cord traction and uterine assessment, all of which reduce the risk of postpartum hemorrhage. Waiting indefinitely for spontaneous separation describes expectant management instead. Avoiding uterotonics in a low risk client removes a key protective benefit. Cord clamping timing is a separate decision from the uterotonic component of active management.
  9. The correct answer is A (Genital tract lacerations occurring from unusually rapid fetal descent): Precipitous labour, with birth occurring very rapidly, increases the risk of genital tract lacerations because tissues do not have adequate time to stretch gradually with descent. It does not cause a prolonged second stage, since by definition the whole labour is short. Precipitous labour is actually associated with an increased, not decreased, risk of postpartum hemorrhage from atony or trauma, and it does not guarantee any need for operative birth.
  10. The correct answer is B (Secondary arrest of dilation occurring within the active first stage): no further cervical change over four hours despite adequate contractions and a well applied presenting part is classified as secondary arrest of dilation, an abnormal active phase pattern that warrants further assessment of fetal position, adequacy of contractions, and possible consultation. This is not an expected normal pause, and eight centimetres with intact membranes and an unengaged sensation is not an indication to push. Fetal position cannot be confirmed from this description alone.
  11. The correct answer is A (Arrange emergency transfer to hospital for further assessment and management): A prolonged second stage without descent falls outside the normal parameters for home birth and warrants transfer to hospital, where assessment for causes such as malposition, cephalopelvic disproportion, or the need for operative delivery can occur. Continuing to push at home for hours longer delays necessary escalation. Stopping pushing entirely and resting overnight is not appropriate management of an active, prolonged second stage. Trying more position changes alone, with no transfer plan, ignores the need for further assessment.
  12. The correct answer is B (Consult obstetrics regarding the possible need for an operative vaginal birth): Operative vaginal birth using forceps or vacuum is outside the scope of practice for midwives in Canada, so the appropriate step is obstetric consultation to determine whether an operative vaginal birth or other intervention is needed. A midwife performing forceps or vacuum delivery independently exceeds her scope of practice. Discharging a client home during an active, prolonged second stage in hospital is not an appropriate option.
Case C665

At a hospital birth, the anterior shoulder becomes impacted after head delivery. The team has already called for help, noted the time, and completed McRoberts positioning with suprapubic pressure without success.

  1. The correct answer is A (Internal rotational maneuvers, aimed at repositioning the impacted fetal shoulders): When initial maneuvers such as McRoberts and suprapubic pressure fail, the algorithm progresses to internal rotational maneuvers aimed at repositioning the fetal shoulders into a more favourable diameter. Repeating identical steps that already failed rarely succeeds. Abandoning vaginal delivery is not appropriate while further maneuvers remain available. Fundal pressure remains contraindicated throughout the shoulder dystocia algorithm because of the risk of worsening impaction.
  2. The correct answer is A (Applying pressure to the more accessible fetal shoulder, pushing it toward the chest): The Rubin maneuver involves applying pressure to the more accessible fetal shoulder, pushing it toward the fetal chest to adduct and rotate the shoulders into an oblique diameter, which can free the impaction. Sharp downward traction on the head risks brachial plexus injury. An emergency caesarean is not performed once the head has already delivered. An episiotomy alone does not address a bony shoulder impaction.
  3. The correct answer is C (Complete the birth of the rest of the body while continuing close monitoring): Once the posterior arm and shoulder are delivered, the rest of the body should be born promptly while the team continues close monitoring for bleeding, neonatal condition, and any signs of birth trauma. Clamping and cutting the cord before the body is fully born is unnecessary and would not aid delivery. Pausing for several minutes delays completion of an already resolving emergency. Repeating a maneuver that has already succeeded serves no purpose.
  4. The correct answer is B (The maneuvers used, their exact sequence, timing, and staff present throughout): Thorough documentation after shoulder dystocia should include the sequence and timing of maneuvers attempted, the time from head to body delivery, staff present, and neonatal condition, since this information is essential for neonatal follow up and future obstetric planning. Recording only the Apgar score omits critical detail. Describing the birth simply as normal misrepresents a recognized emergency. Relying on a routine birth summary with no specific detail is inadequate for this event.
  5. The correct answer is D (Uterine overdistension caused by polyhydramnios or a multiple pregnancy): Uterine overdistension, from conditions such as polyhydramnios, macrosomia, or a multiple pregnancy, stretches the myometrium and impairs its ability to contract effectively after birth, increasing the risk of atony. A short healthy first stage, a normal singleton pregnancy with typical fluid volume, and consistent prenatal attendance are not recognized risk factors for atony.
  6. The correct answer is A (Secondary postpartum hemorrhage, since it is occurring well after the first day): Heavy bleeding requiring frequent pad changes occurring beyond the first twenty-four hours after birth, up to six weeks postpartum, is classified as secondary postpartum hemorrhage and warrants prompt assessment for causes such as retained products or infection. Primary postpartum hemorrhage is specifically defined as occurring within the first twenty-four hours. This level of bleeding is well beyond normal lochia, and a true menstrual period this soon after birth, in this pattern, is not the expected explanation.
  7. The correct answer is A (Vasa previa, involving rupture of an exposed fetal vessel over the cervix): Bright red bleeding occurring immediately at rupture of membranes together with a sudden fetal heart rate abnormality is the classic and dangerous presentation of vasa previa, in which an unprotected fetal vessel runs over the cervix and can rupture, causing rapid fetal exsanguination. A bloody show or a cervical polyp would not typically cause this abrupt combination of bleeding and fetal heart rate change, and this is well beyond normal spotting.
  8. The correct answer is C (Amnioinfusion, in order to relieve some of the pressure on the compressed cord): Amnioinfusion, instilling fluid into the amniotic cavity, can cushion the umbilical cord and reduce the frequency and severity of variable decelerations caused by cord compression. Discontinuing fetal heart rate monitoring at this point would remove essential information during a concerning pattern. A sedative does not address the underlying cord compression. Passive waiting without further assessment risks ongoing fetal compromise going unrecognized.
Case C673

At a planned home birth, membranes rupture and a loop of umbilical cord is immediately visible outside the vagina, pulsating with the fetal heartbeat. The fetal head remains high and unengaged.

  1. The correct answer is D (Overt cord prolapse, since the cord is directly visible outside the vagina): A cord that is directly visible outside the vagina and pulsating is classified as overt cord prolapse, distinguishing it from occult prolapse where the cord is not visible. A funic souffle is simply the sound of blood flow through the cord heard on auscultation and is unrelated to this presentation. This finding is an obstetric emergency, not a normal one, and requires an immediate emergency response.
  2. The correct answer is A (An exaggerated Sims position, with the hips elevated on the left side): An exaggerated Sims position, lying on the left side with hips elevated, uses gravity to shift the fetal presenting part away from the pelvis and cord, reducing compression during the emergency transfer. Flat supine positioning does not achieve this shift and can worsen aortocaval compression. Standing and walking or sitting upright in a car seat would allow the presenting part to settle further onto the cord rather than relieving pressure.
  3. The correct answer is C (Filling the maternal bladder with sterile fluid to help elevate the presenting part): Filling the maternal bladder with several hundred millilitres of sterile fluid can help elevate the presenting part off the cord as an adjunct to manual elevation during a prolonged transfer. Encouraging bearing down would push the presenting part further onto the cord. Fundal pressure worsens compression rather than relieving it. Removing the gloved hand from the vagina would remove the manual elevation that is protecting the cord.
  4. The correct answer is B (Being multiparous, combined with having an adequate amount of amniotic fluid): Multiparity and an adequate amniotic fluid volume both give the fetus more room to move and are associated with a higher success rate for external cephalic version. Reduced fluid volume in a primiparous client makes rotation more difficult. An already engaged fetal head is harder to disengage and turn. A fixed, unmoving fetal back suggests reduced room for manipulation and lowers the likelihood of success.
Case C677

A client continues to bleed heavily immediately after birth despite a firm uterine fundus achieved by massage. The midwife has already given the first line uterotonic and is considering further pharmacologic options in sequence.

  1. The correct answer is B (Oxytocin, given either intramuscularly or intravenously right after the birth): Oxytocin is the standard first line uterotonic for prevention and treatment of postpartum hemorrhage because of its rapid onset and generally favourable side effect profile. Misoprostol and carboprost are typically reserved for later steps when oxytocin alone is insufficient. Ergometrine is an important second line option but is not the universal first choice in every case because of its contraindications.
  2. The correct answer is B (Ergometrine, because it can significantly raise her blood pressure further): Ergometrine causes vasoconstriction and can significantly raise blood pressure, making it contraindicated in clients with hypertensive disorders. Oxytocin does not carry this same contraindication and remains appropriate. Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic, not a vasoconstrictor, and does not raise blood pressure this way. Misoprostol is not absolutely contraindicated by hypertension in the same manner as ergometrine.
  3. The correct answer is C (Carboprost, because it can precipitate significant bronchospasm in asthma): Carboprost, a prostaglandin analogue, can cause bronchospasm and should be used with particular caution or avoided in clients with asthma. Oxytocin does not carry this respiratory risk. Tranexamic acid is not associated with triggering bronchospasm. Misoprostol carries a much lower respiratory risk than carboprost and is not considered absolutely contraindicated in asthma.
  4. The correct answer is C (As early as possible, ideally given within three hours of the birth itself): Tranexamic acid is most effective for postpartum hemorrhage when given as early as possible, ideally within three hours of birth, alongside uterotonic medications rather than instead of them. Waiting twenty-four hours or until all uterotonics are exhausted significantly reduces its effectiveness. It is given in combination with uterotonics as part of a comprehensive hemorrhage management approach, not as an alternative used only alone.
  5. The correct answer is A (Protect her airway carefully and prepare to give magnesium sulfate promptly): During an eclamptic seizure, the priority is protecting the airway, ensuring maternal safety, and preparing to administer magnesium sulfate, the standard treatment for eclampsia and prevention of further seizures. Leaving the client unattended during a seizure is unsafe. Oral feeding during active seizure activity risks aspiration. Increasing the oxytocin infusion has no role in managing an eclamptic seizure and could worsen the overall clinical picture.
  6. The correct answer is C (It can increase the functional pelvic diameter and use gravity to assist): The all-fours or Gaskin maneuver can increase the functional pelvic diameter and use gravity and position change to help dislodge the impacted shoulder, making it a reasonable option earlier in the sequence, particularly at a home birth. It has genuine mechanical benefit beyond comfort. It is not reserved only for after every other maneuver fails, and it does not replace the need to call for emergency help and prepare for possible transfer.
  7. The correct answer is C (A reassuring sign that significant fetal acidosis is currently quite unlikely): An acceleration following fetal scalp stimulation is a reassuring response generally associated with a low likelihood of significant fetal acidosis at that moment, and it can support continued observation rather than immediate intervention. It does not prove that acidosis is present, since it demonstrates the opposite. It is not, on its own, an indication for emergency caesarean. A reassuring response should still be documented as part of the fetal assessment.
  8. The correct answer is D (Consult obstetrics promptly and consider transferring her to hospital care): Thick, particulate meconium-stained fluid is associated with an increased risk of fetal compromise and meconium aspiration, and falls outside normal scope for continued home birth without consultation, so obstetric consultation and consideration of transfer are appropriate. Continuing home birth planning unchanged ignores this risk factor. Thick meconium is not always benign. Waiting until after birth to consult defeats the purpose of anticipatory risk management.
  9. The correct answer is B (It carries a higher risk of cord prolapse and of an incompletely dilated cervix): Footling breech presentation carries a higher risk of cord prolapse because a foot can slip through an incompletely dilated cervix, and it also increases the risk of a trapped head, which is why it is generally considered unsuitable for planned vaginal breech birth. It does not carry the lowest risk of cord prolapse, it does not guarantee an uncomplicated birth, and it clearly requires careful obstetric consultation rather than none at all.
  10. The correct answer is C (Polyhydramnios occurring together with an unstable lie or malpresentation): Polyhydramnios combined with an unstable lie or malpresentation increases the space around the presenting part, raising the risk that the cord can slip past it when membranes rupture, which is the classic combination for cord prolapse risk. A well engaged vertex with normal fluid, an otherwise uncomplicated term singleton labour, or a prior uncomplicated vaginal birth alone are all reassuring rather than risk increasing findings.
  11. The correct answer is C (Uterine rupture, with the fetus moving through an underlying uterine defect): Loss of a previously confirmed station together with fetal parts becoming newly palpable through the abdominal wall suggests the fetus has moved out of the uterine cavity through a defect, which is a concerning sign of uterine rupture requiring urgent obstetric assessment. Normal descent would not cause the presenting part to disappear from examination or fetal parts to become abdominally palpable. This is not a reassuring sign, and it should not be dismissed as simple examiner error.
  12. The correct answer is B (Visual estimation commonly underestimates the true volume of blood actually lost): Research consistently shows that visual estimation of blood loss commonly underestimates the true volume, particularly with larger losses, which is why quantitative methods such as weighing blood-soaked materials or using calibrated drapes are increasingly recommended. Visual estimation is not more accurate than quantitative measurement, and quantitative measurement can be performed at a vaginal birth. Blood loss volume is directly central to diagnosing and grading postpartum hemorrhage.
  13. The correct answer is A (Postpartum hemorrhage from uterine atony, related to her grand multiparity): Grand multiparity, generally five or more prior births, is associated with reduced uterine muscle tone and an increased risk of atony and postpartum hemorrhage. A first pregnancy with no prior history, a short uncomplicated labour with a smaller baby, and consistent prenatal attendance are not recognized risk factors for atony in the same way.
  14. The correct answer is B (Clear, timely communication with EMS and with the receiving hospital team): Clear, timely communication with emergency medical services and the receiving hospital team, including a concise clinical summary and estimated arrival time, allows the hospital team to prepare and reduces delay in urgent care. Withholding details until arrival wastes valuable preparation time. Delaying the call to EMS until every clinical step is finished increases overall time to definitive care. Documentation should begin as soon as practical rather than being avoided until well after the event.
Case C691

A woman who had an uncomplicated spontaneous vaginal birth twelve days ago calls her community midwife reporting a sudden increase in vaginal bleeding over the past two hours, soaking two full pads and passing several large clots, with mild lower abdominal cramping but no fever or foul smelling discharge.

  1. The correct answer is C (Palpate the uterine fundus and assess tone for retained tissue.): Sudden heavy bleeding with clots this far postpartum suggests possible subinvolution or retained products of conception, so the midwife should first palpate the fundus for size, tone, and tenderness. A boggy or enlarged uterus supports uterine atony or retained tissue as the cause. This physical assessment guides whether uterotonics, transfer, or further investigation are needed. Reassurance without assessment could delay recognition of a serious complication.
  2. The correct answer is B (A soft, boggy, and higher than expected uterine fundus on palpation.): A boggy, enlarged uterus that is higher than expected for twelve days postpartum indicates delayed involution, often from retained placental fragments or endometritis, both classic causes of late postpartum hemorrhage. A firm, low fundus would be reassuring rather than concerning. Normal vital signs or labs do not rule out an evolving bleed and should not delay further evaluation of the uterus itself.
  3. The correct answer is B (It stimulates uterine contraction to reduce bleeding from an atonic uterus.): Fundal massage combined with a uterotonic like misoprostol works to stimulate myometrial contraction, which compresses the spiral arteries and reduces bleeding from an atonic or subinvoluted uterus. This is an appropriate first line community response while the midwife reassesses response to treatment. It does not eliminate the possibility that consultation or transfer will still be needed if bleeding continues.
  4. The correct answer is B (Initiate urgent transfer to hospital while continuing resuscitative measures.): Rising heart rate and falling blood pressure despite initial treatment indicate ongoing significant hemorrhage and early hemodynamic compromise, meeting criteria for urgent transfer under provincial consultation and transfer guidelines. The midwife should initiate transport while continuing fundal massage, uterotonics, intravenous access, and fluids en route. Waiting further at home would risk progression to hypovolemic shock.
Case C695

A midwife is conducting a routine postpartum home visit on day three for a client who had a spontaneous vaginal birth with no complications. The client reports feeling well overall and is breastfeeding without difficulty.

  1. The correct answer is A (The fundal height and lochia colour are consistent with normal involution.): By day three the uterus descends roughly one to two centimetres per day below the umbilicus, so a fundus three centimetres below it is expected. Lochia rubra, which is red and moderate in amount, is also normal for the first several days. Neither finding suggests infection or delayed involution on its own, so this presentation reflects a healthy, uncomplicated postpartum course.
  2. The correct answer is C (Lochia typically progresses from red, to pink or brown, then to yellowish white.): Normal lochia follows a predictable progression: lochia rubra, red, for the first few days, lochia serosa, pink or brownish, for about the next week or two, and finally lochia alba, yellowish white, which can persist for several weeks. A return to bright red bleeding later suggests a problem such as subinvolution and warrants further assessment rather than being considered normal.
  3. The correct answer is A (Visits are typically scheduled within the first week, then around six weeks.): Canadian midwifery models typically include several visits in the first week, often days one, three, and five to ten, followed by a comprehensive assessment around four to six weeks postpartum. This schedule allows early detection of physical and emotional complications while supporting the transition to parenting. A single distant visit would miss the period of highest risk for complications.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ice packs and sitz baths along with regular pain relief medication.): Mild redness without fever or discharge is common during normal healing, and comfort measures such as ice in the first day, sitz baths, and appropriate analgesia support healing while reducing pain and swelling. Restricting fluids has no benefit and can worsen constipation, which increases perineal strain. Prolonged bed rest is unnecessary and increases the risk of venous thromboembolism.
  5. The correct answer is B (Increasing pain, spreading redness, foul odour, or fever above thirty eight Celsius.): Worsening pain, spreading redness, purulent or foul smelling discharge, and fever are classic signs of perineal wound infection or dehiscence and warrant prompt midwife reassessment. Minor bruising, brief stinging with urination, and small amounts of clear drainage are common and expected during normal healing. Teaching clients these specific warning signs supports timely recognition of complications without unnecessary alarm.
Case C700

A first time breastfeeding mother is four days postpartum and calls her midwife concerned that her baby seems to want to feed constantly, her nipples are cracked and painful during latch, and she is worried she does not have enough milk.

  1. The correct answer is D (A shallow latch with the baby's lip tucked rather than flanged.): A shallow latch, where the baby takes in less breast tissue and the lower lip is tucked rather than flanged outward, is a leading cause of nipple pain and cracking. Correcting positioning and attachment, such as ensuring a wide open mouth and flanged lips, typically resolves the pain far more effectively than treating the nipple skin alone. Frequent feeding itself is normal newborn behaviour and not the cause of the pain.
  2. The correct answer is A (It reduces nipple trauma while improving effective milk transfer to the baby.): A deep, asymmetric latch with the chin buried in the breast allows the baby to compress milk sinuses more effectively and reduces friction and trauma on the nipple tip. This combination improves comfort for the mother while supporting more effective milk transfer for the baby. It has no effect on sleep patterns or the volume of underlying breast tissue.
  3. The correct answer is B (Frequent feeding every one to two hours is common and helps build supply.): Frequent, closely spaced feeds, often called cluster feeding, are a normal newborn pattern in the first weeks and help establish and increase maternal milk supply through frequent breast stimulation. This pattern does not by itself indicate low supply or require supplementation. Reassurance combined with attention to latch and output helps the mother continue breastfeeding confidently.
  4. The correct answer is D (At least six wet diapers daily and weight loss within the expected range.): By day four, six or more wet diapers daily and weight loss that has stabilized within the expected range, typically less than seven percent of birth weight, are reassuring signs of adequate intake. Fewer wet diapers, absent stooling, or excessive weight loss beyond ten percent would prompt closer evaluation of feeding and possible consultation regarding supplementation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Apply expressed breast milk or a purified lanolin product after each feed.): Applying a small amount of expressed breast milk or purified lanolin after feeds can support nipple skin healing while feeding continues, alongside correcting latch as the primary fix. Frequent soap washing removes protective natural oils and can worsen dryness and cracking. Stopping feeding on the affected side is rarely necessary once latch is corrected and can reduce supply and increase engorgement.
Case C705

At the two week postpartum visit, a client tells her midwife she has been feeling persistently sad, tearful, and anxious most days, has trouble sleeping even when the baby sleeps, and feels guilty that she is not enjoying motherhood the way she expected.

  1. The correct answer is A (Complete a further clinical assessment and discuss referral for mental health support.): An Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale score well above the typical cutoff of around 13 warrants a further clinical conversation, including direct questions about safety, and discussion of referral options such as counselling, a family physician, or perinatal mental health services. Dismissing the score or delaying follow up risks leaving a treatable condition unaddressed for weeks.
  2. The correct answer is D (Asking directly helps identify risk and guides urgency of referral and safety planning.): Directly and sensitively asking about thoughts of self harm or harming the baby is a core, evidence based part of perinatal mental health assessment because it identifies risk level and determines how urgently support is needed, ranging from routine referral to emergency intervention. Avoiding the question does not protect clients and can delay recognition of a safety concern.
  3. The correct answer is C (Provide referral information for counselling and coordinate with her family physician.): Midwives support clients with postpartum mood symptoms by screening, providing psychoeducation, and coordinating referral to appropriate resources such as counselling, perinatal mental health programs, or a family physician who can consider medication if indicated, since this typically falls outside independent midwifery prescribing scope for ongoing psychiatric management. Discharging her from care abandons an ongoing therapeutic relationship during a vulnerable time.
Case C708

A client who is four weeks postpartum and exclusively breastfeeding attends her postpartum visit and asks her midwife about resuming a reliable method of contraception, as she and her partner have already resumed sexual activity.

  1. The correct answer is C (It is effective only if feeding is exclusive, amenorrhea persists, and baby is under six months.): The lactational amenorrhea method is effective when three specific criteria are all met: breastfeeding is fully or nearly fully exclusive, the client remains amenorrheic since birth, and the baby is younger than six months old. If any of these criteria are no longer met, effectiveness drops significantly and an additional method should be used to prevent pregnancy.
  2. The correct answer is A (Combined estrogen containing contraceptives are generally avoided due to possible reduced milk supply.): Estrogen containing combined hormonal contraceptives are generally avoided in the early postpartum period while breastfeeding is being established because estrogen can reduce milk supply, and progestin only methods are typically preferred instead. Timing guidelines also consider venous thromboembolism risk, which remains elevated in the weeks after birth regardless of feeding status.
  3. The correct answer is C (Insertion timing depends on whether it will be placed immediately or after involution.): Intrauterine device insertion can occur immediately postpartum in some settings or be delayed until after uterine involution, typically around four to six weeks, and the appropriate timing and referral pathway should be discussed based on local availability and the client's preferences. Intrauterine devices are compatible with breastfeeding and remain a highly effective method regardless of feeding status.
  4. The correct answer is A (Consistent use of condoms until a longer term method is chosen and started.): Condoms provide an accessible, effective, and immediately available interim method of pregnancy prevention while a client considers longer term hormonal or intrauterine options, and they carry no interaction with breastfeeding. Pregnancy can occur before menstruation returns, so assuming protection without a reliable method or meeting full lactational amenorrhea criteria is unsafe advice.
Case C712

A client who had a cesarean birth five days ago and has a body mass index of 34 calls her midwife reporting unilateral left calf swelling, warmth, and tenderness that started yesterday and has been getting gradually worse.

  1. The correct answer is C (Recent cesarean birth, elevated body mass index, and unilateral leg swelling.): Cesarean birth, obesity, and unilateral leg swelling with warmth and tenderness are all recognized risk factors and clinical signs for postpartum venous thromboembolism, and their combination substantially raises clinical suspicion. Isolated fatigue or normal blood pressure readings do not meaningfully change this assessment, and an unrelated prior vaginal birth carries no relevance to this current presentation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Consult with or refer urgently to a physician for further vascular investigation.): Suspected deep vein thrombosis requires urgent physician consultation or referral for further investigation, typically including compression ultrasound, since this diagnosis falls outside independent midwifery management and carries risk of pulmonary embolism if untreated. Calf massage is contraindicated as it may dislodge a clot, and reassurance without investigation could delay a serious diagnosis.
  3. The correct answer is C (Sudden shortness of breath, chest pain, and a rapid heart rate.): Sudden onset shortness of breath, pleuritic chest pain, and tachycardia are classic warning signs of pulmonary embolism and constitute a medical emergency requiring immediate transfer for assessment. Chronic mild ankle swelling, occasional headache, or reduced appetite are common and nonspecific postpartum complaints that do not indicate this life threatening complication on their own.
  4. The correct answer is A (Spreading redness, purulent discharge, and a low grade fever.): Spreading redness at the incision edges, purulent or yellow discharge, and a low grade fever together are classic signs of a developing surgical site infection and require prompt clinical assessment and likely physician consultation. A well healed scar, mild itching, or localized numbness are common and expected findings during normal cesarean incision healing.
  5. The correct answer is B (Continue collaborative postpartum care while the physician manages the infection treatment.): Once a surgical site infection requiring antibiotic treatment is confirmed, the midwife typically continues to provide collaborative postpartum and newborn care while the physician manages the infection itself, since surgical wound infection treatment generally falls outside independent midwifery scope. Full discharge from midwifery care or leaving the client unsupported would abandon an ongoing collaborative relationship during recovery.
Case C717

A client had a home birth twenty four hours ago attended by her midwife and is now being assessed for the standard newborn and maternal discharge planning conversation before the midwife leaves for the day.

  1. The correct answer is B (Firm fundus, normal lochia, stable vital signs, and no excessive bleeding.): A firm fundus, normal lochia, and stable vital signs with no excessive bleeding are reassuring findings that support continuing routine community based follow up rather than transfer. Heavy bleeding with a boggy uterus, fever with chills, or headache with visual changes and hypertension each represent warning signs that would instead prompt urgent consultation or transfer.
  2. The correct answer is B (Stable temperature, normal colour, and at least one voided urine.): A stable temperature, normal colour, and evidence of adequate hydration such as a voided urine are reassuring newborn findings that support ongoing community based care. Grunting with nasal flaring, central cyanosis, or a high pitched cry with lethargy are all signs of possible neonatal compromise and would instead require urgent pediatric assessment or transfer.
  3. The correct answer is D (Warning signs for both mother and baby along with emergency contact information.): Standard postpartum discharge documentation includes clear written warning signs for both mother and newborn, such as bleeding, fever, or breathing concerns, along with emergency contact information for reaching the midwife or emergency services. This written information supports safe self monitoring between visits and ensures the family knows when and how to seek urgent help.
  4. The correct answer is B (Explain the on call system and how to reach a midwife at any hour.): Midwifery care in Canada includes twenty four hour on call availability, so the midwife should clearly explain how to reach the on call midwife at any hour for urgent concerns, in addition to when to call emergency services directly. Suggesting the family simply wait until morning would leave a genuine emergency unaddressed and is inconsistent with the continuity of care model.
Case C721

A client three weeks postpartum returns for an unscheduled visit reporting that her lochia, which had progressed to a light pink colour, has become bright red again over the past two days along with a low grade fever and mild uterine tenderness.

  1. The correct answer is C (Subinvolution of the uterus possibly related to retained tissue or infection.): The combination of a return to bright red bleeding, fever, and uterine tenderness at three weeks postpartum is characteristic of subinvolution, which can result from retained placental tissue or endometritis. This pattern is not a normal expected finding at this stage and requires further clinical evaluation rather than reassurance about exertion or diet.
  2. The correct answer is B (Consult with a physician promptly for evaluation and likely antibiotic therapy.): Suspected endometritis with fever and uterine tenderness requires prompt physician consultation, since diagnosis often involves further examination and laboratory investigation and management typically requires antibiotic therapy that falls outside independent midwifery prescribing for this indication. Delaying evaluation risks progression to more serious infection or sepsis.
  3. The correct answer is A (Breastfeeding can generally continue since most treatments are compatible with lactation.): Endometritis is a uterine infection, not typically transmitted through breast milk, and most antibiotics used to treat it are compatible with continued breastfeeding, so the midwife can reassure the client that feeding can generally continue while she is evaluated and treated. Advising a complete and unnecessary stop to breastfeeding could reduce supply and increase maternal stress without any real benefit.
Case C724

A breastfeeding client is nine days postpartum and reports one breast has become firm, warm, and painful in a wedge shaped area over the past day, along with a temperature of 38.6 Celsius and flu like body aches.

  1. The correct answer is D (Bacterial mastitis with a localized inflamed and possibly infected area.): A localized, warm, painful, wedge shaped area of the breast combined with systemic fever and flu like body aches is a classic presentation of mastitis, an inflammatory or infectious condition of breast tissue. Simple engorgement is typically bilateral and does not usually cause fever, and nipple thrush presents with different, more superficial symptoms.
  2. The correct answer is D (Continued milk removal helps clear the blockage and reduce inflammation.): Continued frequent and effective milk removal, whether by breastfeeding or expressing, helps clear the underlying ductal blockage and reduce inflammation, which is central to resolving mastitis. Stopping feeding on the affected side can worsen milk stasis and increase the risk of abscess formation, so it is generally discouraged unless there is a specific contraindication.
  3. The correct answer is A (Possible breast abscess formation requiring prompt physician consultation and imaging.): Persistent fever with a new fluctuant, firm area suggests possible abscess formation, a recognized complication of untreated or worsening mastitis, and requires prompt physician consultation, often with ultrasound imaging and possible drainage. This presentation is not consistent with resolution and should not be managed expectantly without further evaluation.
  4. The correct answer is D (The fundus typically descends about one to two centimetres each day.): Normal uterine involution proceeds at a fairly predictable rate of about one to two centimetres of descent per day, with the fundus typically nonpalpable above the pubic symphysis by around ten to fourteen days postpartum. A fundus that stays the same height, rises, or disappears within a day would all be abnormal and warrant further assessment.
  5. The correct answer is C (Yellowish white lochia typically appears later, closer to two to four weeks.): Lochia alba, the final yellowish white stage, more typically appears around two to four weeks postpartum rather than at one week, so its presence this early is somewhat atypical and worth noting alongside the rest of the assessment, though it is not automatically a sign of infection. The midwife should still confirm normal fundal height, absence of fever, and overall wellbeing.
  6. The correct answer is A (The first week carries the highest risk for physical and feeding complications.): The first week postpartum carries the highest risk for complications such as hemorrhage, infection, feeding difficulties, and newborn jaundice or weight loss, so frequent early assessment allows the midwife to detect and address problems promptly. This clinical rationale, not administrative requirements, drives the intensive early visit schedule in Canadian midwifery care.
  7. The correct answer is D (Gradual mobilization with avoidance of heavy lifting and strenuous activity.): Standard postpartum guidance after cesarean birth recommends gradual mobilization, which supports circulation and reduces venous thromboembolism risk, while avoiding heavy lifting and strenuous activity that could strain the incision, typically for several weeks. Complete immobility increases clot risk, while an immediate return to strenuous exercise risks wound complications.
  8. The correct answer is D (Cesarean birth combined with prolonged immobility during recovery.): Cesarean birth and prolonged immobility are well established risk factors for postpartum venous thromboembolism, since surgery and reduced movement both promote venous stasis and hypercoagulability. Uncomplicated vaginal birth with early mobilization, a normal body mass index, and regular gentle walking are all protective or neutral factors rather than risks.
  9. The correct answer is C (Gentle activity can begin early, with gradual return to vigorous exercise as tolerated.): After an uncomplicated vaginal birth, gentle activity such as walking can typically begin early, with a gradual, individualized return to more vigorous exercise guided by comfort, bleeding, and energy levels rather than a fixed universal timeline. A blanket twelve week rule or same day vigorous activity are both inconsistent with individualized, evidence based postpartum guidance.
  10. The correct answer is A (Referral for pelvic floor physiotherapy and daily pelvic floor exercises.): Stress urinary incontinence is common after birth and often improves with pelvic floor physiotherapy and consistent pelvic floor muscle exercises, which should typically be offered as first line management. Surgical referral is reserved for persistent symptoms unresponsive to conservative care, and restricting fluids does not address the underlying pelvic floor weakness and can cause dehydration.
  11. The correct answer is B (Audible swallowing during feeds along with a relaxed, satisfied baby afterward.): Audible swallowing during feeds and a relaxed, satisfied baby afterward are reassuring signs of effective milk transfer and a good latch. Falling asleep almost immediately, persistent clicking sounds, or ongoing sharp pain are all signs that latch or positioning should be reassessed rather than being reassuring findings.
  12. The correct answer is C (Ibuprofen is generally considered compatible with breastfeeding at standard doses.): Ibuprofen is generally considered compatible with breastfeeding at standard therapeutic doses because very little passes into breast milk, making it a commonly recommended choice for postpartum pain relief. Broad statements that all pain medications are forbidden or that breastfeeding must stop are inaccurate and could leave the client with unnecessary untreated pain.
  13. The correct answer is B (Soaking through a pad in under an hour or passing large clots.): Soaking through a pad in under an hour, or passing clots larger than a golf ball, are clear warning signs of excessive postpartum bleeding that clients should be taught to recognize and report immediately. Mild afterpains during feeding, light later pink discharge, and an isolated small clot early on are common, expected findings rather than warning signs.
  14. The correct answer is B (Likely the baby blues, a common and typically self limited postpartum experience.): Mild tearfulness and fatigue in the first few days that do not significantly impair functioning are typical of the baby blues, a common, self limited experience affecting many postpartum clients that usually resolves within about two weeks. This presentation does not meet criteria for postpartum depression or require emergency intervention, though ongoing monitoring is still appropriate.
  15. The correct answer is D (Persistent low mood and functional impairment lasting beyond two weeks.): Persistent low mood, anxiety, or loss of interest that continues beyond about two weeks and significantly impairs daily functioning suggests postpartum depression rather than the milder, self limited baby blues, which typically resolves within the first ten days to two weeks. Recognizing this distinction guides whether further screening and referral are warranted.
  16. The correct answer is D (The copper intrauterine device does not affect milk supply since it is hormone free.): The copper intrauterine device contains no hormones, so it has no effect on milk supply and is considered fully compatible with breastfeeding, making it a suitable nonhormonal option for clients who wish to avoid hormonal contraception. Suggesting it reduces supply or is unsuitable for breastfeeding clients is inaccurate and could steer clients away from a good option.
  17. The correct answer is B (Progestin only pills are generally considered compatible with breastfeeding and milk supply.): progestin only contraceptive pills are generally considered compatible with breastfeeding and do not typically reduce milk supply, unlike estrogen containing combined pills, making them a common choice for lactating clients who want an oral hormonal method. They can be started relatively early postpartum, and while consistent daily timing supports effectiveness, traditionally within about a three-hour window for most progestin-only formulations rather than the twelve-hour window described in option D, the claim that they cause a noticeable, lasting reduction in milk supply is false.
  18. The correct answer is A (Establish ongoing primary care with a family physician or nurse practitioner.): As midwifery care concludes, clients should be supported to establish or reconnect with a family physician or nurse practitioner for ongoing primary and preventive health care, including future reproductive health needs. Suggesting no further care is needed disregards the client's own long term health beyond the immediate postpartum period.
  19. The correct answer is B (Discuss earlier recognition of warning signs and a realistic emergency transport plan.): When a client lives far from hospital services with limited transportation, the midwife should specifically discuss earlier recognition of warning signs and develop a realistic, individualized emergency transport plan as part of safety and discharge planning. Ignoring geographic and transportation barriers would leave a genuine safety gap unaddressed.
  20. The correct answer is C (Depending on the situation, the midwife may resume care once risk factors resolve.): A transfer of care is not always permanent, and depending on the reason for transfer and how the client's condition evolves, the midwife may resume primary or shared care once risk factors resolve or stabilize, in keeping with the collaborative nature of the Canadian maternity care system. Framing transfer as an automatic permanent end, or an automatic immediate resumption, both misstate how transfer and consultation actually work in practice.
  21. The correct answer is C (Place the baby on their back to sleep in a separate, safe sleep space.): Placing the baby on their back to sleep in a separate, firm, safe sleep space free of soft bedding is the evidence based recommendation to reduce the risk of sudden infant death. Stomach or side sleeping positions and bed sharing with soft bedding are all associated with increased risk and are not recommended as routine practice.
  22. The correct answer is C (Resume when comfortable and healed, typically once bleeding has stopped and pain resolves.): Guidance on resuming sexual activity is generally individualized, suggesting clients wait until they feel physically comfortable and emotionally ready, typically once lochia has stopped and any perineal wound has healed, rather than following one fixed universal timeline. A rigid twelve week rule or an absolute permanent restriction do not reflect individualized, evidence based counselling.
  23. The correct answer is D (A summary of the pregnancy, birth, and postpartum course including any complications.): A thorough handoff summary should include the key clinical details of the pregnancy, birth, and postpartum course, including any complications and their management, to support safe, informed ongoing care by the receiving primary care provider. Administrative details like exact appointment times or an unrelated list of family members add little clinical value to this transfer.
  24. The correct answer is C (Increase fluid and fibre intake along with gentle regular mobility.): Postpartum constipation is common and often responds well to increased fluid and fibre intake along with gentle mobility, which are appropriate first line, low risk recommendations before considering pharmacologic options. Suggesting immediate strong laxatives, bowel rest, or dismissing the symptom entirely would each be inappropriate initial responses.
  25. The correct answer is C (Rinse the perineum with warm water after using the toilet and pat dry gently.): Gently rinsing the perineum with warm water after using the toilet and patting the area dry supports cleanliness and comfort without disrupting healing tissue. Harsh antiseptic scrubbing, avoiding all water contact, or applying a tight dressing could each irritate the wound or interfere with normal healing.
  26. The correct answer is B (Menstruation often returns within six to eight weeks in nonbreastfeeding clients.): In clients who are not exclusively breastfeeding, menstruation commonly returns within about six to eight weeks postpartum, though timing varies between individuals, while exclusive breastfeeding typically delays its return further. Ovulation can also occur before the first menstrual period resumes, which is why contraceptive counselling should not assume infertility based on the absence of bleeding alone.
  27. The correct answer is A (Review warning signs, confirm primary care follow up, and provide contact resources.): Comprehensive discharge planning includes reviewing warning signs for both mother and baby, confirming that primary care follow up is arranged, and providing written contact resources for urgent concerns, ensuring the client feels supported even after midwifery care formally ends. Ending abruptly or assuming prior knowledge without confirmation risks leaving important gaps in the client's ongoing safety and care.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

Case C751

A 26-year-old primipara is 6 hours postpartum following an uncomplicated vaginal birth at a birth centre. She is breastfeeding well, her fundus is firm at the umbilicus, and she reports mild afterpains. Her vital signs are stable and lochia is rubra, moderate in amount.

  1. The correct answer is A (The fundus has descended to approximately one to two centimetres below the umbilicus): Normal uterine involution proceeds at roughly one to two centimetres per day after the fundus peaks at the umbilicus on day one. By 24 to 30 hours postpartum a steady downward trend is expected and reassuring. A fundus that fails to descend, or that rises, suggests uterine atony, retained products, or a full bladder and warrants further assessment.
  2. The correct answer is C (Oxytocin released during breastfeeding causes the uterus to contract intermittently): Suckling triggers oxytocin release from the posterior pituitary, producing intermittent uterine contractions that support involution. This is a normal, self limited finding rather than a sign of infection or retained tissue. Simple analgesia and reassurance are usually sufficient management.
  3. The correct answer is A (Lochia rubra transitions to serosa by about day four, then alba by two weeks): Lochia normally progresses from red rubra in the first few days, to pinkish brown serosa around days four to ten, to yellowish white alba by around two weeks postpartum. A reversal back to rubra after serosa has begun, heavy bleeding, or foul odour at any stage should prompt further assessment. Understanding this sequence helps the midwife distinguish normal involution from a complication.
  4. The correct answer is D (A pulse in the 50s to 60s is a normal postpartum physiologic finding): Increased stroke volume and vagal tone following delivery commonly produce a mild physiologic bradycardia in the first postpartum week. This finding alone, without tachycardia, hypotension, or other signs of hemorrhage, does not indicate a complication. Ongoing routine vital sign monitoring remains appropriate.
Case C755

A 32-year-old para 2 is seen for a home visit on postpartum day 5 following a hospital birth. She reports fatigue and a low-grade fever of 38.1 Celsius. Her fundus is soft and boggy, palpable two centimetres above the umbilicus, and her lochia has a foul odour.

  1. The correct answer is D (Subinvoluted boggy fundus, foul smelling lochia, and low grade fever together): This combination is classic for postpartum endometritis, reflecting infection and impaired uterine involution. Isolated fatigue or a single mild vital sign change would not carry the same significance. Recognizing this symptom cluster promptly supports timely consultation and treatment.
  2. The correct answer is B (Consult with an obstetrician for suspected endometritis needing further evaluation now): Suspected postpartum endometritis falls outside normal midwifery scope and typically needs antibiotic therapy and assessment for retained products. Delaying care with reassurance or a routine follow up risks progression to more serious infection. Prompt collaborative management protects maternal wellbeing.
  3. The correct answer is D (Progression to sepsis with worsening systemic infection and hemodynamic instability): Untreated postpartum endometritis can progress to bacteremia and sepsis, a leading contributor to severe maternal morbidity. Early recognition and antibiotic treatment substantially reduce this risk. The other options describe unrelated or implausible outcomes for this clinical picture.
  4. The correct answer is C (Apply ice packs intermittently for the first day, then use warm sitz baths): Cold therapy reduces initial swelling and discomfort, while warm sitz baths after the first day promote circulation and comfort as healing progresses. Gentle perineal hygiene and appropriate ongoing analgesia support healing and comfort. Wrapping the area tightly or stopping all analgesia is not appropriate.
  5. The correct answer is B (Wound edges remain well approximated with decreasing tenderness and no discharge): Normal perineal healing shows progressively less tenderness, well approximated edges, and absence of discharge or spreading erythema. The other findings describe signs of wound breakdown, infection, or hematoma formation that would require further assessment. Reassuring healing findings support continued routine postpartum care.
  6. The correct answer is C (Late postpartum hemorrhage, possibly due to retained products of conception): Sudden heavy bleeding with clots and lightheadedness beyond 24 hours postpartum defines late postpartum hemorrhage, commonly caused by retained placental tissue, subinvolution, or infection. This presentation is well beyond expected lochia changes and requires urgent assessment. Prompt recognition allows timely transfer and management to prevent further blood loss.
  7. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent transfer to hospital for assessment and possible intervention): Late postpartum hemorrhage with signs of significant blood loss requires urgent transfer for evaluation, which may include ultrasound, uterotonics, or evacuation of retained tissue. Delaying assessment with home monitoring or routine scheduling risks continued hemorrhage. This scenario needs prompt collaborative care.
  8. The correct answer is B (Oxytocin, administered intramuscularly or intravenously as the initial agent): Oxytocin is the first line uterotonic for both prevention and treatment of postpartum hemorrhage because it promotes effective uterine contraction and is well tolerated. Additional agents may be added if bleeding continues. The other listed medications have no role in managing postpartum hemorrhage.
  9. The correct answer is C (It stimulates uterine contraction, helping to compress bleeding vessels at the placental site): Fundal massage encourages the myometrium to contract, which compresses the spiral arteries at the placental implantation site and reduces bleeding. It is a first line mechanical intervention used alongside uterotonic medication. It has no role in fetal positioning, which is irrelevant after birth.
Case C764

A first time mother is 3 days postpartum and reports that her breasts feel firm, warm, and tender bilaterally. Her baby is latching but she describes difficulty getting a deep latch due to the firmness. She has no fever and both breasts appear symmetrically engorged.

  1. The correct answer is B (This represents primary engorgement related to increased blood flow and early milk transition): Physiologic engorgement typically occurs around day two to five postpartum as vascular and lymphatic changes accompany the transition to mature milk, causing bilateral warmth and tenderness without fever. This differs from mastitis, which usually presents unilaterally with fever. Reassurance and management strategies can help resolve engorgement while breastfeeding continues.
  2. The correct answer is C (Apply reverse pressure softening around the areola before attempting to latch): Reverse pressure softening gently moves areolar edema backward, making the nipple area more compressible and easier to latch onto deeply. Withholding feeds or binding the breasts can worsen engorgement and reduce milk removal. Continued frequent, effective feeding is the most effective way to resolve engorgement.
  3. The correct answer is D (Feed on cue approximately eight to twelve times in each twenty four hour period): Frequent, responsive feeding of eight to twelve times daily supports milk removal, helps resolve engorgement, and establishes an adequate milk supply. Scheduled or restricted feeding patterns can worsen engorgement and reduce milk transfer. Responding to early hunger cues rather than waiting for crying also supports more effective feeding.
  4. The correct answer is A (Mastitis of the left breast, likely related to a blocked duct or infection): A unilateral, wedge shaped, red, tender area with fever and chills is characteristic of mastitis, often arising from milk stasis or a blocked duct that progresses to infection. This differs from the earlier bilateral, low grade engorgement without fever. Continued breastfeeding or milk removal, along with possible antibiotics if symptoms persist, supports resolution.
  5. The correct answer is B (Continue frequent breastfeeding or pumping on the affected side along with rest and fluids): Continued milk removal is essential to resolving mastitis and preventing abscess formation, alongside supportive measures like rest, fluids, and appropriate analgesia. Stopping breastfeeding on the affected side can worsen milk stasis and prolong infection. If symptoms do not improve within 24 hours, consultation for antibiotic therapy is warranted.
Case C769

A client at her two week postpartum visit tearfully reports feeling persistently sad, having little interest in caring for her baby, and difficulty sleeping even when the baby is asleep. She denies any thoughts of harming herself or her infant, but says these feelings have not improved since the birth.

  1. The correct answer is D (Postpartum depression, given the persistence and severity of her symptoms): Persistent low mood, anhedonia, and sleep disturbance beyond the first two postpartum weeks are more consistent with postpartum depression than the transient baby blues, which typically resolve within ten to fourteen days. Postpartum psychosis involves hallucinations or delusions, which are absent here. Screening and appropriate referral support timely identification and treatment.
  2. The correct answer is A (The Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale, a validated mood screening questionnaire): The Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale is a widely used, validated tool specifically designed to screen for perinatal depression symptoms. The other tools listed assess unrelated clinical parameters such as cervical readiness, newborn adaptation, or consciousness level. Using an appropriate validated tool supports objective assessment and guides referral decisions.
  3. The correct answer is D (Refer her to a mental health provider while continuing supportive midwifery care): Moderate postpartum depression without safety concerns warrants referral to mental health services alongside continued midwifery support, rather than discharge from care or dismissive reassurance. Collaborative management addresses both her emotional wellbeing and ongoing postpartum needs. Stopping breastfeeding is not indicated based on depression screening alone.
  4. The correct answer is D (Progestin only contraceptive methods, which do not typically affect lactation): Progestin only methods are generally preferred during breastfeeding because estrogen containing methods may reduce milk supply, particularly if started early postpartum. Combined hormonal methods are typically avoided or delayed until breastfeeding is well established. This guidance supports both effective contraception and continued lactation.
  5. The correct answer is C (It is effective only under specific criteria including exclusive breastfeeding and amenorrhea): The lactational amenorrhea method is effective only when the infant is under six months old, breastfeeding is fully exclusive, and menstruation has not returned. If any of these criteria are not met, effectiveness drops considerably and a backup method should be used. Providing this specific criteria helps clients make an informed contraceptive choice.
  6. The correct answer is A (Intrauterine device insertion is generally appropriate once involution has occurred): By six weeks postpartum the uterus has typically involuted sufficiently to allow safe intrauterine device insertion, and breastfeeding is not a contraindication. The other statements describe inaccurate blanket restrictions not supported by current evidence. Discussing appropriate timing supports informed contraceptive decision making.
  7. The correct answer is D (Ovulation can occur before the return of menstruation, even while breastfeeding): Ovulation may precede the first postpartum menses, meaning pregnancy can occur before a client realizes fertility has returned. This is an important point for contraceptive counselling regardless of breastfeeding status. The other statements overstate the reliability of breastfeeding or timing alone as contraception.
  8. The correct answer is B (Cesarean birth, obesity, and a prior history of deep vein thrombosis): These are all well established risk factors that combine to significantly raise this client's risk of venous thromboembolism. Recognizing multiple concurrent risk factors is important for risk stratification and clinical decision making. Vaginal birth alone, younger age, and breastfeeding are not associated with this level of increased risk.
  9. The correct answer is B (Refer urgently for medical assessment and possible imaging to evaluate for deep vein thrombosis): Unilateral calf swelling, warmth, and tenderness in a client with significant risk factors requires urgent evaluation, typically with compression ultrasound. Delaying care with home measures or reassurance could allow progression to pulmonary embolism. This scenario requires prompt consultation.
Case C778

A client is being discharged from hospital on postpartum day two following an uncomplicated vaginal birth. She lives forty five minutes from the nearest hospital and will be followed by her community midwife for ongoing postpartum visits at home.

  1. The correct answer is B (Warning signs of postpartum complications and when to seek urgent care): Given the distance from hospital, ensuring the client understands warning signs and knows when and how to seek urgent care is a priority for safe discharge. Exercise, contraception, and weaning are appropriate future topics but are not the immediate discharge priority. Clear safety netting supports timely recognition of complications at home.
  2.
