The correct answer is C (Visits typically occur within the first day or two, then several times in the first week): Canadian midwifery postpartum care typically includes an early visit within the first one to two days after discharge, with additional visits through the first week and beyond, continuing to around six weeks. This reflects the continuity of care model central to midwifery practice. A single late visit or visits only on request would not meet the expected standard.
  3. The correct answer is C (Assess feeding closely and arrange appropriate newborn follow up or consultation): Delayed weight regain combined with jaundice warrants a thorough feeding assessment and appropriate follow up, which may include bilirubin testing or pediatric consultation. Waiting until the two week visit or offering blanket reassurance could delay identification of a treatable issue. Prompt, individualized assessment supports newborn safety.
  4. The correct answer is D (Signs suggestive of significant hyperbilirubinemia requiring medical evaluation and treatment): Significant jaundice or other findings suggesting hyperbilirubinemia requiring phototherapy fall outside independent midwifery management and warrant consultation or transfer to pediatric care. The other findings described are reassuring and consistent with normal newborn progress. Recognizing when a finding exceeds normal scope supports timely referral.
  5. The correct answer is B (A gradual decrease in blood volume as excess fluid from pregnancy is excreted): Blood volume, which expanded significantly during pregnancy, normally decreases over the first one to two postpartum weeks as excess plasma volume is diuresed. Sustained hypertension or persistent tachycardia are not expected normal findings and would warrant further assessment. Understanding these expected changes helps distinguish normal recovery from complications.
  6. The correct answer is C (Postpartum diaphoresis is a normal way the body eliminates excess fluid retained in pregnancy): Postpartum diaphoresis, particularly at night, is a common and normal physiologic response as the body eliminates excess fluid accumulated during pregnancy. It is not itself a sign of infection or thyroid disease unless accompanied by fever or other concerning symptoms. Reassurance and comfort measures are usually sufficient management.
  7. The correct answer is B (Diastasis recti, a separation of the abdominal rectus muscles along the midline): Diastasis recti is a common postpartum finding that the midwife may assess and, if significant, refer for pelvic health physiotherapy. The other conditions listed are not typical postpartum abdominal wall findings related to pregnancy and birth. Appropriate assessment and referral support optimal core recovery.
  8. The correct answer is C (Referral to pelvic health physiotherapy along with pelvic floor exercise guidance): Stress urinary incontinence is common postpartum and often improves with pelvic floor rehabilitation and targeted exercises, making physiotherapy referral an appropriate first line recommendation. Surgical referral is not indicated as an initial step, and fluid restriction is not appropriate management. Supporting evidence based conservative management aligns with midwifery scope.
Case C786

A client at her three week postpartum visit reports lochia that had transitioned to serosa but has now become bright red again, along with increasing lower abdominal cramping. She denies fever but describes the bleeding as heavier than the previous week.

  1. The correct answer is C (Possible subinvolution of the uterus or retained products of conception): A reversal from serosa back to rubra lochia, especially with increased cramping and bleeding, suggests possible subinvolution or retained tissue rather than a normal variation. This finding warrants further clinical assessment rather than reassurance alone. It is unlikely to represent an early return of menstruation given the accompanying symptoms.
  2. The correct answer is D (A uterine fundus that remains larger and softer than expected for this postpartum stage): Subinvolution presents as a uterus that is larger, softer, and higher than expected for the postpartum timeline, often accompanied by prolonged or heavier lochia. A well contracted, appropriately descended uterus with resolving lochia would be reassuring rather than concerning. Identifying this finding supports appropriate further investigation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Consult with an obstetrician regarding possible retained products or subinvolution): Suspected subinvolution or retained products of conception requires obstetric consultation, which may include ultrasound assessment and further management such as uterotonics or evacuation of retained tissue. Reassurance alone or unsupervised home management would delay identification of a potentially significant complication. Collaborative consultation reflects appropriate use of the consultation and transfer framework.
  4. The correct answer is D (This represents normal calibration of milk supply to the infant's needs over time): As lactation matures, breasts often feel less full even though milk production remains adequate, reflecting normal supply and demand regulation. Adequate weight gain and wet diaper output are the most reliable indicators of sufficient intake in this scenario. Reassurance based on objective infant output findings is appropriate here.
  5. The correct answer is D (Regained birth weight along with six to eight wet diapers per day): Regaining birth weight by two weeks combined with six to eight wet diapers daily are well established reassuring indicators of adequate milk intake. The other options describe findings that would raise concern for inadequate intake rather than support reassurance. Using objective output measures helps guide accurate assessment of breastfeeding adequacy.
  6. The correct answer is D (The baby blues, a common and self limited postpartum emotional response): Brief, fluctuating mood changes in the first few days postpartum without functional impairment are characteristic of the baby blues, which typically resolve within about two weeks. This differs from postpartum depression, which is more persistent, and postpartum psychosis, which involves more severe symptoms. Reassurance and ongoing observation are appropriate at this stage.
  7. The correct answer is B (Delusional thoughts and disorganized behaviour appearing suddenly after birth): Postpartum psychosis is characterized by an acute onset of delusions, hallucinations, or severely disorganized behaviour and represents a psychiatric emergency requiring immediate transfer. The other findings describe features more consistent with the baby blues or postpartum depression. Recognizing psychotic features is critical for ensuring urgent, appropriate intervention.
  8. The correct answer is D (Arrange urgent emergency assessment given the risk to the client and infant): Postpartum psychosis carries significant risk of harm to the client and infant and requires urgent psychiatric assessment, often through emergency services. Routine follow up, self management strategies, or informal home monitoring are not appropriate given the acuity of this condition. Prompt action reflects the seriousness of this presentation.
  9. The correct answer is B (A personal or family history of bipolar disorder or a prior psychotic episode): A personal or family history of bipolar disorder, prior postpartum psychosis, or other psychotic illness substantially increases risk for postpartum psychosis. The other listed factors are not established risk factors for this condition. Identifying risk factors antenatally supports proactive mental health planning and monitoring.
Case C795

A client is seen for a home visit five days after a cesarean birth. She reports the incision site is mildly tender but describes increasing redness along the edges, a small amount of yellow discharge, and a low grade fever of 38.0 Celsius that started this morning.

  1. The correct answer is C (Increasing redness, yellow discharge, and a new low grade fever together): The combination of spreading erythema, purulent discharge, and new fever is concerning for surgical site infection and requires further evaluation. Mild tenderness alone, a well healed wound, or expected residual numbness would not raise the same concern. Recognizing this cluster of findings supports timely, appropriate referral.
  2. The correct answer is D (Consult with the surgical or obstetric team regarding a possible wound infection): Suspected surgical site infection following cesarean birth requires timely consultation with the surgical or obstetric team, since it may require wound assessment, cultures, or antibiotic therapy. Delaying with topical self treatment or discharging from care would not adequately address a potential infection. This scenario falls outside independent midwifery management.
  3. The correct answer is D (A sudden gush of serosanguinous fluid from the incision with visible wound separation): A sudden discharge of serosanguinous fluid accompanied by visible separation of the wound edges suggests possible fascial dehiscence, a surgical emergency requiring immediate transfer. The other findings describe expected, benign aspects of normal incisional healing. Recognizing dehiscence promptly is critical to preventing further complications.
  4. The correct answer is A (Keep the incision clean and dry, and monitor daily for any new signs of infection): Gentle cleansing, keeping the incision dry, and daily self monitoring for signs of infection support optimal healing while allowing early detection of complications. Heat application, prolonged immobility, or constant occlusive dressing are not recommended and may impede healing or mask early warning signs. Gradual, gentle mobilization is generally encouraged after cesarean birth.
Case C799

A client who is exclusively formula feeding her newborn attends her three week postpartum visit and wishes to discuss contraceptive options, since she does not want to rely on the lactational amenorrhea method.

  1. The correct answer is D (As early as three weeks postpartum, since lactation is not a limiting factor here): For clients who are not breastfeeding, combined hormonal contraception can generally be considered from around three weeks postpartum once the elevated postpartum venous thromboembolism risk has begun to decline, assuming no other contraindications exist. Waiting a full year or for menstruation to return is not required. This differs from guidance for breastfeeding clients, where estrogen containing methods are typically delayed.
  2. The correct answer is C (A personal history of venous thromboembolism or other significant risk factors): A history of venous thromboembolism or other thrombotic risk factors significantly increases the risk of starting combined hormonal contraception in the early postpartum period, when baseline clotting risk is already elevated. The other options describe preferences or history unrelated to thrombotic risk. Careful risk assessment guides safe contraceptive counselling.
  3. The correct answer is B (It can cause irregular bleeding initially and requires reinjection on a set schedule): Progestin only injectable contraception commonly causes irregular bleeding, particularly in the first few months, and requires repeat injections at regular intervals to maintain effectiveness. It is not a permanent method and is appropriate for both breastfeeding and non breastfeeding clients. Providing accurate expectations supports informed decision making and continued method use.
  4. The correct answer is C (Early and frequent ambulation as soon as she is able following the birth): Early mobilization is a simple, effective, non pharmacologic strategy to reduce venous stasis and lower venous thromboembolism risk. Prolonged bed rest actually increases risk rather than reducing it. Fluid restriction and non graduated bandaging are not recommended thromboprophylaxis measures.
  5. The correct answer is D (It is generally considered compatible with breastfeeding based on current evidence): Low molecular weight heparin has minimal transfer into breast milk and is generally considered compatible with breastfeeding, allowing clients to continue nursing while receiving thromboprophylaxis. It is not a reason to discontinue breastfeeding. This medication is used because a client's individual risk factors warrant prophylaxis, not withheld from all postpartum clients.
  6. The correct answer is A (Ensuring she has clear instructions on when and how to access emergency care): For clients living far from hospital services, clear safety netting about warning signs and how to access urgent or emergency care is a critical discharge planning priority. The other topics, while potentially relevant at other times, are not the priority for safe postpartum discharge planning. This reflects the increased importance of anticipatory guidance in remote or rural settings.
  7. The correct answer is D (A client with a postpartum hemorrhage history who lives far from emergency services): A client with a history of postpartum hemorrhage combined with limited proximity to emergency care represents a higher risk situation requiring closer follow up and clear contingency planning. The other clients described are progressing well and do not represent the same level of concern. Prioritizing follow up based on risk factors reflects sound clinical judgment.
  8. The correct answer is A (Weight loss of up to about ten percent of birth weight can be normal initially): A weight loss of up to approximately ten percent of birth weight in the first several days is generally considered within normal limits, particularly with effective feeding established. Weight loss exceeding this threshold or ongoing loss beyond the expected nadir warrants further assessment. Providing accurate expected ranges reassures parents while supporting appropriate monitoring.
  9. The correct answer is B (By approximately two weeks of age in most healthy, well feeding newborns): Most healthy newborns regain their birth weight by around two weeks of age when feeding is well established. Failure to regain weight by this point warrants further feeding assessment and possible consultation. This milestone is a meaningful and commonly used indicator of adequate newborn nutrition.
  10. The correct answer is C (Mild discomfort commonly improves over the first two to three weeks postpartum): Mild perineal discomfort following a minor laceration typically improves gradually over the first two to three weeks as tissue heals. Resolution within twenty four hours would be unusually fast, while pain persisting unchanged for months or automatically indicating infection are not accurate expectations. Providing a realistic timeline supports appropriate reassurance and monitoring for atypical findings.
  11. The correct answer is D (Persistent significant pain that has not improved since the initial repair): Pain that remains significant and unimproved by two weeks postpartum is atypical and warrants closer examination for possible infection, poor wound healing, or other complications. The other findings described are consistent with expected, improving healing and would not require additional concern. Distinguishing expected healing from atypical findings guides appropriate follow up.
  12. The correct answer is B (The postpartum period is generally considered to last approximately six weeks): The postpartum period is traditionally defined as the six weeks following birth, during which the body undergoes involution and returns toward its prepregnancy state, though some recovery processes continue beyond this window. This defined period underpins standard postpartum visit scheduling in midwifery care. The other timeframes do not reflect the recognized clinical convention.
Case C811

A midwife attends a spontaneous vaginal birth at a birth centre. The newborn is placed skin to skin on the mother's chest immediately after birth. The midwife begins the initial assessment while drying the infant and observing colour, tone, and respiratory effort.

  1. The correct answer is A (A score of 6, reflecting mild difficulty with transition): Each of the five Apgar components, heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, reflex irritability, and colour, is scored 0 to 2, and this newborn scores 2 for heart rate over 100, 1 for weak cry, 1 for some flexion, 1 for grimace, and 1 for acrocyanosis, totaling 6. A score of 6 indicates mild difficulty with transition and warrants continued close observation and stimulation rather than full resuscitation. The midwife should keep the newborn warm, dry, and stimulated while reassessing at 5 minutes. Scores of 8 or 9 would require full flexion, a vigorous cry, and pink colour throughout, none of which are present here.
  2. The correct answer is B (It helps prevent heat loss and supports thermal stability): Newborns lose heat rapidly through evaporation, convection, radiation, and conduction, and skin to skin contact with the mother transfers maternal body heat while a hat and blanket reduce further loss. Thermal stability also supports normal glucose metabolism and reduces newborn stress during transition. Skin to skin contact additionally promotes bonding and early breastfeeding initiation, but its primary physiologic benefit immediately after birth is thermoregulation. It does not eliminate the need for ongoing assessment of temperature and respiratory status.
  3. The correct answer is A (The respiratory effort and rate are within the normal newborn range): A normal newborn respiratory rate is generally 30 to 60 breaths per minute without signs of distress such as grunting, flaring, or retractions. Mild acrocyanosis of the hands and feet is a common and expected finding in the first hours after birth and does not indicate hypoxia on its own. Vigorous crying and full limb movement further support a reassuring transition. The midwife should continue routine observation rather than intervening.
  4. The correct answer is D (Delaying cord clamping for at least one to three minutes): Current evidence supports delayed cord clamping for vigorous term newborns to allow placental transfusion, which improves iron stores and reduces the risk of anemia in infancy. Delayed clamping can occur while the newborn remains skin to skin on the mother, and it does not require waiting for full placental delivery. Immediate clamping is reserved for specific situations such as a newborn requiring urgent resuscitation. Delayed clamping has not been shown to meaningfully increase maternal blood loss.
  5. The correct answer is D (Continue scoring every 5 minutes until the score reaches 7): When the 5 minute Apgar score remains below 7, standard practice is to continue scoring every 5 minutes, up to 20 minutes, until the score reaches 7 or resuscitation efforts change. This ongoing scoring documents the trajectory of transition and supports decisions about consultation or transfer. Stopping after 5 minutes or waiting arbitrarily would miss important information about whether the newborn is improving. A persistently low score is also a trigger for physician consultation.
  6. The correct answer is D (A dislocated hip being reduced back into the socket): A positive Ortolani maneuver produces a palpable clunk as a dislocated femoral head reduces into the acetabulum during abduction, suggesting developmental dysplasia of the hip. This finding requires referral for hip ultrasound and orthopedic follow up rather than reassurance. A negative or normal examination would show smooth, symmetric movement without any clunk or instability. This finding is unrelated to fracture and would not be expected to resolve without assessment.
  7. The correct answer is A (These are common benign newborn variants needing no treatment): Milia, small white bumps from blocked sebaceous glands, Mongolian spots, bluish grey pigmented patches common in darker skinned infants, and small preauricular skin tags are all common, benign newborn findings that typically require no treatment and resolve or remain stable over time. None of these findings alone suggest a syndrome or require urgent referral. Documenting Mongolian spots is important so they are not later mistaken for bruising, but reassurance is the appropriate response here.
Case C818

A midwife performs the routine head to toe newborn examination at 24 hours of age on a term infant who has been feeding well and voiding normally.

  1. The correct answer is C (Consult a physician promptly to investigate for coarctation of the aorta): Weak or absent femoral pulses with normal brachial pulses is a classic sign of coarctation of the aorta or another left sided obstructive cardiac lesion, and this finding falls outside normal midwifery scope. Prompt consultation allows for echocardiography and further cardiac assessment before the newborn becomes symptomatic. Waiting or documenting this as normal risks delayed diagnosis of a potentially serious congenital heart defect. This is not a finding that resolves or should be reassessed weeks later.
  2. The correct answer is A (The lens and posterior structures of both eyes are likely clear): A symmetric, bright red-orange red reflex in both eyes suggests the cornea, lens, and vitreous are clear and there is no obvious opacity or mass obstructing light reflection from the retina. An abnormal, white, dull, or asymmetric reflex would raise concern for cataract, retinoblastoma, or other structural eye disease requiring urgent ophthalmology referral. A normal symmetric red reflex does not require repeat testing within the hour. This is a routine, reassuring component of the newborn eye examination.
  3. The correct answer is A (It confirms two arteries and one vein, the expected normal count): A normal umbilical cord contains two arteries and one vein, and confirming three vessels is a routine part of the newborn examination. A single umbilical artery, by contrast, can be associated with an increased risk of renal or other congenital anomalies and may prompt further assessment. A three-vessel cord with a soft, non-distended abdomen and no palpable masses is a reassuring finding requiring no additional intervention. It does not require reclamping or imaging.
  4. The correct answer is C (This is a normal response to maternal hormone withdrawal): Mild labial swelling and a small amount of white or mucoid vaginal discharge, sometimes with a small amount of blood, are common and benign findings in term female newborns caused by withdrawal from maternal hormones after birth. This finding resolves on its own within the first weeks of life and requires no treatment or referral. It is not indicative of infection, trauma, or a disorder of sexual development. Parents can be reassured this is a normal transitional finding.
  5. The correct answer is C (All three values fall within the expected normal newborn range): Normal newborn heart rate typically ranges from 110 to 160 beats per minute, respiratory rate from 30 to 60 breaths per minute, and axillary temperature from 36.5 to 37.5 degrees Celsius. All three of this newborn's values fall comfortably within those expected ranges, indicating a stable, reassuring examination. None of the values suggest tachycardia, apnea risk, or hypothermia requiring intervention. The midwife should continue routine monitoring per the standard newborn assessment schedule.
Case C823

A term newborn born at 39 weeks gestation is noted to have visible yellowing of the face and chest at 30 hours of age. The mother is Rh negative and the newborn's blood type has not yet been confirmed.

  1. The correct answer is C (Obtain a transcutaneous or serum bilirubin measurement promptly): Visual assessment of jaundice is unreliable for estimating the actual bilirubin level, particularly once jaundice progresses beyond the face, so a transcutaneous or serum bilirubin measurement should be obtained promptly and plotted against age in hours on a validated nomogram. This objective measurement guides decisions about phototherapy and the need for physician consultation. Waiting several days would delay recognition of significant hyperbilirubinemia. Jaundice extending to the chest is not something that can be assumed benign without measurement.
  2. The correct answer is A (Whether hemolytic disease from Rh or ABO incompatibility is present): Jaundice appearing within the first 24 to 48 hours, especially with a known Rh negative mother, raises concern for hemolytic disease from Rh or ABO blood group incompatibility, which can cause rapid bilirubin rise. Confirming the newborn's blood type, direct antiglobulin test, and hemoglobin helps identify hemolysis as the cause. Mode of birth and delayed cord clamping are not primary causes of early pathologic jaundice. Gestational diabetes is a risk factor for other neonatal complications but not specifically for this early jaundice pattern.
  3. The correct answer is D (Consult a physician promptly to arrange phototherapy without delay): A bilirubin level plotting in the high risk zone on a validated nomogram indicates the newborn requires phototherapy, and this exceeds normal midwifery scope of practice, requiring prompt physician consultation and transfer for treatment. Delaying care by a week or relying on home sun exposure, which is not an effective or safe treatment, risks progression toward dangerous bilirubin levels. Routine discharge without arranging treatment would also be inappropriate given the confirmed high risk result.
  4. The correct answer is C (Possible acute bilirubin encephalopathy requiring emergency transfer): Lethargy, poor feeding, a high pitched cry, and increasing muscle tone in a newborn with significant hyperbilirubinemia are warning signs of acute bilirubin encephalopathy, a neurologic emergency requiring immediate emergency transfer for urgent treatment. These findings are not a normal response to stimulation or a minor illness and should never be attributed to routine sleepiness. Recognizing these signs promptly and escalating care immediately is critical to preventing permanent neurologic injury known as kernicterus.
  5. The correct answer is C (Pathologic jaundice typically appears within the first 24 hours): Physiologic jaundice usually appears after 24 hours of age, peaks around day 3 to 5, and resolves without treatment, while jaundice appearing within the first 24 hours is considered pathologic and warrants prompt investigation for causes such as hemolysis or infection. Physiologic jaundice does not always require phototherapy and typically does not appear after two weeks in a healthy term newborn. Recognizing the timing of onset helps the midwife decide when urgent bilirubin testing and consultation are needed.
Case C828

A midwife is preparing to complete the routine newborn screening tests before a planned early discharge home following an uncomplicated vaginal birth.

  1. The correct answer is A (After at least 24 hours of age and adequate feeding): Newborn metabolic screening is most accurate when collected after at least 24 hours of age and once the newborn has fed adequately, since some metabolic conditions are only detectable after sufficient protein intake and metabolic adaptation. Testing immediately after birth or too early can produce false negative results. The test should be completed before discharge whenever possible, with arrangements made for timely collection if early discharge occurs. Waiting until after discharge risks delayed or missed screening.
  2. The correct answer is C (A significant oxygen saturation difference suggesting a critical heart defect): Critical congenital heart disease screening compares pre-ductal oxygen saturation from the right hand with post-ductal saturation from either foot, and a low saturation or a significant difference between the two sites can indicate a critical heart defect requiring urgent cardiology evaluation. This screening is unrelated to hypoglycemia, umbilical vessel count, or jaundice. A passing screen shows saturations at or above the accepted threshold with minimal difference between sites, supporting safe discharge home.
  3. The correct answer is D (Arrange timely follow up rescreening or diagnostic audiology assessment): A refer result on newborn hearing screening does not confirm hearing loss but requires timely follow up rescreening or diagnostic audiology assessment, ideally completed within the first few months of life, to allow for early intervention if hearing loss is confirmed. Even a unilateral refer result requires follow up rather than reassurance, since early identification significantly improves speech and language outcomes. Waiting until the routine two month visit or taking no action risks delayed diagnosis and intervention.
  4. The correct answer is B (It prevents vitamin K deficiency bleeding, a rare but serious risk): Newborns have low vitamin K stores at birth, placing them at risk for vitamin K deficiency bleeding, including rare but serious intracranial hemorrhage, and a single intramuscular dose of vitamin K effectively prevents this condition. Vitamin K does not prevent infection or sepsis and does not replace other newborn screening. Families have the right to decline this injection after informed discussion of the risks and benefits, and the midwife should support informed choice rather than presenting it as mandatory.
  5. The correct answer is A (To prevent serious eye infection from maternal gonococcal exposure): Erythromycin eye ointment is offered prophylactically to prevent ophthalmia neonatorum caused by exposure to gonococcal or, less reliably, chlamydial organisms during birth, which can cause serious eye infection and vision loss if untreated. It does not affect visual acuity development or jaundice and is not a treatment for an already established infection, which would require targeted antibiotic therapy. As with vitamin K, this is an informed choice discussion rather than a mandatory intervention.
Case C833

A midwife is called to reassess a term newborn at home on day 2 of life. The parents report the baby has been unusually sleepy, is feeding poorly, and felt cool to touch overnight.

  1. The correct answer is D (Arrange emergency transfer for urgent physician assessment now): Grunting, a respiratory rate of 70, difficulty rousing, and hypothermia together are concerning signs of possible neonatal sepsis or significant respiratory compromise, and these findings require emergency transfer for urgent medical assessment rather than watchful waiting at home. Delaying care by rechecking vital signs later or waiting for a scheduled visit risks rapid deterioration in an unwell newborn. Skin to skin contact and warming are supportive measures but do not replace urgent evaluation of a sick newborn.
  2. The correct answer is B (Neonatal hypoglycemia requiring prompt glucose assessment and feeding): Jitteriness, floppiness, poor feeding, and lethargy are classic signs of neonatal hypoglycemia, which can occur with poor intake and cold stress, and the midwife should check a blood glucose promptly and support feeding or arrange appropriate treatment as part of the urgent workup. This is not a normal sleep pattern, an allergic reaction, or an unrelated skin finding. Hypoglycemia in a symptomatic newborn is a time sensitive concern that must be addressed alongside the broader evaluation for possible sepsis.
  3. The correct answer is A (Clearly explain the urgent findings and support informed consent for transfer): When parents are hesitant, the midwife's role is to clearly and compassionately explain the specific findings, the reasons transfer is urgently needed, and the risks of delay, supporting the parents to give informed consent for care. Proceeding without communication or abandoning a clinically necessary transfer both fail to respect informed choice and the newborn's safety. Delaying the conversation until the ambulance arrives wastes valuable time when clear communication should begin immediately. Documentation of this discussion is also essential.
  4. The correct answer is A (These signs of respiratory distress require urgent physician consultation): Nasal flaring, subcostal retractions, and a respiratory rate above the normal range together indicate significant respiratory distress in a newborn, which falls outside normal midwifery scope and requires urgent physician consultation regardless of whether fever is also present. These findings are never considered a routine or expected part of the first day of life. Delaying assessment by monitoring for another day risks deterioration in a newborn with an underlying respiratory or cardiac problem.
  5. The correct answer is A (This weight loss is within the expected normal range): Weight loss of up to 7 to 10 percent of birth weight is considered within the normal range for breastfed newborns in the first few days of life, particularly when feeding, voiding, and stooling patterns are otherwise reassuring. Weight loss in this range does not automatically indicate feeding failure, require formula supplementation, or suggest a metabolic disorder. The midwife should continue to support breastfeeding and monitor that the newborn regains birth weight by approximately 2 weeks of age.
  6. The correct answer is D (Audible swallowing, adequate wet diapers, and appropriate weight trend): Reassuring signs of effective breastfeeding include audible or visible swallowing during feeds, an increasing number of wet and soiled diapers over the first days, and a weight trend showing expected loss followed by regain toward birth weight. A newborn sleeping through the night without waking to feed or feeding for only a very brief time at the breast are both concerning for inadequate intake rather than reassuring. Frequent unexplained crying between feeds may also suggest hunger and warrants further feeding assessment.
  7. The correct answer is A (Consult a physician promptly given significant weight loss and jaundice): Weight loss exceeding 10 percent, jaundice extending to the abdomen, and reduced urine output together suggest inadequate intake and possible significant hyperbilirubinemia, warranting prompt physician consultation and further evaluation, including a bilirubin level and feeding assessment. This combination of findings is not common or reassuring and requires action rather than watchful waiting. Delaying assessment until the two week visit risks worsening dehydration and rising bilirubin levels in a vulnerable newborn.
  8. The correct answer is C (Perform a focused feeding assessment and arrange close follow up): Weight loss reaching 10 percent is a threshold that warrants a focused feeding assessment, including latch, milk transfer, and maternal breast changes, along with close follow up to confirm the trend is improving rather than continuing to worsen. It should not be dismissed as needing no action, since 10 percent is at the upper edge of normal and requires closer attention. Stopping breastfeeding or arranging emergency transfer would be premature without first assessing the cause and trend of the weight loss.
Case C841

A midwife attends a spontaneous vaginal birth at 39 weeks gestation. Immediately after birth, the newborn is placed skin-to-skin on the mother's chest. At one minute, the newborn has a heart rate of 130 beats per minute, is crying vigorously, has good flexed tone, grimaces to stimulation, and is pink centrally with blue hands and feet.

  1. The correct answer is C (A score of 9, reflecting the peripheral cyanosis component): Apgar scoring assigns two points each for heart rate over 100, strong cry, good tone, and grimace to stimulation, giving eight points, plus one point for acrocyanosis since the trunk is pink but the hands and feet remain blue. Full central cyanosis or pallor would score zero for colour, while complete pink colour throughout would score two. A total of nine is the expected score for a vigorous newborn with isolated peripheral cyanosis, which is a common and normal finding in the first minutes of life. This does not represent a low or concerning score.
  2. The correct answer is A (Continue skin-to-skin contact and dry the newborn under a warm blanket): a vigorous, crying, well-toned term newborn requires only routine supportive care, which means drying to prevent evaporative heat loss and maintaining uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact with the mother. Routine suctioning of a vigorous newborn is not recommended and can cause vagal stimulation or trauma. Transfer to a radiant warmer and supplemental oxygen are reserved for newborns showing signs of respiratory compromise or poor transition, neither of which is present here. Skin-to-skin contact itself effectively supports thermoregulation in a stable newborn.
  3. The correct answer is C (Delay clamping for at least one minute, or until cord pulsation stops): current evidence-based guidelines recommend delayed cord clamping for uncomplicated term births, allowing placental blood transfusion to the newborn, which improves iron stores and reduces the incidence of anemia in infancy. Immediate clamping within seconds is no longer routinely recommended for vigorous newborns and does not reduce jaundice risk, in fact delayed clamping can slightly increase mild jaundice, which is an acceptable tradeoff. Clamping is not withheld pending full placental delivery, and resuscitation needs would only shorten, not eliminate, the delay when feasible.
  4. The correct answer is B (No consultation is needed, since transition is proceeding normally): a reassuring five-minute Apgar score with a stable heart rate, good colour, and vigorous activity confirms that the newborn has completed a normal transition, so no consultation or transfer is required. The earlier peripheral cyanosis at one minute was a normal finding, not a marker of prolonged distress, and does not itself trigger mandatory consultation once resolved. Routine blood gas sampling is not indicated for an asymptomatic, vigorous newborn. Ongoing routine newborn assessments should still continue as part of standard care.
  5. The correct answer is B (Developmental hip instability suggestive of dysplasia of the hip): the Ortolani maneuver detects a dislocated hip that can be reduced back into the acetabulum, while the Barlow maneuver detects a hip that can be dislocated out of a normally located position, together screening for developmental dysplasia of the hip. Neither maneuver assesses respiratory function, limb movement symmetry generally, or cardiac murmurs, which are evaluated through separate components of the routine newborn examination. A positive finding on either test warrants further evaluation and appropriate referral. This screening is a standard part of every newborn physical examination.
  6. The correct answer is C (The rooting reflex, which supports early breastfeeding and latch): stroking a newborn's cheek and observing the head turn toward the stimulus with mouth opening is the classic rooting reflex, which helps the newborn locate the breast and initiate feeding. The Moro reflex is triggered by a sudden change in position or loss of support and produces symmetric arm extension and flexion, not cheek stimulation. The tonic neck reflex produces an asymmetric fencing posture with head turning, and the Babinski reflex involves toe fanning after stroking the sole of the foot. This reflex is a normal and expected finding in a term newborn.
Case C847

A term newborn is born at a birth centre and dried immediately, then placed skin-to-skin under a warm blanket with a cap applied. At thirty minutes of age, an axillary temperature is 36.2 degrees Celsius, and the newborn is alert and rooting well.

  1. The correct answer is A (Continue uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact with the cap left on): sustained skin-to-skin contact with maternal body heat, combined with a cap to reduce heat loss from the newborn's relatively large head surface area, is the most effective evidence-based method to support thermoregulation in a stable term newborn. Moving the newborn to an incubator is unnecessary when the temperature is already within normal range and interrupts bonding and breastfeeding initiation. Bathing soon after birth increases evaporative heat loss and is delayed for thermoregulation reasons. Separate swaddling in a bassinet removes the direct warming benefit of maternal skin contact.
  2. The correct answer is C (Evaporative heat loss from wet skin exposed to room air): a newborn emerges from birth covered in amniotic fluid, and as this fluid evaporates from the skin surface it draws heat away rapidly, making evaporative loss the most significant mechanism immediately after birth. Prompt drying directly addresses this specific mechanism by removing the wet surface. Radiant, conductive, and convective losses are also relevant to overall newborn thermoregulation, but they are addressed through warm surfaces, warm blankets, and avoiding drafts rather than through drying itself. Recognizing the dominant mechanism guides which intervention is prioritized first.
  3. The correct answer is D (Consult a pediatrician and arrange transfer for further assessment): persistent hypothermia below 36.0 degrees Celsius despite an hour of appropriate warming measures falls outside normal midwifery scope and can indicate an underlying problem such as sepsis or metabolic disturbance, warranting prompt consultation and transfer for further evaluation. Simply repeating the same warming measures for another hour delays necessary escalation. A warm feed alone will not address a persistent thermoregulation failure of this degree. Discharging home without resolving unexplained persistent hypothermia would miss a potentially serious underlying condition.
  4. The correct answer is C (A normal red reflex, suggesting clear ocular media in each eye): an orange-red glow reflected symmetrically from both retinas during the red reflex test indicates that the light path through the cornea, lens, and vitreous is clear, which is the expected normal finding in a healthy newborn. A cataract would instead produce a white, dulled, or absent reflex rather than the described orange-red glow, and would prompt referral. Retinal hemorrhage and congenital glaucoma present with different specific findings on more detailed examination, not a normal bilateral red reflex. This is a routine, reassuring component of the newborn eye examination.
Case C851

A breastfed newborn born at 39 weeks gestation is noted to have visible jaundice of the face and chest at 22 hours of age. The newborn is otherwise alert, feeding, and voiding appropriately. The mother's blood type is O positive and the newborn's blood type is A positive.

  1. The correct answer is B (This is pathologic given the timing, warranting urgent bilirubin measurement and consultation): jaundice appearing before 24 hours of age is always considered pathologic rather than physiologic, because normal physiologic jaundice does not become visible until after the first day of life. This early timing requires prompt total serum bilirubin measurement and pediatric consultation to identify an underlying cause such as hemolysis. Attributing this presentation to simple breastfeeding jaundice or dismissing it as unrelated would delay recognition of a potentially significant hemolytic process. Timely assessment allows appropriate treatment to be started if needed.
  2. The correct answer is D (The level meets criteria for phototherapy and requires prompt initiation): the treatment decision is based on comparing the newborn's measured total serum bilirubin directly against the age-specific phototherapy treatment threshold for infants at this risk level, rather than on nomogram risk-zone placement alone. Here, a total serum bilirubin of 220 micromol per litre at 22 hours of age, in a newborn with ABO incompatibility risk given a type O mother and type A newborn, exceeds the phototherapy treatment threshold for this age and risk category, so phototherapy should be initiated promptly. Waiting a full week to repeat testing would allow the level to rise further and increase risk unnecessarily. Describing the level as too low to matter ignores that it already exceeds the treatment threshold. Exchange transfusion is reserved for markedly higher levels or those not responding to phototherapy, not as an automatic first step here.
  3. The correct answer is B (Possible ABO incompatibility leading to increased hemolysis and bilirubin production): when a mother with blood type O carries a fetus with a different blood type such as A, maternal antibodies can cross the placenta and cause increased breakdown of fetal red blood cells, raising bilirubin production and hyperbilirubinemia risk. Maternal iron intake through breastmilk does not cause hemolytic jaundice in the newborn. An inherited clotting disorder would present with bleeding concerns rather than jaundice. Prolonged rupture of membranes is associated with infection risk rather than being the primary driver of hemolytic jaundice in this scenario.
  4. The correct answer is D (Repeat serum bilirubin levels regularly to track the response to treatment): ongoing monitoring of bilirubin levels during and after phototherapy is essential to confirm the level is trending down appropriately and to determine when treatment can safely be discontinued. Stopping all monitoring once the lights are switched on would risk missing an inadequate treatment response or a rebound rise after discontinuation. Waiting a full week without checking levels is far too long an interval for a newborn actively receiving phototherapy. Weight alone does not reflect bilirubin trends and cannot substitute for direct laboratory monitoring.
  5. The correct answer is C (Between 24 and 72 hours of age, once feeding is established): collecting the newborn screening blood spot sample after at least 24 hours of age and once feeding is underway improves the reliability of results for metabolic conditions that require some postnatal feeding exposure to detect accurately. Collecting the sample immediately at birth, before any feeding, can produce false-negative results for certain conditions. Waiting until the two-week visit delays detection of time-sensitive conditions beyond the recommended window. This screening is offered to all newborns regardless of whether illness is present.
  6. The correct answer is B (Intramuscular injection to prevent vitamin K deficiency bleeding): a single intramuscular dose of vitamin K shortly after birth is the standard recommended method to prevent vitamin K deficiency bleeding, since newborns are born with low vitamin K stores and immature liver synthesis of clotting factors. Oral dosing regimens exist but require multiple doses over weeks and are considered less reliably protective than the single intramuscular injection. Vitamin K is not given intravenously for this indication and does not treat anemia from blood loss. It also has no role in preventing early-onset sepsis.
Case C857

A term newborn weighed 3400 grams at birth. On day three, the newborn weighs 3230 grams and is exclusively breastfeeding eight to ten times per day, with adequate wet diapers and one to two stools daily. The parents are concerned about the weight change.

  1. The correct answer is B (About 5 percent loss, which is within the expected normal range): a weight decrease from 3400 grams to 3230 grams represents approximately a 5 percent loss of birth weight, which falls well within the normal range of up to about 7 to 10 percent expected in exclusively breastfed newborns during the first few days. This finding, combined with adequate wet diapers and regular stooling, is reassuring rather than concerning. A loss in the 15 to 20 percent range would be considered excessive and would warrant closer feeding assessment, which is not the case with this calculation.
  2. The correct answer is D (By approximately ten to fourteen days of age in most cases): healthy term breastfed newborns with adequate feeding and elimination patterns typically regain their birth weight by ten to fourteen days of age, following the expected initial weight loss in the first few days. Regaining weight within the first twenty-four hours is not physiologically realistic given normal initial fluid shifts. Waiting until six months of age would represent a significant and concerning delay in growth. Formula feeding is not required for normal weight regain in an effectively breastfeeding newborn.
  3. The correct answer is D (Weight loss exceeding ten percent combined with lethargy and poor feeding): a weight loss greater than ten percent of birth weight together with lethargy and poor feeding indicates possible inadequate intake or an underlying problem, which falls outside normal expected patterns and warrants midwifery consultation. Continued adequate voiding with steady weight gain, effective audible swallowing, and normal soft yellow stools are all reassuring signs of adequate feeding rather than concerning findings. Recognizing this combination of red flags allows timely referral before dehydration or other complications develop.
  4. The correct answer is B (Preventing ophthalmia neonatorum caused by maternal gonorrhea or chlamydia exposure): erythromycin ophthalmic ointment is applied prophylactically to prevent ophthalmia neonatorum, a serious eye infection that can occur when a newborn is exposed to maternal gonococcal or chlamydial organisms during vaginal birth. This measure does not improve visual acuity development or protect against jaundice-related eye changes. Conjunctival dryness from the birth process is not the indication for this treatment. This prophylaxis is offered as a routine, evidence-based component of newborn care regardless of known maternal infection status.
Case C861

A term newborn born thirty minutes ago at a home birth is breathing at 72 breaths per minute with visible intercostal retractions and audible grunting on expiration. The newborn's colour is pale pink with mild central cyanosis noted around the lips.

  1. The correct answer is C (Signs of respiratory distress that require prompt further assessment): a respiratory rate of 72 breaths per minute with intercostal retractions, expiratory grunting, and central cyanosis are classic signs of respiratory distress in a newborn and indicate that the infant is working hard to maintain oxygenation. These findings are not consistent with normal transition, which would show a settling respiratory rate and pink colour without grunting or retractions. Simple retained fetal lung fluid can present similarly but cannot be assumed without further assessment given the central cyanosis present. This presentation requires prompt evaluation rather than reassurance.
  2. The correct answer is B (Neonatal hypoglycemia related to fetal hyperinsulinemia during pregnancy): infants of mothers with diabetes are exposed to higher maternal glucose levels in utero, leading to fetal hyperinsulinemia, and after birth the sudden loss of maternal glucose supply combined with continued high insulin levels places these newborns at increased risk of hypoglycemia. Congenital hip dysplasia is not specifically linked to maternal diabetes. Delayed meconium passage and clavicle fracture from vacuum use are not the primary anticipated risks in this scenario. Blood glucose monitoring is an important part of care for infants of diabetic mothers.
  3. The correct answer is A (Temperature instability, lethargy, and poor feeding alongside the distress): the combination of temperature instability, lethargy, and poor feeding together with respiratory distress is a concerning constellation of nonspecific signs that can indicate neonatal sepsis and requires urgent evaluation. Stable temperature with a vigorous cry and strong reflexes, normal colour with good tone and active feeding, or a single episode of spitting up are all reassuring findings that would not raise concern for sepsis. Recognizing this pattern of subtle but combined red flags is essential for timely recognition of a potentially serious newborn illness.
  4. The correct answer is B (Arrange urgent transfer to hospital for pediatric assessment and support): a newborn showing ongoing signs of respiratory distress at a home birth requires urgent transfer to a hospital setting where pediatric assessment, monitoring, and any needed respiratory support can be provided promptly. Documenting findings and waiting until the routine two-week visit would dangerously delay necessary care for an acutely unwell newborn. Continuing skin-to-skin contact alone without escalating care ignores the clinical urgency of ongoing distress. Reassuring the family that grunting requires no follow-up would similarly fail to recognize a genuine warning sign.
  5. The correct answer is C (Waiting until feeding is established improves the accuracy of the results): the newborn screening blood spot test relies on detecting metabolic markers that require some period of postnatal feeding to reach measurable levels, so collecting the sample too early can produce inaccurate or false-negative results. There is no legal requirement restricting collection to a hospital setting, and home birth midwives routinely collect this sample. The collection procedure itself carries minimal infection risk when performed with standard technique. The test is designed to detect conditions from early infancy, not only after the first month of life.
  6. The correct answer is A (Oral dosing schedules require multiple doses and have lower overall efficacy): intramuscular vitamin K provides a single reliable dose with well-established protection against vitamin K deficiency bleeding, while oral regimens require several doses over the first weeks of life and are associated with less consistent protection, particularly if a dose is missed or vomited. Injections are strongly recommended but families retain the right to decline after informed discussion, so it is not simply a legal mandate. Pain from a single injection is brief and does not outweigh the efficacy benefit. Vitamin K administration does not remove the need for ongoing newborn monitoring.
  7. The correct answer is D (A clavicle fracture, a recognized complication of birth trauma): palpable crepitus and an irregular contour along the clavicle in a large newborn following vaginal birth are classic findings of a clavicle fracture, which is one of the more common birth-related bony injuries, particularly with larger infants. This finding is not a normal variant and should be documented along with appropriate arm positioning guidance and follow-up. A brachial plexus injury would present with decreased arm movement rather than a bony contour abnormality alone, though the two can coexist. A cardiac anomaly would not present as clavicular crepitus.
  8. The correct answer is B (Family-centred rooming-in care that supports bonding and breastfeeding): keeping a healthy newborn with the mother continuously rather than separating them into a nursery reflects family-centred, rooming-in care, which is associated with improved bonding, easier breastfeeding initiation, and better parental confidence. This practice is not primarily an infection control requirement, since healthy newborns can safely remain with their mothers under standard precautions. It is not related to billing considerations, and it is not merely a temporary staffing-driven arrangement, but rather an intentional model of evidence-based postpartum care.
  9. The correct answer is D (Early identification allows treatment before serious harm develops): newborn screening for conditions such as congenital hypothyroidism and phenylketonuria allows these disorders to be identified and treated before significant, often irreversible, harm such as intellectual disability can occur, since both conditions are asymptomatic at birth but treatable if caught early. This screening has genuine treatment value and is not merely a record-keeping exercise. It does not replace the need for ongoing routine newborn examinations, and its purpose is individual early treatment rather than population statistics tracking alone.
  10. The correct answer is B (Arrange prompt assessment and consultation given these concerning signs): poor feeding combined with unusual lethargy in a newborn on day four postpartum are recognized warning signs that can indicate an underlying problem such as infection, dehydration, or metabolic disturbance, and warrant prompt assessment and consultation rather than reassurance or delay. Advising an immediate switch to formula without assessment would bypass identifying the underlying cause of the concerning symptoms. Waiting for the standard two-week visit would dangerously delay recognition of a potentially serious newborn condition that requires timely evaluation.
Case C871

A term newborn is born via spontaneous vaginal birth at a home birth attended by the midwife. At 1 minute, the infant has a heart rate of 90 beats per minute, slow irregular respirations, some flexion of the limbs, a grimace to stimulation, and a pink body with blue extremities.

  1. The correct answer is B (A score of 5, reflecting reduced points in each Apgar category): Each of the five Apgar components, heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, reflex irritability, and color, is scored 0, 1, or 2, and this newborn scores 1 point in every category. A heart rate under 100 scores 1, slow irregular respirations score 1, some flexion scores 1, a grimace response scores 1, and acrocyanosis with a pink body scores 1. Summing these five values gives a total 1 minute Apgar score of 5, which reflects moderate depression rather than a healthy transition or severe compromise.
  2. The correct answer is D (A score of 10, reflecting the maximum possible in every category): A heart rate of 130 scores 2, a strong spontaneous cry scores 2, active limb movement scores 2, a cough response to suctioning scores 2, and pink color throughout the entire body scores 2. Adding these five maximum values across all five Apgar components produces a total 5 minute score of 10. This reflects a full and reassuring recovery from the depressed findings noted at 1 minute of age.
  3. The correct answer is A (Proceed with routine newborn care and continue ongoing observation at home): A rise from an Apgar score of 5 at 1 minute to 10 at 5 minutes shows the newborn has transitioned well and is now vigorous with a normal heart rate, respirations, tone, reflexes, and color. There is no indication for hospital transfer, repeated scoring beyond routine timing, or supplemental oxygen once the infant is fully pink with normal tone and effort. The midwife should proceed with standard newborn care while continuing usual ongoing observation appropriate for a normal home birth.
Case C874

A midwife attending a home birth dries a healthy term newborn immediately after birth and places the infant skin to skin with the mother under a warm blanket. The room temperature is 21 Celsius. The newborn's axillary temperature at 30 minutes of age is 36.2 Celsius.

  1. The correct answer is D (Evaporation of amniotic fluid from the newborn's wet skin surface): A newborn is born wet with amniotic fluid, and evaporation from this wet skin causes rapid heat loss immediately after birth, more than the other mechanisms at this specific moment. Drying the newborn promptly removes this moisture and substantially reduces evaporative heat loss before skin to skin contact begins. Conduction, radiation, and convection remain relevant thermoregulation concerns throughout the newborn period, but evaporation is the priority reason drying comes first.
  2. The correct answer is D (Mild hypothermia, or cold stress, requiring active warming measures now): Normal newborn axillary temperature is generally considered to be between 36.5 and 37.5 Celsius, so a reading of 36.2 Celsius falls just below this normal range. This level is classified as mild hypothermia, sometimes called cold stress, rather than the more significant moderate hypothermia category. It requires prompt warming interventions rather than reassurance, and it does not meet the threshold for hyperthermia or for immediate hospital transfer on this finding alone.
  3. The correct answer is D (Keep the newborn skin to skin, add a warm hat and blanket): For mild hypothermia in an otherwise well term newborn, continued skin to skin contact with the mother combined with a warm hat and additional blanket is the recommended first line warming measure. This approach uses the mother's body heat effectively while avoiding unnecessary separation of mother and newborn or unnecessary hospital transfer for a mild finding. Bathing the newborn would increase heat loss rather than correct it, and the temperature should be rechecked after warming to confirm improvement.
Case C877

A midwife performs a routine head to toe examination on a 12 hour old term newborn. The infant has a soft swelling over the parietal region that crosses the sagittal suture line and does not change size or shape with the infant's position.

  1. The correct answer is A (Caput succedaneum resulting from pressure sustained during the birth process): Caput succedaneum is a diffuse, soft swelling of the scalp caused by pressure during birth, and it characteristically crosses suture lines and does not change with the infant's position, matching this exact description. A cephalohematoma, in contrast, is confined by the periosteum and therefore does not cross suture lines. Subgaleal hemorrhage is a more dangerous collection that can enlarge and shift with position, and craniosynostosis involves premature suture fusion rather than a soft swelling, so neither matches this presentation.
  2. The correct answer is D (Document the findings and consult a pediatrician for further assessment): A single palmar crease combined with low set ears can be associated with certain chromosomal conditions, and these findings warrant careful documentation and timely consultation with a pediatrician rather than being dismissed as normal variants. Discharging the newborn from midwifery care without referral would fail to ensure appropriate follow up for potentially significant findings. Delaying any action for a full week is not appropriate when findings suggesting a possible underlying condition are identified during the newborn examination.
  3. The correct answer is C (To screen specifically for developmental dysplasia of the hip joint): The Barlow maneuver attempts to dislocate an unstable hip posteriorly, while the Ortolani maneuver attempts to relocate a dislocated hip, and together they are the standard newborn screening tests for developmental dysplasia of the hip. Neither maneuver assesses grasp reflex strength, shoulder range of motion, or spinal alignment, which are evaluated through separate parts of the newborn examination. Early identification of hip instability allows for timely referral and management, which improves long term outcomes.
Case C880

A term newborn born at 39 weeks gestation is exclusively breastfeeding. At 36 hours of age, the midwife notes visible jaundice extending to the infant's trunk. A transcutaneous bilirubin measurement is obtained and plotted on the Bhutani nomogram.

  1. The correct answer is B (To determine this infant's risk of developing significant hyperbilirubinemia later): The Bhutani nomogram plots a bilirubin value against the infant's precise age in hours to stratify the risk of developing significant hyperbilirubinemia requiring treatment, since a given bilirubin value carries different risk depending on how early it appears. This tool does not calculate fluid requirements, estimate gestational age, or assess weight gain, which are evaluated through separate clinical measures. Using age specific percentile zones allows the midwife to identify infants who need closer monitoring or earlier follow up.
  2. The correct answer is A (Exclusive breastfeeding combined with suboptimal milk intake in the newborn): Exclusive breastfeeding with suboptimal intake is a well recognized risk factor for significant hyperbilirubinemia, because inadequate milk transfer can lead to increased enterohepatic circulation of bilirubin and higher levels. Gestational age greater than 41 weeks, maternal blood type O negative with Rh negative status alone, and birth weight above the 90th percentile are not established risk factors for significant hyperbilirubinemia in the same way. Recognizing feeding related risk allows the midwife to assess latch and intake alongside monitoring bilirubin trends.
  3. The correct answer is D (Arrange consultation and closer follow up with repeat bilirubin testing): A bilirubin value in the high risk zone on the Bhutani nomogram indicates an elevated likelihood of progressing to significant hyperbilirubinemia, so consultation and closer follow up with repeat testing is the appropriate response rather than routine reassurance. Discontinuing breastfeeding temporarily is not the recommended first response to jaundice in an otherwise well newborn. Waiting until day 5 without earlier reassessment would delay identification of a rising bilirubin level that may require timely treatment.
  4. The correct answer is D (Pathologic jaundice requiring urgent evaluation and prompt medical consultation): Jaundice appearing within the first 24 hours of life is always considered pathologic rather than physiologic, because it suggests an underlying process such as hemolysis rather than the typical delayed rise seen in normal newborn jaundice. This timing requires urgent evaluation and prompt consultation rather than reassurance or routine follow up. Treating early onset jaundice as a normal variant that resolves without consultation would risk missing a serious underlying cause requiring timely treatment.
Case C884

A breastfeeding term newborn is seen by the midwife for a home visit on day 4 of life. The mother reports the infant is feeding 8 to 10 times per 24 hours, has 4 wet diapers and 3 yellow seedy stools in the past 24 hours, and has lost 8% of birth weight.

  1. The correct answer is C (Within the expected range for a breastfed newborn at this age): Weight loss of up to approximately 7 to 10% of birth weight by day 3 to 5 of life is considered within the expected range for a healthy breastfed newborn, as long as feeding, voiding, and stooling patterns are otherwise reassuring. This finding alone does not indicate that breastfeeding has failed, does not require immediate hospital transfer, and is not a sign of congenital heart disease. The midwife should continue to monitor feeding and confirm weight regain over the following days.
  2. The correct answer is B (Feeding intake is likely adequate for this newborn's current age): Yellow, seedy stools indicate that meconium has cleared and that the newborn is receiving sufficient milk volume, and together with an appropriate wet diaper count and feeding frequency, this pattern suggests adequate intake at day 4 of life. These findings do not indicate a need for immediate formula supplementation, do not suggest biliary obstruction, and do not support stopping breastfeeding. The midwife should continue to monitor weight and elimination pattern to confirm this trend continues.
  3. The correct answer is B (Reweigh the infant to confirm birth weight regained by two weeks): Given the reassuring feeding and elimination pattern alongside an expected degree of weight loss, the appropriate plan is to reweigh the infant to confirm that birth weight is regained by approximately two weeks of age, which is the standard expected timeline. Discontinuing all weight monitoring would miss an opportunity to confirm adequate growth, and recommending formula supplementation at every feed is not indicated when current findings are reassuring. Assuming normalization without any further follow up would not meet standard newborn care practice.
  4. The correct answer is A (Significant respiratory distress requiring urgent emergency hospital transfer now): Grunting, nasal flaring, tachypnea with a respiratory rate of 72, and central cyanosis together are classic signs of significant respiratory distress in a newborn and are not consistent with a normal transitional pattern or a benign finding. These signs indicate the newborn is working hard to breathe and may not be oxygenating adequately, which is a medical emergency rather than a feeding related issue. This presentation requires urgent recognition and immediate action rather than reassurance or a delayed response.
  5. The correct answer is B (Activate emergency transfer to hospital without any further delay): A newborn with grunting, nasal flaring, tachypnea, and central cyanosis requires immediate emergency transfer to hospital, since these findings indicate significant respiratory compromise that can worsen rapidly without prompt medical intervention. Scheduling a routine follow up visit, recommending increased breastfeeding, or advising overnight monitoring at home would all dangerously delay necessary emergency care. The midwife's priority in this situation is rapid activation of the emergency transfer system while providing appropriate supportive care until arrival.
  6. The correct answer is C (Between 24 and 72 hours of age, after adequate feeding occurs): Newborn blood spot screening for metabolic and genetic conditions is most accurate when collected between 24 and 72 hours of age, allowing time for adequate feeding to occur so that certain metabolic markers can be reliably detected. Collecting the sample immediately at birth or before adequate feeding has occurred can produce inaccurate results for some conditions. Waiting until 2 weeks of age would delay identification of conditions that require early treatment to prevent serious harm.
  7. The correct answer is D (To prevent vitamin K deficiency bleeding in the newborn period): Newborns are born with low vitamin K stores, and intramuscular vitamin K prevents vitamin K deficiency bleeding, a potentially serious and sometimes life threatening condition that can affect the newborn in the days to months after birth. Vitamin K administration does not promote meconium passage, does not reduce the risk of neonatal jaundice, and does not support thermoregulation. This is a standard preventive measure offered with informed parental consent as part of routine Canadian newborn care.
  8. The correct answer is C (Prevention of ophthalmia neonatorum from gonococcal or chlamydial infection): Erythromycin ophthalmic ointment is applied to prevent ophthalmia neonatorum, a serious eye infection that can result from exposure to gonococcal or chlamydial organisms during birth and can cause permanent vision loss if untreated. It is a preventive measure rather than a treatment for conjunctivitis already present at birth, and it does not prevent retinopathy of prematurity or improve visual acuity. This is offered as standard preventive newborn care regardless of the specific birth setting.
  9. The correct answer is C (It is a benign dermal melanocytosis that fades over time): A flat, blue gray patch over the lower back or buttocks present at birth is most consistent with dermal melanocytosis, sometimes referred to by an older term, and this finding is benign and typically fades gradually over the early years of childhood. It does not represent bruising from birth trauma, a bleeding disorder, or a vascular malformation requiring treatment. The midwife should reassure the parents and document the finding clearly in the newborn record to avoid future confusion with bruising.
  10. The correct answer is A (To provide a standardized assessment of transition to extrauterine life): The Apgar score gives a standardized, reproducible way to assess how well a newborn is transitioning from intrauterine to extrauterine life at two specific time points, using heart rate, respiratory effort, tone, reflexes, and color. It was not designed to predict long term neurological outcome with certainty, to determine gestational age, or to guide the decision about vitamin K administration. Its main clinical value is communicating a newborn's early condition clearly among care providers.
  11. The correct answer is B (Conduction of heat directly to the cold metal scale surface): Conduction is heat loss through direct contact with a cooler surface, and placing a newborn directly onto an unwarmed metal scale causes rapid heat transfer from the infant's skin to the cold metal through this mechanism. Evaporation, radiation, and convection are also relevant to newborn thermoregulation, but they are not the primary mechanism at work when the newborn is in direct physical contact with a cold surface. Prewarming equipment and surfaces before contact is a standard newborn thermal protection practice.
  12. The correct answer is A (Evaluate feeding technique and consider consultation given this weight loss): A weight loss of 11% by day 3 exceeds the generally accepted upper limit of approximately 7 to 10% for early breastfed newborn weight loss, so the midwife should evaluate feeding technique closely and consider consultation to assess for an underlying cause. Treating this as a normal finding requiring no further assessment would risk missing inadequate intake or an underlying problem. Stopping breastfeeding entirely or waiting until day 10 without earlier reassessment would not address the concern in a timely way.
  13. The correct answer is C (By approximately 2 weeks of age in most healthy newborns): Most healthy term breastfed newborns are expected to regain their birth weight by approximately 2 weeks of age, after the initial expected weight loss in the first several days of life. Regaining weight by 24 hours of age is not physiologically realistic, and waiting until 6 weeks or 6 months would represent a significantly delayed timeline suggesting a feeding or growth concern. This 2 week benchmark is a standard reference point midwives use when assessing newborn growth.
  14. The correct answer is C (Urgent consultation and bilirubin testing given severe hyperbilirubinemia risk here): Jaundice extending to the soles of the feet along with new lethargy and poor feeding suggests a significantly elevated bilirubin level with an increased risk of severe hyperbilirubinemia and possible kernicterus, requiring urgent consultation and testing rather than reassurance. A routine follow up visit scheduled for a week later would dangerously delay necessary treatment. Advising sun exposure through a closed window is not an effective or evidence based treatment for elevated bilirubin levels.
  15. The correct answer is A (Possible neonatal sepsis requiring urgent medical evaluation without delay): Lethargy, poor feeding, temperature instability, and mottled, dusky skin together are recognized warning signs of possible neonatal sepsis, a serious and potentially life threatening condition that can present subtly in the first days of life. These findings are not consistent with normal newborn sleepiness, simple mild dehydration, or an expected finding of the first week. This combination of findings requires urgent medical evaluation without delay given the risk of rapid deterioration in neonatal sepsis.
  16. The correct answer is B (Audible swallowing, an appropriate weight trend, and regular wet diapers): Audible swallowing during feeds, an appropriate weight trend, and regular wet and soiled diapers together are reliable clinical indicators that a breastfeeding newborn is receiving adequate milk intake. A newborn who sleeps through every feeding without waking, feeds infrequently with long gaps, or has minimal wet diapers with no stooling would instead raise concern about inadequate intake. The midwife should use this combination of findings rather than any single sign alone to assess feeding adequacy.
  17. The correct answer is B (Promptly arrange follow up with a pediatrician or relevant specialist): An abnormal newborn screening result requires prompt follow up with a pediatrician or the relevant specialist as directed by the provincial screening program, since early identification and treatment of many screened conditions significantly improves outcomes. Waiting until the next scheduled well baby visit at 2 months would delay necessary evaluation for a potentially time sensitive condition. Disregarding the result or repeating the panel only if requested by the family would not meet the standard of care for an abnormal screening result.
Case C901

A term newborn is delivered at home following an uncomplicated vaginal birth. The midwife performs skin-to-skin contact and prepares to assess the newborn's transition using standard scoring criteria at one and five minutes of life.

  1. The correct answer is D (Heart rate, respiratory effort, muscle tone, reflex irritability, and colour): the Apgar score is a rapid, standardized assessment of a newborn's transition to extrauterine life performed at one and five minutes after birth. Each of the five components is scored 0, 1, or 2, and the total guides decisions about resuscitation and ongoing observation. It is not a diagnostic test but a communication tool used consistently across Canadian midwifery and obstetric practice. The other listed combinations mix in items, such as blood pressure, blood glucose, or weight, that are not part of the standard Apgar criteria.
  2. The correct answer is A (A score of 5, indicating moderate respiratory and tone depression at transition): scoring each component gives one point for a heart rate under 100, one point for slow irregular respirations, one point for some flexion of limbs, one point for grimace on stimulation, and one point for acrocyanosis, for a total of 5. This score reflects a newborn who needed some stimulation but is not in severe distress. A score this low still warrants close ongoing observation and reassessment at five minutes, and the midwife should be prepared to support the newborn further if the score does not improve.
  3. The correct answer is B (Continue routine care, maintaining skin-to-skin contact and monitoring for improving colour): a five-minute Apgar of 8 with a heart rate over 100, a good cry, and only mild acrocyanosis reflects a newborn who has transitioned well and needs no active intervention. Acrocyanosis alone, without central cyanosis, is a normal finding in the first hours of life and typically resolves as circulation stabilizes. Continued skin-to-skin contact supports thermoregulation, bonding, and early breastfeeding, and the midwife should simply keep observing the newborn's colour and behaviour as part of routine postpartum care.
  4. The correct answer is B (A newborn's large surface area relative to weight and wet skin promote evaporative heat loss): newborns have a proportionally much larger body surface area compared to their weight than adults, and wet skin immediately after birth accelerates evaporative heat loss. This physiology, combined with limited subcutaneous fat and an immature ability to shiver, makes rapid drying, skin-to-skin contact, and delaying the first bath essential interventions. Recognizing this mechanism is why standard newborn care emphasizes immediate drying and warmth rather than relying on the newborn's own limited heat-generating capacity.
  5.
