The correct answer is C (Continue skin-to-skin contact with the mother and add a warmed blanket layer): a temperature of 36.0 degrees Celsius with mild lethargy and reluctance to feed reflects early, mild cold stress that typically responds well to conservative warming measures. Skin-to-skin contact with the mother is a highly effective and evidence-based first-line strategy for rewarming a mildly cold newborn while also supporting feeding. The midwife should recheck the temperature after a short interval, and if it does not improve or the newborn's condition worsens, further assessment and possible physician consultation would be warranted.
  6. The correct answer is D (A white or absent reflex instead of the expected symmetric red-orange glow): a normal red reflex appears as a symmetric red-orange glow in both pupils when light is shone into the eyes, reflecting light bouncing off a healthy retina. A white, dull, or absent reflex can indicate serious conditions such as congenital cataracts or retinoblastoma and requires urgent referral to a pediatric ophthalmologist for further evaluation. Prompt identification and referral are important because early treatment of these conditions can significantly affect long-term visual outcomes for the newborn.
Case C907

A term newborn born at 39 weeks gestation appears visibly jaundiced at 30 hours of age. The mother's blood type is O positive and the newborn's blood type is A positive. The newborn is breastfeeding but has had only two wet diapers since birth.

  1. The correct answer is C (ABO blood group incompatibility combined with inadequate breastfeeding intake in the first day): a mother with type O blood and a newborn with type A blood creates a genuine risk for ABO incompatibility, which can accelerate red blood cell breakdown and raise bilirubin levels. Combined with minimal wet diapers suggesting low fluid and caloric intake, this newborn has two overlapping risk factors for significant hyperbilirubinemia. Recognizing both factors together, rather than either one alone, helps the midwife appropriately prioritize closer monitoring and earlier bilirubin testing.
  2. The correct answer is C (A pathologic process such as hemolysis, infection, or significant blood group incompatibility): jaundice appearing within the first 24 hours of life is never considered purely physiologic and always warrants prompt investigation. This early timing suggests an underlying pathologic driver of bilirubin production, such as hemolytic disease from blood group incompatibility, infection, or another significant condition, rather than the normal, gradual physiologic jaundice that typically appears after the first day. Recognizing this timing distinction is central to appropriate consultation and timely bilirubin testing in midwifery practice.
  3. The correct answer is C (A weight loss of more than ten percent from birth weight by day three): a weight loss exceeding ten percent of birth weight is a well-recognized indicator of inadequate breastfeeding intake and correlates with higher risk for significant hyperbilirubinemia due to reduced fluid intake and delayed clearance of bilirubin through stool. This finding, combined with the low wet diaper count already noted, would strengthen the picture of insufficient milk transfer. Identifying this pattern early allows the midwife to intervene with focused feeding support and closer monitoring before the situation worsens further.
  4. The correct answer is B (Obtain a transcutaneous or serum bilirubin level and consult with a physician promptly): given the combination of early-onset jaundice, ABO incompatibility risk, and signs suggestive of inadequate breastfeeding, objective bilirubin measurement is the appropriate next step to guide management. Consulting a physician aligns with standard Canadian midwifery guidelines for indications requiring collaborative care, since significant hyperbilirubinemia can progress quickly and may require treatment such as phototherapy. Reassurance alone or unsupported interventions like sun exposure would not adequately address the identified risk factors in this newborn.
  5. The correct answer is C (Between 24 and 72 hours of age, after feeding is underway): newborn screening blood spot samples are typically collected between 24 and 72 hours of age because certain metabolic markers, such as those for phenylketonuria, need time to accumulate after feeding begins to be reliably detected. Collecting the sample too early, such as immediately at birth, can produce false negative results for some conditions. This timing balances early detection of treatable conditions with the need for feeding to be established, and it is a standard part of routine newborn follow-up across Canada.
Case C912

A breastfeeding newborn is seen by the midwife at day 4 of life. The newborn weighed 3400 grams at birth and now weighs 3150 grams. The mother reports six wet diapers and three yellow, seedy stools in the past 24 hours, with a strong latch.

  1. The correct answer is A (A loss of approximately 7 percent, within the expected normal range for day 4): calculating the weight difference of 250 grams from a birth weight of 3400 grams gives a loss of approximately 7 percent, which falls within the commonly accepted normal range of up to about 7 to 10 percent for breastfed newborns in the first several days of life. This finding, alongside adequate wet diaper and stool output and a strong latch, is reassuring and does not suggest inadequate feeding. The midwife can interpret this weight loss as part of the expected physiologic pattern rather than a concerning trend.
  2. The correct answer is D (Six wet diapers, three seedy stools, a strong latch, and expected weight loss): adequate breastfeeding is best assessed by looking at multiple findings together rather than any single indicator in isolation. Six or more wet diapers and several yellow, seedy stools within 24 hours, combined with a strong latch and weight loss within the expected range, together provide strong reassurance that milk transfer is sufficient. Relying on only one of these findings, such as latch quality alone, would not give the midwife a complete picture of overall feeding adequacy for this newborn.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reassure the family and continue routine follow-up with the usual newborn schedule): given the reassuring combination of adequate wet diapers, appropriate stooling, a strong latch, and weight loss within the normal range, this newborn is showing signs of adequate feeding and normal transition. No additional intervention such as supplementation, hospital admission, or stopping breastfeeding is indicated at this time. The midwife should continue standard postpartum follow-up, providing ongoing support and monitoring as part of routine newborn and breastfeeding care.
  4. The correct answer is D (A dislocatable hip suggestive of developmental dysplasia of the hip): a palpable clunk felt during the Barlow maneuver indicates that the femoral head is being displaced out of the acetabulum, which is a classic finding of developmental dysplasia of the hip. This finding differs from a fixed, irreducible dislocation and instead represents an unstable but reducible hip that requires further evaluation and referral. Early identification through routine newborn hip examination allows for timely orthopedic assessment and, if needed, early treatment to support normal hip development.
  5. The correct answer is A (To prevent vitamin K deficiency bleeding, since newborns have low reserves at birth): newborns are born with low vitamin K stores and immature gut flora that would normally help produce this vitamin, placing them at risk for vitamin K deficiency bleeding, a rare but potentially serious condition. A single injection shortly after birth provides adequate stores to reduce this risk significantly during the vulnerable early weeks of life. This is distinct from immune function, jaundice prevention, or bowel motility, none of which are the primary purpose of vitamin K administration.
  6. The correct answer is B (Document the informed refusal and continue providing respectful ongoing care): Canadian midwifery care is grounded in the principle of informed choice, which means parents have the right to decline recommended interventions for their newborn after receiving balanced information about risks and benefits. The midwife's professional responsibility is to thoroughly document this informed refusal in the newborn's chart and continue to provide respectful, non-judgmental, ongoing care to the family. Refusing further care, acting covertly, or involving child protective services without clear evidence of harm would not be appropriate responses to a fully informed parental decision.
  7. The correct answer is D (Untreated hypothyroidism can cause irreversible intellectual disability if treatment is delayed): congenital hypothyroidism, if left undetected and untreated, can lead to severe and permanent intellectual disability because thyroid hormone is essential for normal early brain development. Newborn screening allows detection before clinical symptoms typically appear, enabling treatment with thyroid hormone replacement to begin promptly, often preventing the developmental consequences entirely. This is why congenital hypothyroidism screening is considered one of the most valuable components of the Canadian newborn screening panel.
Case C919

A midwife is doing a home visit on day 2 for a newborn born at 40 weeks gestation. The newborn has a respiratory rate of 72 breaths per minute with grunting and nasal flaring, and appears lethargic with poor feeding since the previous night.

  1. The correct answer is A (Grunting and nasal flaring together with a respiratory rate of 72): grunting and nasal flaring are classic signs of significant respiratory distress in a newborn, reflecting the newborn's attempt to maintain lung volume and improve oxygenation. Combined with a markedly elevated respiratory rate of 72 breaths per minute, well above the normal newborn range, these findings together strongly suggest a serious underlying respiratory or systemic problem. This combination is far more concerning than any single isolated finding and warrants urgent medical evaluation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Arrange immediate emergency transfer to hospital for urgent pediatric assessment): the combination of tachypnea, grunting, nasal flaring, lethargy, and poor feeding in this newborn represents a significant deviation from normal newborn behaviour and falls well outside the scope of routine midwifery management. These findings together are consistent with possible serious illness, such as sepsis or respiratory compromise, that requires urgent hospital-based evaluation. Delaying transfer for a follow-up visit or simply advising increased feeding would put this newborn at unnecessary risk.
  3. The correct answer is A (A single brief episode of sneezing after a diaper change): a brief episode of sneezing is a normal newborn reflex commonly triggered by minor stimuli such as light, cold air, or handling, and it is not, by itself, a sign of illness. In contrast, a sustained elevated respiratory rate, grunting with nasal flaring, or new lethargy with poor feeding are each individually concerning findings that warrant further evaluation. Recognizing which isolated findings are benign helps the midwife avoid unnecessary alarm while still remaining vigilant for genuinely concerning signs.
  4. The correct answer is A (Possible neonatal sepsis or significant cardiorespiratory compromise needing urgent workup): the combination of tachypnea, grunting, nasal flaring, lethargy, and poor feeding is a recognized clinical picture consistent with serious neonatal illness, including sepsis or significant cardiorespiratory compromise. These findings together are far more concerning than any benign explanation such as normal weight loss, simple jaundice, or typical sleep-wake cycling. Prompt recognition of this pattern is essential so that the midwife can facilitate urgent physician evaluation and appropriate hospital-based workup without delay.
  5. The correct answer is C (This presentation falls outside normal midwifery scope and requires urgent physician involvement): a newborn showing signs of possible sepsis or significant respiratory compromise requires collaborative or transferred care, consistent with provincial guidelines for consultation and transfer that govern Canadian midwifery practice. Managing this newborn independently, waiting to reassess later, or permanently stepping away from the case would all fail to meet the standard of care for this level of clinical urgency. Midwives remain involved in coordinating care even after transfer, supporting the family throughout the hospital-based evaluation.
  6. The correct answer is C (A clavicle fracture sustained during a difficult vaginal delivery of the shoulders): an asymmetric Moro reflex combined with localized crepitus and tenderness over the clavicle is a classic presentation of a clavicle fracture, which can occur during a vaginal birth complicated by difficulty delivering the shoulders. This is generally a benign injury that heals well with conservative management, but it still requires documentation, gentle handling of the affected arm, and appropriate follow-up. Distinguishing it from a brachial plexus injury, which involves nerve rather than bone, is important for accurate assessment and family counselling.
  7. The correct answer is C (Regaining birth weight, since most healthy newborns do so by two weeks of age): the majority of healthy breastfed newborns regain their birth weight by approximately two weeks of age after the expected initial postnatal weight loss. Confirming this milestone at the two-week visit provides strong reassurance that feeding has been adequate and that the newborn's growth trajectory is on track. Continued weight loss or a stalled weight at this stage would instead raise concern and prompt further feeding assessment and possible intervention.
  8. The correct answer is B (Keep the cord stump clean and dry and let it separate on its own): current evidence-based Canadian practice recommends dry cord care, meaning the umbilical cord stump should be kept clean and exposed to air without the routine application of alcohol, antibiotic ointments, or tight coverings. This approach supports natural drying and separation of the cord stump, typically within one to three weeks, while minimizing the risk of irritation or infection. Parents should be advised to fold diapers below the cord and watch for signs of infection such as redness, swelling, or discharge.
  9. The correct answer is D (Neonatal hypoglycemia, due to excess fetal insulin production before birth): infants of mothers with poorly controlled gestational diabetes are exposed to higher glucose levels in utero, which stimulates the fetal pancreas to produce excess insulin. After birth, once the maternal glucose supply is removed, this excess insulin can cause a rapid drop in the newborn's blood glucose, making neonatal hypoglycemia a well-recognized and clinically significant risk that requires close monitoring and early feeding in the first hours of life.
  10. The correct answer is D (Critical congenital heart disease that may not be apparent on physical exam alone): pulse oximetry screening measures oxygen saturation in the newborn's blood and is specifically designed to detect critical congenital heart disease, some forms of which can be present without obvious symptoms or abnormal findings on a standard physical examination. This screening complements, rather than replaces, the heel-prick blood spot test, which instead screens for metabolic, endocrine, and genetic conditions such as congenital hypothyroidism and cystic fibrosis.
  11. The correct answer is C (Provide balanced information and refer the family to an appropriate provider): newborn male circumcision is generally outside the typical clinical scope of Canadian midwifery practice, so the midwife's appropriate role is to provide the family with balanced, evidence-based information about the procedure and refer them to a physician or other qualified provider who performs it. Performing the procedure herself, refusing to discuss it, or presenting it as medically necessary for every newborn would all be inconsistent with respectful, informed decision-making support.
  12. The correct answer is C (Place the newborn supine on a firm, separate sleep surface without loose bedding): current evidence-based safe sleep guidance recommends placing newborns on their backs on a firm, separate sleep surface free of soft bedding, pillows, and loose blankets, as this position and setup is strongly associated with a reduced risk of sudden infant death syndrome. Prone positioning, bed sharing, and soft bedding are all associated with increased risk and should be actively discouraged during anticipatory guidance provided by the midwife before discharge from a home birth.
Case C931

A 29-year-old woman with well-controlled type 1 diabetes attends her midwife for preconception counselling. She plans to conceive within six months, currently takes insulin and folic acid 0.4 milligrams daily, and has a body mass index of 24. Her most recent HbA1c result was 7.2 percent.

  1. The correct answer is C (Take a high-dose folic acid supplement of 5 milligrams daily starting now): Pre-existing insulin-dependent diabetes independently raises the baseline risk of neural tube defects above that of the general population, so Canadian preconception guidance recommends a high dose of 5 milligrams of folic acid daily rather than the standard 0.4 milligram dose. This higher dose should ideally begin at least three months before conception and continue through the first trimester. Her midwife should coordinate this recommendation alongside her ongoing diabetes management plan.
  2. The correct answer is A (Lower her HbA1c below 6.5 percent with her diabetes care team's support): Achieving an HbA1c below 6.5 percent before conception is associated with a significantly lower risk of congenital anomalies, miscarriage, and other adverse outcomes compared with higher pre-pregnancy values. Her current 7.2 percent result suggests further optimization is needed before she actively attempts to conceive. Working closely with her diabetes care team allows safe insulin adjustment while she works toward this target.
  3. The correct answer is B (Check her rubella IgG status and vaccinate now if she is non-immune): Rubella infection during pregnancy carries serious risks to the fetus, so confirming immunity before conception allows any needed vaccination to be given safely, since the live vaccine is contraindicated once pregnancy has begun. If she is found non-immune, she should be advised to avoid conception for at least four weeks after vaccination. This screening is a routine part of comprehensive preconception counselling.
  4. The correct answer is A (Arrange preconception consultation with a diabetes-in-pregnancy team before she conceives): Pre-existing insulin-dependent diabetes falls outside routine low-risk midwifery care and warrants collaborative planning with a team experienced in diabetes and pregnancy, even before conception occurs. This reflects the consultation and transfer principles that guide Canadian midwifery practice when a finding or history falls outside normal scope. Early collaboration supports optimal glycemic control and coordinated monitoring once she becomes pregnant.
  5. The correct answer is A (The combined pill is contraindicated because aura increases her stroke risk): Migraine with aura is a recognized contraindication to combined hormonal contraception because the estrogen component further increases an already elevated risk of ischemic stroke in this population. Progestin-only or non-hormonal methods are safer alternatives for her. This counselling point should be discussed clearly before any prescription is provided.
Case C936

A 34-year-old woman who is 6 weeks postpartum and exclusively breastfeeding presents to her midwife requesting contraception counselling. She has no identified contraindications and specifically wishes to avoid combined hormonal methods while she continues to breastfeed her infant.

  1. The correct answer is B (A progestin-only method such as the mini-pill or a contraceptive implant): Progestin-only methods do not carry the theoretical concerns about milk supply sometimes associated with estrogen-containing contraception and align with her stated wish to avoid combined hormonal methods while breastfeeding. Options such as the progestin-only pill or a subdermal implant are considered compatible with breastfeeding. This choice supports both her contraceptive needs and her breastfeeding goals.
  2. The correct answer is C (Exclusive breastfeeding, amenorrhea, and an infant younger than six months): The lactational amenorrhea method is only considered reliable when all three criteria are met together, exclusive breastfeeding with no significant supplementation, absence of menstrual bleeding since delivery, and an infant under six months of age. If any criterion is not met, effectiveness drops substantially and an additional method should be used. Her midwife should review all three conditions with her before she relies on this method alone.
  3. The correct answer is B (Insertion at this six-week visit is generally considered safe and appropriate): Postpartum copper intrauterine device insertion is generally considered safe from around four to six weeks postpartum once the uterus has involuted appropriately, and it does not require her to interrupt breastfeeding. This timing allows for long-acting contraception without hormonal exposure. Her midwife should still assess for any individual contraindications before proceeding.
  4. The correct answer is A (Screening every three years using cytology, or longer intervals with HPV testing): Current Canadian cervical screening guidance for average-risk women without prior abnormal results generally recommends cytology screening every three years, or extended intervals when primary HPV testing is used instead. Annual screening is no longer recommended for this population since it does not improve outcomes and increases unnecessary interventions. Her age and normal history place her in this routine screening interval.
Case C940

A 26-year-old woman's routine cervical screening shows a low-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion with a positive high-risk human papillomavirus result. She is asymptomatic, has no history of abnormal cytology, and asks her midwife to explain what this result means.

  1. The correct answer is A (It shows mild cell changes linked to HPV that often resolve spontaneously): A low-grade squamous intraepithelial lesion with positive high-risk HPV reflects mild cellular changes on the cervix caused by HPV infection, and most such changes resolve on their own as the immune system clears the virus. It does not indicate invasive cancer and does not mean her cervix is entirely normal either. Clear, accurate explanation helps reduce her anxiety while ensuring she understands the need for follow-up.
  2. The correct answer is D (Referral for colposcopy to further evaluate the cervical abnormality directly): A low-grade lesion combined with a positive high-risk HPV result typically warrants referral for colposcopy so the cervix can be directly visualized and, if needed, biopsied to rule out higher-grade disease. This follow-up pathway reflects standard Canadian cervical screening guidelines for this combination of results. Waiting years for a repeat test or taking no action would delay appropriate evaluation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Midwives can order screening but must refer abnormal results for further assessment): Ordering cervical cytology and HPV testing falls within the midwifery scope of practice in most Canadian jurisdictions, but an abnormal result such as this one requires consultation or referral for colposcopy, which is typically outside independent midwifery management. This reflects the collaborative consult-and-refer model central to Canadian midwifery care. The midwife remains involved in supporting the woman through this referral process.
  4. The correct answer is C (Most HPV infections in young women clear spontaneously within one to two years): The majority of high-risk HPV infections, particularly in younger women, are cleared naturally by the immune system within one to two years without ever progressing to significant disease. Persistent infection over a longer period is what carries meaningful risk for progression, which is why follow-up monitoring is recommended rather than immediate aggressive treatment. This evidence-based reassurance can reduce unnecessary anxiety while still supporting appropriate follow-up.
  5. The correct answer is D (Routine chlamydia and gonorrhea screening should be offered given her age and risk): Sexually active women under twenty-five, particularly those with multiple recent partners and inconsistent condom use, are considered higher risk for chlamydia and gonorrhea even in the absence of symptoms, so routine screening is recommended regardless of symptom status. Waiting for symptoms to appear would miss many asymptomatic infections that can still cause complications. Offering screening proactively aligns with evidence-based well-woman care.
Case C945

A 22-year-old woman presents for a well-woman visit reporting two new sexual partners over the past year and inconsistent condom use. She mentions mild vaginal discharge but denies pain, fever, or abnormal bleeding.

  1. The correct answer is D (Vaginal or urine testing for chlamydia and gonorrhea along with a physical exam): Given her recent new partners, inconsistent condom use, and reported discharge, testing for chlamydia and gonorrhea alongside a focused physical examination is the appropriate initial approach to identify a likely sexually transmitted cause. A complete blood count and thyroid panel would not address her presenting concern, and imaging alone would not identify an infectious cause. This targeted approach reflects standard well-woman assessment for her symptoms and risk profile.
  2. The correct answer is B (A single recommended oral antibiotic course, with retesting in three months): Uncomplicated chlamydia infection in a nonpregnant woman is typically treated with a standard recommended oral antibiotic regimen, and guidelines recommend retesting around three months later to check for reinfection rather than treatment failure. Watchful waiting is inappropriate since untreated chlamydia can lead to complications such as pelvic inflammatory disease. Hospital-based intravenous treatment is not required for this uncomplicated presentation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Partner notification and treatment to prevent reinfection and further transmission): Partner notification and treatment are essential components of chlamydia management, since untreated partners can reinfect the woman and continue transmitting the infection to others. This is typically supported through public health processes alongside her own treatment plan. Leaving partner treatment solely to symptom development would allow ongoing asymptomatic transmission within the community.
  4. The correct answer is B (Cycle lengths between 21 and 35 days are generally considered normal): Normal adult menstrual cycle length typically falls within a range of about 21 to 35 days, with some individual variation considered acceptable rather than a fixed 28-day standard. Cycles outside this range, or significant changes from a woman's own usual pattern, may warrant further evaluation. Helping her understand this normal range supports accurate self-monitoring going forward.
Case C949

A 39-year-old woman presents to her midwife with heavy, prolonged menstrual bleeding over the past four months, requiring hourly pad changes on her heaviest days. She has no known bleeding disorder and reports increasing fatigue and difficulty concentrating at work.

  1. The correct answer is A (Complete blood count and ferritin level to assess for anemia): Given her four months of heavy prolonged bleeding and new fatigue, checking a complete blood count and ferritin level is an appropriate initial step to identify iron deficiency anemia resulting from ongoing blood loss. This information also helps determine urgency and guides further investigation into the underlying cause of her bleeding. A pelvic ultrasound remains useful but should accompany, not replace, this basic laboratory assessment.
  2. The correct answer is B (Uterine fibroids or perimenopausal hormonal changes common at her age): At 39 years old, new-onset heavy menstrual bleeding is commonly associated with structural causes such as uterine fibroids or with hormonal fluctuations that often begin during the perimenopausal transition. A congenital bleeding disorder presenting for the first time at this age would be unusual, and pregnancy-related bleeding does not fit her presentation of an ongoing four-month pattern. Considering age-appropriate causes helps guide appropriate further investigation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Consult with or refer to a physician for further investigation such as imaging): Persistent heavy menstrual bleeding of unclear cause in a woman of this age falls outside independent midwifery management and warrants consultation or referral for further investigation, such as pelvic ultrasound or additional testing to identify the underlying structural or hormonal cause. This reflects the collaborative consult-and-refer approach central to Canadian midwifery scope. Managing this independently or dismissing her symptoms would not meet the standard of appropriate well-woman care.
  4. The correct answer is D (Oral iron supplementation along with dietary counselling on iron-rich foods): Confirmed iron deficiency anemia from ongoing heavy bleeding is appropriately managed with oral iron supplementation combined with dietary counselling on iron-rich foods, while investigation into the underlying bleeding source continues in parallel. Withholding treatment until the cause is confirmed would allow her anemia and fatigue to worsen unnecessarily. Blood transfusion would only be considered for more severe or symptomatic anemia.
  5. The correct answer is A (Arrange urgent assessment given signs suggesting hemodynamic compromise from bleeding): Soaking a pad every thirty minutes alongside dizziness and a racing heart suggests significant ongoing blood loss with possible hemodynamic compromise, which requires urgent assessment rather than reassurance or a delayed follow-up plan. These symptoms represent a meaningful change from her earlier presentation and should prompt escalation of care. Prompt evaluation can identify the need for more immediate intervention to stabilize her condition.
  6. The correct answer is A (She should discuss individualized mammography screening timing based on her own risk): Current Canadian guidance supports individualized discussion of mammography screening timing for women in their forties, taking her personal risk factors and preferences into account rather than applying a single rigid rule. Formal monthly breast self-examination is no longer emphasized as mandatory, though general breast awareness remains valuable. This shared decision-making approach reflects informed choice principles within well-woman care.
  7. The correct answer is D (Oral emergency contraception can still be effective when taken within this timeframe): Oral emergency contraception remains effective when taken within a window of several days after unprotected intercourse, so her presentation at 30 hours still falls well within the effective timeframe for most available options. Effectiveness generally decreases the longer treatment is delayed, so encouraging prompt use remains important. There is no mandatory waiting period before taking emergency contraception.
  8. The correct answer is B (Bacterial vaginosis for this woman, and vulvovaginal candidiasis for her friend): A thin, grayish, fishy-smelling discharge without itching is classically associated with bacterial vaginosis, while a thick, itchy, cottage-cheese-like discharge is the typical presentation of vulvovaginal candidiasis. Recognizing these distinct clinical patterns helps guide appropriate testing and treatment for each woman. Neither presentation is typical of trichomoniasis, gonorrhea, chlamydia, or pelvic inflammatory disease as described.
  9. The correct answer is B (Lifestyle counselling and symptom management can be offered, with referral if needed): Midwives can provide lifestyle counselling and general symptom management strategies for perimenopausal symptoms such as hot flashes and sleep disruption, while consulting or referring for options such as hormone therapy when appropriate or requested. This reflects the midwife's role in well-woman care beyond the childbearing years within provincial scope. Refusing to discuss these symptoms or mandating a single treatment approach would not reflect appropriate, individualized care.
  10. The correct answer is C (HPV self-collection sampling is becoming available as an alternative in some areas): Some Canadian jurisdictions are introducing HPV self-collection sampling as an alternative to a clinician-performed speculum examination, which can reduce a barrier to screening for women who find the traditional examination uncomfortable or anxiety-provoking. This option is not yet universally available everywhere, but represents an evolving part of current cervical screening programs. Blood or urine testing does not currently replace cervical cell sampling for this purpose.
  11. The correct answer is A (Refer promptly for evaluation, since postmenopausal bleeding always warrants investigation): Any vaginal bleeding occurring after twelve months of amenorrhea in a postmenopausal woman is considered abnormal and warrants prompt evaluation to rule out serious causes, including endometrial pathology. Waiting for heavier bleeding or a longer observation period before acting would delay identification of a potentially significant underlying cause. This principle applies even when the bleeding is light or occurs only once.
  12. The correct answer is D (Discuss her options and refer her for surgical consultation as needed): Within an informed choice framework, the midwife's role is to discuss permanent contraception options thoroughly, including risks, benefits, and alternatives, and then refer her for surgical consultation since tubal ligation itself falls outside the midwifery scope of practice. Refusing to discuss the topic or falsely claiming it is unavailable would not support her right to informed decision-making. This collaborative approach respects her autonomy while directing her to the appropriate provider for the procedure.
Case C961

A 34-year-old client at 39 weeks gestation with an uncomplicated pregnancy tells her midwife she wants to decline the recommended group B streptococcus screening swab. She has listened to the full explanation of risks and benefits and remains firm in her decision.

  1. The correct answer is D (Respect her informed refusal and document the discussion and her decision): Informed choice is a foundational principle of Canadian midwifery practice, and a capable client has the right to decline any recommended test or intervention once she has received adequate information. The midwife must record that risks, benefits, and alternatives were discussed and that the client made a voluntary, informed decision. Declining GBS screening alone is not an indication for consultation or transfer of care, and repeated pressure to change her mind would undermine her autonomy.
  2. The correct answer is D (The specific risks and benefits discussed and confirmation of her voluntary decision): A legally defensible record of informed refusal must reflect that the client understood the specific information relevant to her decision and chose freely without coercion. A bare statement that she declined does not demonstrate the informed nature of the choice. A second professional's signature or the midwife's personal opinion about the reasonableness of the decision are not required elements of a valid informed refusal record.
  3. The correct answer is B (Manage as GBS status unknown and apply risk-based intrapartum antibiotic guidelines): When GBS status is unknown at the onset of labour, standard practice is to apply the intrapartum risk factor criteria used for unknown status rather than assuming either colonization or its absence. Withholding antibiotics or taking no action based solely on her earlier screening refusal ignores present intrapartum risk factors. Reporting a lawful prior refusal to the regulatory college is not appropriate and is unrelated to current clinical management.
Case C964

A midwife caring for a client in early labour at home notes recurrent late decelerations on intermittent auscultation despite maternal position changes and hydration. The client remains hemodynamically stable and is progressing well in labour.

  1. The correct answer is C (Initiate urgent consultation and arrange transfer to hospital for further assessment): Recurrent late decelerations that persist despite maternal position changes and hydration are a nonreassuring fetal heart rate finding that falls outside normal midwifery scope for home birth management. Provincial consultation and transfer guidelines require obstetric involvement for this finding regardless of maternal hemodynamic stability. Continuing to manage the situation at home or simply waiting risks delaying recognition of fetal compromise.
  2. The correct answer is A (The provincial Indications for Discussion Consultation and Transfer of Care guideline): Provincial guidelines of this type set out specific clinical findings, including nonreassuring fetal heart rate patterns, that require consultation or transfer of care from a midwife to a physician. The Canadian Midwifery Regulators Council sets national competencies rather than clinical consultation triggers, and a hospital nursing policy does not govern a midwife's out-of-hospital consultation decisions. Personal judgment alone is not an adequate substitute for the established guideline.
  3. The correct answer is A (The midwife remains involved in a collaborative consultative role as appropriate): Consultation and transfer of care in the Canadian midwifery model is not an all-or-nothing handoff, and depending on the level of transfer the midwife often continues to provide support and collaborative care alongside the consulting physician. Her legal responsibility does not simply end at the moment of transfer, and resigning from the case is not required or appropriate. Documentation duties remain shared according to each provider's own scope of involvement.
  4. The correct answer is C (Report the suspected child abuse or neglect to the local child welfare authority): Regulated health professionals across Canada have a mandatory legal duty to report reasonable suspicion of child abuse or neglect to the designated child welfare authority, and an inconsistent explanation for the pattern of bruising meets that reasonable suspicion threshold. Confronting the parent or relying on other family members to decide is not a substitute for this legal reporting duty. Simply documenting the observation without reporting does not fulfill the midwife's obligation.
  5. The correct answer is C (No, mandatory reporting duties override confidentiality and do not require consent): Mandatory child welfare reporting legislation specifically overrides ordinary confidentiality obligations, so a regulated professional must report reasonable suspicion of abuse or neglect without needing parental permission. Waiting for consent, for a specific request from the authority, or for agreement from another professional would improperly delay a legally mandated report. The duty to report arises from the professional's own reasonable suspicion.
Case C969

A midwifery client requests a complete copy of her prenatal and birth records after transferring her ongoing care to a family physician in another city. She also asks whether her partner may access the same records without her separate written authorization.

  1. The correct answer is B (The record must be accurate, timely, and considered a permanent legal document): Health records created during midwifery care are legal documents that must be accurate and completed in a timely manner, and this obligation does not change once a client transfers ongoing care elsewhere. Records cannot be summarized informally, kept only in anticipation of litigation, or destroyed simply because the client has changed providers. Retention and accuracy requirements continue regardless of the reason for record completeness being questioned.
  2. The correct answer is C (She has a legal right to access and receive a copy of her own health records): Privacy legislation across Canadian jurisdictions gives individuals a legal right to access and obtain copies of their own personal health information, subject to limited exceptions that rarely apply to routine prenatal and birth records. A court order is not required for a client to obtain her own records, and she is entitled to receive copies rather than being restricted to viewing them in person. Her request does not depend on the new provider first asking for the records.
  3. The correct answer is D (No, privacy legislation requires her explicit consent before releasing records to anyone else): Personal health information belongs to the client, and provincial privacy legislation requires her explicit authorization before records are disclosed to a third party, including a partner, regardless of his involvement in her care. Being present at appointments or at the birth does not create an automatic right of access. The reason he lacks access is the consent requirement itself, not his status as an emergency contact.
  4. The correct answer is B (Send records securely with her documented consent directly to the receiving provider): Transferring health records between providers should occur through a secure method, supported by the client's documented consent, sent directly to the receiving provider rather than through the client herself or an unsecured channel. An unencrypted email attachment risks a privacy breach, and requiring a lawyer to request routine records introduces unnecessary delay and cost. Direct secure provider-to-provider transfer with consent is the standard and appropriate approach.
  5. The correct answer is D (Her demonstrated capacity to understand and appreciate the decision, not her age alone): Under the mature minor doctrine recognized across Canadian jurisdictions, a minor's ability to provide valid informed consent depends on her demonstrated capacity to understand the relevant information and appreciate the consequences of her decision, not on reaching a specific chronological age. Parental consent or a court order are not automatically required if the minor herself demonstrates sufficient capacity. Capacity is assessed individually rather than assumed from age alone.
  6. The correct answer is D (Obtain obstetric consultation per the provincial breech birth practice guideline): A confirmed breech presentation in labour falls outside normal low-risk parameters, and provincial guidelines require obstetric consultation before proceeding with a planned vaginal breech birth, even when the midwife has additional breech skills training. Proceeding entirely independently or scheduling an unrelated induction does not meet this consultation requirement. Refusing to continue care outright is not the appropriate professional response to a request for consultation-required care.
  7. The correct answer is B (Report the case to public health as legally required despite her request): Reportable communicable diseases must be disclosed to public health authorities under provincial legislation regardless of a client's personal preference, because this reporting duty exists to protect broader public health rather than being subject to individual consent. Honouring her request to withhold the report would place the midwife in breach of a clear legal obligation. Reporting cannot be delayed pending future pregnancy complications or referred to a colleague's discretion.
  8. The correct answer is A (Add a clearly dated late entry noting it was added after the fact): The legally correct way to correct or complete a health record after the original entry was made is to add a clearly dated and identified late entry explaining that the information is being added retrospectively. Altering the original entry to appear complete from the start, leaving the omission uncorrected, or destroying and rewriting the record would all compromise the integrity of the legal record. Transparency about the timing of the addition preserves the record's evidentiary value.
  9. The correct answer is A (The midwife must obtain the client's specific informed consent before posting): Sharing an identifiable photograph of a client's newborn on a public platform involves personal health information and requires the client's specific informed consent, separate from any general clinic policy about marketing. The fact that the mother is not shown or that the newborn cannot personally object does not remove the parent's right to control disclosure of the child's image and information. Consent must be obtained before any posting occurs, not assumed from silence.
  10. The correct answer is B (The college reviews the complaint and requests a written response from the midwife): Regulatory college complaints processes typically begin with an initial review of the complaint followed by a request for the midwife's written response before any further investigation or hearing occurs. Immediate revocation or a mandatory practice suspension at this early stage would bypass procedural fairness owed to the midwife. A complaint is not automatically dismissed simply because a lawyer is not yet involved.
  11. The correct answer is C (Remedial education, practice restrictions, reprimand, fines, or licence suspension or revocation): Provincial regulatory legislation typically gives a discipline committee a graduated range of possible outcomes proportionate to the seriousness of the misconduct found, from remedial education and practice restrictions through to suspension or revocation of registration. Committees do not file criminal charges directly, since that is a separate legal process outside college jurisdiction. A single fixed outcome, such as only a verbal warning or automatic permanent revocation, does not reflect the real range of available measures.
  12. The correct answer is A (Obstetric consultation is required given the increased risk associated with prior cesarean): A planned home birth after cesarean carries increased risk, most notably of uterine rupture, so most provincial guidelines require obstetric consultation before this care plan proceeds, even when the client otherwise meets standard eligibility criteria. Meeting eligibility criteria alone does not remove the consultation requirement, and a signed waiver does not substitute for the required medical consultation. A second midwife's opinion alone is not equivalent to the obstetric consultation the guidelines specify.
Case C981

A registered midwife in her sixth year of practice is randomly selected by her provincial regulatory college for a peer chart review as part of the mandatory Quality Assurance Program. She is asked to submit five client charts, complete a written self-assessment tool, and participate in a peer assessor interview scheduled for the following month.

  1. The correct answer is A (To confirm the midwife continues to practise safely and competently for the protection of the public): Quality assurance programs exist because self-regulating colleges hold a public interest mandate rather than a professional advocacy role. Random and scheduled review of practice, including chart audits and self-assessment, allows the college to identify learning needs early rather than waiting for a complaint to arise. This process is developmental and supportive in nature, not punitive, and is kept separate from the formal discipline process. Protecting the public through ongoing competency monitoring is the legal foundation for the self-regulation granted to the midwifery profession in every Canadian province.
  2. The correct answer is D (A trained colleague who supports reflective, non-judgmental review rather than investigation or discipline): Peer assessors are practising midwives trained by the college to conduct collegial, constructive conversations about practice rather than to investigate or impose sanctions. Their role is to help the midwife reflect on documentation, clinical reasoning, and any gaps identified, then support development of a learning plan if one is needed. Because assessors are peers rather than college staff, lawyers, or investigators, the process is designed to feel collaborative rather than adversarial for the participating midwife. Findings from routine quality assurance review remain separate from the complaints and discipline process unless a serious public safety concern is uncovered.
  3. The correct answer is A (Development of an individualized learning plan with a defined college follow-up timeline): A minor, correctable gap identified during a quality assurance review is exactly the kind of finding this program is designed to catch and remediate early, before it affects client safety. The college typically works with the midwife to create a targeted learning plan, which may include continuing education, mentorship, or a repeat review within a defined timeframe. Escalation to the discipline committee is reserved for serious, unresolved, or public-safety-threatening concerns, not routine documentation improvement of this kind. This graduated, supportive response reflects the developmental purpose that continuing competency programs share across Canadian midwifery regulatory colleges.
  4. The correct answer is B (It allows midwives to critically examine decision-making together in a supportive, blame-free environment) Collegial peer review after a significant clinical event gives midwives a structured opportunity to reflect on their reasoning, timing, and communication without the pressure of a formal investigation hanging over the discussion. This kind of review strengthens clinical judgment and can identify practice or system improvements that benefit future clients in similar situations. It does not replace mandatory chart documentation, college reporting obligations, or any process actually required by regulation itself. The goal is continuous quality improvement driven by the profession's own commitment to reflective, evidence-based practice.
  5. The correct answer is C (Reflection-on-action, because the insight emerged afterward through structured collegial review) Schon's model distinguishes reflection-in-action, which happens in the moment as a clinician adapts to a changing situation, from reflection-on-action, which happens afterward when deliberately reviewing what occurred. Recognizing a communication gap during a later peer review meeting is a clear example of reflection-on-action, since the insight comes from retrospective analysis rather than in-the-moment adjustment. This distinction matters because both types of reflection are core continuing competency skills expected of every practising midwife. Reflection-on-action often generates concrete practice changes that a midwife can carry forward into future clinical situations.
  6. The correct answer is C (Protecting and serving the interests of the public rather than the interests of the profession itself) Self-regulation is a privilege granted by provincial legislation on the condition that the college acts in the public interest rather than to advance midwives' own economic or professional interests. This is why colleges set entry-to-practice requirements, standards of practice, continuing competency programs, and a complaints and discipline process for their registrants. Government retains oversight authority and can revoke a profession's self-regulation if a college fails to protect the public adequately. Understanding this public-interest foundation is essential to interpreting every regulatory requirement a practising midwife will encounter throughout her career.
  7. The correct answer is D (Attending a peer-reviewed workshop on postpartum hemorrhage management and documenting the specific learning outcomes) Continuing competency programs require structured, clinically relevant learning activities, along with documentation of what was learned and how it will change future practice. A workshop on a core clinical topic, combined with a documented learning outcome, satisfies both the content and documentation expectations most Canadian midwifery colleges require. Passive activities without reflection, or purely administrative tasks like insurance renewal, do not meet the definition of continuing education for this purpose. Colleges typically audit a sample of midwives each renewal cycle to confirm that learning activities genuinely meet these established standards.
  8. The correct answer is D (Verifying documentation quality and clinical reasoning in order to support ongoing competent practice) Chart review conducted under a quality assurance program checks that documentation reflects sound clinical reasoning, appropriate consultation, and adherence to standards of practice throughout a midwife's care. It is a developmental tool meant to catch gaps early and support learning, not a mechanism for comparing midwives against one another or making employment decisions within a practice group. Charts remain a core piece of evidence in this process precisely because they demonstrate the informed choice discussions, assessments, and decisions that occurred during care. Strong documentation habits therefore directly support successful continuing competency review over time.
  9. The correct answer is A (Establishing clear communication protocols in advance while respecting each provider's own scope of practice): Interprofessional collaboration standards emphasize clear, timely communication and mutual respect for each provider's scope of practice between midwives and other healthcare professionals. Establishing protocols in advance, rather than only during an emergency, builds the trust needed for smooth consultations and transfers whenever they later become necessary. Neither profession should be expected to defer entirely to the other, since safe collaborative maternity care depends on integrating each provider's expertise appropriately. Strong interprofessional relationships built ahead of time are associated with better outcomes and smoother transfers of care.
  10. The correct answer is D (Providing a complete and timely summary of care to support genuine continuity for the client) Professional accountability standards require midwives to support continuity of care whenever a client transfers between providers, which means promptly sharing a complete, accurate summary of assessments, decisions, and care plans. Continuity of care is a core value of the Canadian midwifery model, and gaps in information transfer between practices can genuinely compromise client safety. Midwives do not have authority to judge whether another registered practice is qualified to accept a client, since that determination ultimately belongs to the client and the receiving provider. Timely record transfer also reflects respect for the client's autonomy and ownership of her own health information.
Case C991

A midwife experiences a client outcome that did not proceed as expected during a home birth that ultimately required intrapartum transfer to hospital. Before her scheduled peer review meeting, she completes a structured written reflection using a recognized reflective practice framework taught during her midwifery education.

  1. The correct answer is B (Gibbs' reflective cycle, which moves through description, feelings, evaluation, and future action planning) Gibbs' reflective cycle is a widely used structured model across health professions that guides a practitioner through describing an event, exploring feelings, evaluating what went well or poorly, analyzing why, and planning future action. This structure helps ensure that reflection is thorough rather than superficial, moving beyond a simple retelling of events toward genuine professional learning. The Angoff method and Apgar scoring system serve entirely different purposes unrelated to structured reflective practice of this kind. Using a recognized framework also produces documentation that a peer reviewer or the college can meaningfully assess afterward.
  2. The correct answer is D (It allows her to organize her thinking and identify specific learning points well in advance) Preparing a written reflection beforehand gives a midwife time to process a difficult event thoughtfully rather than reacting in the moment, which tends to produce more specific and genuinely actionable insights. This preparation also makes the peer review conversation itself more productive, since colleagues can respond to concrete reflections rather than starting entirely from scratch. Reflection of this kind does not replace open disclosure with the client about what actually occurred, which remains a separate professional and ethical obligation. Structured self-reflection is considered a core continuing competency skill precisely because it improves the quality of collegial learning conversations.
  3. The correct answer is A (A personal action plan that supports concrete future improvement in her own practice): The end goal of structured reflective practice is a concrete, personal action plan that translates insight into a specific change in future behaviour, such as adjusting how risk information is communicated to a labouring client. This is a self-directed professional development outcome, distinct from any formal regulatory action such as discipline, a complaint, or a mandatory external report to a ministry. Reflective practice is most valuable when it produces changes the midwife can genuinely carry forward into her next similar clinical situation. Colleges expect this kind of self-generated learning as part of ongoing competency, separate from any external review findings.
  4. The correct answer is C (Ensuring midwives maintain current knowledge of the legal and regulatory framework governing their own practice): Jurisprudence modules focus on legislation, standards of practice, scope of practice, and the complaints and discipline process, rather than on clinical skills, which are addressed through separate continuing education activities. Regulatory frameworks change over time, so periodic review helps midwives stay current on their legal obligations, including consent, documentation, and reporting duties owed to clients and the college. This requirement supports self-regulation by confirming that registrants genuinely understand the system that holds them accountable to the public. It is distinct from, and does not replace, professional liability insurance or hospital-specific privileging processes.
  5. The correct answer is A (Requesting completion of the outstanding requirements within a clearly defined remediation period) An incomplete continuing education log discovered during a routine audit is typically addressed through a proportionate, remediation-focused response, giving the midwife a defined timeframe to complete any outstanding requirements. Immediate revocation or referral to discipline is reserved for serious or repeated non-compliance, not a first-time administrative gap identified through a routine audit of this kind. This graduated approach reflects the developmental philosophy underlying continuing competency programs across Canadian midwifery regulatory colleges. Colleges generally escalate consequences only if a midwife fails to respond to reasonable remediation requests over time.
  6. The correct answer is C (Working respectfully within a network of care while maintaining autonomous accountability for midwifery care) International Confederation of Midwives standards describe midwives as autonomous professionals who collaborate respectfully with other providers as part of an integrated maternity care network, rather than functioning either in isolation or under blanket physician direction. Midwives remain accountable for the midwifery care they personally provide even while working collaboratively within a broader interprofessional team. Effective collaboration includes routine, ongoing communication rather than only coordination during emergencies. This balance of professional autonomy and collaboration reflects the core professional identity of midwifery internationally, including within Canada.
  7. The correct answer is C (A structured transition to practice that reinforces clinical judgment, confidence, and professional identity) Mentorship relationships, whether formal college programs or informal peer arrangements, support new practitioners in consolidating clinical judgment and confidence during the vulnerable early transition-to-practice period. This kind of support is a recognized professional development activity, but it does not replace regulatory requirements such as the jurisprudence module or quality assurance participation, which remain mandatory regardless of mentorship. Many colleges and midwifery associations formally encourage mentorship for newly registered midwives specifically because it improves retention and overall practice quality. Mentorship complements, rather than substitutes for, structured continuing competency programs required by regulation.
  8. The correct answer is D (The complaints process addresses concerns raised externally, while quality assurance is a proactive routine review): The complaints process is reactive, triggered by a specific concern raised by a client, colleague, or other party, and can lead to investigation and discipline if the concern is warranted. Quality assurance programs, by contrast, are proactive and routine, applying to all registrants on a defined cycle regardless of whether any concern has actually been raised. Both processes support the same public-interest mandate but operate through entirely different mechanisms and timelines. Understanding this distinction helps midwives recognize that quality assurance participation is mandatory and separate from, not contingent on, any complaint history.
  9. The correct answer is A (The ability to conduct objective, constructive, and confidential reviews of a colleague's practice) Peer assessors are trained specifically to review colleagues' practice objectively, provide constructive feedback, and maintain strict confidentiality throughout the process, since trust in that confidentiality is essential for honest engagement with review. Assessors hold no independent authority to suspend registration or impose sanctions, since those powers belong exclusively to the college's registration and discipline committees. Findings from these reviews are also never shared publicly, which protects both the assessed midwife and the integrity of the developmental review process. A balanced focus on both strengths and areas for growth supports genuine learning rather than a purely critical review.
  10. The correct answer is C (Active participation in continuing competency programs, peer review, and adherence to standards of practice) Self-regulation only functions if individual registrants genuinely engage with continuing competency programs, peer review, and the standards of practice that define safe midwifery care, since the college's public-interest mandate depends on registrants upholding these obligations themselves. Avoiding contact with the college, or relying on an employer to fill this role, would undermine the entire regulatory model, since employers hold no legal authority or mandate to regulate the profession. This obligation applies equally across hospital, birth centre, and home birth practice settings alike. Ongoing professional engagement is therefore both a legal requirement and a core professional value in Canadian midwifery identity.
